# Design: what does a screen require?

The claims about screens. Apple's Human Interface Guidelines are the client's first reference, and the taste behind every claim is logical, science-based design: a number from a source over an opinion (Timmy, 2026-10-13). Where Apple and a government design guide disagree, the claim follows Apple (Timmy, 2026-10-15).

The claims are filed under the one of Apple's eight design principles in `top-tier.md` that each serves, as its evidence, in that order (Timmy, 2026-11-05). Under each claim's heading, a line says what on a screen calls for it and which of Nielsen's heuristics it also answers; a review pulls the claims whose triggers the screen has, and every claim marked `every screen` on every screen. The triggers are listed in `INDEX.md`.

## Purpose: Make something meaningful.

### The screen shows the bones

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**A screen shows the thing the app holds, or the thing is changed so what the screen shows is true.** A screen that presents a notion the model beneath does not hold confuses unless the illusion is perfect, and it never is: the model's limits break through in the wrong words, or data is quietly altered to fit. Either the model is shown, so a user can use what they know of it, or the thing is restructured to carry its own rules; in both cases there is one model, and the screen is not a second one. Check: does this screen show a thing the model does not hold, and does every message on it speak the thing's own words? Source: Evans, chapter 3, Binding Model and Implementation, Letting the Bones Show: Why Models Matter to Users.

Example, not part of the claim: A banking app's accounts screen. Pass: it lists Current and Savings, the two accounts the bank holds, each with its own balance. Fail: the same screen shows one Total pot, and a payment is then refused with a message about savings funds never shown.

### A new screen is walked through the seven stages

Called for by: new. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Before a new screen is built, or a new action added to one, the seven questions a user asks are written down, what do I want, what are my options, what can I do now, how, what happened, what does it mean, am I done, and the part of the screen that answers each is named.** These are Norman's seven stages of action, goal, plan, specify, perform, perceive, interpret, compare; the first four questions are answered by what the screen shows before the act, signifiers, constraints and mappings, the last three by feedback and the user's model of the thing, and a question with no answer on the screen is a gulf, of execution or of evaluation in Norman's words. It is a tool reached for at a new screen or a new action, never a rite on every change. The cycle in three, goal, execute, evaluate, runs at many levels at once, and under evaluate the person can back out of a step that did not take them toward the goal. Check: for the new screen or action in this change, which part answers each of the seven questions, and which question has none? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 2, The Psychology of Everyday Actions (The Seven Stages of Action; The Seven Stages of Action: Seven Fundamental Design Principles), `reference/books/norman-2013/02-chapter-2-the-psychology-of-everyday-actions.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 8, Limits on Attention Shape Thought and Action (Our Thought Cycle: Goal, Execute, Evaluate), `reference/books/johnson-2010/10-chapter-8-limits-on-attention-shape-thought-and-action.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A parking app's new Extend button. Pass: before it is built, each of the seven questions gets a part, what happened answered by the new end time under it. Fail: the same walk-through finds no part for what happened, as the end time never changes.

### On the way to the goal, or not seen

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**What a screen needs a person to notice sits on the path to what they came for and is worded in the goal's words; a thing off that path is not ignored but unseen.** Our goals filter our perceptions: things unrelated to our goals tend to be filtered out preconsciously, never registering in our conscious minds; when people navigate through software, they scan screens quickly and superficially for items that seem related to their goal, and they often don't even notice the rest. Ensure that at every point in an interaction, the information users need is available, prominent, and maps clearly to a possible user goal, so users will notice and use the information; and if nothing in a spot attracts attention, our eyes may never move our fovea to that spot, so we may never see what is there. A control is named with the word the person has in mind for their goal, read literally: if the goal is a reservation, anything reading buy, flight or ticket draws the eye and bargain hotels does not, and a Search labelled Query is missed. Observers told before a brief showing which feature they would report judged it more accurately than those told after it (Chapman, reported by Miller), so what a person is to check is named before the thing to check appears. A notice off the person's task is missed (the page links to "evidence that people often miss them", banner-blindness findings), so anything "directly relevant to the thing the user is doing on that page" goes in the content itself, and set-apart text "near to other visually prominent elements" is not relied on for what must be seen. A founder of a notes app asks of each design "If someone's drunk, can they understand it?", since the people meeting it are not attending to it: "They're tired. They're distracted. They're going to scroll past it." Check: for each thing this screen needs the person to see, what are they looking for when it is shown, does it share those words, and does it sit between them and the thing? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 1, We Perceive What We Expect (Perception Biased by Goals; Design Implications, Understand the goals), `reference/books/johnson-2010/03-chapter-1-we-perceive-what-we-expect.md`; chapter 6, Our Peripheral Vision Is Poor (Is the Visual Periphery Good for Anything?), `reference/books/johnson-2010/08-chapter-6-our-peripheral-vision-is-poor.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 8, Limits on Attention Shape Thought and Action (We Follow Information "Scent" Toward Our Goal), `reference/books/johnson-2010/10-chapter-8-limits-on-attention-shape-thought-and-action.md`; Miller, The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two: Some Limits on Our Capacity for Processing Information, Psychological Review 63 (1956), p. 89 (Absolute Judgments of Multidimensional Stimuli), `reference/papers/miller-1956-the-magical-number-seven.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Notification banner (When not to use this component), `reference/govuk/components/notification-banner.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Inset text (When not to use this component), `reference/govuk/components/inset-text.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Alert (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/alert.md`; Zhao, profiled by Blumenrose, The Ivanisms that power Notion, Designer Founders (2025) (1. Keep it dumb), `reference/interviews/zhao-notion.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: An airline app's fare list. Pass: the baggage limit, 1 bag, 23 kg, sits under each fare price the traveller is comparing. Fail: the same limit sits in a footer under Terms, below the Book button.

### Each fork offers every goal

Called for by: steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**At each point where a screen asks the person to choose, there is an option for every goal a person brings there, and each option says which goal it leads to.** Designers need to understand the goals users are likely to have at each decision point in a task, and ensure that each choice point provides options for every important user goal and clearly indicates which option leads to which goal; on the ATM screen "Pay your dentist by funds transfer" sends the eye to the wrong option; don't just display a bunch of options that appear equally likely and expect users to know how to start, especially if they won't perform the task very often. Each link says where it goes in its own words, or with the words around it, so the person can decide whether to follow it. A link lands on the thing it names, not on a place from which the person must look for it again: "Avoid links to pages that require further user action to locate the intended information." A tap on a notification opens the thing it is about, since "When people tap a notification or its preview, they expect your app to display related content". A notification carries no button that merely opens the app. Check: for each choice on this screen, what goals bring a person here, which has no option, and which option could be read as leading to two goals? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 8, Limits on Attention Shape Thought and Action (We Follow Information "Scent" Toward Our Goal; We Prefer Familiar Paths, Guide users to the best paths), `reference/books/johnson-2010/10-chapter-8-limits-on-attention-shape-thought-and-action.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 10, Learning from Experience and Performing Learned Actions are Easy; Problem Solving and Calculation are Hard (Implications for User Interface Design, Guide users toward their goals), `reference/books/johnson-2010/12-chapter-10-learning-from-experience-and-performing-learned-a.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.4.4 Link Purpose (In Context), Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/link-purpose-in-context.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.4.9 Link Purpose (Link Only), Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/link-purpose-link-only.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.4.4 Link Purpose (In Context) (Intent of Link Purpose (In Context)), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/link-purpose-in-context.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Link (Usability should), `reference/uswds/components/link.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Notification actions: Avoid providing an action that merely opens your app), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Offering help (Creating tips: Use buttons to direct people to information or options), `reference/hig/offering-help.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A cash machine's main menu. Pass: options read Withdraw cash, Check balance, Pay a bill and Transfer money. Fail: the same menu offers only Withdraw and Balance, with paying a bill hidden under Other services.

### No step away to get there

Called for by: steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**No step toward a goal reads as a step away from it, since a person will not take an action that seems to lead away from what they want, even where it is the only road.** Don't make users choose actions that seem to take them away from their goal in order to achieve it; the person who wants the chapter's page numbers to start at 23 will not tap Insert Page Numbers, since the chapter already has page numbers and they do not want to insert any; from its first screen, software should show users the way to their goals. Check: on the way to each goal through this screen, which tap reads, by its word or its place, as leaving the goal behind? Source: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 8, Limits on Attention Shape Thought and Action (Our Thought Cycle: Goal, Execute, Evaluate; We Prefer Familiar Paths, Guide users to the best paths), `reference/books/johnson-2010/10-chapter-8-limits-on-attention-shape-thought-and-action.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A slideshow app, for starting slide numbers at 5. Pass: Format slide numbers sits beside the existing numbers. Fail: the same app sets the start number only under Insert Slide Numbers, though the slides already have numbers.

### A concept is added on evidence

Called for by: new. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**A new concept, a thing, an action or a setting the person must learn, is added only on evidence that many of the people the app is for need it, since each one multiplies the complexity of the rest; and two levels are offered before ten.** Simpler means fewer concepts; in a to-do list application, are two priority levels, low and high, enough, rather than 1 to 10; resist the pressure to add functionality "in case a user might want it" unless there is considerable evidence that a significant number of potential users really need it, because each concept interacts with most of the others, so complexity grows not linearly but multiplicatively. The same holds for a value's steps: a list of minutes may run in quarter hours rather than sixty steps where that serves. Statuses start at the fewest, "for example 'Completed' and 'Incomplete'", since "The more you add, the harder it is for users to remember them", and more are added only "if your user research shows there's a need for them". In four online focus groups of 18 adults aged 60 to 83, apps were described as "cluttered and filled with unnecessary features, creating confusion and discouraging sustained use", even by "those who self-identified as digitally competent"; this is what they said, not a measured cost. A design team's account, practice that cites no study, says how the cost arrives: software "contorts out of shape one useful feature at a time: a new control here, another state there, an exception for one workflow followed by yet another. Even when each decision makes sense in isolation, over time, the product begins to feel crowded, inconsistent, and hard to use." A founder of a notes app built from parts that combine gives the same reason in a builder's words: "When you add a new LEGO brick, it might pollute the whole thing", and "So you have to think system-first." Check: what concept does this change add, what evidence from the people the app is for says they need it, and is its scale the smallest that serves? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 11, Many Factors Affect Learning (We Learn Faster When Operation Is Task-Focused, Simple, and Consistent, As simple as possible), `reference/books/johnson-2010/13-chapter-11-many-factors-affect-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Pickers (Best practices: Consider providing less granularity when specifying minutes in a date picker), `reference/hig/pickers.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Complete multiple tasks (Show the status of the tasks), `reference/govuk/patterns/complete-multiple-tasks.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Tag (Showing multiple statuses), `reference/govuk/components/tag.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Time picker (When to use), `reference/uswds/components/time-picker.md`; Berry et al., Designing an mHealth App to Encourage Uptake of Muscle-Strengthening Exercise in Older Adults: Co-Design Focus Group Study, JMIR Aging (2026) (Results: Theme 3: Drawbacks of Current Mobile Apps), `reference/studies/PMC13022557.md`; Aufmann and Heckel, A calmer interface for a product in motion (2026) (introduction), `reference/essays/linear-calmer-interface.md`; Zhao, profiled by Blumenrose, The Ivanisms that power Notion, Designer Founders (2025) (2. Build systems, not a suite of features), `reference/interviews/zhao-notion.md`. It sits beside Complex, not confusing: that one governs how what is there is structured, this one what is added.

Example, not part of the claim: A to-do app's task details. Pass: a task's priority is Normal or High. Fail: the same task has priority 1 to 10 and a separate urgency setting, added in case someone wants them.

### Whether it is for them, before the work

Called for by: steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Where a task has conditions a person may not meet, the screen finds out whether they meet them before asking for the work of the task, and where they do not, says why and what to do instead.** Asking first "helps users save time as it tells them upfront whether they are eligible to use it and if they need it". "You should help people find out whether your service is right for them as soon as you can." "If a user is not eligible to use your service, explain why and, if possible, tell them what they should do instead." Plain conditions, "such as an age limit or fixed deadline", are stated before the start. Check: what conditions does this task have, and how much has the person entered before they learn whether they meet them? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Check a service is suitable (introduction; How it works; Presenting results), `reference/govuk/patterns/check-a-service-is-suitable.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Start using a service (Eligibility), `reference/govuk/patterns/start-using-a-service.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A mortgage app. Pass: three questions at the start show that it cannot lend on a holiday home, before any document is asked for. Fail: the same app asks for payslips and bank statements, then turns down the holiday home at the end.

## Agency: Let people do things their own way.

### Every action is answered at once

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Every action on a screen is answered within a tenth of a second by what happened, in words that read as what to do next, quiet for the small and seen for the large; where the work takes longer, the screen shows at once that the request was received and is under way.** Where the wait is known it is given, the longest where a range is known. Feedback that only says something happened is worse than none, since it distracts without informing; too much is ignored, and then the one that mattered is missed; a delay past what is expected reads as the action not taken. A warning that tells the person what they already know, or sounds when nothing is wrong, is silenced, and when a different incident happens nobody notices; a warning both attracts attention and says what it is about. The quiet answer for the small is the menu item that blinks once before the menu closes, so quick that most people are unaware of it and would miss it if it were gone; pop-ups, sound, motion and blinking used often bring habituation, so the message that matters is blocked. The numbers: cause and effect are seen as one past 0.14 second, so a control has 0.1 second to show it was pressed, the work finishing later; the app has about a second to do what was asked or say how long it will take, and past two seconds has left the pace of a conversation; a thing dragged or scrolled stays under the finger, the gesture handled "as responsively as possible": ink behind a pen on the screen, the nearest measured case of a hand moving a thing on the glass, is seen to lag past a hundredth of a second, and past 0.1 second a dragged thing can no longer be placed where it is wanted; where the real thing cannot keep up, a light stand-in stays under the finger and the real work follows. Any action that holds the screen while it works shows that it is busy, even one that usually finishes before the sign could be seen, the sign tied to the work so it stops when the work does; and a wait past a few seconds shows within a second how much of the whole remains, in a person's units, about four minutes and not 240 seconds. A control the app draws itself shows a pressed state, since without one it can feel unresponsive, and where its work takes a moment the busy sign sits on the control itself with words for what is under way, Checkout becoming "Checking out…". People expect an action to succeed and need to know only when it does not, so a failure is always told and a completed success is confirmed outright only where it matters. At the end of a task, the answer says "what happens next and when" and how to get in touch. Where an action takes the person to another screen, as sending a form does, its answer sits at the top of the screen they land on, not in a box over it: a message about a whole page belongs "at the top of the page where the user is taken next". Where the length of the work is known, or becomes known part way, the wait shows how much is done, since a sign that only turns "doesn't help people estimate how long a task will take". A turning sign is never swapped for a bar of another shape part way through, since "transitioning between them can disrupt your interface and confuse people." A bar moves at the pace of the work, since "Showing 90 percent completion in five seconds and the last 10 percent in 5 minutes can make people wonder if your app is still working and can even feel deceptive". A sign that stops reads as a frozen app, so where the work stalls the screen says so: "provide feedback that helps people understand the problem and what they can do about it." The words beside a wait name what is being done, "accurate and succinct", never "vague terms like *loading* or *authenticating* because they seldom add value." The platform's own controls answer at the finger, the material lighting "from within as a form of feedback", "Starting right under your fingertips", and Apple names the opposite as the mark of an interface "rushed out the door": "You tap a button and you just have to wait for it do something." Apple's talk on gestures asks the same of every touch, "It's not just swipes. It's taps, it's presses, it's every interaction with the object.", and names a delay that hides in the design: a thing that takes a double tap holds back each single tap on it while the screen waits for a second, "about half a second" in Apple's photo viewer, so a double tap is given to a thing only where its single tap can bear the wait. A gesture is answered all through it and not only when it ends, as a button that acts on a firmer press grows under the finger as the press deepens; where one touch may become a tap, a press or a scroll, as on a row of a scrolling list, the screen answers it from the first moment and turns to the one meant as soon as that is clear, since waiting to know which "would have to introduce a delay". In a practice standard that cites no study, the pressed state of a control the app draws is a slight shrink, to between 0.95 and 0.98 of its size within 100 to 160 ms, which "takes the label and icons with it, which is what makes it read as physical". The same holds while the app writes or makes something: "instead of 'Processing…', say 'Finding substitutions for ingredients' or 'Summarizing key themes from your notes.'", since "Specific feedback reduces uncertainty and makes waiting feel purposeful." Where the screen is a web page, a tap on a control is not held back while the browser waits to see whether a second tap will zoom, "The 300ms click delay" of a practice guide that cites no study, in which "Browsers wait after a tap to see whether a second tap is coming, because double-tap zooms." The browser's own grey flash over a tapped thing gives way to the control's pressed look, never to nothing, since removing it alone takes away "the only feedback the browser was giving". Where the work is likely to succeed, the screen shows its result at once and squares it with the reply after, and where the reply is a failure it says so and puts the thing back or offers Undo: "Update the UI immediately when success is likely; reconcile on server response. On failure, show an error & roll back or provide Undo." The second is a limit, not an allowance: in a company report of Google's experiments on its live search traffic, which gives no count of users, a delay of 100 to 400 ms added to the results page cut daily searches per user by 0.2% to 0.6% against a control group, and "users do fewer searches the longer they are exposed", from 0.44% fewer in the first three weeks of a 400 ms delay to 0.74% fewer in the last three, with 0.21% fewer still over the five weeks after the delay was taken away. What people say does not find this cost: in a lab study of 30 adults aged 24 to 45 searching on their own phones, the page hidden behind the words "loading..." for the whole delay, their ratings did not differ significantly across delays from 338 ms up to 3.8 or 5.8 seconds and fell only at 8.7 and 13 seconds, where "users felt significantly more tensed, tired, terrible, frustrated and sluggish". The authors add that "we cannot entirely discount the possibility that there are sizeable unconscious effects", so a delay is judged by measuring it and what people do, not by asking whether they noticed it. A turning sign the app draws itself, where the platform's own cannot be used, turns briskly, since in a practice account that cites no study "a faster-spinning spinner makes the app seem to load faster, even though the load time is the same". Check: for each action on this screen, what does the user see within a tenth of a second, does it say what happened and what next, and if the work waits on anything beyond the screen, what shows it under way? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 1, The Psychopathology of Everyday Things (Feedback), `reference/books/norman-2013/01-chapter-1-the-psychopathology-of-everyday-things.md`; chapter 2, The Psychology of Everyday Actions (Blaming the Wrong Things; Human Cognition and Emotion, The Behavioral Level), `reference/books/norman-2013/02-chapter-2-the-psychology-of-everyday-actions.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? Example, not part of the claim: A shop's product page with an Add to Bag button. Pass: within a tenth of a second the button shows pressed and reads Adding…, then the bag badge reads 1. Fail: the same button shows nothing for two seconds while the server replies.

### The state and the place are on the screen

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**A screen shows the state of the thing it holds and where the user is in a sequence, so the result of an action is judged from the screen and not from memory of the action.** The gulf of evaluation is small when the state is on the screen in a form easy to get, easy to interpret and matching how the user thinks of the thing; what a physical thing signifies by accident, the pages left in a book, a screen must signify on purpose; the thermostat that shows the current, the target and what it is doing is the pattern. Decisions already made and answers already given stay in view once acted upon, since a design that removes every sign of them once made leaves the person asking what still needs to be done. A list made from what the person entered shows the entry above it, since attention moves to the results and the words are gone; and which screen the person is on is told by its look at a glance, one style across the app and a systematic variation per part. The bar of the app's sections stays in view in every section, since a person who loses it forgets which part of the app they are in; a row chosen to go deeper stays marked to show the path taken, a chosen option keeps its mark, and a status sits near the things it describes. Each screen has a title naming its topic or purpose. The place in a sequence is given in its lightest form, such as Question 3 of 9, where the count is reliable, since one team removed a 12-step progress bar with no effect on completion. A count of steps is shown only where the steps run one way, number three or more and will not change with the person's answers, and the count is never itself a way to move between steps, since it "isn't appropriate for nonlinear forms" and is "not to be navigation of its own". What the screen shows is kept current by the app at intervals, the person's pull to refresh a way to hurry it and never the only way: "Don't make people responsible for initiating every update." A count of what is new falls as soon as the person has seen those things: "You don't want people to think there are new notifications available, only to find that they've already viewed them all." After a break of 15 seconds or more, how fast a person takes a task up again depends on what the eye can find on the screen: in 110 adults in a lab, the speed of resuming went with how fast they picked a target out among lookalikes, not with their working memory. Showing the state can be the simpler design: a play and pause control that also shows "where I am and how much time is left" serves the person who comes back to it, since "Sometimes, simple means adding context and information, so people can make informed decisions." A task the person performs for the app, such as scanning their face, gives them "an explicit goal" and shows "their progress toward it", as Face ID "changes the appearance of the tick marks encircling the face as people progress through scanning." A success the screen does not announce is still seen in the state it leaves: Nielsen's failing case is the early command line, where "you only get any feedback from the computer if your command results in an error message", so "No errors meant no information from the computer about the new state, which made it harder for users to compose the following command"; this is practice and cites no study. Check: after any action on this screen, can the user tell from it alone what is held now, what it is doing, and how far along they are? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 2, The Psychology of Everyday Actions (How People Do Things: The Gulfs of Execution and Evaluation; How Technology Can Accommodate Human Behavior, Providing a Conceptual Model for a Home Thermostat), `reference/books/norman-2013/02-chapter-2-the-psychology-of-everyday-actions.md`; chapter 1, The Psychopathology of Everyday Things (Signifiers), `reference/books/norman-2013/01-chapter-1-the-psychopathology-of-everyday-things.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? Our Memory Is Imperfect (Implications of Short-Term Memory Characteristics for User Interface Design, Search results), `reference/books/johnson-2010/09-chapter-7-our-attention-is-limited-our-memory-is-imperfect.md`; chapter 9, Recognition Is Easy; Recall Is Hard (Recognition versus Recall: Implications for UI Design, Use visual cues to let users recognize where they are), `reference/books/johnson-2010/11-chapter-9-recognition-is-easy-recall-is-hard.md`; chapter 10, Learning from Experience and Performing Learned Actions Are Easy (Implications for User Interface Design, Prominently indicate system status and users' progress toward their goal), `reference/books/johnson-2010/12-chapter-10-learning-from-experience-and-performing-learned-a.md`; Apple, Principles of great design, WWDC26 session 250 (2026) (Simplicity, 13:06), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2026-250.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Calibration: Make sure people know how to perform calibration successfully), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Nielsen, AI Is First New UI Paradigm in 60 Years (2023) (Paradigm 2: Command-Based Interaction Design), `reference/essays/nielsen-new-ui-paradigm.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A train ticket app's booking flow. Pass: a header reads Step 2 of 4, Seats, and the chosen 08:15 train stays listed above the seat map. Fail: the same flow shows only the seat map, with no step count or chosen train.

### A second press does one thing

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**A control pressed again before its feedback arrives, or in its absence, does what it does once, never twice and never the reverse of the first press.** When nothing visibly happens a person's next move is to repeat the action, harder; a screen that counted every press has then done it twice, or an even number of presses has done nothing. People press twice from habit, on a slow connection where "they are not given feedback on their action quickly enough", or from a tremor, and one team "discovered that a number of users were receiving invitations twice, because the person sending them was double clicking the 'send' button"; after an act that is done once, such as a payment, going back shows "a sensible message rather than let them perform the action again". Check: if the user taps this control twice, what happens, and is the second tap harmless? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 2, The Psychology of Everyday Actions (Blaming the Wrong Things), `reference/books/norman-2013/02-chapter-2-the-psychology-of-everyday-actions.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 12, We Have Time Requirements (Designing to Meet Real-Time Human Interaction Deadlines, 0.1 second), `reference/books/johnson-2010/14-chapter-12-we-have-time-requirements.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Button (Stop users from accidentally sending information more than once), `reference/govuk/components/button.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Question pages (Back link), `reference/govuk/patterns/question-pages.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A banking app's Send payment button. Pass: a second tap during a slow reply is ignored and one £50 payment goes. Fail: the same button, tapped twice during a slow reply, sends £50 twice.

### Guidance in place, not an error message

Called for by: entry, errors. Cross-check: Nielsen 9, Help users recognize, diagnose, and recover from errors.

**A wrong or incomplete entry gets guidance where it sits, what is wanted and how to fix it, with what was entered kept and the way on left open; the screen never says error, never blames, and never makes the user start over.** What is called an error is bad communication; a person's entry is taken as partly right, and the screen says what is missing beside the place it is missing. In a conversation a misstatement is taken as an approximation to what was meant and met with a question for more, never a warning signal; the action is an approximation to what is desired, and the screen helps the person complete it. The guidance is marked at the entry by a symbol beside it, not by colour alone, since a red word in the periphery is a small blob that can fall into a gap in the visual field; and a message that reports a situation says what to do in it. A message about a problem is direct and neutral in tone, neither oblique nor accusatory nor masking how serious the problem is, and its title says what happened, in what context and why, never "Error" or an error number. Both the passing and the failing answers are kept on the screen, so the person can "see what went wrong", "edit their previous answer" and "avoid re-entering information", and the message avoids "please", "sorry", "oops", "forbidden", "invalid", "illegal", "incorrect" and "you forgot", and any joke, which goes stale for a person who meets the message often; in live services, people shown such messages "understood what went wrong", "knew how to fix the problem" and "were able to recover from the error". At a sign-in check alone, and only where a password is typed, the screen says the details do not match without saying which part, keeps the name or email typed and clears only the password, since saying which part helps someone guessing. A refusal is the moment a person reads guidance they skip elsewhere, so its message may carry the one short reason behind the rule, since "Users will read a message that helps them resolve an error even if they generally won't read documentation". Where the device's own way in does not finish, because the person cancels it or the device holds no passkey for the name, the screen offers by name the other ways in this device has, since the sign-in standard asks to "support alternative authenticator types and allow users to choose the type that best meets their needs". The message is in view, never held behind an icon to tap or hover, since an alert icon is hard to notice and the person may not know a message waits there; and the entry it is about is marked as well as the message. Beside the entry, the message stays in view while the person fixes it, rather than being held in mind. Where the app can tell what was likely meant, the fix is offered as a tap, such as the town that matches the postcode entered, rather than only saying the two do not match. Where the app cannot use what the person asked of it, it says so plainly and coaches the next try, as Image Playground says it is "Unable to use that description", with "example requests that might lead to better results." Where a person's progress through a task the app guides stalls, help comes at once as "actionable recommendations that quickly get them back on track", and it must "never imply that something's wrong or that people are at fault, and never leave people without a clear next step." Check: for each entry this screen refuses, where does the guidance appear, is the entry kept, and does any message here say error or start the user over? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 2, The Psychology of Everyday Actions (Positive Psychology; Falsely Blaming Yourself), `reference/books/norman-2013/02-chapter-2-the-psychology-of-everyday-actions.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? Use an error symbol), `reference/books/johnson-2010/08-chapter-6-our-peripheral-vision-is-poor.md`; chapter 8, Limits on Attention Shape Thought and Action (We Follow Information "Scent" Toward Our Goal), `reference/books/johnson-2010/10-chapter-8-limits-on-attention-shape-thought-and-action.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Alerts (Content: In all alert copy, be direct, and use a neutral, approachable tone; Write a title that clearly and succinctly describes the situation), `reference/hig/alerts.md`; Keep Error Messages Next to Fields; 9. Example, not part of the claim: A doctor's surgery app's registration form. Pass: an empty date of birth field shows a warning symbol and Add your date of birth beneath it, every other field kept. Fail: the same form shows Error 422: invalid input at the top and clears every field.

### Any way a person would write it

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**A field for a number, a date or a time takes any form a person would write it in, and the code turns it into the form it needs once, at the edge; the wanted form is never told only after it is got wrong.** Fussiness about format is the machine's demand put above the person's and is never a requirement of the machine; the lenient field is the screen's, and the model's type stays strict. Most machines require precise commands, forcing people to enter numbers perfectly, and people are not good at great precision and frequently make errors when asked to type sequences of numbers. A long number is shown broken into its parts, and it is entered in one field that takes spaces or punctuation between them, never in one field per part, since a field that strips the spaces makes the number hard to scan or to check, and fields split by part are hard to label for a screen reader and cost more taps. Where a field still needs one form, its label says that form before the entry is made. Stray characters that a person cannot see or did not mean are ignored, not refused: spaces, hyphens, brackets and full stops in numbers and codes, characters before or after a pasted answer, and punctuation added by dictation, since "If these characters caused a validation error, it would be difficult for the user to see and fix". A month is taken as a word as well as a number, since in one live service "hundreds of users were inputting months using full or abbreviated month names and getting an error" and, once names were accepted, "the number of errors has dropped dramatically". A unit symbol the person types into a field that already shows it, such as a £ before an amount, is accepted without a message, since "Some users may miss that the input already has a suffix or prefix". An example value is chosen so it can be read only one way, a date such as 27 3 2007, with a day over 12 and a month under 10, which "helps users enter the date in the correct order and shows them they do not need to include leading zeroes". A two-digit year is turned into four only where one century is possible; where two are, the four digits are asked for beside the field, with the entry kept. The same goes for the space a keyboard's text replacement leaves at the end: "Some input methods add trailing whitespace. The input should trim the value to avoid showing a confusing error message." Check: what forms would a person write in this field, does each one take, and is the turning done once at the edge? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 2, The Psychology of Everyday Actions (How Technology Can Accommodate Human Behavior, Entering Dates, Times, and Telephone Numbers), `reference/books/norman-2013/02-chapter-2-the-psychology-of-everyday-actions.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Understanding Why There Is Error), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 3, We Seek and Use Visual Structure (Structure Enhances People's Ability to Scan Long Numbers), `reference/books/johnson-2010/05-chapter-3-we-seek-and-use-visual-structure.md`; WCAG 2.2, 3.3.2 Labels or Instructions, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/labels-or-instructions.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 3.3.2 Labels or Instructions (Intent of Labels or Instructions), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/labels-or-instructions.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Recover from validation errors (How it works), `reference/govuk/patterns/validation.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Addresses (Allow different postcode formats), `reference/govuk/patterns/addresses.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Date input (How it works; Research on this component), `reference/govuk/components/date-input.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Text input (Prefixes and suffixes), `reference/govuk/components/text-input.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Dates (How to write dates), `reference/govuk/patterns/dates.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Phone number (What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/phone-number.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Date of birth (What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/date-of-birth.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Date picker (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/date-picker.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Memorable date (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/memorable-date.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Text input (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/text-input.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Date of birth (What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/date-of-birth.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Forms: Text replacements & expansions), `reference/vercel/README.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A calendar app's new event time field. Pass: 3pm, 15:00 and 3.00 pm all become 15:00. Fail: the same field takes only HH:MM, and says so only after 3pm is refused.

### A slip costs nothing

Called for by: loses. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Two controls that look alike or sit alike do not differ in cost, and an action that loses what the user has entered or saved is not reached by one tap: it is set apart, made different, or made recoverable.** A slip, the glossary's word for a mis-tap, is certain; a design that assumes it, the watch stem pulled before it will set the time, makes it harmless, and one that does not, Return beside Enter with one losing the work, blames the user for it. Gestures and body movements are fun, but not very precise. Check: which control on this screen loses something if tapped by mistake, what sits or looks like it, and is the loss recoverable? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 2, The Psychology of Everyday Actions (Falsely Blaming Yourself), `reference/books/norman-2013/02-chapter-2-the-psychology-of-everyday-actions.md`; chapter 1, The Psychopathology of Everyday Things (The Paradox of Technology), `reference/books/norman-2013/01-chapter-1-the-psychopathology-of-everyday-things.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 7, Design in the World of Business (As Technologies Change, Will People Stay the Same?), `reference/books/norman-2013/07-chapter-7-design-in-the-world-of-business.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A notes app's editing toolbar. Pass: Done sits at the top right and Delete Note sits in a menu, undoable. Fail: the same toolbar puts Delete Note, shaped like Done, right beside it, deleting at one tap with no way back.

### As it was left

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**Whatever the person comes back to is as they last saw it, and editing a saved thing works exactly as making a new one did; the only change made while it was out of sight is one the person can plainly see they caused, and even that is shown, not slipped in.** The client's rule, in the client's words: "if a user leaves a view of a thing or a thing goes away and then they go back to it, it should be in the same state as it was the last time they saw it. Unless some other action clearly changed it while it was out of view, which even that seems like it would be kind of screwy" (Timmy, 2026-11-06). It covers every way a thing leaves and returns: leaving the app, moving elsewhere in it and back, going back a step, a sheet or card that closes and opens again, and a list the app rebuilds; each comes back with the same entries, choices, scroll, expanded parts and selection. Editing what was saved is the same flow as making it new, in the same steps and order, each filled "as you answered it" (Timmy, 2026-11-06), so going back walks the same steps and finds each as it was last seen; an edit screen of its own, with other fields, another order or values reset, is a second way to do one thing. A change the person caused while away, say by changing the same thing on another screen, shows on return where the change is, and nothing else moves. Guidance agrees: answer pages "should look the same way they did when the user last used them", and answers gone back to are "pre-populated"; Apple asks designs to "Preserve a person’s context" by keeping "content and controls in consistent, predictable positions", and, once a behaviour is set, to "apply it throughout your design" so "new interactions will work the way they expect". The held "A place is kept across an interruption" and "Back is in sight and loses nothing" are two cases of this claim, for leaving the app and for going back inside a task; the held "The screen holds still" keeps a screen unchanged while it is in view, and this claim keeps it so while it is out of view. Check: leave each thing on this screen by every way out it has, close it, go back, switch away, edit it once saved, then return; is everything as it was last seen, does editing look and run exactly like making it, and is any change the person did not make here plainly shown? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Check answers (Let users go back and change their answers), `reference/govuk/patterns/check-answers.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Design principles (Flexibility: Preserve a person's context; Familiarity: Keep visuals and interactions consistent), `reference/hig/design-principles.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A recipe app's saved recipe. Pass: Edit opens the same steps used to write it, every field filled as saved, and the ingredients sheet, closed and reopened, shows the same ticks and scroll. Fail: Edit opens a different single long form with the servings reset to 1, and the reopened sheet starts at the top with the ticks cleared.

### A place is kept across an interruption

Called for by: steps, entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**A screen keeps the user's place across an interruption: what was entered stays entered, and the screen comes back where it was.** People are interrupted continually and must recover their place, and on return they skip a step, repeat one, or lose what they were about to enter; the screen holds the place so the person need not. To resume, the person must recall the goal, where they were in the action and the state of the thing, so the screen on return shows all three, not only the entries. Moving to another part of the app and back is an interruption too, so each part keeps its own place; and people keep several apps open and switch among them often. After being signed out the person goes on with nothing lost once signed back in, what they entered is kept for more than 20 hours idle, and where it is not, the idle time that loses it is said at the start of the task. Where the app fails mid-task, what was entered is kept "so users can resume a journey with re-populated information when the service becomes available again", and answering a consent question mid-task does not lose the person's place in a form. Where a long task can be left and taken up later, the person is told so at its start: "If users can save and resume the form, tell them so up front, so they know this is an option." Where what is on the screen keeps changing while the person is away, memory cannot stand in for the screen: in 110 adults in a lab tracking 5 moving dots among 12 that were hidden for 5, 15 or 30 seconds, accuracy fell and the time to take the task up again rose with each longer break, and after 30 seconds people found 2.53 of the 5 against 2.08 by chance. A list of the steps shown on return does not by itself keep the place: in 42 students in a lab doing six steps in a fixed order, with all six named at the top of the screen, the first step after 2 or 4 seconds of another task was the wrong one 7.3 per cent of the time against 1.5 per cent uninterrupted. What is being recorded is kept as it is made, not only at its end: in posts on X about five top-grossing health and fitness apps, people wrote of a whole record lost when "my phone died at the end, so none of it recorded", and of a disappointment that outweighed what they had done; these are posts, not a measure of how often it happens. Going back to a list finds it scrolled where it was left, "Back/Forward restores prior scroll", and where the app runs as a web page its address holds the tab, the filter or the page that is open, "so share, refresh, Back/Forward navigation work". This claim is one case of the held "As it was left", for leaving the app and coming back. Check: if the user leaves this screen midway, by a lock, a call or another app, what is there when they return? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 2, The Psychology of Everyday Actions (Falsely Blaming Yourself), `reference/books/norman-2013/02-chapter-2-the-psychology-of-everyday-actions.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Designing for Error), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Tab bars (introduction), `reference/hig/tab-bars.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Designing for iOS (App interactions), `reference/hig/designing-for-ios.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.2.5 Re-authenticating, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/re-authenticating.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.2.6 Timeouts, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/21/timeouts.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.2.6 Timeouts (Intent), `reference/wcag/understanding/21/timeouts.md`; GOV.UK Design System, There is a problem with the service pages (How it works), `reference/govuk/patterns/problem-with-the-service-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Cookie banner (Help users keep their place using progressive enhancement), `reference/govuk/components/cookie-banner.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Establish trust (What to do; Usability guidance), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/establish-trust.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Progress easily (What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/progress-easily.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Design principles (Promote continuity, Practical actions), `reference/uswds/design-principles/overview.md`; Labonté and Vachon, Resuming a Dynamic Task Following Increasingly Long Interruptions: The Role of Working Memory and Reconstruction, Frontiers in Psychology (2021) (Materials and Methods; Results), `reference/studies/PMC8247645.md`; Hirsch et al., Opportune moments for task interruptions: examining the cognitive mechanisms underlying interruption-timing effects, Frontiers in Psychology (2024) (Materials and methods; Results), `reference/studies/PMC11775001.md`; Sheen et al., Living well? The unintended consequences of highly popular commercial fitness apps through social listening using Machine-Assisted Topic Analysis: Evidence from X, British Journal of Health Psychology (2025) (Theme 3: Loss of data disrupts the continuity of the self-monitoring experience; Discussion: Implications of quantification-of-the-self on human experience and behaviour), `reference/studies/PMC12541294.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Interactions: URL as state; Scroll positions persist; Deep-link everything), `reference/vercel/README.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A mail app's half-written reply. Pass: after a phone call, the app reopens on the reply, the text and cursor where they were. Fail: the same reply, after a phone call, the app reopens on the inbox and the text is gone.

### Before a constraint, no distinction

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**A difference a user could get wrong is first made not to matter; what must differ is then constrained so the wrong way cannot be taken; what cannot be constrained is signified; and a sign saying how is the last resort and a mark of failure.** Norman's battery: better than a plug that fits one way is a plug that works both ways; better than a label is a shape; and the root need is solved where it can be, the key made irrelevant rather than redesigned. Check: for each way this screen can be used wrongly, was the distinction removed, then constrained, then signified, in that order? Source: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 4, Knowing What to Do: Constraints, Discoverability, and Feedback (Four Kinds of Constraints, Physical Constraints; The Problem with Doors), `reference/books/norman-2013/04-chapter-4-knowing-what-to-do-constraints-discoverability-and.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A mail app's Attach Photo button. Pass: any photo the phone takes attaches, its format converted without a word. Fail: the same button refuses HEIC photos, with a note beside it saying JPG only.

### A constraint is seen before the tap

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**A constraint is visible before anything is done, so an action that cannot be taken now is shown as not available or is absent, never offered live and refused after.** A physical constraint seen in advance restricts the set of actions before the act; unseen, it stops a wrong action only once tried, and the user has learned nothing but a refusal. A control that cannot act now looks plainly different from one that can, and a gesture that cannot act shows why, or the person takes the app for frozen or the gesture for wrong. It governs actions; a place the person goes to is not one, so a tab or a section with nothing in it stays open and says why it is empty, as An empty place says why requires. A part of a task that cannot be started yet says "Cannot start yet" in its row and is not a link, and the kinds of card a form accepts are shown before the number is typed, "so users can see whether their card is supported". Absent is the better of the two where nothing is lost by the control's absence. A dimmed control in the platform's unavailable style stands alone where the screen already shows why it cannot act, such as the empty fields it waits on, and says why beside it only where nothing on the screen does. A control or a field that cannot be used stays reachable for a person moving item by item, so they can learn why it cannot act, since a disabled one gives "lack of meaningful feedback for users of screen readers, and no focus capability for keyboard navigators". Where only some values cannot be chosen, only those are barred and the control stays open, as a calendar with some dates unavailable rather than a calendar shut: "Use date restrictions when only some dates are unavailable." In a touch-and-hold menu an action that cannot run now is left out, not dimmed, since such a menu "displays only the actions that are relevant to the currently selected view or content." A Done that waits, inactive, in the top bar of a view of several steps until the last step needs no reason beside it, since the steps still to come are the reason and each step's heading says which it is. Check: which controls on this screen cannot succeed in some state, and does the screen show that state before the tap? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 4, Knowing What to Do: Constraints, Discoverability, and Feedback (Physical Constraints), `reference/books/norman-2013/04-chapter-4-knowing-what-to-do-constraints-discoverability-and.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Gestures (Best practices: Indicate when a gesture isn't available), `reference/hig/gestures.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Tab bars (Best practices: Don't disable or hide tab bar buttons, even when their content is unavailable), `reference/hig/tab-bars.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Complete multiple tasks (Tasks that cannot yet be started), `reference/govuk/patterns/complete-multiple-tasks.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Payment card details (Help users enter valid card information), `reference/govuk/patterns/payment-card-details.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Button (Disabled buttons), `reference/govuk/components/button.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Button (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/button.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Text input (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/text-input.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Progress easily (What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/progress-easily.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Date picker (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/date-picker.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Date range picker (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/date-range-picker.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Context menus (Best practices: Hide unavailable menu items, don't dim them), `reference/hig/context-menus.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Sheets (iOS, iPadOS), `reference/hig/sheets.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A train ticket app's date calendar. Pass: dates before today are greyed and cannot be picked. Fail: the same calendar lets yesterday be picked, then refuses on Search with Date in the past.

### A reminder has a signal and a message

Called for by: pushes. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where the app reminds, the reminder carries both the signal that something is to be done and the message of what, and arrives at the time or the place it is to be done, never before.** A string on the finger is signal without message, a note is message without signal, and a reminder too early is as useless as none. One thing gets one notification, never a second because the first went unanswered, since "People attend to notifications at their convenience" and after several "people may turn off all notifications from your app." Where the person has hidden previews, the line the system shows in their place names the kind of thing without its details, such as "Reminder" or "Shipment". The signal then still says what kind of thing waits. No signal the person can turn off, whether a badge, a sound or the notification itself, is the only place something important is found. It also waits where the person looks first on opening the app: "Always make sure that you make important information easy for people to find as soon as they open your app." A reminder comes as the system's notification; where what is to be done is done in the app, the notification carries it as a button, such as Mark as Taken, or a tap on it opens that very task, and it never only tells the person what to go and do there. A prompt answered in one tap where it arrives was answered more often than one that opens a run of questions: over six to twelve months in the lives of 177 adults aged 18 to 29, a watch prompt of one question with three one-tap answers was answered 80.0 per cent of the times it came, against 70.7 per cent for a phone prompt of 18 to 23 questions, though device, length, how often it came and pay all differed together. Its words name what is to be done and never ask the person to account for not having done it: in posts on X about five top-grossing health and fitness apps, read qualitatively from 13,799 filtered for negative sentiment, reminders asking why a meal had not been logged were met with being "sick of" the app and with shame ("I'm ashamed I just ate dominos"), and 18 adults aged 60 to 83 in focus groups asked for reminders "in supportive and nonjudgmental language"; both are what people wrote or said, not a measured effect. The time is the person's to set and to change: in focus groups of 18 adults aged 60 to 83, many wanted a reminder in the morning while one said "a reminder later in the afternoon would be beneficial", and in a 28-day pilot of 18 adults aged 20 to 39, 4 changed the reminder times they had chosen, all in the arm that also had spoken reminders. A reminder for what the person has already done is not sent: the app in one pilot sent none for a meal logged before its reminder was due, a design choice the study did not measure apart. Check: for each reminder in this change, does it say both that and what, and does it arrive at the moment of use? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 3, Knowledge in the Head and in the World (Reminding: Prospective Memory), `reference/books/norman-2013/03-chapter-3-knowledge-in-the-head-and-in-the-world.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Best practices: Avoid sending multiple notifications for the same thing, even if someone hasn't responded), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Content: Provide generically descriptive text to display when notification previews aren't available), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Badging: Make sure badging isn't the only method you use to communicate essential information; Content: Consider providing a sound to supplement your notifications), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (introduction; Best practices: Provide concise, informative notifications), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Best practices: Avoid sending a notification that tells people to perform specific tasks within your app), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Ponnada et al., Longitudinal User Engagement with Microinteraction Ecological Momentary Assessment (μEMA), Proceedings of the ACM on Interactive, Mobile, Wearable and Ubiquitous Technologies (2025) (Methodology; Results), `reference/studies/PMC12439519.md`; Sheen et al., Living well? The unintended consequences of highly popular commercial fitness apps through social listening using Machine-Assisted Topic Analysis: Evidence from X, British Journal of Health Psychology (2025) (Theme 4: Emotional and psychological impacts of logging daily activity), `reference/studies/PMC12541294.md`; Berry et al., Designing an mHealth App to Encourage Uptake of Muscle-Strengthening Exercise in Older Adults: Co-Design Focus Group Study, JMIR Aging (2026) (Discussion: Theme 4: Desired App Elements and Features), `reference/studies/PMC13022557.md`; Berry et al., Designing an mHealth App to Encourage Uptake of Muscle-Strengthening Exercise in Older Adults: Co-Design Focus Group Study, JMIR Aging (2026) (Results: Theme 4: Desired App Elements and Features), `reference/studies/PMC13022557.md`; Chikwetu et al., Automated Diet Capture Using Voice Alerts and Speech Recognition on Smartphones: Pilot Usability and Acceptability Study, JMIR Formative Research (2023) (Results: User Engagement Metrics), `reference/studies/PMC10230351.md`; Chikwetu et al., Automated Diet Capture Using Voice Alerts and Speech Recognition on Smartphones: Pilot Usability and Acceptability Study, JMIR Formative Research (2023) (Methods: Personalized Alerts), `reference/studies/PMC10230351.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A pharmacy app that reminds about medicine. Pass: at 8am, when the tablet is due, a notification reads Take 1 blood pressure tablet. Fail: the same app buzzes at 8am with a notification that says only Reminder.

### Sequences differ from the start

Called for by: steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Two sequences a user performs on a screen, one common and one rare, do not share their opening steps; where they must, the rare one shows a reminder at the step where they part.** A capture slip is where, instead of the desired activity, a more frequently or recently performed one gets done instead; it needs part of the action sequences in the two activities to be identical, with one far more familiar, and all it takes is a lapse of attention at the junction where the sequences diverge. Seldom does the unfamiliar sequence capture the familiar one, the more experienced the user the more likely the capture, and whenever possible sequences should be designed to differ from the very start. Making something new and using what exists are told apart from the first word, "Create a password" rather than "Password", and "Presenting the options side by side is not enough because users might miss one of them or not understand the difference". Check: which two paths on this screen begin with the same taps and end differently, which is the common one, and what at the fork says this is the other? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Capture Slips; Minimizing Slips), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), appendix, Well-known User Interface Design Rules (Norman (1983a), Inferences from research), `reference/books/johnson-2010/16-well-known-user-interface-design-rules.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Create accounts (Use clear and consistent language; Create a simple user journey), `reference/govuk/patterns/create-accounts.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A messaging app's compose screen. Pass: Schedule starts from its own clock button beside the text field. Fail: the same Schedule is reached by tapping Send and then a small Later link, so a habitual tap sends the message at once.

### Lookalikes sit apart

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Two controls that do different things do not look alike and do not sit in a row; the pair most easily confused is put furthest apart, and a control that is not part of the operation under way is not on the screen.** A description-similarity slip is to act upon an item similar to the target, the correct action on the wrong object; the more the wrong and right objects have in common, and the more objects present at the same time, the more likely the error. Rows of identical controls or meters is a sure recipe; electronic systems can segregate controls so that easily confused ones are far from one another, and use separate modules so that any control not directly relevant to the current operation is not visible. A task with one clear path carries no links off it, "Make sure the account creation screen is solely about that task. Do not add any distracting content or links", and where the order is clear, menus are left out in favour of a list of the task's parts. Check: on this screen, which two controls could be taken for each other, how far apart are they, and is every control here part of the operation under way? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Description-Similarity Slips; Design Lessons from the Study of Errors, Adding Constraints to Block Errors; Minimizing Slips), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), appendix, Well-known User Interface Design Rules (Norman (1983a), Lessons), `reference/books/johnson-2010/16-well-known-user-interface-design-rules.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Create accounts (Make the sign-up process clear), `reference/govuk/patterns/create-accounts.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Navigate a service (When not to use this pattern), `reference/govuk/patterns/navigate-a-service.md`; GOV.UK Design System, GOV.UK footer (Adding secondary navigation), `reference/govuk/components/footer.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Button group (Default button group, When to consider something else), `reference/uswds/components/button-group/guidance/variants/default/when-to-consider-something-else.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A video call app's bottom bar. Pass: Mute is a microphone at the far left and End Call is red at the far right. Fail: the same bar puts Mute and End Call side by side as identical grey circles.

### No modes, or the mode shown

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**A control means one thing on a screen; where it must mean two things in two states, the state is shown beside the control itself at the moment of the tap, never remembered.** A mode error occurs when a device has different states in which the same controls have different meanings, and it is inevitable in anything that has more possible actions than controls; if I select a mode and then immediately adjust, I am not confused, but what if I get interrupted, or the mode is maintained for considerable periods? Mode errors are especially likely where the equipment does not make the mode visible; designers must try to avoid modes, but if they are necessary, the equipment must make it obvious which mode is invoked. Where the app enters a mode on its own, what it makes in that mode shows which mode made it: a browser whose Skills "are automatically invoked" styles a Skill's answer "to help reinforce that you are actively using a skill"; practice that cites no study. Check: does any control on this screen do a different thing in a different state, and is that state shown next to it when the user taps? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Mode-Error Slips; Minimizing Slips), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 7, Our Attention Is Limited; Our Memory Is Imperfect (Implications of Short-Term Memory Characteristics for User Interface Design, Modes), `reference/books/johnson-2010/09-chapter-7-our-attention-is-limited-our-memory-is-imperfect.md`; Deets, The strategy behind Dia's design (2025) (The novelty budget), `reference/essays/browser-company-dia.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A drawing app's canvas. Pass: with the eraser chosen, its tool button stays highlighted and the cursor is drawn as an eraser. Fail: the same canvas looks identical in pen and eraser, so a stroke made after a break erases.

### Two quantities, two shapes

Called for by: entry, numbers. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Two values a person could type into each other's place are shown in forms that cannot be read as each other, so a number entered under the wrong meaning looks wrong.** After the Airbus accident, where -3.3 entered as an angle of descent was taken by the other mode as -3,300 feet per minute, Airbus changed the display so that vertical speed would always be displayed with a four-digit number and angle with two digits, reducing the chance of confusion; seven a.m. and seven p.m. on a twelve-hour display are the everyday case, hence 24-hour time through most of the world. A unit shows beside the field as well as in its label ("put 'Cost, in pounds' in the input label and use the '£' symbol in the prefix"), never by the symbol alone, which a screen reader does not read out. A unit sits beside a field only where every true answer is in that unit, so a question that may be answered "$1,000" or "a full refund" carries none. Check: for each pair of numbers on this screen, does each carry a shape or a unit of its own, so a value in the wrong field is seen as wrong? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Mode-Error Slips), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Text input (Prefixes and suffixes), `reference/govuk/components/text-input.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Input prefix/suffix (When to consider something else; Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/input-prefix-suffix.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: An oven app's cook screen. Pass: the temperature reads 180 °C and the time reads 0:45, each in its own shape and unit. Fail: the same screen shows 180 and 45 as bare numbers in alike boxes, so 45 typed as the temperature looks fine.

### The answer names what was acted on

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**The answer to an action names the thing it was done to and shows the state that results, since a slip is noticed only as a difference between what was meant and what the screen says happened.** Action slips are relatively easy to detect because it is usually easy to notice a discrepancy between the intended act and the one that got performed, but this detection can only take place if there is feedback: if the result of the action is not visible, how can the error be detected? The best way of mitigating slips is perceptible feedback about the nature of the action being performed, then very perceptible feedback describing the new resulting state, coupled with a mechanism that allows the error to be undone. Where a finger slides off the thing it pressed, the slip is shown or felt as it happens: of 12 adults typing on a phone's touchscreen, some found the plain keyboard "frustrating as there was no feedback when the fingertip had moved off the edge of the button", and with a felt mark for each slip they corrected more of their errors, which the author reads as slips noticed that "could go unnoticed" without it. Check: after each action on this screen, does the answer say which thing it was done to and what that thing now holds? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Detecting Error; Minimizing Slips), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Hoggan, Crossmodal Audio and Tactile Interaction with Mobile Touchscreens, PhD thesis, University of Glasgow (2010) (chapter 6, 6.3.1 Results; 6.4.4 Results), `reference/papers/hoggan-2010-crossmodal-audio-and-tactile-interaction.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A cloud drive's Delete action. Pass: a bar reads Budget.xlsx moved to Bin, with Undo. Fail: the same delete shows only Done, naming no file.

### Undo before friction

Called for by: loses. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Every action a screen takes can be reversed afterwards, several steps back; what the user deletes is moved out of sight and kept, not destroyed; and only an action that cannot be reversed is made harder to take.** Perhaps the most powerful tool to minimize the impact of errors is the Undo command, reversing the operations performed by the previous command wherever possible; the best systems have multiple levels of undoing, so an entire sequence of actions can be undone. Make it possible to reverse actions or make it harder to do what cannot be reversed; when files are "deleted" they are simply moved from sight into the Trash, so the erroneously deleted file can be retrieved. A screen in which mistakes are easy to make and costly discourages exploration, so people keep to familiar paths and never find what is there; one in which they are hard to make, cheap and easy to correct invites it. Undo says before it is taken what it will reverse, "Undo Typing" and not Undo alone, shows the restored thing even where that means scrolling to it, so that people do not repeat it thinking it had no effect, and may reverse a run of related adjustments as one. Apple's principles say the same as a matter of agency: "People accidentally send, change, and delete things all the time", forgiveness is "making it easy for them to undo any of their actions", and "Recovering from the unexpected shouldn't cost people their time or work." What the app made or changed on its own, a draft, a summary or an automatic edit, can be dismissed, reverted or tried again from beside it, with "controls like Edit, Undo, Retry, or Adjust near generated content", since people need "the ability to dismiss new content they don't want, and revert or retry content transformations or other actions they don't agree with." Check: for each action on this screen, how does the user take it back a minute later, and where does a deleted thing go? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Designing for Error; Design Lessons from the Study of Errors, Undo; Confirmation and Error Messages), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 11, Many Factors Affect Learning (We Learn Faster When Risk Is Low), `reference/books/johnson-2010/13-chapter-11-many-factors-affect-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Undo and redo (introduction), `reference/hig/undo-and-redo.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Undo and redo (Best practices: Help people predict the results of undo and redo; Show the results of an undo or redo; Consider giving people the option to revert multiple changes at once; iOS, iPadOS: Briefly and precisely describe the operation to be undone or redone), `reference/hig/undo-and-redo.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Alerts (Best practices: Avoid displaying alerts for common, undoable actions, even when they're destructive), `reference/hig/alerts.md`; WCAG 2.2, 3.3.4 Error Prevention (Legal, Financial, Data), Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/error-prevention-legal-financial-data.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Design principles (Earn trust), `reference/uswds/design-principles/overview.md`; Apple, Principles of great design, WWDC26 session 250 (2026) (Agency, 2:44), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2026-250.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Design principles (Agency: Help people recover from mistakes), `reference/hig/design-principles.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Best practices: Keep people in control; Outputs: Make it easy for people to refine or revert generated results, and acknowledge when their corrections take effect), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A photo app's Delete button. Pass: the photo goes to Recently Deleted for 30 days and Undo Delete appears. Fail: the same button erases the photo for good after an Are you sure? dialog.

### A confirmation shows the thing, not only the act

Called for by: loses. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.
