**Where a screen asks before acting, it shows the thing the action will be done to, prominently, beside the act, with cancel and the act as the two choices; a question that names the act alone is answered yes without looking.** Requests for confirmation are usually ill-timed, because after requesting an operation people are certain they want it done, and the person tends to focus upon the action rather than the object being acted upon; a better check would be a prominent display of both the action to be taken and the object, perhaps with the choice of "cancel" or "do it". Make the item being acted upon more prominent: enlarge it, or change its colour. The act's button is named by the act, never OK or Yes, and where the act is itself a cancellation OK and Cancel mislead, since the person reads the words literally against their goal. Before a legal, financial or deleting act is final, the person can review and correct what they entered. The review before sending covers any task of several steps, not only a legal, financial or deleting one, showing every answer with a Change beside it, since it gives people "a second chance to notice and correct errors before submitting data" and lets them "clearly see that they have completed all the sections and that their data has been captured". A menu of commands opened on an item shows the item beside them, as the platform's preview "shows a condensed version of the actual content to help people confirm that they're working with the item they intend." Check: does each confirmation on this screen show the thing that will be changed or lost, large enough to be read as what it is, and are its two choices the act and cancel? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Confirmation and Error Messages), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 8, Limits on Attention Shape Thought and Action (We Follow Information "Scent" Toward Our Goal), `reference/books/johnson-2010/10-chapter-8-limits-on-attention-shape-thought-and-action.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Alerts (Buttons: Create succinct, logical button titles; Avoid using OK as the default button title unless the alert is purely informational), `reference/hig/alerts.md`; WCAG 2.2, 3.3.4 Error Prevention (Legal, Financial, Data), Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/error-prevention-legal-financial-data.md`; WCAG 2.2, 3.3.6 Error Prevention (All), Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/error-prevention-all.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Check answers (When to use this pattern; How it works), `reference/govuk/patterns/check-answers.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Email addresses (Help users to enter a valid email address), `reference/govuk/patterns/email-addresses.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Modal (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/modal.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Establish trust (What to do; Considerations), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/establish-trust.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Context menus (iOS, iPadOS: Prefer a graphical preview that clarifies the target of a context menu's commands), `reference/hig/context-menus.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A contacts app, where deleting asks first. Pass: the prompt shows Ana Ruiz's photo and name large, with Delete Contact and Cancel. Fail: the same prompt reads Are you sure? with OK and Cancel.

### A confirmation is read only when it surprises

Called for by: loses. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**A confirmation stops a slip only where the person did not expect it; one that appears on a path taken on purpose is dismissed unread, so a loss the person may mean is guarded by reversal, not by asking.** When a slip produced the command, the dialog is a surprise and alerts me to the slip; when I meant to close a window and closed the wrong one, the dialog box was expected, so I didn't bother to read it, and accepted the option not to save. Warning messages are surprisingly ineffective against mistakes; the cure is to make the operation reversible: if the person elects Don't Save, the system could secretly save the contents and next time ask whether to restore. Where the person has turned on the device's own mode for cognitive support, the app follows it, a second confirmation for a loss hard to undo included, as The device's settings hold here requires; the claim holds for the usual case. A switch takes effect as it is flipped, with no question after it and no Save to tap, since flipping it back undoes it: "Toggles should immediately take effect, not require confirmation". Check: for each confirmation on this screen, is it on a path the user takes on purpose, and if so, what else stands between the tap and the loss? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Confirmation and Error Messages), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Cognitive: Optimize your app's UI for Assistive Access), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Feedback (Best practices: Warn people when they initiate a task that can cause data loss that's unexpected and irreversible), `reference/hig/feedback.md`; Freiberg, Web Interface Guidelines (2023) (Interactivity), `reference/freiberg/README.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A spreadsheet app's Close Window, which always asks about unsaved edits. Pass: Don't Save closes, and the edits are kept and offered back at next launch. Fail: the same Don't Save, tapped from habit on the wrong window, discards the edits for good.

### Two guards of different kinds

Called for by: loses. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Between one tap and the loss of a thing the user kept stand at least two guards that fail in different ways, one before the act and one after it, so that no single slip reaches the loss.** An accident happens only when the holes in every slice of cheese line up; well-designed systems have many errors and no accident, since a leak through one hole is most likely blocked at the next level. The Swiss cheese model says add more slices, reduce the holes, and use very different mechanisms in the different subparts so the holes do not line up; it should not be possible for one simple error to cause widespread damage. In posts on X about popular health and fitness apps, one person clearing pictures "ended up deleting the run" they had recorded and found "No its gone I looked for it": one slip reached the loss, with nothing after it to take it back. Check: for each thing this screen can lose, what stops the loss before the tap, what undoes it after, and would one slip pass both? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Designing for Error; The Swiss Cheese Model of How Errors Lead to Accidents), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Sheen et al., Living well? The unintended consequences of highly popular commercial fitness apps through social listening using Machine-Assisted Topic Analysis: Evidence from X, British Journal of Health Psychology (2025) (Theme 3: Loss of data disrupts the continuity of the self-monitoring experience), `reference/studies/PMC12541294.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A cloud drive's folder list. Pass: Delete Folder sits in a menu apart from Open, and a deleted folder waits in Bin for 30 days. Fail: the same folder deletes from one swipe and is erased at once, so one slip loses it.

### An entry far from the usual is queried

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**An entry far outside what this user usually enters here, or outside what any entry here could be, is queried before it is taken, the entry kept and the usual shown beside it; the screen does not meekly comply.** Electronic systems can check that the requested operation is sensible; medical personnel can accidentally request a radiation dose a thousand times larger than normal and have the equipment meekly comply, and in some cases it isn't even possible for the operator to notice. Intelligent systems would take note of the normal size of my transactions, querying if the amount was considerably larger than normal; a quick glance at the amount would indicate that something was badly off. The query also covers an answer that "conflicts with existing information" and a likely slip in a known value, such as "'homtail.com' instead of 'hotmail.com'", where the person is warned but allowed to go on "in case it's a genuine email address". The worst message is the one never given: a slip the app can foresee before an act that cannot be called back, such as a message sent without the attachment it mentions, is queried the same way. Check: which entries on this screen would any person call impossible or far off this user's own, and what does the screen say when one is entered? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Designing for Error; Sensibility Checks; Minimizing Slips), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Interruption pages (When to use this pattern), `reference/govuk/patterns/interruption-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Email addresses (Help users to enter a valid email address), `reference/govuk/patterns/email-addresses.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Email address (What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/email-address.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Name (What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/name.md`; Neusesser and Sunwall, Error-Message Guidelines, Nielsen Norman Group (2023, updated 2024) (Efficiency Guidelines: Safeguard against likely mistakes), `reference/nngroup/error-message-guidelines.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A taxi app's tip screen. Pass: a 200% tip asks Tip £60 on a £30 fare?, the entry kept and the usual 15% shown beside it. Fail: the same screen takes the 200% tip without a word.

### An error more than one person makes is the screen's

Called for by: entry, errors. Cross-check: Nielsen 9, Help users recognize, diagnose, and recover from errors.

**A wrong entry that more than one person makes on a screen is the screen's to fix, not the people's to learn, and the screen is judged by what each slip costs, never by whether the person should have known better.** When many people all have the same problem, shouldn't another cause be found? If the system lets you make the error, it is badly designed, and if it induces you to make the error, it is really badly designed; when I turn on the wrong stove burner, teaching me the relationship will not stop the error from recurring, redesigning the stove will. Assume that every possible mishap will happen, so protect against them; make actions reversible, make errors less costly. The same holds for one person refused on the same entry three or more times in one go: the screen offers more help there, such as a way to reach a person, since the repeat points to an unclear message, a mismatch with what they need, or a rule too complex. Check: which entry on this screen has been got wrong by more than one person, and what on the screen induced it? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Understanding Why There Is Error; Root Cause Analysis; Design Principles for Dealing with Error), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Krause, 10 Design Guidelines for Reporting Errors in Forms, Nielsen Norman Group (2019, updated 2024) (10. Provide Extra Help for Repeated Errors), `reference/nngroup/errors-forms-design-guidelines.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A cinema ticket app's seat map, where many buyers pick the front row thinking it is the back. Pass: the map is redrawn with SCREEN printed along the front. Fail: the same map is kept, and a help line tells buyers to read the legend.

### No order the task does not have

Called for by: steps, entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where the task has no order, the screen's entries take no order either: any field is filled first and the rest wait, since a step that cannot be done when it is reached is skipped and then forgotten.** Printed checklists force the steps to follow a sequential ordering even where this is not necessary or even possible; the fuel check reached before fueling is skipped, intending to come back to it, and this is a clear opportunity for a memory-lapse error. In general it is bad design to impose a sequential structure to task execution unless the task itself requires it. Check: does this screen make the user enter things in an order the task does not have, and what becomes of a thing not known when its turn comes? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Checklists), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Complete multiple tasks (Group tasks into steps), `reference/govuk/patterns/complete-multiple-tasks.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Task list (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/task-list.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Progress easily (What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/progress-easily.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A hotel booking's guest form, whose fields have no order. Pass: name, arrival time and requests fill in any order, arrival time left for later. Fail: the same form opens one field at a time, so a guest unsure of arrival must guess or stop.

### A skipped step stays open

Called for by: steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**A step the user passed over stays shown as open, and the whole is not shown as done until every needed step is, so the skipped one is found again on the screen and not remembered.** This is one of the major benefits of electronic checklists: they can keep track of skipped items and ensure that the list will not be marked as complete until all items have been done; memory lapses lead to failing to do all of the steps of a procedure, repeating steps, or forgetting the outcome of an action. In a list of answers, a missing one shows in its value's place as a link to the question that fills it, not as a Change, and a list of tasks lets the person move on only "when all tasks are shown as 'Completed'". Check: on this screen, where does a step the user skipped show as still open, and can the screen read as finished while one is? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Checklists; Memory-Lapse Slips), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 8, Limits on Attention Shape Thought and Action (We Use External Aids to Keep Track of What We Are Doing), `reference/books/johnson-2010/10-chapter-8-limits-on-attention-shape-thought-and-action.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Summary list (Showing missing information), `reference/govuk/components/summary-list.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Task list (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/task-list.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A tax filing app's checklist. Pass: the skipped Bank interest section stays marked Not started and Submit stays off. Fail: the same checklist shows a green All done banner while Bank interest is still empty.

### The difficult part is one part

Called for by: loses. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Where an action is made hard on purpose, the hardness sits on that one action alone and the rest of the screen stays easy; the hard part is hard by a chosen means, hidden, far or two actions, never by accident.** Some things are deliberately difficult to use and ought to be: the acknowledgment required before permanently deleting a file, safeties on pistols, pins in fire extinguishers; even deliberately difficult designs aren't entirely difficult, usually there is one difficult part, and the rest should follow the normal principles of good design. The rules reversed say how: hide critical components, require two simultaneous actions. The hard part still answers a light touch and shows what more it needs, as the lock screen's Flashlight, which needs a "more intentional gesture to activate the Flashlight", swells under the finger and bounces back after a tap: "that hint is teaching you not only that the button is working, but that it's responding to you. But, it's kind of teaching you that if you press just a little bit more firmly, it'll activate." Check: which action on this screen is meant to be hard, by which means, and is anything else on the screen hard without meaning to be? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 6, Design Thinking (Deliberately Making Things Difficult), `reference/books/norman-2013/06-chapter-6-design-thinking.md`; Apple, Designing Fluid Interfaces, WWDC18 session 803 (2018) (Character, 37:46; Continuous feedback, 53:47), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2018-803.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A phone's Settings app, where erasing is made hard on purpose. Pass: Erase All Content asks for the passcode and a second tap, while everything else takes one tap. Fail: the same Settings also asks for the passcode to change the wallpaper.

### Over the screen, not in its place

Called for by: covers. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**New information that answers a step appears over the screen the person was on, which stays visible beneath, rather than replacing it, so they keep their place.** Figure/Ground is often used to pop up information over other content: content that was formerly the figure temporarily becomes the background for new information, which appears briefly as the new figure. This approach is usually better than temporarily replacing the old information with the new information, because it provides context that helps keep people oriented regarding their place in the interaction. A control for choosing a field's value opens beside or over that field, not on another screen. Its subject is the answer to a step; a task of its own that needs the whole screen, as editing a photo does, may take it, so long as the view names its task and shows a way out. Closing a view over the screen leaves what lies beneath as it was, neither reloaded nor changed: "The page underneath should not reload or change to new content." Where what lies beneath still matters while a view is open, the view rests at about half the screen's height, the platform's medium detent, with the screen beneath in sight. Where the person goes on acting on what lies beneath, as when formatting chosen text, the view leaves it free: "people use its functionality to affect the parent view without dismissing the sheet". A view whose own task needs the room, as writing a message does, opens at full height. Where the view over the screen is see-through, a thinner material keeps more of what lies beneath in sight, since "Thinner materials, which are more translucent, can help people retain their context by providing a visible reminder of the content that's in the background", while a thicker one gives "better contrast for text". The screen beneath is dimmed where the view over it must be finished or closed first, the platform's "familiar cues, like using a dimming layer to signal modality when a sheet appears", and is left undimmed where the task over it "happens in parallel". Check: when this screen shows something new mid-task, is what the person was doing still visible beneath it, or gone? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 2, Our Vision Is Optimized to See Structure (Gestalt Principle: Figure/Ground), `reference/books/johnson-2010/04-chapter-2-our-vision-is-optimized-to-see-structure.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Modality (Best practices: Aim to keep modal tasks simple, short, and streamlined; Consider using a full-screen modal style for in-depth content or a complex task; Make it easy to identify a modal view's task), `reference/hig/modality.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Pickers (Best practices: Avoid switching views to show a picker), `reference/hig/pickers.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Modal (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/modal.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Sheets (introduction; Anatomy; Best practices: Use a nonmodal view when you want to present supplementary items that affect the main task in the parent view; iOS, iPadOS: In an iPhone app, consider supporting the medium detent to allow progressive disclosure of the sheet's content), `reference/hig/sheets.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Materials (Standard materials: Consider contrast and visual separation when choosing a material to combine with blur and vibrancy effects), `reference/hig/materials.md`; Apple, Get to know the new design system, WWDC25 session 356 (2025) (Structure, 7:35), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-356.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A taxi app's map of nearby cars. Pass: tapping a car opens a sheet over the lower half, the map still visible above. Fail: the same tap replaces the map with a full page of driver details.

### The answer lands at the tap

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**What a screen says after a tap appears where the eyes are, on the thing tapped or touching it, since sharp sight covers only about a thumbnail's width held at arm's length: about a centimetre on a phone held a foot away, one or two on a computer screen; a message elsewhere is in the periphery and is missed.** When someone clicks a button or a link, that is usually where his or her fovea is positioned; everything on the screen that is not within 1 to 2 centimeters of the click location, at the usual distance from a computer screen, is in peripheral vision, where resolution is low, so if after the click an error message appears in the periphery, it should not be surprising that the person might not notice it. The user reenters the username and password, clicks Sign In again, and only on sitting back to scan the screen says "Aha! Has that error message been there all along?"; put it where users are looking, and place the message near what it refers to unless that would place it too far from where users are likely to be looking. The fovea is a fixed angle of sight, about a thumbnail held at arm's length, so the patch it covers on the glass shrinks with the distance; a phone is held at "no more than a foot or two", and at a foot, about half the distance of a computer screen, the patch is about a centimetre across, half the computer's figure. Where the keyboard is up, the answer sits above it, as The keyboard covers nothing being worked on requires. What a control opens, opens from that control: a menu "pops open" from its button, which "keeps everything right where you just tapped", and an action sheet that once rose from the foot of the screen now "springs from the action itself". A small success is answered on the control itself and not by a notification elsewhere: "Show a temporary inline checkmark on a successful copy, not a notification". Where the thing tapped is no bigger than the fingertip, as a key is, the finger hides the answer drawn on it, "Any visual feedback was masked by the fingertip over the button", so the answer does not rest on that mark alone. In a study of 12 adults aged 18 to 38 typing on a phone's touchscreen, a felt click on each key, with a different one for sliding off a key, raised the phrases typed exactly right from about 70 to 83 per cent in a lab and from 66 to 80 per cent on a moving train, and cut the time per phrase from 25 to 27 seconds to 20 to 22. Check: for each message this screen shows after a tap, is it on or touching the control tapped or the entry it is about, and is it clear of the keyboard? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 6, Our Peripheral Vision Is Poor (Resolution of the Fovea Compared to That of the Periphery; Examples from Computer User Interfaces; Common Methods of Making Messages Visible, Put it where users are looking; Mark the error), `reference/books/johnson-2010/08-chapter-6-our-peripheral-vision-is-poor.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Designing for iOS (Ergonomics), `reference/hig/designing-for-ios.md`; Apple, Meet Liquid Glass, WWDC25 session 219 (2025) (Dynamics, 4:32), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-219.md`; Apple, Get to know the new design system, WWDC25 session 356 (2025) (Structure, 6:54), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-356.md`; Freiberg, Web Interface Guidelines (2023) (Design: Display feedback relative to its trigger), `reference/freiberg/README.md`; Hoggan, Crossmodal Audio and Tactile Interaction with Mobile Touchscreens, PhD thesis, University of Glasgow (2010) (chapter 6, 6.2.1 Feedback Design; 6.3 Experiment 4a(i): Crossmodal Tactile Text Entry; 6.3.1 Results), `reference/papers/hoggan-2010-crossmodal-audio-and-tactile-interaction.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A clothing shop app's product page. Pass: after Add to Bag is tapped, the button itself reads Added to Bag. Fail: the same page shows Added to bag beside the price, two centimetres above the button.

### Guidance comes as the entry is made

Called for by: entry, errors. Cross-check: Nielsen 9, Help users recognize, diagnose, and recover from errors.

**A wrong entry is answered the moment it is made, while the person's eyes are still on that field, not after they tap to go on; a field passed by and left empty is told only when they try to go on.** At AOL.com, data fields with errors are marked near the error, and most of the error messages appear as soon as an erroneous entry is made, when the user is still focused on that part of the form, rather than only after the user submits the form; it is unlikely that users will miss seeing these. A button dimmed until the entries are complete is a constraint, not guidance, so the guidance beside the missing entry is still given. Nothing is flagged while the person is still typing in a field, and every refusal is told again when they try to go on. Moving on from a field without typing is exploring, not an error, and "Presenting errors too early is a hostile pattern"; nor is a field the person has not reached yet flagged because they went back to an earlier one. Where an entry can be checked only once it is sent, its message stands beside it when the screen comes back. Check: for each entry this screen can refuse, does the person learn it as they type or leave the field, or only after a later tap? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 6, Our Peripheral Vision Is Poor (Common Methods of Making Messages Visible, the AOL.com example), `reference/books/johnson-2010/08-chapter-6-our-peripheral-vision-is-poor.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Entering data (Best practices: Dynamically validate field values; When data entry is necessary, make sure people understand that they must provide the required data before they can proceed), `reference/hig/entering-data.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Text fields (Best practices: Validate fields when it makes sense), `reference/hig/text-fields.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Recover from validation errors (When to tell the user about validation errors), `reference/govuk/patterns/validation.md`; Krause, 10 Design Guidelines for Reporting Errors in Forms, Nielsen Norman Group (2019, updated 2024) (1. Aim for Inline Validation Whenever Possible; 7. Don't Validate Fields Before Input is Complete), `reference/nngroup/errors-forms-design-guidelines.md`; Neusesser and Sunwall, Error-Message Guidelines, Nielsen Norman Group (2023, updated 2024) (Visibility Guidelines: Avoid prematurely displaying errors), `reference/nngroup/error-message-guidelines.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: An airline app's passenger form. Pass: a passport number with too few digits is flagged beside the field as it is left. Fail: the same field is checked only when Continue at the bottom is tapped.

### An interruption only where something would be lost

Called for by: errors, covers. Cross-check: Nielsen 9, Help users recognize, diagnose, and recover from errors.

**A message that stops the work and must be dismissed is shown only where the person's data would otherwise be lost; a missing or wrong entry is answered in place.** Displaying an error message in a dialog box sticks it right in the user's face, but error dialog boxes interrupt the user's work and demand immediate attention, which is good if the message signals a critical condition and annoying for a minor one; application-modal pop-ups should be used sparingly, only when application data may be lost if the user doesn't attend to the error, and system-modal ones extremely rarely. REI.com's pop-up for omitted required fields is overkill, since AOL.com shows missing-data errors can be signaled quite well without pop-up dialogs. A stop for anything else, such as information the person would otherwise miss, is used only where there is evidence of a clear need and no other way to give it, since such stops weaken the more often they are seen. A dialog has a cost of its own at an entry: it must be sent away before the fix, so its words are carried in mind while fixing. A query of a likely slip, made as the person sends what cannot be called back, such as a message that mentions an attachment it lacks, may stop the work, where it is short, names the slip and lets them send it as it is. Apple's talk on its principles keeps the stop for the large loss: "use interruptions carefully, and only when someone is about to make a big mistake." Leaving a screen with entries not yet kept is one such place, "Warn before navigation when data could be lost", unless the entries are kept and offered back on return, which needs no stop. A browser maker's account, practice that cites no study, shows the rule at work: "We never show the user an update banner", the update running "when the application is backgrounded, not in use, or when the computer is asleep", and the person is stopped only to avoid a loss, "by alerting the user to tabs that have incomplete input fields." Check: for each dialog this screen can raise, what would be lost without it, and could its message sit at the entry instead? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 6, Our Peripheral Vision Is Poor (Heavy Artillery for Making Users Notice Messages: Use Sparingly, Pop-up message in error dialog box; Use them sparingly), `reference/books/johnson-2010/08-chapter-6-our-peripheral-vision-is-poor.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Alerts (Best practices: Use alerts sparingly; Avoid using an alert merely to provide information), `reference/hig/alerts.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Feedback (Best practices: Warn people when they initiate a task that can cause data loss that's unexpected and irreversible), `reference/hig/feedback.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Feedback (Best practices: Use alerts to deliver critical — and ideally actionable — information), `reference/hig/feedback.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Action sheets (Best practices: Use action sheets sparingly), `reference/hig/action-sheets.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Interruption pages (When to use this pattern; How it works), `reference/govuk/patterns/interruption-pages.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Modal (When to consider something else; Usability), `reference/uswds/components/modal.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Link (Usability should not), `reference/uswds/components/link.md`; Krause, 10 Design Guidelines for Reporting Errors in Forms, Nielsen Norman Group (2019, updated 2024) (6. Use Modals or Confirmation Dialogs Sparingly), `reference/nngroup/errors-forms-design-guidelines.md`; Neusesser and Sunwall, Error-Message Guidelines, Nielsen Norman Group (2023, updated 2024) (Efficiency Guidelines: Safeguard against likely mistakes), `reference/nngroup/error-message-guidelines.md`; Apple, Principles of great design, WWDC26 session 250 (2026) (Agency, 2:55), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2026-250.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Forms: Unsaved changes), `reference/vercel/README.md`; Deets, The strategy behind Dia's design (2025) (Simplicity.), `reference/essays/browser-company-dia.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A car insurance claim form. Pass: a missing policy number is flagged under its field. Fail: the same form raises a Required field missing dialog that must be dismissed.

### A wait can be abandoned

Called for by: wait. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**A wait can be cut short from where it is shown, and what the person had before it is still there when it is.** Responsive systems allow users to abort (cancel) lengthy operations they don't want; time-consuming operations that block other activity and cannot be aborted are a mark of poor responsiveness; in a web browser clicking Back or Stop immediately aborts the loading of the page, and given how long a page can take, the ability to abort is critical to user acceptance. Apple now says this of a wait itself: "If people can interrupt a process without causing negative side effects, include a Cancel button." Where stopping would lose work already done, as part of a download, Pause sits beside Cancel. A Cancel that loses progress first asks, with a way to go on: "it's helpful to provide an alert that includes an option to confirm the cancellation or resume the process." Where stopping cannot be done without leaving things worse than finishing, the wait offers no Cancel it cannot keep, and says instead what is under way and that it will finish. Check: for each wait on this screen, where is the control that ends it, and what is the state of the thing when it is ended? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 12, We Have Time Requirements (Responsiveness Defined; Designing to Meet Real-Time Human Interaction Deadlines; Additional Guidelines for Achieving Responsive Interactive Systems, Process user input according to priority, not the order in which it was received), `reference/books/johnson-2010/14-chapter-12-we-have-time-requirements.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Progress indicators (Best practices: When it's feasible, let people halt processing; Let people know when halting a process has a negative consequence), `reference/hig/progress-indicators.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A video streaming app's film download. Pass: a Cancel button sits beside the progress bar, and cancelling leaves the library as it was. Fail: the same download shows a progress bar with no way to stop it.

### Free while waiting

Called for by: wait. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**While the app works on one thing the person can go on with another; the wait holds the thing, not the screen.** Responsive systems free you to do other things while waiting; ignoring user input while performing housekeeping tasks users did not request is poor responsiveness; between unit tasks people look up from their work and check whether anything else requires attention. On a surface people glance at rather than watch, a long wait shows no endlessly turning sign, which keeps them watching the display, and promises instead a notice when the work is done, as Apple asks on watchOS. A result a model takes long to make is made "in the background while a person uses another part of the app", or under a loading view of its own, since generative models "typically take longer to produce a result". Check: during each wait on this screen, what can the person still do, and is anything they enter meanwhile lost when the wait ends? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 12, We Have Time Requirements (Responsiveness Defined; Designing to Meet Real-Time Human Interaction Deadlines), `reference/books/johnson-2010/14-chapter-12-we-have-time-requirements.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Loading (Best practices: Let people do other things in your app or game while they wait for content to load), `reference/hig/loading.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Feedback (watchOS: Avoid displaying an indeterminate progress indicator), `reference/hig/feedback.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Progress indicators (Best practices: When possible, use a determinate progress indicator), `reference/hig/progress-indicators.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Outputs: Factor processing time into your design), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A podcast app's episode download. Pass: while it downloads, other episodes can be browsed and played. Fail: the same download covers the screen with a spinner until it ends.

### Something to read before everything is ready

Called for by: wait. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**A screen shows the part a person can read and act on as soon as it has it and fills the rest after, never nothing until all is ready.** Don't wait until a display is fully rendered before letting users see it; give users something to think about and act upon as soon as possible; research indicates that users prefer progressive results to progress indicators (Geelhoed, Toft, Roberts and Hyland, 1995); a search displays items as soon as it finds them while continuing to search. For text, a low-resolution first pass is not recommended: it annoys. A blank is the worst answer to a wait, worse than a loading sign, and a problem at the start shows the data last held or a placeholder, with a quiet line naming the problem. What arrives later fills a place kept for it from the first moment, so nothing already read is pushed down the screen as it lands: "Skeletons mirror final content exactly to avoid layout shift", and a picture's space is held before it loads. A field shown before the rest of the screen is ready keeps what is typed into it, and the place being typed in, when the rest arrives: "Inputs must not lose focus or value after hydration." A founder whose app writes down a conversation shows the words as they are made, accepting that "The quality is a bit worse", because "It's very comforting to see the transcript come in real-time. You trust that the thing is working." Check: when this screen opens or a list on it loads, what is on it in the first tenth of a second, and is it the thing the person came to read? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 12, We Have Time Requirements (Additional Guidelines for Achieving Responsive Interactive Systems, Display important information first), `reference/books/johnson-2010/14-chapter-12-we-have-time-requirements.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Loading (Best practices: Show something as soon as possible), `reference/hig/loading.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Loading (watchOS: As much as possible, avoid showing a loading indicator in your watchOS experience), `reference/hig/loading.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Alerts (Best practices: Avoid showing an alert when your app starts), `reference/hig/alerts.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Content: Stable skeletons; Performance: No image-caused CLS), `reference/vercel/README.md`; Kowalski, Breaking UI: Worst-Case Catalog, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Images and media; States), `reference/kowalski/skills/break-ui/CATALOG.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Interactions: Hydration-safe inputs), `reference/vercel/README.md`; Stephenson, interviewed by Labenz, Calm AI for Crazy Days, The Cognitive Revolution (2026), `reference/interviews/stephenson-granola.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A news app's front page. Pass: headlines from the last load appear at once and pictures fill in after. Fail: the same page stays blank white until every picture has loaded.

### A wait sits between steps

Called for by: wait, steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where the app must make a person wait, the wait falls after a step is closed, never inside one, since during a step the person holds the goal and the entries in mind and a wait inside loses them.** Unit tasks, the bottom-level subtasks completed without a break in concentration, last 6 to 30 seconds (Card, Moran and Newell, 1983); during one, users keep their goal and necessary information in working memory or within their perceptual field; unexpected system delays during a unit task are particularly harmful and annoying, while delays between unit tasks are not as harmful, though they may slow the overall rate; actions at the lowest level of closure, typing a character and seeing it echoed, are the most sensitive to delay. Check: where on this screen can a wait fall, and is each one after a step the person would call done? Source: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 12, We Have Time Requirements (The Many Time Constants of the Human Brain, Duration of unbroken attention to a single task; Additional Guidelines for Achieving Responsive Interactive Systems, Delays between unit tasks are less bothersome than delays within unit tasks), `reference/books/johnson-2010/14-chapter-12-we-have-time-requirements.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A hotel booking's search form, where availability takes four seconds to check. Pass: the check runs after Search is tapped, dates and guests done. Fail: the same check runs after the arrival date, freezing the form before the departure date can be entered.

### A fault is not theirs to diagnose

Called for by: errors, wait. Cross-check: Nielsen 9, Help users recognize, diagnose, and recover from errors.

**A fault beneath the screen, a connection down, a store that will not open, a value that did not load, is told to the person as what it means for their task and what to do now, and the technical report of it goes to whoever fixes it; the person is never asked to find the cause.** Don't make users diagnose system problems, such as a faulty network connection; such diagnosis requires technical training which most users don't have. Continental's error messages speak geek, so few users understand what the site is saying and are unsure what to do; such messages should be rewritten in terms users understand, or displayed to the administrators who monitor the site rather than to the users; Southwest's explain the problem in task-focused, familiar language. The message says what has become of the person's answers, when the app will work again where that is known, and another way to do what they came to do, with no code such as 404 or 500, no "oops", no "We are experiencing technical difficulties" and no blame; tested with 5 people, whose needs were "when the service will be available" and "how they can do what they came to do". The person has a way to report a problem from inside the app: "Offer users a mechanism to report bugs and issues, and be responsive to these reports." Left to find a fault's cause, people turn to strangers: in posts on X about popular health and fitness apps, people whose records would not sync between apps asked others online, who "suggested solutions such as waiting for a period of time before trying again, connecting their devices to a personal computer, and manually downloading and uploading files between devices". Check: for each failure this screen can meet, what does the person read, does it say what to do, and where does the technical report go? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 10, Learning from Experience and Performing Learned Actions are Easy; Problem Solving and Calculation are Hard (Implications for User Interface Design, Don't make users diagnose system problems; Solving Technical Problems Requires Technical Interest and Training), `reference/books/johnson-2010/12-chapter-10-learning-from-experience-and-performing-learned-a.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 11, Many Factors Affect Learning (We Learn Faster When Vocabulary Is Task-Focused, Familiar, and Consistent, Terminology should be familiar), `reference/books/johnson-2010/13-chapter-11-many-factors-affect-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Feedback (Best practices: Show people when a command can't be carried out and help them understand why), `reference/hig/feedback.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Alerts (Best practices: Avoid showing an alert when your app starts), `reference/hig/alerts.md`; GOV.UK Design System, There is a problem with the service pages (How it works; Research on this pattern), `reference/govuk/patterns/problem-with-the-service-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Service unavailable pages (How it works; Research on this pattern), `reference/govuk/patterns/service-unavailable-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Page not found pages (How it works), `reference/govuk/patterns/page-not-found-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Confirm an email address (Set expiry conditions), `reference/govuk/patterns/confirm-an-email-address.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Design principles (Listen, Practical actions), `reference/uswds/design-principles/overview.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Progress indicators (Best practices: Keep progress indicators moving so people know something is continuing to happen), `reference/hig/progress-indicators.md`; Sheen et al., Living well? The unintended consequences of highly popular commercial fitness apps through social listening using Machine-Assisted Topic Analysis: Evidence from X, British Journal of Health Psychology (2025) (Theme 3: Loss of data disrupts the continuity of the self-monitoring experience), `reference/studies/PMC12541294.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A video call app's dropped call. Pass: it reads Your connection dropped. Reconnecting…, with Retry. Fail: the same message reads ICE negotiation failed; check NAT and firewall settings.

### The loose end is the app's

Called for by: steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**A step that comes after the person's goal is reached, a thing to close, switch off, take back or clear, is done by the app, or the app reminds at the moment it is due, since the mind drops everything about a goal the moment it is met.** When we complete a task the attentional resources focused on its goal are freed to be refocused, so once we achieve a goal everything related to it often immediately falls out of short-term memory; people forget to turn headlights off, remove the last page from the copier, log out of public computers, set devices back into normal mode; these end-of-task lapses are completely predictable and avoidable; designers should consider whether the tasks they support have cleanup steps users are likely to forget, and design the system either to help users remember or to eliminate the need. Check: for each goal reached on this screen, what remains to be done after it, and does the app do it or remind at the time? Source: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 8, Limits on Attention Shape Thought and Action (After We Achieve a Task's Primary Goal, We Often Forget Cleanup Steps), `reference/books/johnson-2010/10-chapter-8-limits-on-attention-shape-thought-and-action.md`. What the user came for comes last puts the asks before the goal; this one takes the step after the goal off the person altogether.

Example, not part of the claim: A car-sharing app's End Rental button. Pass: ending the rental locks the car. Fail: the same End Rental leaves the car unlocked unless the driver also taps Lock.

### A mode lets go by itself

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**A state the person switches a screen into for one action returns to the usual state on its own, after the action or when the hand lets go, never waiting for the person to switch it back.** Special software modes should revert to normal automatically, either by timing out, as some appliances do, or through spring-loaded mode controls, which must be physically held in the non-normal state and revert to normal when released; setting software back into normal mode after putting it into a special one is among the loose ends people forget. It is the companion of No modes, or the mode shown: shown while it lasts, and not lasting. A mode does not let go on a timer while the person may still be reading or acting, since people who need longer, or who move through the screen with assistive technology, are cut off; it lets go after the action, or when the hand lets go. Check: which state on this screen does the person switch into for an action, and what switches it back? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 8, Limits on Attention Shape Thought and Action (After We Achieve a Task's Primary Goal, We Often Forget Cleanup Steps), `reference/books/johnson-2010/10-chapter-8-limits-on-attention-shape-thought-and-action.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 7, Our Attention is Limited; Our Memory is Imperfect (Implications of Short-Term Memory Characteristics for User Interface Design, Modes), `reference/books/johnson-2010/09-chapter-7-our-attention-is-limited-our-memory-is-imperfect.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Cognitive: Minimize use of time-boxed interface elements), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A phone keyboard's Shift key. Pass: Shift capitalises the next letter, then turns itself off. Fail: the same Shift stays on until it is tapped again.

### The user's own mark

Called for by: list. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**A list a person works through lets them mark an entry done, or move it, by their own hand, since people keep their place by marking the world, and a list they cannot mark is copied so it can be.** Because our short-term memory and attention are so limited we mark up our environment to show us where we are in a task: counted objects moved to a pile, bookmarks, checked-off items; a checklist that we can't mark up is hard to use, so we copy it and mark the copy; interactive systems should allow users to mark or move objects to indicate which ones they have worked on versus which they have not, as Mac OS lets users assign colours to files. Where a part of a task holds optional questions, long answers or things to look up, the person says when it is done, asked "Have you completed this section?" with "Yes, I've completed this section" or "No, I'll come back later". Check: on each list on this screen the person works down, can they mark an entry done themselves, and does the mark stay? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 8, Limits on Attention Shape Thought and Action (We Use External Aids to Keep Track of What We Are Doing), `reference/books/johnson-2010/10-chapter-8-limits-on-attention-shape-thought-and-action.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Lists and tables (Best practices: Let people edit a table when it makes sense), `reference/hig/lists-and-tables.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Complete multiple tasks (Marking tasks as completed), `reference/govuk/patterns/complete-multiple-tasks.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A shopping list app's list. Pass: tapping an item ticks it and the tick stays. Fail: the same list cannot be ticked, so every item looks the same.

### The screen holds still

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**After an action, the screen changes only where the action changed something; nothing else moves, reorders or disappears, so the person's eye finds everything where it left it.** Johnson's own rule is "preserve display inertia", under "the screen belongs to the user"; the appendix gives it as a line without its reasons, and the reasons sit in chapter 8's cycle, where a person evaluates an action by what changed, and in chapter 7's change blindness, where people don't notice differences in features other than those their goals made them pay attention to, so a change outside the thing acted on is both unlooked-for and unseen. A number that changes where it stands, a timer, a counter or a running total, is set in figures of one width, so its digits do not jump sideways as it changes: "Fixed-width digits so numbers don't shift around as they change. Essential for tickers, timers, and counters". What the app suggests changes at the pace the person expects of it, at once for words suggested as they type and never under their eyes for a list they are reading through, since "continuously updated song recommendations makes it hard to consider individual songs and could make them feel rushed or overwhelmed." A chosen or pressed state changes the colour or the mark of its words, never their weight, which would widen them and push what sits beside: "Font weight should not change on hover or selected state to prevent layout shift". What the app learns from one act does not remake all it shows: Amershi's eighteen guidelines, tested by 49 design practitioners on 20 products for whether they could find each applied or violated, not for its effect on people, ask apps to "Limit disruptive changes when updating and adapting the AI system's behaviors", and one practitioner recorded as a violation a shop whose recommendations all changed after one look at a camera, "really jarring, especially when the browsing is a result of a curious moment or an accident". A change that reaches the screen from elsewhere, another person's edit or another device's, lands in place in the same way and never by reloading the view, which a founder of a shared design tool recalls from before his tool could do so: "I make a change and your browser force-reloads, and you make a change and my browser force-reloads, it sucks." Check: after each action on this screen, what changes beyond what the action itself changed, and does anything move from where the person last saw it? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), appendix, Well-known User Interface Design Rules (Johnson (2007), Principle 7, Deliver information, not just data: The screen belongs to the user; Preserve display inertia), `reference/books/johnson-2010/16-well-known-user-interface-design-rules.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 8, Limits on Attention Shape Thought and Action (Our Thought Cycle: Goal, Execute, Evaluate), `reference/books/johnson-2010/10-chapter-8-limits-on-attention-shape-thought-and-action.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 7, Our Attention is Limited; Our Memory is Imperfect (Characteristics of Short-Term Memory), `reference/books/johnson-2010/09-chapter-7-our-attention-is-limited-our-memory-is-imperfect.md`; WCAG 2.2, 3.2.5 Change on Request, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/change-on-request.md`; Kowalski, Animation Vocabulary, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Polish & Effects: Tabular numbers), `reference/kowalski/skills/animation-vocabulary/SKILL.source.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Implicit feedback: Use feedback to update predictions on a cadence that matches the person's mental model of the feature), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Freiberg, Web Interface Guidelines (2023) (Typography), `reference/freiberg/README.md`; Amershi et al., Guidelines for Human-AI Interaction, CHI (2019) (Table 1, Guideline 14: Update and adapt cautiously; Appendix, Guideline 14), `reference/papers/amershi-2019-guidelines-for-human-ai-interaction.md`; Field, interviewed by Thompson, An Interview with Figma CEO Dylan Field About Design and AI, Stratechery (2026) (WebGL and the Foundation of Figma), `reference/interviews/field-figma.md`. A thin source: the rule's full statement is in Johnson's GUI Bloopers 2.0 (2007), not on the shelf, unverified.

Example, not part of the claim: A to-do app's list. Pass: ticking a task strikes it through where it sits. Fail: the same tick re-sorts the whole list, so the other tasks jump.

### A choice is not hurried

Called for by: list. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**A screen does not press a person to choose against a clock, since a choice made faster than the person can take it in is partly a guess, wrong in proportion to the time cut.** Hick's second experiment had two people answer the ten-choice task faster "at the cost of a proportion of mistakes". He predicted that the uncertainty left over "should vary directly with the reduction in the average reaction time" (p. 15). It did: fast runs with errors fell on the same curve as slow runs without, read at the information actually gained (Fig. 1, p. 15). One subject "managed to achieve a run containing 70 per cent. of errors, without losing control of the situation" (p. 16). Hyman demanded errorless answers precisely because Hick had shown that people can buy speed with errors (p. 189). The evidence is two people, one of them the author, deliberately trading accuracy for speed on lamps and keys. The screen's words do not press either, and no hurry is claimed that is not true: "Do not create a false sense of urgency." Check: does anything on this screen ask for a choice within a time, a countdown, an auto-advance or an option that lapses, and where speed is truly needed, what makes a wrong choice cheap to undo? Sources: Hick, On the Rate of Gain of Information, Quarterly Journal of Experimental Psychology 4 (1952), p. 11 (abstract), pp. 15–16 (V, Experiment II; Fig. 1; Fig. 2), `reference/papers/hick-1952-on-the-rate-of-gain-of-information.pdf`; Hyman, Stimulus Information as a Determinant of Reaction Time, Journal of Experimental Psychology 45 (1953), p. 189 (Statement of the problem), `reference/papers/hyman-1953-stimulus-information-as-a-determinant-of-reaction-time.pdf`, PDF page 1; U.S. Web Design System, Establish trust (What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/establish-trust.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A streaming service's plan picker. Pass: the three plans wait while the person compares them. Fail: the same picker shows a 60-second countdown, after which its discount is withdrawn.

### A way out in sight

Called for by: covers. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where a view takes over the screen, it shows an obvious way to leave, in the place the platform puts it, so no one is held in a view they did not mean to stay in.** A modal view "prevents interaction with the parent view and requires an explicit action to dismiss", so the way out is the one action the person cannot do without. Apple: "Always give people an obvious way to dismiss a modal view", following "the platform conventions people already know". On iPhone that is a button in the top toolbar or a swipe down. A gesture alone is not the obvious way: where an app moves through a hierarchy, people "expect to find a Back button in a top toolbar that lets them return to the previous view with a single tap", and the edge swipe is a shortcut beside it. Where the view asks a question that must be answered before going on, a plainly worded answer that declines, such as No, go back, is its way out, and it is never dimmed or hidden. What the way out says follows what leaving loses (Timmy, 2026-11-05). A view that holds nothing to lose, a video, a picture, a page to read, closes with Close, drawn as the platform's X symbol: Apple names the button "Cancel (or Close)" and, for a sheet's changed label on the watch, says "prefer using SF Symbols to represent the action", and Material makes "the close 'X' icon button" the only way out in a full-screen dialog's top bar. A view whose edits leaving throws away has Cancel at the leading edge of its top bar on iPhone and Done or Save at the trailing edge, since Cancel says something is given up. Where leaving would lose what the person entered, it is kept or the person is asked before it goes, by the button and by the swipe alike, never dropped without a word: "Regardless of whether people use a dismiss gesture or a button, if closing the view could result in the loss of user-generated content, be sure to explain the situation and give people ways to resolve it." The swipe down closes a sheet as its button does, since "People expect to swipe vertically to dismiss a sheet instead of tapping a dismiss button", but never replaces the button; the grabber at a sheet's top edge resizes it and does not close it. Done never stands alone, since "Relying solely on the Done button implies that completing the task is the only way to exit the sheet, which can feel restrictive or misleading." Where a keyboard is attached, Esc leaves the view as Cancel does, the platform's shortcut to "Cancel the current action or process." A screen shown just before the system's permission request is the one exception: its single Continue opens the request, whose own answer that refuses is the way out, so the screen carries no Cancel or Close of its own. In a view of several steps, the first step's Cancel is the way out and each later step's Back leads toward it, as the platform places them, with the swipe down closing it from any step; Cancel, Done and Back never stand together. A guided flow, such as a tour or a setup at first launch, can be skipped or left, since people are not to be "locked into specific flows or modes": "When a guided flow is necessary, make it easy to skip or escape so people can get to the main experience quickly." Leaving is free of reproach: a setup can be left "at any point", with nothing "implying any judgment about their choice" and no message about it afterwards, since "the next time people attempt to use the feature, they'll have another chance to participate." Check: for each view on this screen that covers another, where is the control that leaves it, is it visible without a gesture, is it where the platform puts it, does its word, Close or Cancel, say what leaving loses, and is anything entered kept or asked about when the view is left by button or by swipe? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Modality (introduction; Best practices: Always give people an obvious way to dismiss a modal view), `reference/hig/modality.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Gestures (Custom gestures: Use shortcut gestures to supplement standard gestures, not replace them), `reference/hig/gestures.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Modal (When to use), `reference/uswds/components/modal.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Sheets (Best practices: Provide an alternative to the Done button; iOS, iPadOS), `reference/hig/sheets.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Keyboards (Standard keyboard shortcuts: Esc), `reference/hig/keyboards.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Privacy (Pre-alert screens, windows, or views: Don't include additional actions in your custom screen or window, unless needed to obtain a legal consent), `reference/hig/privacy.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Sheets (iOS, iPadOS), `reference/hig/sheets.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Design principles (Agency: Give people the freedom to explore), `reference/hig/design-principles.md`; Apple, Principles of great design, WWDC26 session 250 (2026) (Agency, 2:17), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2026-250.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Calibration: Let people cancel calibration at any time), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Sheets (iOS, iPadOS: Support swiping to dismiss a sheet; Include a grabber in a resizable sheet; watchOS: If you change the default label, prefer using SF Symbols to represent the action), `reference/hig/sheets.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Modality (Best practices: When necessary, help people avoid data loss by getting confirmation before closing a modal view), `reference/hig/modality.md`; Material Design 3, Dialogs (Full-screen dialog: Saving selections; Dismissing; Navigation), `reference/material/m3-dialogs-guidelines.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A news app's full-screen video, which covers the article and holds nothing to lose. Pass: a Close button, an X, sits at the top of the video. Fail: the same video closes only by swiping down, with no button; or its button says Cancel, as if leaving would give something up.

### An empty place says why

Called for by: list. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**A place in the app with nothing to show stays where it is and stays open, and it says why it is empty and what would fill it; it is not hidden, disabled or left blank.** "Don't disable or hide tab bar buttons, even when their content is unavailable. Having tab bar buttons available in some cases but not others makes your app's interface appear unstable and unpredictable. If a section is empty, explain why its content is unavailable." Where a problem empties a view, as with no connection at startup, Apple offers "cached or placeholder data and a nonintrusive label that describes the problem". A section or a tab with nothing in it is not disabled, since "Disabling controls is normally confusing for users", but removed or opened to say "why there is no content". Where a search or a request finds nothing, the screen offers another way to the goal in place of the empty result: "Suggest alternative ways to accomplish the goal instead of showing no results." Where the person is the one who fills it, the empty place holds the control that starts filling it, beside the words: "Empty states should prompt to create a new item, with optional templates". Check: for each place and list on this screen, what does it show when it is empty, and does that say why and what would fill it? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Tab bars (Best practices: Don't disable or hide tab bar buttons, even when their content is unavailable), `reference/hig/tab-bars.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Alerts (Best practices: Avoid showing an alert when your app starts), `reference/hig/alerts.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Accordion (Do not disable sections), `reference/govuk/components/accordion.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Tabs (Do not disable tabs), `reference/govuk/components/tabs.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Limitations: Demonstrate how to get the best results), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Freiberg, Web Interface Guidelines (2023) (Design), `reference/freiberg/README.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A library app's Holds tab. Pass: when empty it reads No holds yet. Search for a book to place one. Fail: the same tab is greyed out and cannot be tapped.

### The default is never the loss

Called for by: loses. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**The control a person may take without reading never destroys anything: not the prominent one, not the default, not the one a keyboard's Return or a double tap fires. A loss the person did not deliberately choose wears the platform's mark of destruction and sits beside a plain Cancel.** "Don't assign the primary role to a button that performs a destructive action, even if that action is the most likely choice. Because of its visual prominence, people sometimes choose a primary button without reading it first." And: "Use the destructive style to identify a button that performs a destructive action people didn't deliberately choose." Also: "If there's a destructive action, include a Cancel button to give people a clear, safe way to avoid the action", with the warning: "you don't want to make a Cancel button the default button. If you want to encourage people to read an alert and not just automatically press Return to dismiss it, avoid making any button the default button." On Apple Watch, a double tap on a notification "acts on the first nondestructive action". The held "A slip costs nothing" keeps a loss from being one tap away; this claim keeps it off the tap made without reading. This holds even where the person chose the destructive act on purpose, as Buttons has it, since a prompt on a deliberate path is dismissed unread, and the Alerts page's Empty Trash default is not followed. Where a loss is confirmed in two steps, the first button is plain and the mark of destruction is kept for the final one ("use another style of button for the initial call to action, and a warning button for the final confirmation"), and the button's words and context say what will happen, not its red alone. In a menu, a command that destroys sits at the end, in the platform's destructive style: "list them at the end of the menu and identify them as destructive". A notification's buttons are kept to what loses nothing, a destructive one only where people have "enough context to avoid unintended consequences." Check: on this screen and in each prompt it raises, which control is prominent or the default, what does it do, and is every destructive control marked as one? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Buttons (Role: Don't assign the primary role to a button that performs a destructive action, even if that action is the most likely choice), `reference/hig/buttons.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Alerts (Buttons: Use the destructive style to identify a button that performs a destructive action people didn't deliberately choose; If there's a destructive action, include a Cancel button), `reference/hig/alerts.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Action sheets (Best practices: Make destructive choices visually prominent), `reference/hig/action-sheets.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Gestures (watchOS: Double tap), `reference/hig/gestures.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Button (Warning buttons), `reference/govuk/components/button.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Context menus (Best practices: In iOS, iPadOS, and visionOS, warn people about context menu items that can destroy data), `reference/hig/context-menus.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Notification actions: Prefer nondestructive actions), `reference/hig/notifications.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A password manager's Delete Vault prompt. Pass: Delete is red, Cancel sits beside it, and Return fires nothing. Fail: the same prompt makes Delete the bold default that Return fires.

### One place, one gesture

Called for by: controls, motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**A control is not placed where another gesture begins (the device's own edge swipes, a scroll, an index, another control's swipe), since a hand meant for one sets off the other.** "Avoid conflicting with gestures that access system UI." A custom gesture must be "Distinct from other gestures". "Avoid adding an index to a table that displays controls — like disclosure indicators — in the trailing ends of its rows... it can be difficult for people to use one element without activating the other." "scrolling an action sheet can be hard to do without inadvertently tapping a button." On Apple Watch, a primary action in a scrolling view "conflicts with the default navigation behaviors that people expect when they double-tap". The held "Small and far is mis-tapped" is about two targets beside each other; this claim is about a target and a gesture in the same place. An item has one menu on touch and hold, a menu of commands or an editing menu, never both, since with both "it can be confusing to people — and difficult for the system to detect their intent." A scroll inside a sheet or a panel stops at its end and does not move the page behind it, and a strip that swipes sideways leaves up and down to the page, so a sideways swipe does not set off "the page jittering up while the carousel moves". Check: for each control on this screen, what other gesture starts on it or crosses it, and what happens when the person meant that other gesture? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Gestures (Custom gestures: Add custom gestures only when necessary; Avoid conflicting with gestures that access system UI; watchOS: Avoid setting a primary action in views with lists, scroll views, or vertical tabs), `reference/hig/gestures.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Lists and tables (iOS, iPadOS, visionOS: Avoid adding an index to a table that displays controls), `reference/hig/lists-and-tables.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Action sheets (iOS, iPadOS: Avoid letting an action sheet scroll), `reference/hig/action-sheets.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Context menus (iOS, iPadOS: Provide either a context menu or an edit menu for an item, but not both), `reference/hig/context-menus.md`; Kowalski, Feeling Native On Mobile, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (6. Pull-to-refresh hijacks scroll; 9. Carousel scrolls vertically), `reference/kowalski/skills/mobile-native/SKILL.source.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Interactions: Overscroll behavior), `reference/vercel/README.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A shop's carousel of product cards that swipes sideways. Pass: it stops a thumb's width short of the screen's left edge, where the system's back swipe begins. Fail: the same carousel runs to the edge, so a swipe meant for the next card leaves the page.

### Nothing plays without a way to stop it

Called for by: sound. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where the app plays sound or video, it does not start by itself, or it starts with a stop control in sight that ends it at once.** "Let people control audio and video playback. Avoid autoplaying audio and video content without also providing controls to start and stop it. Make sure these controls are discoverable and easy to act upon, and consider global settings that let people opt out of auto-playing all audio and video." Sound that plays by itself for more than three seconds can be paused, stopped or turned down apart from the device's volume, since muting the device also silences a screen reader. A player has controls to pause or stop, change the volume and turn captions on and off. Check: does anything on this screen play on its own, and where is the control that stops it? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Cognitive: Let people control audio and video playback), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.4.2 Audio Control, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/audio-control.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.4.2 Audio Control (Intent of Audio Control), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/audio-control.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Accessibility (Sensory-sensitive users), `reference/uswds/documentation/guidance/accessibility.md`; Android Developers, Principles for improving app accessibility (Make media content more accessible), `reference/android/principles.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A news feed with videos. Pass: each video plays muted, with pause and unmute in sight. Fail: the same feed plays a video with sound as it loads and shows no controls until the video is tapped.

### A time limit is the person's to lengthen

Called for by: wait. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where a screen sets a time limit, the person can turn it off, or lengthen it to at least ten times its default, before they meet it. Otherwise they are warned before it ends and given at least 20 seconds to extend it with one simple action, at least ten times over. Only a live event, a limit whose extension would undo the activity, or a limit over 20 hours stands as set.** People with "blindness, low vision, dexterity impairments, and cognitive limitations may require more time to read content or to perform functions". A limit set "to address security concerns" still counts, and AAA asks that timing not be part of the activity at all, "except for non-interactive synchronized media and real-time events". Where a short hold is truly essential, as for seats that sell out, the screen still moves "as much of the process out of the time-critical period as possible". This sits beside the held "A choice is not hurried", which says why a clock hurts a choice; this one says what the person can do about a clock. A message that leaves the screen by itself after a time does not spend that time while the app is out of sight, so it is still there to read when the person comes back: "Pause toast timers when the tab is hidden." Check: what on this screen ends on a clock, can the person turn it off or set it longer before it starts, and if not, what warns them, how long do they have to answer, and how many times can they extend with one tap? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 2.2.1 Timing Adjustable, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/timing-adjustable.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.2.3 No Timing, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/no-timing.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.2.1 Timing Adjustable (Intent of Timing Adjustable; Notes regarding time limits; Examples of Timing Adjustable), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/timing-adjustable.md`; Kowalski, Design Engineering, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (The Sonner Principles (Building Loved Components)), `reference/kowalski/skills/emil-design-eng/SKILL.source.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A banking app that signs out after five minutes idle. Pass: at four minutes a sheet asks Still there?, and Stay signed in restarts the clock, as many times as needed. Fail: the same app signs out at five minutes with no warning, losing a half-typed payment.

### What moves on its own can be stopped

Called for by: motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where something on a screen moves, blinks, scrolls or updates by itself beside other content, the person can pause, stop or hide it, or set how often it updates, unless the movement is the activity. Movement that ends within five seconds is exempt; repeated updating is not.** Such content is "a barrier to anyone who has trouble reading stationary text quickly as well as anyone who has trouble tracking moving objects". "Five seconds was chosen because it is long enough to get a user's attention, but not so long that a user cannot wait out the distraction". For updating content "there is no five second exception". Check: what on this screen moves or changes without being asked, does it run past five seconds or keep updating, and where is the control that stops it? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 2.2.2 Pause, Stop, Hide, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/pause-stop-hide.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.2.2 Pause, Stop, Hide (Intent of Pause, Stop, Hide), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/pause-stop-hide.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Accessibility (Sensory-sensitive users), `reference/uswds/documentation/guidance/accessibility.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A shopping app's home screen with a carousel of offers. Pass: the carousel advances every four seconds and has a Pause button on it. Fail: the same carousel advances for as long as the screen is open, with no way to stop it.

### What the app pushes in can wait

Called for by: pushes. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where the app brings in updates, alerts or messages the person did not ask for, the person can put them off, and can turn them off for good. The exception is a warning of danger to health, safety, property or their data.** The aim is "to allow users to turn off updates from the author/server except in emergencies". Emergencies are messages that "warn of danger to health, safety, or property, including data loss, loss of connection". Putting updates off lets people with attention difficulties focus "on the content", and lets screen-reader users keep their place. The held "An interruption only where something would be lost" governs the app's own dialogs; this one governs what arrives unasked. A notice that stays on the screen can be closed by the person once read, wherever nothing is lost by closing it: "Allow a user to dismiss a notification whenever appropriate."
