Where news arrives while the person is in the app, it comes in quietly where it belongs, "such as incrementing a badge or subtly inserting new data into the current view", never as a notice over what they are doing, since "sending a notification about it would be unnecessary and distracting." This holds for reminders the person likes: in a 28-day pilot, 7 of 9 adults who got spoken reminders liked them yet "valued the ability to turn them off when they were busy", which the app allowed for a day at a time. Check: what does this screen bring in unasked while the person works, and where can they hold it for later or turn it off? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 2.2.4 Interruptions, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/interruptions.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.2.4 Interruptions (Intent of Interruptions), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/interruptions.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Alert (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/alert.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Best practices: Handle notifications gracefully when your app is in the foreground), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Chikwetu et al., Automated Diet Capture Using Voice Alerts and Speech Recognition on Smartphones: Pilot Usability and Acceptability Study, JMIR Formative Research (2023) (Results: User Engagement Metrics; Methods: Personalized Alerts), `reference/studies/PMC10230351.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A share trading app that drops market headlines in as banners. Pass: a Hold headlines switch keeps them in the News tab while an order is being written. Fail: the same banners slide over the order form every few minutes, with no way to hold them.

### A tap acts when the finger lifts

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**A tap acts when the finger is lifted on the target, so a person who presses the wrong thing can slide off it and let go to cancel. Acting on the press is kept for keys of a keyboard or keypad.** The aim is "to make it easier for users to prevent accidental or erroneous pointer input". "If they discover they have selected the wrong item, they can cancel the action by moving their pointer or finger away from the target before releasing." A drag can be cancelled by returning the thing to where it started, or undone after. "Functions that emulate a keyboard or numeric keypad key press are considered essential". Apple's own account adds what the finger meets on the way: the control "should highlight immediately when I touch down on it", sliding back onto it before the lift highlights it again and lets the tap go through, and the area that holds the press reaches a margin past the control's edge, which "will make our taps more comfortable, and avoid accidental cancellations if a touch moves during interaction". Check: for each control on this screen, does it show pressed the moment the finger lands, does it act on the press or on the lift, and does sliding off before the lift cancel it? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 2.5.2 Pointer Cancellation, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/21/pointer-cancellation.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.5.2 Pointer Cancellation (Intent; Up-Event activation or completion; Up-Event Abort or Undo), `reference/wcag/understanding/21/pointer-cancellation.md`; Apple, Designing Fluid Interfaces, WWDC18 session 803 (2018) (Tap, 50:00), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2018-803.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A video call app's End call button. Pass: pressing it and sliding the thumb off before lifting leaves the call running. Fail: the same button ends the call the instant it is touched.

### Typed stays typed

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**What a person types is not changed under them, corrected, capitalised or rewritten in another form, and what they gave is shown back to them as they gave it, save a number the platform formats for its kind.** Spellchecking is turned off "for information which is not appropriate to spellcheck, like a reference number, name, email address". The same goes for automatic capitals, since "Some browsers might automatically change what the user is typing when the input's text is visible, such as correcting spelling or automatically turning on upper case letters at the start of sentences." A page names an observation: "The GOV.UK Notify team have observed some users becoming confused when presented with a reformatted version of a phone number that they provided, for example, with the +44 country code added." An input mask, which forces the shape as the person types, makes it harder to "type a number in their preferred way" and to "transcribe a number from another place and check that they've got it right". The held "Any way a person would write it" turns the value into the code's form once, at the edge; this claim keeps the person's own form on the screen. The exception is a number of a kind the platform formats, an amount, a share or a value to set decimal places, which is shown in that kind's form for the person's locale, as currency, as a percentage or to its decimal places, as Apple's number fields do: a formatter "can also display the value in a specific way, such as with a certain number of decimal places, as a percentage, or as currency", and the format is never assumed, since it "can vary significantly based on people's locale". A name is shown back whole wherever it is shown, however long, with its capitals as typed: "Do display the complete name wherever the information is displayed (even if it is long), including on a confirmation page." A template that puts in its own brackets, hyphens or spaces as the person types is a mask too, and is not used; the field takes the separators the person types, and the code removes them. Nor is a key refused as it is pressed: a field that takes only digits still shows a letter typed into it and says beside it what it takes, since "Blocking keystrokes entirely is confusing because the user gets no explanation." Check: type a surname, a code and a phone number into this screen; does anything change them as they are typed, or when they are shown back? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Text input (How and when to spellcheck a user's input), `reference/govuk/components/text-input.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Password input (Do not spellcheck or autocapitalise the user's input), `reference/govuk/components/password-input.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Phone numbers (Avoid input masking; Avoid reformatting phone numbers), `reference/govuk/patterns/phone-numbers.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Names (Do not spellcheck user's names), `reference/govuk/patterns/names.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Name (What to do; What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/name.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Text fields (Best practices: Use a number formatter to help with numeric data), `reference/hig/text-fields.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Forms: Don’t block typing), `reference/vercel/README.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A messaging app's new contact form. Pass: the surname Mcdonagh stays as typed. Fail: the same field corrects it to McDonald as it is typed.

### A limit is told, never cut

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Where an entry has a limit, the person sees the limit and how near they are as they write, can go past it and is then told by how much, and nothing they typed or pasted is cut off without a word.** A hard cut-off "means there is no feedback to users that their text input is truncated. This is especially true where the text has been copied and pasted from elsewhere. This can cause users to accidentally provide incorrect or incomplete information." The character count works the other way: "The user can enter more than the character limit, but are told they've entered too many characters. This lets them type or copy and paste their full answer, then edit it down." The limit is set "higher than most users will need", and "If your users keep hitting the character limit imposed by the backend of your service then try to increase the limit". The page names research: in 2017 the component "was tested with 17 users, including those with low digital skills and users with disabilities". A count of characters is shown only where people may reach the limit, not where the kind of value already implies its length, as with a phone number, nor where "well over 99%" of people never come near it. Check: for each entry on this screen with a limit, where is the limit shown, what happens to the first character past it, and how many people reach it? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Text input (Avoid restricting the length of a user's input), `reference/govuk/components/text-input.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Character count (When not to use this component; How it works; Avoid narrow limits; Research on this component), `reference/govuk/components/character-count.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Password input (Do not use maxlength to restrict password length), `reference/govuk/components/password-input.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Character count (When to consider something else), `reference/uswds/components/character-count.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A marketplace app's item description, limited to 500 characters. Pass: a pasted 540-character description stays whole, with 40 characters too many shown beneath it. Fail: the same paste is cut at 500 characters without a word.

### Checked by sight, not typed twice

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Where a person must be sure an entry is right, the screen lets them see it, a hidden entry shown when they ask and a typed one shown back to them, rather than asking them to type it a second time.** "It's not necessary to add a second password field, also known as a 'confirm password' field, particularly as this component allows users to show and hide passwords." Showing the password "allows users to visually check their password before they submit it, which helps them reduce errors". For email, "Some services ask users to repeat their email address. Only do this if your user research shows it to be effective", and the address is checked instead by "confirming their address back to them so they can check and change it". Kind: a decision of the system's team, that "having a second field is not helpful for users", which it says it would like to "better support ... with real life examples". A spoken entry is shown back as the words the app heard, with a way to say it again: the app in one pilot showed the transcribed text, and "If the user notices any discrepancies in the transcribed text, they can rerecord"; several of its 18 adults, "especially those with foreign accents", said recognition did not always transcribe them accurately. Check: does this screen ask for any entry twice, and could the person see it to check it instead? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Password input (How it works; Avoid adding a 'confirm password' field; Research on this component), `reference/govuk/components/password-input.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Email addresses (Help users to enter a valid email address), `reference/govuk/patterns/email-addresses.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Email address (What not to do; Usability guidance), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/email-address.md`; Chikwetu et al., Automated Diet Capture Using Voice Alerts and Speech Recognition on Smartphones: Pilot Usability and Acceptability Study, JMIR Formative Research (2023) (Methods: Study Design; Limitations), `reference/studies/PMC10230351.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A streaming app's new-password screen. Pass: one field with a Show button beside it. Fail: the same screen asks for the password twice, both fields hidden.

### Only what the person can fix is told at the entry

Called for by: errors. Cross-check: Nielsen 9, Help users recognize, diagnose, and recover from errors.

**A message beside an entry is kept for what the person can fix by changing the entry. That they are not eligible, not allowed, or that the app cannot take them now is told on a screen of its own, which says why and what they can do instead.** "Do not use error messages to tell a user that they are not eligible or do not have permission to do something. Or to tell them about a lack of capacity or other problem the user cannot fix - because the problem is with the service rather than with the information the user has provided." Instead, "take the user to a page that explains the problem (for example, telling them why they're not eligible) and provides useful information about what to do next." The held "A fault is not theirs to diagnose" covers a failure beneath the screen; this claim covers a refusal that is not a failure. Check: for each refusal this screen can give, could the person fix it by changing what they entered, and where they cannot, where are they taken and what are they told? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Error message (When not to use this component), `reference/govuk/components/error-message.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Recover from validation errors (When not to use this pattern), `reference/govuk/patterns/validation.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Check a service is suitable (Presenting results), `reference/govuk/patterns/check-a-service-is-suitable.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A car-sharing app's sign-up, open only to drivers aged 21 and over. Pass: a 19-year-old is taken to a screen saying members must be 21, which names the bike scheme they can join. Fail: the same app shows Date of birth is invalid under the field.

### Back is in sight and loses nothing

Called for by: steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where a task runs over several steps, each step shows a way back to the one before, and going back, or elsewhere and returning, finds every entry as it was left.** "Some users do not trust browser back buttons when they're entering data." A back link sits at the top of every question page "to reassure them it's possible to go back and change previous answers". The way back must take "users to the previous page they were on, in the state they last saw it". The back-link page gives the reason: some sites break when the browser's back button is used, "so many users avoid it, instead of losing their progress in a service. Also, not all users are aware of the back button." Answers gone back to are "pre-populated", and switching language must not lose "any data they've entered". The held "A way out in sight" is for a view over the screen, and the held "A place is kept across an interruption" is for leaving the app; this claim is for moving backwards inside a task, and is one case of the held "As it was left". Check: on each step, where is the way back, and after going back two steps and forward again, is every entry still there? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Question pages (Back link), `reference/govuk/patterns/question-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Back link (introduction; How it works), `reference/govuk/components/back-link.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Check answers (Let users go back and change their answers), `reference/govuk/patterns/check-answers.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Language navigation (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/language-navigation.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A train booking app's three-step purchase. Pass: each step has Back at the top left, and the seats chosen are still chosen on return. Fail: the same steps have no Back button, and the edge swipe returns to an empty seat map.

### A change from the review returns to the review

Called for by: steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where answers are reviewed before they are sent, each has a Change of its own beside it, and a change made from the review returns there, asking on the way only what the change has made necessary.** "You should provide a 'Change' link next to each section". "When they've finished, the 'Continue' button should return them to the check answers page. They should not need to go through the rest of the transaction again." "If a user changes their response in a way that means you need to ask them more questions, do this before returning them to the check answers page." Where changing one answer makes answers already given no longer apply, the person is told which answers it will clear before they are cleared: "inform the user of potential impacts and confirm before invalidating previous form entries". Check: from the review on this screen, change one answer; where does the person land afterwards, and what are they asked on the way? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Check answers (Let users go back and change their answers), `reference/govuk/patterns/check-answers.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Summary list (Adding actions to each row), `reference/govuk/components/summary-list.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Progress easily (What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/progress-easily.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A furniture shop's order review. Pass: Change beside the delivery date opens the date step and then returns to the review. Fail: the same Change opens the date step and then walks through address and payment again.

### Where it was sent is shown, and it can be sent again

Called for by: account. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where the app sends something the person must fetch, such as a link or a code, the screen shows where it went, lets them have it sent again, and lets them correct where it goes.** The waiting page should "show the user the email address that you sent their activation email to" and "let them resend the activation email to a different email address if necessary". Behind 'Not received a text message?', the person can "check which mobile number they entered, and to change it if necessary. This prevents the user becoming stuck if they entered a mobile number incorrectly." A link that has expired is explained: "explain that it's expired, and why". At sign-in the number is not shown, for security ("Do not reveal the mobile number you sent it to"). Check: after the app sends something, where on the screen are the address it went to, a way to have it sent again and a way to change the address? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Confirm an email address (Set expiry conditions; Let users resend the email; Designing the 'activate your account' page), `reference/govuk/patterns/confirm-an-email-address.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Confirm a phone number (When the user creates an account; When the user signs in), `reference/govuk/patterns/confirm-a-phone-number.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A food delivery app's sign-up code by text. Pass: it reads Sent to 07700 900123, with Send again and Change number. Fail: the same screen reads Enter the code we sent you, with no number and no way to resend.

### What is about the person can be changed by them

Called for by: account. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**A thing the app keeps about the person themselves, such as their name, their contact address or how they sign in, can be changed later by them without starting over.** "If your service stores personal information, you should allow users to update their details, including their name." Doing so "helps your service respect their personal identity. It also means they can continue using your service without having to start over." "Avoid making it hard for users to change their name. As well as causing them distress, it may make them reluctant to use your service." For usernames: "make sure you let people change their email address or username". Details that "can change over time" can be updated. What the person gave to set a feature up can be changed or removed later, outside the setup itself, so that they "feel free to make changes at any time". Check: for each fact about the person this app keeps, where can they change it, and what do they lose by changing it? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Names (Allow users to change their name), `reference/govuk/patterns/names.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Create a username (Always let people make changes), `reference/govuk/patterns/create-a-username.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Equality information (Updating equality information), `reference/govuk/patterns/equality-information.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Calibration: Give people a way to update or remove information they provided during calibration), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A ride-hailing app's profile. Pass: the name drivers see is edited under Profile. Fail: the same name can be changed only by deleting the account and signing up again.

### A long task opens on its parts

Called for by: steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where a task is long, has parts that can be done in any order, or takes more than one sitting, it opens on a list of its parts at the start and at each return, each part named for what it involves and showing its state.** The list is shown "at the start of the transaction" and "at the start of each returning session". Related questions are summed up as a task, "for example, 'Provide financial evidence' and 'Give medical information'. This will help users understand and plan what they need to do." It is used only where there is evidence that users "do not want to, or cannot, complete all the tasks in one sitting" or "need to be able to choose the order", and "Try to simplify the transaction before you use a complete multiple tasks page." The held "A place is kept across an interruption" brings the person back to where they were; this claim gives them the whole on each return. Check: does this task take more than one sitting or allow any order, and if so, what does the person see first on each return? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Complete multiple tasks (introduction; When to use this pattern; How it works; Show related actions as a task), `reference/govuk/patterns/complete-multiple-tasks.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Task list (When to use this component), `reference/govuk/components/task-list.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A moving-house app that switches the energy, the broadband and the post. Pass: each return opens on Energy, Completed; Broadband, In progress; Post, Not yet started. Fail: the same app reopens on whichever form was last seen, with no list.

### Easy questions before hard ones

Called for by: steps, entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**Where a task asks a run of questions, it opens with the simple ones and moves to the hard or painful ones later, so the person is under way before the heaviest are asked.** USWDS: "Progressively disclose information, guiding the user from simple to more difficult questions", and "use principles of progressive disclosure to gradually move from simple to more difficult questions". It gives the reason for people under strain: "People under stress may have poor recall, make frequent errors completing forms, and may not notice the errors they make." The page rests on guidance; its references include trauma-informed design papers, such as "Trauma-informed computing: towards safer technology experiences for all" (CHI '22), which are not on the shelf, so the evidence behind it is unverified. The held "No order the task does not have" still lets the person answer in any order the task allows, as USWDS also asks ("Allow the user to complete the form in the order they choose, if possible"); this claim sets the order the screen offers first. Check: in the order this task offers its questions, where does the first hard or painful one fall, and which simple ones come after it? Sources: U.S. Web Design System, Progress easily (What's the solution; What to do; Usability guidance), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/progress-easily.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Establish trust (What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/establish-trust.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A mortgage app's application. Pass: it opens on name and address, then income, then debts and missed payments. Fail: the same application opens on List every debt and missed payment of the last six years.

### The rules for an entry are shown before it and ticked as met

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Where an entry must meet more than one rule, each rule is listed beside it before the person types, and each is marked as met while they type, so the rules are learned by meeting them, not by breaking them.** USWDS: "Stating validation requirements up front, with live feedback, means users won't be left guessing", and the component is there to "give immediate feedback on which validation requirements have been successfully met, and which are not completed yet." It marks what is met and flags nothing as wrong while the person types, so it keeps within the held "Guidance comes as the entry is made". The held "Any way a person would write it" removes rules where it can, and "Getting in asks nothing to be remembered" keeps a password to one; this claim covers the rules that remain. The sources are thin: a component page whose usability and accessibility sections are empty, with no research, and two practice articles that name no study. Live checking suits an entry most people are "unlikely to enter" right "on their first try", such as a new password, since it keeps them from "guessing or checking multiple times". A tick is kept for an entry with rules to meet: a field whose only rule is that it is filled gets none, since the tick tells the person nothing. Check: for each entry on this screen with more than one rule, are all the rules in view before typing, and does each show when it is met? Sources: U.S. Web Design System, Validation (introduction; When to use), `reference/uswds/components/validation.md`; Krause, 10 Design Guidelines for Reporting Errors in Forms, Nielsen Norman Group (2019, updated 2024) (2. Indicate Successful Entry for Complex Fields), `reference/nngroup/errors-forms-design-guidelines.md`; Neusesser and Sunwall, Error-Message Guidelines, Nielsen Norman Group (2023, updated 2024) (Visibility Guidelines: Avoid prematurely displaying errors), `reference/nngroup/error-message-guidelines.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A games console app's username field, which takes 3 to 16 characters, letters and numbers only. Pass: both rules sit under the field and each ticks as it is met. Fail: the same rules are told one at a time, each only after a refusal.

### The keyboard covers nothing being worked on

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**Where the on-screen keyboard is up, the field being typed in, what the person needs to read to fill it and the button that sends it stay in view above the keyboard, and any controls placed on the bar above the keyboard belong to the entry under way.** Apple asks that the screen move with the keyboard so as to "keep important parts of your interface visible while the virtual keyboard is onscreen". The page's pictures, whose captions alone are on the shelf, show the two failures: "Without the layout guide, the keyboard could make entering text more difficult" and "Without the layout guide, the keyboard could make tapping a button more difficult." Controls on the bar above it are kept to the task: "If your app offers custom controls that augment the keyboard, make sure they're relevant to the current task." The source is a guideline with its pictures missing. The held "Focus is seen, and seen whole" keeps a focused item uncovered for a person moving item by item; this claim covers the system's keyboard, which no held claim does. Check: with the keyboard up, on the smallest phone the app supports and at the largest text size, can the person see the field, its label and hint, and the button that sends it? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Virtual keyboards (iOS, iPadOS: Use the keyboard layout guide to make the keyboard feel like an integrated part of your interface; Place custom controls above the keyboard thoughtfully), `reference/hig/virtual-keyboards.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A parking app's number plate field. Pass: as the keyboard rises, the field, its label and the Pay button move up to sit just above it. Fail: the same keyboard rises over the Pay button, which can be reached only by closing the keyboard.

### What the finger sets stays in sight

Called for by: controls, motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where a person drags or holds a control to set a value, the value it is set to stays in sight while the finger is on it, never hidden under the finger until it lifts.** The platform's own sliders and switches do this. While held, they "lift up into Liquid Glass temporarily", which "works great for things like controls, where the transparent liquid lens can be seen through to precisely observe the value underneath it". At rest they stay "visually quiet". A control in the content takes the floating layer's look only for that moment, as "the element takes on a Liquid Glass appearance to emphasize its interactivity when a person activates it". This is Apple's guidance on its own controls; no study on the shelf measured what a hidden value costs. The held "Every action is answered at once" keeps a dragged thing under the finger; this claim keeps what it sets in view. The platform does the same wherever a finger covers what it places: dragging the text caret raises a magnifying loupe above the touch point, which goes once the finger no longer covers the caret, and a pressed key shows enlarged above the finger, "which gives you confidence that the interface understood what you meant", as a practice essay describes them. Where the control is too thin to show around the finger, as a slider is, the drag keeps hold when the finger strays off the control while still pressed, so the finger can move clear to see the value: "It helps to ensure that the dragging gesture does not cancel when moving away from the slider and still pressing down". Check: for each control on this screen that is dragged or held to set a value, where does the value show while the finger is down, is any of it under the fingertip, and does the drag keep hold when the finger strays off the control? Sources: Apple, Meet Liquid Glass, WWDC25 session 219 (2025) (Dynamics, 4:10), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-219.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Materials (Liquid Glass: Don't use Liquid Glass in the content layer), `reference/hig/materials.md`; Freiberg, Invisible Details of Interaction Design (2023) (Touch Content Visiblity), `reference/essays/freiberg-invisible-details.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A photo editor's Exposure slider, marked in steps. Pass: while it is held, the thumb swells into a clear lens, so the step marks beneath show around the fingertip. Fail: the same slider's thumb stays a solid white disc under the fingertip, so the marks beneath are hidden until the finger lifts.

### A correction holds

Called for by: generated. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where the person corrects what the app guessed, made or suggested, the corrected result shows at once, stays corrected wherever the thing appears and the next time it does, and can itself be corrected the same way.** Apple: "Reward people's effort by instantly displaying corrected content", and "be sure to persist the updates so people don't have to make the same corrections again." What the person tells the app they do not want goes from everywhere: "hide it from their view and ensure it doesn't appear elsewhere in your app. When you react immediately to feedback and show that your app remembers it, you build people's trust in the value of providing it." A correction may itself be a slip, so "Let people correct their corrections", answered at once and kept the same way. When a correction takes, the screen gives "a clear signal that their action had an effect". The held "Typed stays typed" keeps what the person typed from being changed under them; this claim keeps the app's next guess from undoing what they corrected. The pages are platform guidance and cite no study. A correction changes the part the person pointed at and leaves the rest as it was: Nielsen calls editing a part of a generated picture "vastly superior to the 'reroll' randomness of prompting for a revision", which remakes the whole and can only "hope it matches"; this is practice and cites no study. A correction may stand for more than the one thing corrected: a listener in Song's study who knew a field the app had not been told of "wished they could indicate that they did not need support for quantum-related terms" once the first appeared, and a replay of the seven listeners' own labels, a simulation and not people using it, found that letting each Already Knew shape what was shown next raised the share of shown terms they had judged helpful from 77.5% to 83.4%. Check: correct one thing the app guessed on this screen; does the change show at once, is it still corrected when the screen opens again and elsewhere in the app, and can the person change it back? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Corrections: Provide immediate value when people make a correction; Let people correct their corrections), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Explicit feedback: Act immediately when you receive explicit feedback and persist the resulting changes), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Outputs: Make it easy for people to refine or revert generated results, and acknowledge when their corrections take effect), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`; Nielsen, 18 Predictions for 2026 (2026) (Prediction 13, Editing AI-Generated Images), `reference/essays/nielsen-2026-predictions.md`; Song et al., Breaking the Curse of Knowledge: Designing Personalized Jargon Support for Real-Time Online Meetings, arXiv (2025) (3.4 Design Implications; 4.2 Refined Personalization and Interface; 5.2 Personalization Evaluation), `reference/papers/song-2025-breaking-the-curse-of-knowledge.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A photo app's face labels. Pass: after the person renames a face from Tom to Sam, every photo of him reads Sam, and still does after the next scan. Fail: the same rename changes one photo, and the next scan labels him Tom again.

### The keyboard comes when it is asked for

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**Where a screen opens on a field, the on-screen keyboard rises when the person taps into the field, not as the screen opens, unless typing that one entry is all the screen is for.** "Inputs should not auto focus on touch devices as it will open the keyboard and cover the screen"; and "On desktop screens with a single primary input, autofocus. Rarely autofocus on mobile because the keyboard opening can cause layout shift." A keyboard that rises unasked covers the lower part of the screen before the person has read what the screen holds, and it moves what was there. The held "The keyboard covers nothing being worked on" governs the screen once the keyboard is up. This claim governs when the keyboard comes up. Both sources are practice guides that cite no study, and Apple's guidelines on the shelf do not speak to it. The exception follows Vercel's "Rarely", against Freiberg's flat rule; see Tensions. Check: when this screen opens, is the keyboard already up, and if so, is typing one entry all the screen is for? Sources: Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Interactions: Autofocus for speed), `reference/vercel/README.md`; Freiberg, Web Interface Guidelines (2023) (Touch), `reference/freiberg/README.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A hotel booking app's search screen, with a destination, dates and guests. Pass: it opens with all three in view, and the keyboard rises when Destination is tapped. Fail: the same screen opens with the keyboard already up, covering the dates and the guests.

### A swipe that loses something waits for the lift

Called for by: motion, loses. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where a swipe removes, closes or leaves something, it acts only when the finger lifts, however far the swipe has gone, so a person who changes their mind partway can turn back and lose nothing.** In a practice essay's account, the platform's own app switcher "will never dismiss an app before the gesture ends. No matter the distance or the fact that the app is partially off-screen", because "dismissing an app is destructive" and "it wouldn't feel nice if the app were to dismiss in the middle of the swipe", when "I could potentially lose some important progress in an app!" Going to another view waits for the lift too: a swipe that has brought the next view fully into sight does not settle on it until the finger lifts, which makes it "lightweight to briefly peek at another screen" and look "without committing to it". A swipe that only shows something over the screen and loses nothing, such as a search panel, may act once its moving parts reach their place while the finger is still down, since there "Waiting for the gesture to end would feel inappropriate", and it is taken back if the finger turns back before lifting. This is the swipe's form of the held "A tap acts when the finger lifts". Its standard asks the same of any action done with a single pointer: "Completion of the function is on the up-event, and a mechanism is available to abort the function before completion or to undo the function after completion". With drag and drop, it notes, the person aborts by returning the item "to its original location". The essay is practice and cites no study. Check: for each swipe on this screen that removes, closes or leaves something, does it act only when the finger lifts, and does turning back before the lift leave everything as it was? Sources: Freiberg, Invisible Details of Interaction Design (2023) (Swipe Gestures), `reference/essays/freiberg-invisible-details.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.5.2 Pointer Cancellation, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/21/pointer-cancellation.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.5.2 Pointer Cancellation (Up-Event Abort or Undo), `reference/wcag/understanding/21/pointer-cancellation.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A mail app's inbox, where a long swipe left on a message deletes it. Pass: the row slides with the thumb and Delete grows beside it, the message goes only when the thumb lifts, and sliding back before lifting leaves it in the inbox. Fail: the same row deletes the message the moment the swipe passes halfway, while the thumb is still on the glass.

## Responsibility: Act in people’s best interest.

### A forcing function costs the least that still forces

Called for by: loses. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**An interlock, a lock-in or a lockout is reached for only where the failure it stops is grave, is the lightest restraint that stops it, and a lock-in's prompt offers the wanted action from the prompt itself.** A forcing function that is a nuisance in normal use is disabled or propped open, the brick in the secure door, and then guards nothing; the fire-stair gate restrains enough to notice and not enough to prop; the save-before-exit prompt with Save on it becomes the user's shortcut. A guard that removes one danger often creates a second, the barrier round the hole that then needs its own lights and signs, so a guard added to a screen is itself a thing to be signified; and a rule that stops the work getting done is violated routinely, so a restraint the activity cannot live with is propped open. Where leaving a view would lose what the person made, the guard stands on every way out, a gesture or a button alike, and its prompt offers saving among its choices. A lockout after wrong sign-ins comes no sooner than the tenth try, since the current sign-in standard says "Allow at least 10 entry attempts"; a wait that grows after each miss, from seconds to an hour, is preferred to a lock, and the screen says how many tries remain or how long the wait is. A step that makes a person think before taking what the app suggests is such a restraint, and it costs about what it saves: in Buçinca's online experiment with 199 crowd workers in the United States, who chose a low-carbohydrate swap for a meal in a photo with help from a simulated AI that was right three times in four, three such steps, a See AI's suggestion button, deciding before the suggestion was shown, and a 30-second wait, raised how often people picked the right ingredient to replace when the suggestion was wrong from 8% to 27%, did not differ significantly from one another, and left accuracy over all decisions where it was, 33% fully right against 35% without them. People rated the steps more complex, the gain went mostly to those high in what the authors call need for cognition, how much a person "enjoys engaging in cognitively demanding activities", people "performed best in conditions that they preferred and trusted the least", and the authors would keep such steps to "a small fraction of cases where using them is predicted to substantially improve the final decision". Check: for each confirmation or blocked step in this change, what failure does it stop, is that grave, is the restraint the lightest that works, and does the prompt carry the action? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 4, Knowing What to Do: Constraints, Discoverability, and Feedback (Constraints That Force the Desired Behavior: Forcing Functions; Interlocks; Lock-Ins; Lockouts), `reference/books/norman-2013/04-chapter-4-knowing-what-to-do-constraints-discoverability-and.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 6, Design Thinking (Deliberately Making Things Difficult), `reference/books/norman-2013/06-chapter-6-design-thinking.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Deliberate Violations), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Modality (Best practices: When necessary, help people avoid data loss by getting confirmation before closing a modal view), `reference/hig/modality.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Alerts (iOS, iPadOS: Use an action sheet — not an alert — to offer choices related to an intentional action), `reference/hig/alerts.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Passwords (Handling incorrect login attempts), `reference/govuk/patterns/passwords.md`; NIST SP 800-63B-4 (2025), Authenticator and Verifier Requirements (Rate Limiting (Throttling)); Customer Experience Considerations (Common Usability Considerations for Authenticators), `reference/nist/sp800-63b.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Sheets (iOS, iPadOS: Support swiping to dismiss a sheet), `reference/hig/sheets.md`; Buçinca et al., To Trust or to Think: Cognitive Forcing Functions Can Reduce Overreliance on AI in AI-assisted Decision-making, CSCW (2021) (3.1 Task description; 3.2 Conditions; 3.5 Participants; 4.1 Objective measures; 4.2 Subjective measures; 5 Ethical considerations; 6 Discussion), `reference/papers/bucinca-2021-to-trust-or-to-think.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A photo editor's Close button with unsaved edits. Pass: the prompt asks Save changes to Beach.jpg? with Save, Don't Save and Cancel. Fail: the same prompt says only Unsaved changes with OK, so saving means closing it and finding Save in a menu.

### What the app filled in looks like the app's

Called for by: generated, entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**A value the screen supplied in the user's place, a default, a value carried from last time, a value derived by a rule, a value or a text the app predicted or generated, or a fallback when its usual source failed, is shown as the screen's and told apart from what the user entered, plainly and not in small letters.** The Royal Majesty's navigation system silently switched from GPS to dead reckoning when a cable came loose, and the design didn't make this apparent, so the crew did not read the tiny letters "dr" and the ship ran aground days later; the automation had performed flawlessly for years, which increased people's trust and so the normal manual checks were not done. When automation fails, it often does so without warning, and the person is out of the loop. Data that changes over time, or comes from elsewhere, shows where it came from and when it was last brought up to date: "provide the source and/or last updated date". A list the app keeps current says when it last did so, as the platform's podcast list tells "when the last podcast update occurred", and never how to refresh. What a model wrote or made is marked as the app's where it appears and never passed off as a person's: "Clearly identify when and where you use AI", and "Never trick someone into thinking they're interacting with or viewing content authored by a human if they're actually interacting with AI." Since a model "may convincingly present the information as factual, even when it's not", the screen says plainly that such content "may contain errors". People seldom correct what the app filled in: in a 28-day pilot of 18 adults aged 20 to 39, whose logs the app filled in from what they said or typed, all viewed their logs and none changed one, though 12 said they valued being able to, and the authors suggest "the average user inherently trusts autopopulated data or may not think it is worth the effort to change it". Saying so does not on its own make people check: a review of studies of people using AI advisers reports that "Generic 'ChatGPT can make mistakes' warnings did not alter diagnostic advice-taking relative to a no-warning control", and concludes that "Organizations should be cautious about assuming that users will be skeptical or that verification will occur simply because users have been warned", so the mark and the warning are kept because they are true, not counted on to catch a wrong answer. Nor is what a model writes dressed as a person writing it: "visual cues like typing indicators" "contribute to the illusion of human-like behavior", and a position paper argues from the studies it cites that human-like cues and language lead people to rely on what a model says beyond what it can do. Check: which values on this screen did the user not enter, is each marked as the screen's, and when the screen falls back from one source to another, does it say so where the value sits? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (The Paradox of Automation), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Table (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/table.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Progress indicators (iOS, iPadOS, Refresh content controls: Supply a short title only if it adds value), `reference/hig/progress-indicators.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Best practices: Keep people in control; Transparency: Communicate where your app uses AI; Inputs: Raise awareness about and minimize the chance of hallucinations), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`; Chikwetu et al., Automated Diet Capture Using Voice Alerts and Speech Recognition on Smartphones: Pilot Usability and Acceptability Study, JMIR Formative Research (2023) (Results: User Engagement Metrics; Discussion: Principal Findings), `reference/studies/PMC10230351.md`; Blanchard, Garvey and O'Laughlin, Hallucinations in Organization-backed AI advisors, arXiv (2026) (2. Skepticism, verification, and reliance in goal-directed use; 3. Organizational and policy responses), `reference/papers/blanchard-2026-hallucinations-in-organization-backed-ai-advisors.md`; Ibrahim et al., Measuring and Mitigating Overreliance to Build Human-Compatible AI, arXiv (2025) (Model characteristics; System-level mitigations), `reference/papers/ibrahim-2025-measuring-and-mitigating-overreliance.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A map app's location dot. Pass: when GPS is lost, the dot turns grey with Approximate location beside it. Fail: the same dot looks unchanged while the app silently guesses the position from Wi-Fi.

### Getting in asks nothing to be remembered

Called for by: account. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**Where the app asks a person to prove who they are, the first way in asks them to remember nothing: the device's own sign-in, such as a passkey opened by face, fingerprint or the phone's own code, or an account they already have. A password, where one is kept, is one the person chose under a single rule, and nothing they must recall is ever a question, a hint or a string the app made up.** WCAG counts "authentication methods provided by the user's operating system (such as Windows Hello, or Touch ID/Face ID on macOS and iOS)", apps on the person's own device "that expect the user to confirm that it is indeed them trying to log in", and a login "with a third-party provider" as no test of memory. A link or a code sent by email gets a person back in once the way in is lost, and is never the everyday way in, since "Email SHALL NOT be used for out-of-band authentication"; a code by text may serve as a second step, though NIST names it its one "restricted authenticator". A second step, which follows any password the app keeps, as the platform asks, is a code sent to the person that the device fills in or that can be pasted, or a tap on a prompt on their own device, since "A service that requires manual transcription of a verification code is not compliant"; GOV.UK asks for the code "every time a user signs in or only once per device, depending on the risk level of your service". What is imposed is written down and then lost: a web application told users to choose a PIN easy to remember, then imposed restrictions that made that impossible, and ended by advising them to write it down; Intuit required a security question from a fixed menu, so users with no answer, or several, had to remember which answer they gave. Pasting, the device's password filling and showing the password are never blocked, since remembering a password is itself a test of memory. No test of seeing or recognising stands in the way in, since tests that ask people to "recognise words or pictures" are "tests of cognitive function, which might be difficult for some users", and a password is never forced to change on a schedule, since people then "are more likely to forget their passwords" and "will tend to pick simple variations". The one rule is said before the entry: a least length of 8 characters, the shortest the sign-in standard allows where a second step follows, as one always does here, with the commonest passwords refused and no "other composition rules"; a forgotten password is recovered by a link or a code sent to the person, never by a question or a hint, since such answers are too obscure to recall, too easy for others to find, or change over time. The button for the device's own way in names it, "a phrase like 'Sign In with Face ID' instead of a generic phrase like 'Sign In.'", and names only a way this device has: "don't reference Face ID on a device that doesn't offer it." The app's own sign-in is never called a passcode, the word for the device's own unlocking code, since otherwise "people might think you're asking them to reuse their passcode in your app or game." No setting in the app turns sign-in by face or fingerprint on, since "People turn on biometric authentication at the system level, so presenting an in-app setting is redundant and could be confusing." Where the app stays signed in on the device, it may ask for the face or fingerprint as it opens, which asks nothing to be remembered: "To further protect access to apps that people keep logged in on their device, use biometric identification like Face ID, Optic ID, or Touch ID." Check: what must a person remember to get into this app, is there a way in that needs nothing remembered, and does any step make them copy a code by hand? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 7, Our Attention is Limited; Our Memory is Imperfect (Implications of Long-Term Memory Characteristics for User Interface Design), `reference/books/johnson-2010/09-chapter-7-our-attention-is-limited-our-memory-is-imperfect.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 9, Recognition is Easy; Recall is Hard (Recognition versus Recall: Implications for UI Design, Make authentication information easy to recall), `reference/books/johnson-2010/11-chapter-9-recognition-is-easy-recall-is-hard.md`; WCAG 2.2, 3.3.8 Accessible Authentication (Minimum), Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/22/accessible-authentication-minimum.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 3.3.8 Accessible Authentication (Minimum) (Cognitive Function Tests; Login forms; Copy and paste; Two-factor authentication systems (verification codes); Personal Content; Hiding characters; Examples of Accessible Authentication (Minimum)), `reference/wcag/understanding/22/accessible-authentication-minimum.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Entering data (Best practices: Never prepopulate a password field), `reference/hig/entering-data.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Create accounts (Avoid using security measures such as CAPTCHAs), `reference/govuk/patterns/create-accounts.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Passwords (Do not make users keep changing their passwords; Helping users choose strong passwords; Send a link to trigger password resets; Avoid password reset questions; Avoid password reminders), `reference/govuk/patterns/passwords.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Confirm a phone number (When to use this pattern; When the user signs in), `reference/govuk/patterns/confirm-a-phone-number.md`; NIST SP 800-63B-4 (2025), Authenticator and Verifier Requirements (Password Verifiers; Out-of-Band Authenticators; Restricted Authenticators; Syncable Authenticators), `reference/nist/sp800-63b.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Managing accounts (Best practices: Always identify the authentication method you offer; Refer only to authentication methods that are available in the current context), `reference/hig/managing-accounts.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Managing accounts (Best practices: Avoid using the term passcode to refer to account authentication), `reference/hig/managing-accounts.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Managing accounts (Best practices: In general, avoid offering an app-specific setting for opting in to biometric authentication), `reference/hig/managing-accounts.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Privacy (Protecting data: Avoid relying solely on passwords for authentication), `reference/hig/privacy.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Managing accounts (introduction; Best practices: If you don't use Sign in with Apple in your iOS, iPadOS, macOS, or visionOS app, prefer using a passkey), `reference/hig/managing-accounts.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Privacy (Protecting data: Avoid relying solely on passwords for authentication; Avoid inventing custom authentication schemes), `reference/hig/privacy.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Managing accounts (Best practices: If you don't use Sign in with Apple in your iOS, iPadOS, macOS, or visionOS app, prefer using a passkey), `reference/hig/managing-accounts.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A water company app's sign-in. Pass: Sign in with passkey opens the phone's face check, with Sign in with Apple beside it and a password of at least 8 characters, then a code the phone fills in, only as the fallback. Fail: the same sign-in asks for a password of 12 characters and a symbol, then the name of a first pet.

### Nothing flashes more than three times a second

Called for by: motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Nothing on a screen flashes more than three times in any one second, unless the flashing area is small and dim enough to fall below the general and red flash thresholds.** Flashing "at certain frequencies for more than a few flashes" can trigger seizures, and "People are even more sensitive to red flashing than to other colors". The thresholds count area against "a 10 degree viewport", 341 x 256 CSS pixels, the central vision where people "are most susceptible to photo stimuli". The AAA level drops the small-area exception altogether. Check: does anything on this screen flash, such as an alert, a loading mark or a video, and how many times in one second? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 2.3.1 Three Flashes or Below Threshold, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/three-flashes-or-below-threshold.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.3.2 Three Flashes, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/three-flashes.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.3.1 Three Flashes or Below Threshold (Intent of Three Flashes or Below Threshold), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/three-flashes-or-below-threshold.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Accessibility (Sensory-sensitive users), `reference/uswds/documentation/guidance/accessibility.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A weather app's severe storm alert. Pass: the alert banner turns red once and stays red. Fail: the same banner flashes red and white five times a second.

### Only what is needed is asked

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**A screen asks a person only for what the task cannot be done without, and a question the task can do without is cut, not made easier to answer.** GOV.UK puts it first on every question page: "You should make sure you know why you're asking every question and only ask users for information you really need." The same rule comes back field by field: "Only ask for people's names if you need that information to deliver a service"; "Only collect phone numbers from people if you genuinely need them"; "You should only ask users to upload something if it's critical to the delivery of your service". A title is avoided because "It's extra work for them and you're asking them to potentially reveal their gender and marital status, which they may not want to do." Every question costs the person work, and some cost them privacy. Access to what the device guards, such as location, contacts, photos, the camera or the microphone, is asked for no more widely than a feature needs. It is asked when the person first uses that feature, and at launch only where the app cannot work without it: "Ideally, wait to request permission until people actually use an app feature that requires access." Where a feature needs the person's location once, it takes it once at that moment, as the platform's location button gives "one-time permission", rather than asking for standing access. A request made before the person knows what the app does is the failure Apple names, interfaces that "throw permission prompts the second you launch them. Long before you've figured out what the app actually does", while "Responsible interfaces wait for the right moment to ask for personal data" and "Only collect what your product needs to function". A step that sets a feature up before its first use is asked for only where "your feature can't function without that initial information", takes "a few pieces of important information", and avoids "asking for information that most people would have to look up." The person's view of what the app made is asked for only where their use cannot tell it, "Request explicit feedback only when necessary", is always theirs to skip, and the way to give it sits "in a clear location that doesn't interrupt the experience." Check: for each question on this screen, what would the task fail to do without its answer? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Question pages (When to use this pattern), `reference/govuk/patterns/question-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Names (When to use this pattern; Avoid asking for a person's title), `reference/govuk/patterns/names.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Phone numbers (When to use this pattern), `reference/govuk/patterns/phone-numbers.md`; GOV.UK Design System, File upload (When to use this component), `reference/govuk/components/file-upload.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Establish trust (Considerations), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/establish-trust.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Name (When to use this pattern), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/name.md`; Always make providing explicit feedback a voluntary task), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Continuous improvement: Let people share feedback on outputs), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A pizza delivery app's checkout. Pass: it asks for the address and a phone number for the driver. Fail: the same checkout also asks for a date of birth and a title.

### Why it is asked is said

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 10, Help and documentation.

**Where a question's purpose is not plain from the task, the screen says beside it why it is asked and what the answer will be used for.** On every question page, "make sure it's clear to users why you're asking each question". Saying why an email address is wanted means that "users feel confident that you're not going to abuse it" and that "users with multiple email addresses can choose which one to give you". For a phone number, "Tell users why you might contact them and when." Where the purpose is plain the reason is left out: "If the email address field is part of a sign-in box, you do not need to say 'We need your email so we can sign you in'." A request for feedback says "why you want feedback" and "what you'll do with their feedback". A question that may distress, about health, money or a death, says why it is asked just before it, since a team should "not ask for information that may be disturbing to the user without context". Where the system asks for access on the app's behalf, the app's reason in that request is "a brief, complete sentence that's straightforward, specific, and easy to understand", saying what the app does with the access and why. A pass is "The app records during the night to detect snoring sounds."; a vague benefit such as "Microphone access is needed for a better experience." fails, and so does a bare order such as "Turn on microphone access.". Best of all, "the current context helps people understand why you're requesting their permission." Apple's principles ask for "a clear rationale when asking for permission", and its talk names the failure, interfaces that "ask you for information, without providing context for what it's for." Where a feature must be set up before use, the screen says what it will do for the person, emphasising "what it does rather than how it works". Where what the person gives is sent off the device, the screen says so and what goes, "making sure people know their information may be sent to a server, showing them what's shared, and helping them understand what data may be stored off-device or used for training." Check: for each question on this screen whose purpose a stranger could not guess, where does the screen say why it asks and what it will do with the answer? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Question pages (When to use this pattern), `reference/govuk/patterns/question-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Email addresses (Tell users why you want the email address), `reference/govuk/patterns/email-addresses.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Phone numbers (Explain why you need a phone number), `reference/govuk/patterns/phone-numbers.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Feedback (Asking users for feedback), `reference/govuk/components/feedback.md`;
