Pre-alert screens, windows, or views), `reference/hig/privacy.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Privacy (Best practices: Be transparent about how your app collects and uses people's data), `reference/hig/privacy.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Design principles (Responsibility: Be fully transparent about what your product does and why), `reference/hig/design-principles.md`; Apple, Principles of great design, WWDC26 session 250 (2026) (Responsibility, 4:22), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2026-250.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Calibration: Be clear about why you need people's information), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Privacy: Choose a model type that fits your feature's needs and protects people's privacy; Clearly disclose how your app and its model use and store personal information), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A pharmacy app's sign-up, which asks for a mobile number. Pass: under the field it reads We text you when your prescription is ready. Fail: the same field reads Mobile number and nothing else.

### A finished task leaves a receipt

Called for by: steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where a task ends in something the person may need to prove or follow up, the end gives them a reference and a way to keep a copy, and stays reachable when they come back to it later.** A confirmation must include "a reference number, if there is one" and "a way for users to save a record of the transaction, for example, as a PDF". "Some users will bookmark the confirmation page as a form of receipt. You should allow them to return to the page, whenever possible." A reference the person must use later is not left to their memory: "Unique references are hard to remember so you should send them in an email or text message to the user." The copy the person keeps holds every question asked and its answer, skipped ones included, the date it was sent and what happens next, and the app makes the file itself rather than leave the person to print to PDF, which "is not widely understood in general". Check: when this task ends, what does the person keep, and if they open it again tomorrow, what do they find? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Confirmation pages (How it works; Help users who bookmark the page), `reference/govuk/patterns/confirmation-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Create accounts (When not to use this pattern), `reference/govuk/patterns/create-accounts.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Keep a record (What problem does this solve?; What to do; What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/keep-a-record.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A concert ticket app's purchase. Pass: the end shows order 48213 and an Add to Wallet button, and the order stays under Orders. Fail: the same purchase ends on a Thank you! screen that cannot be found again.

### What it takes is said before it starts

Called for by: steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 10, Help and documentation.

**Before a task that takes more than a moment, the screen says what the person will need to have to hand, roughly how long it will take and what it costs, so they can choose to start now or later.** A start point should include "any other information that most users are likely to need before they start using the service online – for example, how much it costs to use the service and roughly how long it will take", and should list "any documents or information the user will need", but not "information that a user is likely to know from memory". For contact channels, "Tell users when you'll respond to them. This helps users choose which contact channel to use." Check: what must a person fetch or know to finish this task, and is it named before the first step? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Start using a service (How it works), `reference/govuk/patterns/start-using-a-service.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Contact a department or service team (Tell users how long they'll have to wait), `reference/govuk/patterns/contact-a-department-or-service-team.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A bank app's open-an-account flow. Pass: the first screen reads You'll need your passport. It takes about 10 minutes. Fail: the same flow first asks for the passport number on step 6.

### Use before an account

Called for by: account. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**Where a person can be given part of what they came for without an account, they are given as much as can be before they are asked to make one, since an account is where many stop.** "Do not create user accounts if you can provide a usable service without them", because accounts are "a barrier for many users and make it more likely they will drop out". Where one is needed, "let them use as much of your service as possible before they need to create an account". The held "What the user came for comes last" orders the steps after a goal; this claim is about a gate before it. Where an account must be made, the sign-in screen says in a line why it is needed and what it gives: "write a brief, friendly description of the reasons for the requirement and its benefits. Display this message in your sign-in view." Check: what can a person do here before being asked to make an account, and which of it really needs one? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Create accounts (When not to use this pattern; How it works), `reference/govuk/patterns/create-accounts.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Managing accounts (Best practices: Explain the benefits of creating an account and how to sign up), `reference/hig/managing-accounts.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Managing accounts (introduction; Best practices: Delay sign-in for as long as possible), `reference/hig/managing-accounts.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A recipe app. Pass: recipes open and can be read, and an account is asked for only at Save. Fail: the same app opens on Create an account before any recipe shows.

### Consent is asked, not assumed

Called for by: account. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where the app would collect or keep something about the person that the task does not need, nothing is collected until they say yes; no is offered as plainly as yes, the choice opens on no, and they are asked again when what is collected changes.** "You must get the user's consent before you set any cookies that are not strictly necessary." The choice is two buttons, accept and reject. On the settings page, "Load the page with the radios set to 'no' on the user's first visit." New consent is likely needed if "you start using a type of non-essential cookie you have not used before", and "Do not set any new non-essential cookies until the user has given their consent again." The legal duty behind these pages is set aside; what is kept is what the person meets. Where a number or an address given will sign the person up to calls, texts or reminders, the screen says so where it is given and offers a way out of them: "clearly inform the user and provide a way for the user to opt out". Where the app would track the person, the system's own request comes before any tracking data is collected, and nothing is offered for a yes or withheld for a no: "you can't withhold functionality or content or make your app unusable until people allow you to track them." Nothing on a screen of the app's own copies, pictures or points at the system's request, since "People sometimes tap quickly to dismiss alerts without reading them". So the app has no button of its own named Allow, no image of the request and no mark drawing the eye to its Allow. Notifications are something the app asks leave to send ("Before you can send any notifications to people, you have to get their consent"). A choice given or refused can be changed later where the system keeps it: "People can also view the description — and update their choice — in Settings > Privacy." Where the request is the system's own, its two answers are the yes and the no and the app does not reshape them; a screen the app shows before it, kept for where the moment does not already say why, gives the reason and holds one button, Continue or Next, which opens the request, and never a button named Allow or drawn like the request's Allow. What other users of the app would see of the person is asked for the same way and opens on no: after two weeks of use, most of 17 adults interviewed had not shared their results with other users where the app let them, for fear of others' judgement, as they reported. Personal information a feature would use is used only once the person agrees, with "a clear way to opt out of its use" always offered, and sensitive data kept to improve a model or stored needs "explicit permission". Older adults in four focus groups asked the same of features shared with others, which they wanted "optional rather than a default requirement", some wanting company and others not wanting to be seen. A founder's notes app opens every note as private, "only visible to you unless you take an action to put it in a shared space", since "you only have to say one personal or dodgy thing" to make a whole record unfit for others to see. Check: what does this screen collect before the person answers, is no as plain as yes, and what does the choice open on? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Cookies page (Which cookies you need consent for; Letting users accept or reject cookies on the cookies page; Keeping your cookies page up to date and asking for new consent), `reference/govuk/patterns/cookies-page.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Cookie banner (When to use this component; How it works), `reference/govuk/components/cookie-banner.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Phone number (Considerations), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/phone-number.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Privacy (Tracking requests: Never precede the system-provided alert with a custom screen or window that could confuse or mislead people; Incentive), `reference/hig/privacy.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Privacy (Tracking requests: Never precede the system-provided alert with a custom screen or window that could confuse or mislead people; Imitation request; Alert image; Alert annotation), `reference/hig/privacy.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (introduction), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Privacy (Requesting permission), `reference/hig/privacy.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Privacy (Pre-alert screens, windows, or views: Include only one button and make it clear that it opens the system alert), `reference/hig/privacy.md`; Baretta et al., Exploring Users' Experiences of the Uptake and Adoption of Physical Activity Apps: Longitudinal Qualitative Study, JMIR mHealth and uHealth (2019) (Results: Need for Relatedness, Subtheme 3: Social Comparison, Baseline and Follow-Up; Discussion: Summary of Main Findings), `reference/studies/PMC6384536.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Privacy: Ask permission before using personal information and usage data), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`; Berry et al., Designing an mHealth App to Encourage Uptake of Muscle-Strengthening Exercise in Older Adults: Co-Design Focus Group Study, JMIR Aging (2026) (Results: Theme 4: Desired App Elements and Features; Discussion: Theme 4: Desired App Elements and Features), `reference/studies/PMC13022557.md`; Stephenson, interviewed by Labenz, Calm AI for Crazy Days, The Cognitive Revolution (2026), `reference/interviews/stephenson-granola.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A weather app's first launch, where location could be shared with advertisers. Pass: it asks Share your location with advertisers?, with Yes and No side by side, and nothing is shared until Yes. Fail: the same app shares at once and puts a Manage choices link at the foot.

### Hidden where others may see

Called for by: account. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Where an entry or a detail is secret and the person may not be alone, it is hidden at first and shown only when they choose.** "Hide passwords by default until the user chooses to show it using the 'show' button. Users might not be in a private space when entering or creating a password, so you should hide passwords by default." At sign-in the number a code went to is not revealed, and before showing an uploaded file again, "Consider users on public devices before choosing to make the file available to preview or download." A secret number in a kept copy shows no more than its last four characters, enough for the person to know it as theirs: "display no more than the last four (4) digits to ensure the user can still recognize their entry as the proper value". A suggestion drawn from something private or sensitive is not shown at all, since "People often share their accounts and devices with others": "avoid offering recommendations based on that feedback." What the app shows is hidden too where the system shows it outside the app: some apps blur their contents when the platform's app switcher opens, which a practice essay reads as "a deliberate attempt to conceal possibly sensitive data, like medical records or a bank statement". Check: what on this screen would harm the person if a stranger saw it over their shoulder, is it hidden until they choose to show it, and is it hidden too in the system's view of open apps? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Password input (Showing and hiding passwords), `reference/govuk/components/password-input.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Confirm a phone number (When the user signs in), `reference/govuk/patterns/confirm-a-phone-number.md`; GOV.UK Design System, File upload (Let users reuse uploaded files), `reference/govuk/components/file-upload.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Keep a record (What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/keep-a-record.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Progress easily (What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/progress-easily.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Implicit feedback: Consider withholding private or sensitive suggestions), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Freiberg, Invisible Details of Interaction Design (2023) (Implicit Input), `reference/essays/freiberg-invisible-details.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A banking app's card details. Pass: the number shows as •••• 4821 with a Show button. Fail: the same screen shows the full number and the security code as it opens.

### A fast way out where being seen could harm

Called for by: account. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where being seen using the app could put the person at risk, a control in the same place on every screen blanks the screen at once and leaves for somewhere harmless. A second way to set it off needs no pointing, and how it works is explained before it is first needed.** The control is "a button, that when activated, will take the user to another website", with "a loading overlay, to immediately clear any content off the browser". There is also a keyboard way, "pressing shift 3 times", and a hidden link for assistive technology. The place it goes to avoids "websites that might show personalised pages". The person is told what it does and what it does not erase before they first see it, "their internet browsing history will not be erased". The page names its research: "based on research with people with a lived experience of domestic abuse and people with accessibility needs". It also warns that "seeing this component might discourage certain users". Check: if someone walked in now, how many actions would hide this screen, and what would they see? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Exit this page (When not to use this component; Enhancement features; Accessibility features and considerations; Research on this component), `reference/govuk/components/exit-this-page.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Exit a page quickly (How it works; Interruption page), `reference/govuk/patterns/exit-a-page-quickly.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A sexual health clinic's booking app. Pass: an Exit button at the top right of every screen blanks it and opens a weather page. Fail: the same app's exit is in the menu, two taps deep, and opens the phone's home screen with the app still in the switcher.

### Every option offered is carried out

Called for by: settings. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**An option the person can choose is one the app will carry out; a way of being contacted, a delivery method or a setting the app cannot yet honour is not offered.** USWDS: "Do not provide the user with preference options for a communication channel you don't currently support. If you cannot SMS message a user, for example, don't provide that as a preference option." The cost is trust as well as the lost message: "Allowing a user to select a preferred communication option that is not operational risks communication gaps and can destroy a user's trust in a program or service." The page rests on guidance, not research. The held "A constraint is seen before the tap" covers an action that cannot run now and shows it; this claim covers a choice that is accepted and then never acted on. Check: for each option on this screen, what does the app do once it is chosen, and is any option accepted and then not acted on? Sources: U.S. Web Design System, Contact preferences (When to consider something else; What to do; What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/contact-preferences.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A dentist's booking app, whose Send reminders by question picks how appointments are recalled. Pass: it offers Email and Phone call, the two reminders the app sends. Fail: the same question also offers Text message, though the app sends no texts.

### Asked before anything private is sent where others may read it

Called for by: account, pushes. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Where the app will send the person messages, by email, text or voicemail, that could reveal something private, it first asks whether such things may be said there, since a phone or an inbox is often shared.** USWDS returns to this on each contact page: "Do consider the safety implications of users who share email addresses with others. Consider asking for permission to leave potentially sensitive messages", and the same for "users who share phones and voicemail boxes". The reason: "Some users may not want sensitive information left in messages for privacy or safety reasons", so "Do not assume that email messages are private unless explicitly stated through permission-based settings." The pages rest on guidance, not research. The held "Hidden where others may see" keeps a secret off the screen until the person asks; this claim covers what leaves the screen in a message. A notification shown on the device's own screen holds nothing private even with the person's leave, since "You can't predict what people will be doing when they receive a notification"; it names the kind of thing, and the private part waits inside the app. A reminder the phone speaks aloud is heard by whoever is near, so it says less than a written one: the app in one pilot left the person's name out of its spoken reminders "To protect the user’s privacy, especially in public spaces", a design choice the study did not test. Check: what could each message this app sends reveal, who else could read or hear it, and where did the person say it may say that? Sources: U.S. Web Design System, Email address (What to do; What not to do; Considerations), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/email-address.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Phone number (What to do; What not to do; Considerations), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/phone-number.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Contact preferences (What to do; What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/contact-preferences.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Best practices: Avoid including sensitive, personal, or confidential information in a notification), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Chikwetu et al., Automated Diet Capture Using Voice Alerts and Speech Recognition on Smartphones: Pilot Usability and Acceptability Study, JMIR Formative Research (2023) (Methods: Personalized Alerts), `reference/studies/PMC10230351.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A pharmacy app that texts when a prescription is ready. Pass: at sign-up it asks Name the medicine in texts?, with Yes and No, and until Yes its texts read only Your prescription is ready. Fail: the same app texts the medicine's name without asking.

### A tap that leaves, downloads or needs a sign-in says so in its words

Called for by: navigation. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where a tap will take the person out of the app, start a download, jump elsewhere on the same screen or reach something behind a sign-in, its words say so before the tap, with a download's kind and size; an icon alone does not say it, and no stop is put in the way.** USWDS tested an external link icon against an "Exit" badge with people, in a qualitative comparison with no numbers on the page, and found that "Users understand a link's destination when that destination is included in the link text or the context of the link" and that "Users are more likely to ignore link icons and badges than think about their meaning"; neither mark was consistently understood. So the words carry it: "(requires login)" for a link behind a sign-in; "[PDF, 18 pages]" for a file, since teams should "tell users ahead of time if the link may trigger a file download, and show the size and format of that file"; and "jump to", "above" or "below" for a link that stays on the page. Leaving is not stopped: "Allow users to follow external links without taking a separate action to acknowledge leaving your site." The held "Each fork offers every goal" makes a link say where it goes; this claim makes it say what the tap will do to where the person is. People new to an app object to being sent out of it unwarned: in think-aloud sessions with 20 adults, the authors report "a strong dislike for obtrusive interactions such as inappropriate reminders or unexpected advertisements that redirect users to external websites", a stated dislike, not a measured rate. Check: for each link or button on this screen that leaves the app, downloads a file, moves within the screen or needs a sign-in, do its words say so, and is a download's size given? Sources: U.S. Web Design System, Link (Usability should: Clearly identify external links; Indicate nonpublic links that require authentication; Provide text context for external links; Indicate file type and size for links to non-HTML content; Identify jump links in body text; Usability should not: Don't block external links with disruptive notifications; Research findings), `reference/uswds/components/link.md`; Baretta et al., Exploring Users' Experiences of the Uptake and Adoption of Physical Activity Apps: Longitudinal Qualitative Study, JMIR mHealth and uHealth (2019) (Methods: Mobile Apps; Results: A Fair and Simple User Experience, Baseline), `reference/studies/PMC6384536.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: An energy supplier's app, on the bill screen. Pass: the link reads Download your bill (PDF, 2 pages). Fail: the same link reads Your bill, and tapping it starts a download without warning.

### Nothing about the person is guessed

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**A screen does not fill in, word or shape a question about the person on a guess drawn from another answer, and its words and examples take in everyone it is for; what one answer only suggests is asked, not assumed.** USWDS: "Use care to avoid making gender, pronoun, or biological sex assumptions based on previous answers." "Do not assume that partners or dependents of the person completing the form will have the same family name." "Do not assume that people can receive mail at their physical location." The words follow the same rule: "Do not ask for maiden name. Women are not the only ones who change their names. Ask for previous name." Teams are told to "Choose words that include the broadest appropriate group", and "Don't provide examples that may make some groups feel excluded or targeted". The pages rest on guidance. The held "What the answers settle is not asked" fills in what an answer settles; this claim keeps out what an answer only makes likely. Apple asks the same of a feature that writes about or pictures people: "ask people to provide the information needed for the feature to work well rather than solely inferring personal or cultural characteristics", and "Seek clarity before making assumptions that may lead to common stereotypes, such as about gender identities or relationship types." Check: which question, word or filled-in value on this screen rests on a guess about the person, drawn from another answer or from who the app expects them to be? Sources: U.S. Web Design System, Establish trust (What to do; What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/establish-trust.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Name (What not to do; Considerations), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/name.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Address (Physical address, What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/address.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Best practices: Ensure an inclusive experience for all), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A family holiday booking app, adding a child to the booking. Pass: the child's surname field opens empty. Fail: the same field opens filled with the parent's surname.

### An account made in the app can be ended in the app

Called for by: account. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where a person can make an account in the app, they can delete it from the app by a way as easy to find and no longer than any other way the service offers, and they are told when the deletion will be done, told when it is, and told what deleting it does not end.** Apple: "If you help people create an account within your app or game, you must also help them delete it, not just deactivate it." The way to it is in plain view: "Make the link easy to discover — for example, don't bury it in your Privacy Policy or Terms of Service pages". It is no harder in one place than another: "avoid making one version of the deletion flow longer or more complicated than the other". A deletion may be put off, "If you offer a way to schedule account deletion, offer an option for immediate deletion as well". The person is kept told: "Tell people when account deletion will complete, and notify them when it's finished." What goes on regardless is said outright, since "Billing for an auto-renewable subscription continues through Apple until people cancel the subscription, regardless of whether they delete their account." This is a store requirement as well as a guideline, and the legal duties behind it are set aside; what is kept is what the person meets. The held "What is about the person can be changed by them" lets the person change what the app keeps; this claim lets them end it. Check: from the app's own screens, how many steps reach the deletion of the account, are they as few as anywhere else the service offers it, and what does the person learn about when it ends and what keeps billing? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Managing accounts (Deleting accounts: Provide a clear way to initiate account deletion within your app or game; Provide a consistent account-deletion experience whether people perform it within your app or game or on the website; Consider letting people schedule account deletion to occur in the future; Tell people when account deletion will complete, and notify them when it's finished; If you support in-app purchases, help people understand how billing and cancellation work when they delete their account), `reference/hig/managing-accounts.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A photo printing app's Account screen. Pass: Delete account sits under Account and says deletion completes within 7 days and that a subscription bought through the App Store must be cancelled there. Fail: the same screen offers only Deactivate, and deletion is by emailing support.

### A guess is shown only as sure as it is

Called for by: generated. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where the app shows a result it guessed, a match, a prediction or a suggestion, how plainly it shows it follows how often such guesses are right: a reliable one is shown as it is, a less certain one is put to the person to confirm, and one below a set bar is not shown, with the bar set higher for what arrives unasked.** Apple's case: "when confidence is high, the face recognition feature in Photos simply displays the photos that contain a specific person, but when confidence is lower, the feature asks people to confirm whether the photos contain the person before showing more." The app's own measure of how sure it is counts only once checked against how often it is right: "You need to verify that your confidence values correspond to the quality of your results", and "presenting low-quality results in a prominent way is likely to erode trust in your app." What arrives unasked is held to more, since people "may have less tolerance for low-quality information" there: "For suggestions and proactive features, it's a good idea to set a confidence threshold below which you don't offer results." Where a wrong result could do harm, Apple advises against a generated one at all: "Avoid using AI-generated content in situations where a possible hallucination could misinform and harm someone." The held "A likely value is offered filled in" covers the value a field opens with; this claim covers every result the app guesses, and the held "What the app filled in looks like the app's" says how such a result is marked. Apple's pages are platform guidance and cite no study. How often a guess is right decides whether it is used: in Gajos's laboratory study of 23 practised users of office software at desktop computers, a row of predicted buttons that held the one needed 70% of the time rather than 50% was used 86.4% rather than 70.6% of the times it held it, cut the median time to a button on it "from 1.86s to 1.70s", and was rated more useful and less frustrating. Making the row change by a rule people could follow made them feel more in control but not faster, and the authors conclude that "improvements to one of these factors cannot fully offset the losses to the other". The words carry how sure a guess is, not only how it is placed: "plain statements without epistemic markers are perceived as confident assertions, which may cause users to overestimate response reliability", a position paper's reading of the studies it cites, and a review reports that aligning a model's explanations with its actual confidence "narrowed the gap between user confidence and model accuracy". The bar rises with what a wrong result would cost as well: Nielsen's guideline for an assistant that acts is "don't attempt actions with low confidence if the cost of failure is high", practice drawn from the usability heuristics that cites no study. A notes app suggests the folder a finished note goes in but does not file it there itself, "It's not accurate enough for us to turn that on automatically at the moment", and its founder doubts even "99% and 98% of the time" is enough where a wrong guess sends a note to others: "If there's even a tiny risk that something sensitive goes into a public place, then that's not okay for a lot of people." A number for how sure the app is does not by itself make people doubt a wrong guess: in Buçinca's online experiment with 199 crowd workers, adding "The AI is X % confident in its suggestion" whenever the simulated AI was unsure made no significant difference to how often people took its wrong suggestions, against the suggestion and its explanation alone. Check: for each result on this screen that the app guessed, how often is that kind right, and does the screen show it plainly, ask about it or hold it back to match, with a higher bar for what the person did not ask for? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Confidence: introduction; Know what your confidence values mean before you decide how to present them; Consider changing how you present results based on different confidence thresholds; When you know that confidence values correspond to result quality, you generally want to avoid showing results when confidence is low), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (The role of machine learning in your app: Proactive or reactive; Mistakes: Be especially careful to avoid mistakes in proactive features), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Inputs: Raise awareness about and minimize the chance of hallucinations), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`; Gajos et al., Predictability and Accuracy in Adaptive User Interfaces, CHI (2008) (Results: Satisfaction; Utilization; Performance; Conclusions), `reference/papers/gajos-2008-predictability-and-accuracy-in-adaptive-user-interfaces.md`; Ibrahim et al., Measuring and Mitigating Overreliance to Build Human-Compatible AI, arXiv (2025) (Model-level mitigations), `reference/papers/ibrahim-2025-measuring-and-mitigating-overreliance.md`; Blanchard, Garvey and O'Laughlin, Hallucinations in Organization-backed AI advisors, arXiv (2026) (2. Skepticism, verification, and reliance in goal-directed use), `reference/papers/blanchard-2026-hallucinations-in-organization-backed-ai-advisors.md`; Nielsen, Hello AI Agents: Goodbye UI Design, RIP Accessibility (2025) (Agent Usability and Design Guidelines), `reference/essays/nielsen-ai-agents.md`; Stephenson, interviewed by Labenz, Calm AI for Crazy Days, The Cognitive Revolution (2026), `reference/interviews/stephenson-granola.md`; Buçinca et al., To Trust or to Think: Cognitive Forcing Functions Can Reduce Overreliance on AI in AI-assisted Decision-making, CSCW (2021) (3.2 Conditions; 4.1 Objective measures), `reference/papers/bucinca-2021-to-trust-or-to-think.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A banking app's spending categories. Pass: a charge from a known coffee chain is filed under Eating out, and one from an unfamiliar merchant shows Shopping? with Yes and Change beside it. Fail: the same app files the unfamiliar merchant under Shopping as plainly as the coffee charge.

### A suggestion's reason is what the person did

Called for by: generated. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**Where the app says why it suggests a thing, the reason names something the person did, in plain words, never a score and never a claim to know what they feel, like or believe.** Apple's pass is "Because you've read mysteries". A score says too little to choose by: "displaying '97% match' next to the new song as an attribution doesn't communicate enough information to help people make a choice", where "Because you listen to pop music" can be acted on. "Don't provide an attribution that implies understanding or judgment of people's emotions, preferences, or beliefs", so the reason is "Because you've read nonfiction" and not "Because you love nonfiction". It is neither so general that it says nothing nor so exact that it reads as watching: "overly specific attributions can make people think that your app is watching them too closely." A reason that is wrong makes the mistake worse: "if you update the UI with an attribution that turns out to be wrong, the effect of the original mistake is magnified." The pages are platform guidance and cite no study. Nielsen gives the reason the same work: "simple language justifications to help users calibrate their trust and double-check critical outputs", so that "Why did it do that?" has an answer on the screen; this is practice and cites no study. Check: for each reason this screen gives for a suggestion, does it name a thing the person did, and does any word give a score or claim to know their feelings or beliefs? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Attribution: introduction; Avoid being too specific or too general; Keep attributions factual and based on objective analysis; In general, avoid technical or statistical jargon), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Confidence: In general, translate confidence values into concepts that people already understand), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Mistakes: When possible, address mistakes without complicating the UI), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Nielsen, Hello AI Agents: Goodbye UI Design, RIP Accessibility (2025) (Agent Usability and Design Guidelines), `reference/essays/nielsen-ai-agents.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A podcast app's suggestion row. Pass: it reads Because you listened to The History Hour. Fail: the same row reads Because you love war stories.

### The app does not delete or spend on its own

Called for by: generated, loses. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Where the app would act on the person's behalf without their tap, it does not delete, buy, send or do anything hard to undo until they say yes to that act; it may prepare the act and put it before them, never take it.** Apple: "Avoid automating destructive actions, like deleting photos, and actions that are hard to undo, like making a purchase on a person's behalf. Generally, ask for confirmation before performing a significant action on someone's behalf." What is weighed is what a wrong act costs the person: "consider whether a mistake or the inability to reverse the action might cause more work or stress for people." People stay "in charge of decision making and the overall experience." The yes this asks for is of the kind the held "A confirmation is read only when it surprises" finds read, since the person did not start the act, and the held "A confirmation shows the thing, not only the act" says what it shows. The page is platform guidance and cites no study. Check: list what this screen does without a tap from the person; does any of it delete, pay, send or resist undo, and what yes from the person does each wait for? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Inputs: Consider consequences and get permission before performing irreversible or potentially problematic tasks), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Best practices: Keep people in control), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: An email app's suggested reply. Pass: it puts Sounds good, see you at 3 in the reply box, and nothing goes until Send is tapped. Fail: the same app sends the suggested reply by itself when it is sure.

### What a feature does poorly is told before and as it happens

Called for by: generated. Cross-check: Nielsen 10, Help and documentation.

**Where a feature works poorly in conditions a person can meet or change, the screen says so before they rely on it, and when a poor result comes from such a condition, it names the condition and what to change, as it happens.** Apple: "When there's a mismatch between people's expectations about a feature and what the feature can actually accomplish, a limitation can seem like a defect." So the screen will "Set people's expectations before they use the feature", "Show people how to get the best results while they're using the feature" and, "When inferior results occur, explain why". Memoji is the platform's case: it "suggests how people can improve their results by adjusting the lighting or moving closer to the camera", and "tells people that it doesn't work well in the dark." A limit that is rare but serious is told before use, "within the feature's context so that people can decide how they want to rely on the feature", and when a limit is removed, the people who learned to avoid it may be told, so that they "return to interactions they'd previously avoided." The held "A fault is not theirs to diagnose" covers a failure beneath the screen; this claim covers a feature working as built in conditions where it does poorly. Apple's pages are platform guidance and cite no study. Studies bear this out where they have looked: a review of studies of people using AI advisers, studies not on the shelf, reports that "Descriptions of the model's behavior that identify where an AI system tends to fail improved error verification and appropriate reliance across three domains", while a general warning that errors are possible shows "the weakest and most mixed effects", so what is told names the conditions, not only that mistakes happen. The same holds when the feature itself changes: Amershi's eighteen guidelines, tested by 49 design practitioners on 20 products for whether they could find each applied or violated, not for its effect on people, ask apps to "Inform the user when the AI system adds or updates its capabilities", and a position paper asks that changes to a model be told "to ensure that users are aware when they may want to explicitly adjust their expectations for models". The name a feature goes by is part of what it promises: Nielsen asks for "straightforward branding and names that avoid exaggerated terms to not overinflate expectations", since "users easily misinterpret the agent's true capabilities, partly because overly eager marketing statements can mislead them", a finding he reports from studies of partly self-driving cars that are not on the shelf. An allowance the person has used up is such a condition: Nielsen faults a service where, once it runs out, "their image generations suddenly render in lower resolution" without a word, where it should "tell users they had used up the credits included with the subscription"; this is practice and cites no study. Check: in what conditions does each feature on this screen do poorly, does the screen say so before it is relied on, and when a result is poor for that reason, does it say which condition and what to change? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Limitations: introduction; Help people establish realistic expectations; Demonstrate how to get the best results; Explain how limitations can cause unsatisfactory results; Consider telling people when limitations are resolved), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Transparency: Set clear expectations about what your AI-powered feature can and can't do), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`; Blanchard, Garvey and O'Laughlin, Hallucinations in Organization-backed AI advisors, arXiv (2026) (Abstract; 2. Skepticism, verification, and reliance in goal-directed use; 3. Organizational and policy responses), `reference/papers/blanchard-2026-hallucinations-in-organization-backed-ai-advisors.md`; Amershi et al., Guidelines for Human-AI Interaction, CHI (2019) (Table 1, Guideline 18: Notify users about changes), `reference/papers/amershi-2019-guidelines-for-human-ai-interaction.md`; Ibrahim et al., Measuring and Mitigating Overreliance to Build Human-Compatible AI, arXiv (2025) (Model-level mitigations), `reference/papers/ibrahim-2025-measuring-and-mitigating-overreliance.md`; Nielsen, Hello AI Agents: Goodbye UI Design, RIP Accessibility (2025) (Agent Usability and Design Guidelines), `reference/essays/nielsen-ai-agents.md`; Nielsen, 18 Predictions for 2026 (2026) (Prediction 6, Google AI Gets Its Act Together), `reference/essays/nielsen-2026-predictions.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A document scanner app in a dim room. Pass: the viewfinder reads Too dark to read the text. Turn on the light or move somewhere brighter, with a Flash button beside it. Fail: the same scan returns garbled text with no word about the light.

### What the app works out could be true

Called for by: generated, numbers. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**A number the app works out for the person and puts forward, a target, a limit or a forecast, is checked against what could be true before it is shown, and one that could not be true is not shown as advice.** Electronic systems "can check to make sure that the requested operation is sensible"; Norman writes this of what a person asks the machine to do, and the same check belongs on what the machine itself puts forward, since its sums reach the person with its authority. In posts on X about five top-grossing health and fitness apps, read qualitatively from 13,799 filtered for negative sentiment, one person reported being told that to reach their goal they needed to consume "(negative 700) calories a day", and the authors found the apps' goals "dictated by the individual user's weight goals, which could lead to unrealistic or unsafe recommendations being given by the app". People seldom question such a number: from a 28-day pilot of 18 adults in which no one corrected what the app had filled in, the authors conclude that "the average user tends to trust information received from a mobile app without question". The evidence is one reported post, two sets of authors' readings and a survey of what people say they do, not a measure. The held "An entry far from the usual is queried" checks what the person enters; this claim checks what the app hands back. The more a person trusts the tool, the less they say they check it: in a survey of 319 knowledge workers on 936 of their own uses of generative AI at work, confidence in the AI doing the task went with less critical thinking (β = −0.69, p < 0.001) and confidence in doing it themselves with more (β = 0.26, p = 0.026), a survey of what people report, of which the authors write "Our analysis does not establish causation". Check: for each number this screen works out and puts to the person, what range could it truly take, and what does the screen show when the sum falls outside it? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Sensibility Checks), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Sheen et al., Living well? The unintended consequences of highly popular commercial fitness apps through social listening using Machine-Assisted Topic Analysis: Evidence from X, British Journal of Health Psychology (2025) (Theme 2: The complexity of tracking calories through oversimplified algorithms for diet and physical activity; Discussion: Implications of quantification-of-the-self on human experience and behaviour), `reference/studies/PMC12541294.md`; Chikwetu et al., Automated Diet Capture Using Voice Alerts and Speech Recognition on Smartphones: Pilot Usability and Acceptability Study, JMIR Formative Research (2023) (Discussion: Principal Findings), `reference/studies/PMC10230351.md`; Lee et al., The Impact of Generative AI on Critical Thinking: Self-Reported Reductions in Cognitive Effort and Confidence Effects From a Survey of Knowledge Workers, CHI (2025) (4.2 When knowledge workers perceive the enaction of critical thinking; 6.1.1 Self-Confidence and Task Confidence), `reference/papers/lee-2025-the-impact-of-generative-ai-on-critical-thinking.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A household budgeting app's suggested monthly food budget. Pass: when the month's bills take all the income, it reads Your bills take all of this month's income, so there is no food budget to suggest, with Review bills beside it. Fail: the same app shows Suggested food budget: −£120 a month.

### An answer the app made can be checked where it stands

Called for by: generated. Cross-check: Nielsen 10, Help and documentation.

**Where the app gives a person an answer it generated, one they may act on, what the answer rests on can be reached from the answer itself, so checking it costs almost nothing; an explanation of the answer does not take the place of its source.** A right answer and a wrong one can look alike, since "correct and hallucinated responses can share fluency, confidence, and rhetorical coherence". People also seldom check of their own accord: "Spontaneous veracity assessments in cooperative communication are relatively infrequent absent an active trigger". A review of studies of people using AI advisers, for tasks such as product search and medical decisions, finds that "Source citations and noticeable inconsistencies are associated with reduced reliance on incorrect responses, whereas explanations alone can increase reliance on both correct and incorrect responses". The reason it gives: "Sources lower the marginal cost of verification; explanations may instead make an answer feel complete without making it easier to evaluate." It concludes that fewer wrong answers are acted on where "the interaction makes higher-risk content visible and verification feasible", not where people are warned. The review reports studies that are not on the shelf, mostly laboratory and online tasks, and it finds field studies of such advisers rare. The held "What the app filled in looks like the app's" marks such an answer as the app's; this claim makes it checkable. Nor does it undo "A suggestion's reason is what the person did": a reason that names what the person did can be checked against their own memory, where an explanation of a factual answer cannot. A study measures how little an explanation catches: in Buçinca's online experiment with 199 crowd workers in the United States, people shown a simulated AI's suggested swap for a meal at once, each option with its estimated cut in carbohydrate and its likeness in flavour, picked the right ingredient to replace 8% of the time when the suggestion was wrong, against 49% for people given no suggestion at all, and the authors read earlier studies as finding that explanations "are interpreted as a general signal of competence" rather than weighed one by one. Check: for each answer on this screen that the app generated and a person may act on, what does it rest on, and can the person reach that from the answer without leaving it or searching? Sources: Blanchard, Garvey and O'Laughlin, Hallucinations in Organization-backed AI advisors, arXiv (2026) (1. Introduction; 2. Skepticism, verification, and reliance in goal-directed use; 3. Organizational and policy responses), `reference/papers/blanchard-2026-hallucinations-in-organization-backed-ai-advisors.md`; Buçinca et al., To Trust or to Think: Cognitive Forcing Functions Can Reduce Overreliance on AI in AI-assisted Decision-making, CSCW (2021) (1 Introduction; 3.2 Conditions; 4.1 Objective measures), `reference/papers/bucinca-2021-to-trust-or-to-think.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: An airline app's assistant answering How much can my checked bag weigh?. Pass: it reads 23 kg per bag, with From: Baggage policy beneath it, which opens the policy at that line. Fail: the same answer reads 23 kg per bag, followed by a paragraph explaining why, with no way to see the policy.

## Familiarity: Build on what people know.

### Signified, not labelled after

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**Every action on a screen has a visible mark, a signifier in Norman's word, that says where to tap and what it will do; a hint added after people failed to find an action means the mark was missing.** Norman's affordance is what the screen allows, which on a touch screen is everything; the signifier is the perceivable mark that says where to tap, swipe or type and what it will do, so a user tells from the screen alone what can be done, and a gesture with no mark is not discoverable. Each control added dims the rest, so a control is added only for an action the screen's task needs. A screen that has come to need a guide has outgrown its constraints, as the Lego motorcycle that grew from fifteen pieces to twenty-nine needed instructions where the first did not; and a multi-finger gesture is a chord, fast for the trained and unknowable by looking. A gesture that cannot be described in simple words and a simple picture is one people will find hard to learn and to perform. An icon added to draw the eye to something hard to find is the same failure, since "Icons don't fix unclear page hierarchy or confusing content organization." A view that can be resized by dragging shows its handle, since a grabber "shows people that they can drag the sheet to resize it; they can also tap it to cycle through the detents." A tip may tell of a faster way to an action whose own mark is already in view, or of an action newly added, shown once to a person who has not used it; it never stands in for a missing mark, and an action found only through a tip is given a mark instead. That more lies past what is shown is signified as well: a list's last row cut off at the bottom says there is more below, a row of dots says there are pages, a grabber says a pane slides, and a thing that can be dragged stands on its own plane above the content, as a switch's knob does. A gesture is explained in words only where it is used again and again across the app, once and up front, and never for one used now and then, since "People won't remember that." Check: can a user who has not seen this screen tell from it what can be done and where, and does any action here rest on a tap or a gesture with no visible signifier? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 1, The Psychopathology of Everyday Things (Fundamental Principles of Interaction; Affordances; Signifiers; Affordances and Signifiers: A Conversation; The Complexity of Modern Devices), `reference/books/norman-2013/01-chapter-1-the-psychopathology-of-everyday-things.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 7, Design in the World of Business (Featuritis: A Deadly Temptation; The Long Process of Development of the Typewriter Keyboard), `reference/books/norman-2013/07-chapter-7-design-in-the-world-of-business.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Gestures (Custom gestures: Make custom gestures easy to learn), `reference/hig/gestures.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Icon (When to consider something else), `reference/uswds/components/icon.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Tooltip (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/tooltip.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Sheets (iOS, iPadOS: Include a grabber in a resizable sheet), `reference/hig/sheets.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Offering help (Creating tips), `reference/hig/offering-help.md`; Apple, Designing Fluid Interfaces, WWDC18 session 803 (2018) (Teaching, 58:02), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2018-803.md`; Apple, Designing Fluid Interfaces, WWDC18 session 803 (2018) (Teaching, 60:02), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2018-803.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A voice memo app's recordings list. Pass: a Share button shows on each recording's row. Fail: the same row shares only on an unmarked two-finger swipe, and a tip banner was added after people could not find it.

### The platform's way for a control

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**Where the platform already has a control or a gesture for an action, the screen uses it the platform's way, so it is learned once; the app draws its own only where the platform has none.** Where every device invents its own gestures nobody can learn or remember them, and an agreed standard is the way out; for the app the standard is the platform's guidelines, read from the shelf's pages rather than recalled. The backward clock is as logical as the standard one and still cannot be read, since reading was learned on the standard; a layout the app invents, however logical, is a backward clock. The platform's names for its controls are reserved words: a control renamed, or a standard name given a new meaning, confuses. The platform's gestures keep their meaning in every context: a familiar gesture is not given an action unique to the app, a new gesture is not made for a standard action, and the platform's own undo gestures are never redefined. The platform's components and colours carry meanings of their own and are used only for them: an alert for news the person did not cause, an action sheet for choices that follow their own act, a menu only when they ask for it, a disclosure indicator for going deeper and an info button for detail, a segmented control between closely related views, and a system colour only in its named role. An error is never told by a notification: "Use an alert — not a notification — to display an error message." The count on the app's icon is the number of unread notifications and nothing else, since a badge is not for "numeric information that isn't related to notifications, such as weather-related data, dates and times, stock prices, or game scores." The platform's standard keyboard shortcuts keep their meanings in the app. A command of the app's own gets a new shortcut rather than a borrowed one, and a new shortcut is never an old one with a modifier added for an unrelated command, since "People can get confused when the shortcuts they know work differently in your app or game." A keypad the app draws in place of the system keyboard is used only where its benefit is plain, or people "may wonder why they can't regain the system keyboard while in your app". Its keys click as the system's do, a sound "People can turn ... off for all keyboard interactions in Settings > Sounds." The platform's own controls also come ready for screen readers, voice and switches, so using them avoids "reimplementing functionality that already exists". Two number controls invented for the task, one of them designed by the people who would use it, were less accurate than the keypad and the platforms' own stepper and wheel: in 146 hospital staff, 93 and 93.9 per cent right against 97.2 to 98.2. Apple's principles ask the same of feedback, "use system patterns to display alerts and offer choices", and of common acts: "For common actions, there's no need to reinvent the wheel. Just use the metaphors people are already familiar with, and make sure they do what people expect." The platform's motion comes with its parts and is kept: moving between screens, a sheet, the tab bar, a touch-and-hold menu and a large title that shrinks on scroll move as the platform moves them, never as the app redraws them, and for a move between screens a practice standard that cites no study gives "the platform default — don't override it". Microsoft's design principles put a share on it, a practice figure that cites no study: a layout aware of the platform it is on lets a product "reuse native platform components and patterns 80% of the time, focusing our energy on signature experiences instead." Check: does the platform have a control or a gesture for this action, and does this screen use it as the platform does? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 1, The Psychopathology of Everyday Things (The Paradox of Technology), `reference/books/norman-2013/01-chapter-1-the-psychopathology-of-everyday-things.md`; chapter 10, Learning from Experience and Performing Learned Actions Are Easy (Implications for User Interface Design, Make the system familiar), `reference/books/johnson-2010/12-chapter-10-learning-from-experience-and-performing-learned-a.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Undo and redo (iOS, iPadOS: Avoid redefining standard gestures for undo and redo), `reference/hig/undo-and-redo.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Cognitive: Keep actions simple and intuitive), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`;
