Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Lists and tables (iOS, iPadOS, visionOS: Use an info button only to reveal more information about a row's content), `reference/hig/lists-and-tables.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Segmented controls (iOS, iPadOS: Consider a segmented control to switch between closely related subviews), `reference/hig/segmented-controls.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Color (System colors: Avoid redefining the semantic meanings of dynamic system colors), `reference/hig/color.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Best practices: Use an alert — not a notification — to display an error message), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Badging: Use a badge only to show people how many unread notifications they have), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Keyboards (Best practices: Respect standard keyboard shortcuts; Standard keyboard shortcuts: In general, don't repurpose standard keyboard shortcuts for custom actions; Custom keyboard shortcuts: Avoid creating a new shortcut by adding a modifier to an existing shortcut for an unrelated command), `reference/hig/keyboards.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Virtual keyboards (Custom input views: Make sure your custom input view makes sense in the context of your app; Play the standard keyboard sound while people type), `reference/hig/virtual-keyboards.md`; Android Developers, Principles for improving app accessibility (Use built-in accessibility features), `reference/android/principles.md`; Ehrler et al., Assessing the Usability of Six Data Entry Mobile Interfaces for Caregivers: A Randomized Trial, JMIR Human Factors (2015) (Methods: Instruments; Results; Table 5), `reference/studies/PMC4802909.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Design principles (Familiarity: Provide clear feedback), `reference/hig/design-principles.md`; Apple, Principles of great design, WWDC26 session 250 (2026) (Familiarity, 7:42), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2026-250.md`; Kowalski, Building Animations in Expo, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (3. Pick the tool — cheapest that works; 5. Timing or spring), `reference/kowalski/skills/animate-expo/SKILL.source.md`; Microsoft, Fluent 2 Design System (undated) (Design principles: Natural on every platform, What makes it functional?), `reference/essays/fluent-design-principles.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A photo app's single-photo view. Pass: a pinch zooms and the system trash button in the toolbar deletes. Fail: the same view, but a pinch, which zooms everywhere else on the phone, deletes the photo.

### Found again as it was kept

Called for by: list. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**A thing a user kept is found again under the words and in the place they kept it, since what is filed under one reading is not found under another.** Long-term memory is reconstructed, and a thing stored under one interpretation cannot be retrieved when sought under a different one; a screen that files by its own words and offers only those breaks the user's route back. What is retained of a word is often the concept rather than the word, town recalled later as city, so a thing is found again under a synonym of the word it was kept under, not the word alone. Miller gives the mechanism: a thing is kept by recoding it into the person's own words, and 'upon recall we recreate by secondary elaboration the details that seem consistent with the particular verbal recoding we happen to have made'. Check: by what words and route did the user keep the thing this screen lists, and does it offer them again? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 3, Knowledge in the Head and in the World (Long-Term Memory), `reference/books/norman-2013/03-chapter-3-knowledge-in-the-head-and-in-the-world.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 7, Our Attention Is Limited; Our Memory Is Imperfect (A Long-Term Memory Test; Characteristics of Long-Term Memory, Error prone), `reference/books/johnson-2010/09-chapter-7-our-attention-is-limited-our-memory-is-imperfect.md`; Miller, The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two: Some Limits on Our Capacity for Processing Information, Psychological Review 63 (1956), p. 95 (Recoding), `reference/papers/miller-1956-the-magical-number-seven.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A notes app's search field. Pass: a note titled Car repair is found by typing car or vehicle. Fail: the same search finds it only by the exact title words, so vehicle finds nothing.

### Stays where it was learned

Called for by: change. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**A control, an option or an order a user has learned stays where it was, and is moved only for a gain large enough to pay for the relearning, and then everywhere at once.** Knowledge in the world depends on the stability of the environment, change it and the knowledge is lost; every change of convention is resented regardless of merit, a slightly better way is not worth the change, and a mixed system, old here and new there, confuses everyone. The Dvorak layout is superior to QWERTY but not by enough, and the gain is not worth the pain of upsetting the legacy; an alphabetic layout, though it seems logical, was no faster than a random one. Where a thing is moved, the place it left says where it went: "Provide proper notice and forwarding when content is moved or removed." A short way that changes with recent use, place or time of day changes only as the person would expect: "Make sure that actions change in ways that people can predict." Where the app ranks its own guesses, a short row of them kept apart from the list, such as suggestions above it, may run likeliest first and change with the hour, the place and past picks, as Apple asks, "consider using contextual information, such as the time of day or the current location, to help you determine the most likely option"; the list the person picks from by place keeps its order, the last choice included. Gajos's laboratory study of such a row, with 23 practised users of office software at desktop computers, copied the buttons the app predicted into "an adaptive area (clearly designated as hosting changing content)" and left every button in its place, which "allows the user to either follow their familiar route or potentially save time by exploiting the adaptation". A row that changed by a rule people could follow, the most recently used, left them feeling more in control than one that changed at random, and 10 of the 23 "wished the adaptive toolbar were closer to the original locations of the buttons used in order to aid opportunistic discovery of adaptation". An interface remade for each moment loses what the practised person knows by place: Nielsen, arguing for generated interfaces, grants that "If the interface morphs to suit the context, expert performance based on rote learning becomes impossible", trading learnability for immediacy; this is practice and cites no study. Check: does this change move a thing a user has learned the place of, and if so, is the gain large and the move complete? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 3, Knowledge in the Head and in the World (The Tradeoff Between Knowledge in the World and in the Head), `reference/books/norman-2013/03-chapter-3-knowledge-in-the-head-and-in-the-world.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 4, Knowing What to Do: Constraints, Discoverability, and Feedback (When Conventions Change; People's Responses to Changes in Conventions), `reference/books/norman-2013/04-chapter-4-knowing-what-to-do-constraints-discoverability-and.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 7, Design in the World of Business (The Long Process of Development of the Typewriter Keyboard), `reference/books/norman-2013/07-chapter-7-design-in-the-world-of-business.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 8, Limits on Attention Shape Thought and Action (We Prefer Familiar Paths), `reference/books/johnson-2010/10-chapter-8-limits-on-attention-shape-thought-and-action.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Design principles (Promote continuity, Practical actions), `reference/uswds/design-principles/overview.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Home Screen quick actions (Best practices: Avoid making unpredictable changes to quick actions), `reference/hig/home-screen-quick-actions.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Multiple options: List the most likely option first), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Gajos et al., Predictability and Accuracy in Adaptive User Interfaces, CHI (2008) (Task; Results: Satisfaction; Other User Comments), `reference/papers/gajos-2008-predictability-and-accuracy-in-adaptive-user-interfaces.md`; Nielsen, 18 Predictions for 2026 (2026) (Prediction 9, Generative UI (GenUI) and the Disposable Interface), `reference/essays/nielsen-2026-predictions.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A phone dialer's Call button. Pass: after an update the green Call button stays at the bottom centre on every screen. Fail: the same update moves Call to the top right on the keypad only, leaving it bottom centre on contact cards.

### A direction that is a point of view gets a word

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**Where a direction has no natural reading, up for next or back, left for past, the control does not rest on the mapping alone but carries a word or a signifier; and the app's own convention is set on how a person conceives the action, not on the mechanism beneath.** Audiences split on which button is next, since what moves, the person or the pictures, is a point of view; two conventions in one population, the headlight blink, make the signal worthless; blade handles are consistent as pulls though they rotate opposite ways. Check: does any control here rely on a direction people read two ways, and is the app's convention stated in the person's terms? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 3, Knowledge in the Head and in the World (Culture and Design: Natural Mappings Can Vary with Culture), `reference/books/norman-2013/03-chapter-3-knowledge-in-the-head-and-in-the-world.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 4, Knowing What to Do: Constraints, Discoverability, and Feedback (Cultural Norms, Conventions, and Standards; The Faucet: A Case History of Design), `reference/books/norman-2013/04-chapter-4-knowing-what-to-do-constraints-discoverability-and.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A calendar app's week header, where an arrow can be read two ways. Pass: the buttons read Previous week and Next week. Fail: the same header has two bare arrows, which some read as moving the weeks and others as moving themselves.

### A direction is named by the flow, not by a side

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**Name a direction by the flow of what is on the screen, never by a side of the screen.** Along a line the words are leading and trailing, Apple's words; along the page, the start and the end of the page, with before and after for where one thing sits relative to another; along a list or a sequence, first and last, next and previous. This way a layout read right to left, turned on its side or kept in reverse keeps its words, and only its sides move. Leading is where a line begins in the person's language: left in English, right in Arabic or Hebrew. Whatever means forward or back flips: Back, next and previous, a slider, a progress bar, and a list kept in order. A number's digits, a clock, a photo, a logo, a tick, a control that points at a real direction, and the play and progress controls of audio and video, which follow the direction of a tape and not of reading, do not; in Hebrew, timelines and linear progress also keep running left to right. A side of the screen may still be named only for what is fixed to the device or the world: the device's own parts, the keyboard coming up from the screen's bottom edge, a control that means "to the right", and a visual effect kept the same everywhere, such as a shadow's offset. So in right to left, leading and trailing swap sides; in landscape no flow word changes, and only the device's sides turn with it; in a reversed list, first and last follow the list's order wherever it sits, and the reversal changes only the layout, never the order a screen reader speaks; and in a vertical writing mode the start and end of the page move to the sides, which iOS does not lay its controls out with, though the words would still hold. Head and tail are not direction words: in SwiftUI they already mean where a line of text is cut short, "Truncate at the beginning of the line" and at its end, and using them for edges would give one word two meanings. Check: for every direction this design or its rules name, is it a flow word, an order word, or a side fixed to the device; and with the layout set to right to left, does what should flip flip, and does what should stay stay? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Right to left (Text alignment; Numbers and characters; Controls; Images; Interface icons), `reference/hig/right-to-left.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Layout (Visual hierarchy: Order content by relative importance), `reference/hig/layout.md`; SwiftUI, HorizontalAlignment.leading, `reference/swiftui/horizontalalignment-leading.md`; SwiftUI, LayoutDirection, `reference/swiftui/layoutdirection.md`; SwiftUI, HorizontalEdge, `reference/swiftui/horizontaledge.md`; SwiftUI, Edge, `reference/swiftui/edge.md`; SwiftUI, flipsForRightToLeftLayoutDirection(_:), `reference/swiftui/view-flipsforrighttoleftlayoutdirection.md`; SwiftUI, Text.TruncationMode (head; tail), `reference/swiftui/text-truncationmode-head.md`, `reference/swiftui/text-truncationmode-tail.md`; CSS Logical Properties and Values Level 1 (1 Introduction), `reference/css/css-logical-1.md`; CSS Writing Modes Level 4 (6.2 Flow-relative Directions), `reference/css/css-writing-modes-4.md`; CSS Flexible Box Layout Level 1 (5.1 Flex Flow Direction: the flex-direction property), `reference/css/css-flexbox-1.md`; Material Design 3, Bidirectionality & RTL (Mirroring; Icons & symbols; Time; Media players), `reference/material/m3-bidirectionality-rtl.md`; Material Design 2, Bidirectionality (Mirroring layout; Mirroring elements: The passage of time), `reference/material/m2-bidirectionality.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A mail app's message screen. Pass: Back sits at the leading edge of the top bar and points leading, so in Arabic it sits on the right and points right. Fail: the same Back is placed at the left, pointing left, so in Arabic it points the way the text reads forward.

### Same job, same place on every screen

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**A control or a display that does the same job on two screens sits in the same place on both, in the same colour and type, since people tap where they expect it and do not see that it moved.** Users of software often click buttons without looking carefully at them; their perception of the display is based more on what their past experience leads them to expect than on what is actually on the screen. If the positions of the Next and Back buttons on the last page of a multipage dialog switched, many people would not immediately notice the switch, and even after going backward a few times might continue to perceive the buttons in their standard locations; if the Submit button on one form is shaped or coloured differently from those on other forms, users might not find it. In a row of buttons the likeliest sits on the trailing side and Cancel on the leading side, and in a stack the likeliest is on top and Cancel at the bottom, the same in every prompt. The way around comes in the same order on every screen, and help, whether contact details, a contact form or self-help, sits in the same place on every screen that has it. Ways of getting help sit in the same place and the same order on every screen ("Show contact channels in the same order throughout your service"), as do language choices, since "some users often switch languages throughout their journey". A long or hard task offers on each of its screens a way to reach a person, such as a phone number or a chat, "if they get stuck or have a question". The sign of a wait appears in the same place each time, so people "reliably find the status of an operation". A thing shown in a form it seldom takes is misread far more often than in its usual form: in 69 students in a lab looking for a letter, it was answered wrongly on 16.43 per cent of the trials where it came in the form it took one time in ten, against 1.25 per cent in the form it took eight times in ten. Apple's talk on its principles gives the gain in time, as a Mac window always closes from its top left corner: "When someone can find an action in the same location across screens and devices, it speeds them up. They don't have to think about it." A design team's account, practice that cites no study, finds the same failure grown inside one product, header actions "like sharing a page, copying a link, and opening a PR no longer appeared in predictable places", and its fix made "Headers, navigation, and view controls ... consistent across projects, issues, reviews, and documents, making it simpler to orient yourself and move between workflows". Check: for each control on this screen that another screen also has, is it in the same place, colour and type there, and which control here is where its counterpart is not? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 1, We Perceive What We Expect (Perception Biased by Experience; Design Implications, Be consistent), `reference/books/johnson-2010/03-chapter-1-we-perceive-what-we-expect.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Alerts (Buttons: Place buttons where people expect), `reference/hig/alerts.md`; WCAG 2.2, 3.2.3 Consistent Navigation, Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/consistent-navigation.md`; WCAG 2.2, 3.2.6 Consistent Help, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/22/consistent-help.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Contact a department or service team (How it works), `reference/govuk/patterns/contact-a-department-or-service-team.md`; GOV.UK Design System, GOV.UK footer (Adding links), `reference/govuk/components/footer.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Language navigation (Placement on pages), `reference/govuk/components/language-navigation.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Progress easily (What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/progress-easily.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Progress indicators (Best practices: Display a progress indicator in a consistent location), `reference/hig/progress-indicators.md`; Upshaw et al., The hidden cost of a smartphone: The effects of smartphone notifications on cognitive control from a behavioral and electrophysiological perspective, PLOS ONE (2022) (Methods; Results), `reference/studies/PMC9671478.md`; Apple, Principles of great design, WWDC26 session 250 (2026) (Familiarity, 8:15), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2026-250.md`; Aufmann and Heckel, A calmer interface for a product in motion (2026) (introduction), `reference/essays/linear-calmer-interface.md`; Linear, UI refresh – Changelog (2026) (UI refresh), `reference/essays/linear-ui-refresh.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A shop's four-step checkout. Pass: Continue is a blue button at the bottom right on every step. Fail: the same checkout puts Continue at the top left, in grey, on the payment step only.

### One reading, or the convention's

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**Each element on a screen is read one way by everyone who meets it; where two readings are possible, a convention the platform already uses settles it, and the design is tested on people to see that it did.** Avoid ambiguous information displays, and test your design to verify that all users interpret the display in the same way; where ambiguity is unavoidable, either rely on standards or conventions to resolve it, or prime users to resolve the ambiguity in the intended way. Shaded buttons look raised only by the convention that the light source is at the top left; depicted as lit from elsewhere, users would not see the object as raised. Check: which element on this screen could two people read two ways, what convention settles it, and has anyone but the builder read it? Source: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 1, We Perceive What We Expect (Design Implications, Avoid ambiguity), `reference/books/johnson-2010/03-chapter-1-we-perceive-what-we-expect.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A smart lights app's lamp control. Pass: it uses the platform's switch, green with the knob right for on. Fail: the same lamp uses a custom half-filled slider, which some read as on and others as half bright.

### Alike in look, alike in kind

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**Things of one kind share one look on a screen and nothing of another kind borrows it, since whatever looks alike is read as belonging together whether or not that was meant.** Objects that look similar appear grouped, all other things being equal; in the Mac OS Page Setup dialog three menus look similar enough that they appear related even though that probably wasn't intended. With all the Gestalt principles operating at once, unintended visual relationships can be implied by a design, so a recommended practice, after designing a display, is to view it with each principle in mind to see if the design suggests any relationships between elements that you do not intend. Options offered as one group share one size and one form, the preferred one marked by its style and not its size, one control holds text or pictures but not a mix of both, and controls are drawn so they are told from content at a glance. A status never looks like a control: "Research from multiple teams found that some users perceived these as buttons and tried to click on them", and "some users currently try to select task statuses, thinking they are buttons or links", so statuses were made lighter and the whole row became the link. A mark that cannot be tapped shows no pressed or hover state and is not placed among buttons, and within one app such marks are all tappable or none are, since "Users frequently confuse tags as buttons" and people "expect an icon or badge alongside a link to be clickable". An option drawn differently from the others in its group is read as the one recommended, so the options of one group share one form, since mixed forms "can appear to indicate a bias or preference toward that option". Nothing the app draws looks like the system's badge, since people who turned badges off "will become frustrated if they have done so and then see what appears to be a badge." A look also promises a behaviour: "things that look the same should behave the same", and where one of three alike buttons goes to another screen, one toggles and one opens a view over the screen, "there's no pattern to understanding how this interface works." On Apple's platforms the controls and navigation float on a layer of their own above the content, and the content never takes that layer's look: "Don't use Liquid Glass in the content layer", since it "can result in unnecessary complexity and a confusing visual hierarchy", and a list drawn in it "would make it compete with other elements and muddy the hierarchy." Likeness can be used on purpose to join: a browser extends "the color of the page up into the active tab UI to help reinforce the relationship between the browser and the tab contents"; practice that cites no study. Check: group this screen's elements by look alone; does any group hold two kinds of thing, or any kind span two looks? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 2, Our Vision Is Optimized to See Structure (Gestalt Principle: Similarity; Gestalt Principles: Combined), `reference/books/johnson-2010/04-chapter-2-our-vision-is-optimized-to-see-structure.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Color (Best practices: Avoid using the same color to mean different things), `reference/hig/color.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Buttons (Style: Use style — not size — to visually distinguish the preferred choice among multiple options), `reference/hig/buttons.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Segmented controls (Content: Prefer using either text or images — not a mix of both — in a single segmented control), `reference/hig/segmented-controls.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Layout (Visual hierarchy: Differentiate controls from content), `reference/hig/layout.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Tag (When to consider something else; Usability), `reference/uswds/components/tag.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Link (Research findings), `reference/uswds/components/link.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Checkbox (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/checkbox.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Radio buttons (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/radio-buttons.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Button group (Segmented button group, Implementation), `reference/uswds/components/button-group/guidance/variants/segmented/implementation.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Badging: Avoid creating a custom image or component that mimics the appearance or behavior of a badge), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Apple, Principles of great design, WWDC26 session 250 (2026) (Familiarity, 7:59), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2026-250.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Design principles (Familiarity: Keep visuals and interactions consistent), `reference/hig/design-principles.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Materials (Liquid Glass: Don't use Liquid Glass in the content layer), `reference/hig/materials.md`; Apple, Meet Liquid Glass, WWDC25 session 219 (2025) (Principles, 13:13), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-219.md`; Deets, The strategy behind Dia's design (2025) (Craft.), `reference/essays/browser-company-dia.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A music app's now-playing screen. Pass: Like and Add to Playlist look like buttons and the artist name is plain text. Fail: the same screen sets the artist name in a blue pill like the buttons, so it reads as a control.

### A value of a known kind gets its own control

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**A value whose kind is known, a number, a date, a time, a choice from a list, is entered with the control made for that kind, never a bare text field.** A step up in structure from segmented data fields are data-specific controls: instead of using simple text fields, designers can use controls that are designed specifically to display, and accept as input, a value of a specific type, as dates presented as menus combined with a pop-up calendar. Segmented fields can improve readability and help prevent data entry errors. The control for a kind includes its keyboard: a date can always be typed on a numeric keypad, and where it is likely near today a calendar beside the field, opening on today, is added, never in place of typing, and a number is never set by a spinner that a stray scroll can change. A time the person knows exactly, such as a time of birth, is typed, and a time being booked is picked from even steps such as half hours. For a date, the control is the platform's date picker, since it takes typing as well as a calendar or wheels: Apple's date pickers "offer additional ways to choose values, like selecting a day in a calendar view or entering dates and times using a numeric keypad", and its wheels also support "data entry through built-in or external keyboards". A known date, such as a birth date, is typed whole on the keypad or set on the platform's wheels, whichever the person reaches for, rather than in a separate box for each part. Of six ways measured to enter a number on a phone, the numeric keypad was the quickest and among the most accurate, and handwriting for a recogniser the slowest and least accurate: in 146 hospital staff entering numbers shown on the screen, the keypad took 2.81 to 4.34 seconds a number and was right 96.3 to 99.3 per cent of the time, handwriting 6.32 to 14.68 seconds and 81.5 to 86.8 per cent. Among numbers, a code opens the keypad of digits and an amount the keypad with a decimal point, in a practice guide that cites no study. Check: for each field on this screen, what kind of value does it take, and does the platform have a control or a keyboard for that kind that it uses? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 3, We Seek and Use Visual Structure (Data-Specific Controls Provide Even More Structure; Structure Enhances People's Ability to Scan Long Numbers), `reference/books/johnson-2010/05-chapter-3-we-seek-and-use-visual-structure.md`; Use a number formatter to help with numeric data), `reference/hig/text-fields.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Date input (When to use this component), `reference/govuk/components/date-input.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Dates (Asking for memorable dates; Helping users to pick a date), `reference/govuk/patterns/dates.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Text input (Avoid using inputs with a type of number), `reference/govuk/components/text-input.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Pickers (introduction; When to consider something else), `reference/uswds/components/time-picker.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Date picker (When to use; When to consider something else), `reference/uswds/components/date-picker.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Virtual keyboards (Best practices: Choose a keyboard that matches the type of content people are editing), `reference/hig/virtual-keyboards.md`; Ehrler et al., Assessing the Usability of Six Data Entry Mobile Interfaces for Caregivers: A Randomized Trial, JMIR Human Factors (2015) (Methods: Instruments; Results; Table 3; Table 4), `reference/studies/PMC4802909.md`; Kowalski, Feeling Native On Mobile, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (4. Page zooms into the input), `reference/kowalski/skills/mobile-native/SKILL.source.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Forms: Correct types & input modes), `reference/vercel/README.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A dentist booking app's appointment form. Pass: the date field opens the number keypad with a calendar button beside it, and the time is picked from listed slots. Fail: the same form asks for the date and time in a plain text box.

### Words the reader already has

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**Every word on a screen is one its readers use themselves, from a small and constant vocabulary; a word they do not have drops them out of automatic reading into working it out.** Software often disrupts reading by using unfamiliar vocabulary, words the intended readers don't know very well or at all: most nontechnical users would not understand "reauthenticate", so they drop out of automatic reading mode into conscious wondering about the message's meaning, where "Login again" would not. Use restricted, highly consistent vocabularies, sometimes referred to as plain language. The word is the task's and never the machine's or the trade's: database for templates kept on a server is the developer's word and shared the user's, and a word a person must translate is learned twice. Text that needs more than nine years of school to read has a simpler version beside it. A short form is written out ("Do not use CVV or other acronyms for the card security code"), and a language is named in itself, as Cymraeg for Welsh, which "helps speakers of that language recognise the link". The words fit the device in the person's hand, so a phone's screen says tap and never click: "don't write copy that tells people to click a button on an iPhone or tap a menu item on a Mac." Apple's principles ask the same: "Concise interfaces use plain language", which "strip away jargon and speak naturally." How sure the app is, is said in the person's terms, "high chance" and "low chance", or as what to do, "This is a good time to buy", and as a number only where people expect one, as in "weather predictions, sports statistics, and polling numbers". Check: which word on this screen would its reader not say themselves in the place the app is used, and what is their word for it? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 4, Reading Is Unnatural (Poor Information Design Can Disrupt Reading, Uncommon or unfamiliar vocabulary; Design implications: don't disrupt reading; support it!), `reference/books/johnson-2010/06-chapter-4-reading-is-unnatural.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 11, Many Factors Affect Learning (We Learn Faster When Vocabulary Is Task-Focused, Familiar, and Consistent, Terminology should be task-focused; Terminology should be familiar), `reference/books/johnson-2010/13-chapter-11-many-factors-affect-learning.md`; WCAG 2.2, 3.1.5 Reading Level, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/reading-level.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 3.1.5 Reading Level (Intent of Reading Level), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/reading-level.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Payment card details (Help users enter valid card information), `reference/govuk/patterns/payment-card-details.md`; GOV.UK Design System, National Insurance numbers (How it works), `reference/govuk/patterns/national-insurance-numbers.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Language navigation (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/language-navigation.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Link (Research findings), `reference/uswds/components/link.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Alert (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/alert.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Establish trust (What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/establish-trust.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Offering help (Best practices: Use relevant and consistent language and images in your help content), `reference/hig/offering-help.md`; Apple, Principles of great design, WWDC26 session 250 (2026) (Simplicity, 11:42), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2026-250.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Confidence: In situations where attributions aren't helpful, consider ranking or ordering the results in a way that implies confidence levels; In scenarios where people expect statistical or numerical information, display confidence values that help them interpret the results; Whenever possible, help people make decisions by conveying confidence in terms of actionable suggestions), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A video streaming app's sign-in prompt. Pass: it reads Your session ended. Sign in again. Fail: the same prompt reads Token expired. Reauthenticate.

### A meaning's colour is one of the four

Called for by: colour. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**A colour that stands for a meaning on a screen is one of red, green, yellow and blue, in the platform's own versions that adapt to light and dark; black and white, though the eye tells them apart as fast, are the text and the background, which swap when the appearance does, so neither carries a meaning; a meaning on any other hue is told slower, and two meanings on one hue slower still.** Our visual system combines the signals from retinal cone cells to produce three color-opponent channels, red-green, yellow-blue and black-white; the colors people can distinguish most easily are those that cause a strong signal on one of the three channels and neutral signals on the other two, and those colors are red, green, yellow, blue, black and white. All other colors cause signals on more than one channel, so our visual system cannot distinguish them from other colors as quickly and easily. On a phone the black-white channel is spent on the appearance: in Dark Mode the palette has "dimmer background colors and brighter foreground colors", and "while many colors are inverted, some are not", so what is black in light is white in dark; the platform's system colors "vary subtly depending on the system appearance, adjusting to ensure proper color differentiation and contrast", and a custom colour has a bright and a dim variant. Accuracy puts a ceiling of its own beside this claim's speed: hue judged alone was identified to about 3.1 bits, some eight categories (Eriksen), and colours of equal luminance to some 11 to 15 (Halsey and Chapanis), both reported by Miller. A practice guide puts errors on red, warnings on orange or yellow and success on green or blue; of these, orange is off the four, so a warning is yellow. Check: list each meaning this screen gives a colour; is each on red, green, yellow or blue in the platform's adapting version, does any rest on black or white, and do two meanings share a hue? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 5, Our Color Vision Is Limited (Guidelines for Using Color, guideline 2), `reference/books/johnson-2010/07-chapter-5-our-color-vision-is-limited.md`; Miller, The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two: Some Limits on Our Capacity for Processing Information, Psychological Review 63 (1956), p. 86 (Absolute Judgments of Unidimensional Stimuli), p. 88 (Absolute Judgments of Multidimensional Stimuli), `reference/papers/miller-1956-the-magical-number-seven.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Dark Mode (Dark Mode colors: Embrace colors that adapt to the current appearance), `reference/hig/dark-mode.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Color (Best practices: Make sure all your app's colors work well in light, dark, and increased contrast contexts), `reference/hig/color.md`; Krause, 10 Design Guidelines for Reporting Errors in Forms, Nielsen Norman Group (2019, updated 2024) (4. Use Color to Differentiate Errors from Normal Field States), `reference/nngroup/errors-forms-design-guidelines.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A weather app's warnings map. Pass: warnings are yellow and red, flood areas blue. Fail: the same map marks warnings in teal and mauve.

### Red means wrong and nothing else

Called for by: colour, errors. Cross-check: Nielsen 9, Help users recognize, diagnose, and recover from errors.

**Red on a screen marks a problem and is used for nothing else, since red is the platform's word for alert, and a red title or a red brand beside a red message hides the message.** By convention, in interactive computer systems the color red connotes alert, danger, problem, error; using red for any other information invites misinterpretation. At Airborne.com the error message was red and near the Login button, yet some users did not see it, because the page title was also red: resolution in the periphery is low, so the visual system may not register any change, there was something red up there before, and there still is. The platform's destructive button uses the system red, so red on a control marks a loss, which is the same meaning. The platform's own controls keep the platform's red, as the badge that marks new information on a tab, and this claim binds the red the app draws itself. A fault the person did not cause is not told in red ("red text to warn people" is on each failure page's do-not list), and a red status is kept for a part that holds a mistake: "Do not use the red background colour for any status text except errors." A warning that leaves the way on open, such as a delay the person may accept, is not drawn in red, and is told by a lighter means than a refusal, a line or a banner rather than a stop, since a message's look says how serious it is. Check: where is red that the app draws itself on this screen, and is every such red thing a problem? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 6, Our Peripheral Vision Is Poor (Examples from Computer User Interfaces, the Airborne.com example; Common Methods of Making Messages Visible, Reserve red for errors), `reference/books/johnson-2010/08-chapter-6-our-peripheral-vision-is-poor.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Color (Best practices: Avoid using the same color to mean different things; Inclusive color: Consider how the colors you use might be perceived in other countries and cultures), `reference/hig/color.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Buttons (Role), `reference/hig/buttons.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Tab bars (Best practices: Use a badge to indicate that critical information is available), `reference/hig/tab-bars.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Page not found pages (How it works), `reference/govuk/patterns/page-not-found-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Complete multiple tasks (Tasks containing an error), `reference/govuk/patterns/complete-multiple-tasks.md`; Neusesser and Sunwall, Error-Message Guidelines, Nielsen Norman Group (2023, updated 2024) (Visibility Guidelines: Design errors based on their impact), `reference/nngroup/error-message-guidelines.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A utility bill app's account page. Pass: red appears only on the Overdue label. Fail: the same page has a red logo and red title bar above the red Overdue label.

### One name per thing

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**Each thing the app shows has one name, used everywhere, and no name is used for two things; the product's lexicon is the list, and a screen's words are read against it.** Same name, same thing; different name, different thing (Caroline Jarrett); terms and concepts map strictly 1:1; never use different terms for the same concept, as Photoshop's "Fuzziness" and "Tolerance" for one parameter, nor the same term for different concepts, as WordPress's "Dashboard" for an area and for one page of it; a development team should create a product lexicon with a name and definition for each object, action and attribute the product exposes, and follow it in the software, the manuals and the marketing. Check: for each word on this screen, is it the glossary's word for that thing, does the same thing carry another word elsewhere, and does this word name a second thing anywhere? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 11, Many Factors Affect Learning (We Learn Faster When Vocabulary Is Task-Focused, Familiar, and Consistent, Terminology should be consistent; Developing task-focused, familiar, consistent terminology is easier with a good conceptual model), `reference/books/johnson-2010/13-chapter-11-many-factors-affect-learning.md`; WCAG 2.2, 3.2.4 Consistent Identification, Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/consistent-identification.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 3.2.4 Consistent Identification (Examples of Consistent Identification), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/consistent-identification.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Link (Usability should not), `reference/uswds/components/link.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Icon (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/icon.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A recipe app's saved recipes. Pass: they are called Favourites on every screen. Fail: the same recipes are Favourites on the tab, Saved on the recipe page and Bookmarks in settings.

### The machine's objects stay inside

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**Nothing that exists only in the implementation, a record, a database, a buffer, a sync, a mode, a table, is shown or named on a screen; the person knows only the things of the task.** The objects/actions analysis is a declaration of the concepts exposed to users: if it isn't in the objects/actions analysis, users shouldn't know about it; the implementation may include other objects, but those should be invisible; a checking-account application exposes transaction, check and account and excludes buffer, dialog box, mode, database, table and text string; a model that carried "transaction record format" would not be task-focused, and forcing users to care about it would detract from usability no matter how much effort went into the interface. Check: which things does this screen show or name, and is each one a person doing the task would name without the app? Source: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 11, Many Factors Affect Learning (We Learn Faster When Operation Is Task-Focused, Simple, and Consistent, Objects/actions analysis), `reference/books/johnson-2010/13-chapter-11-many-factors-affect-learning.md`. The appendix carries it as Johnson (2007), Principle 3, Keep program internals inside the program, `reference/books/johnson-2010/16-well-known-user-interface-design-rules.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A notes app's note menu. Pass: it offers Move to Folder and Share. Fail: the same menu also offers Reindex Database and Flush Cache.

### An exception is never learned

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**An action works the same way on every thing it applies to; where every thing but one takes it the same way, the one is made the same or its action is made to look different, since a rule with one exception is got wrong for longer than no rule at all.** Of three keyboard designs, Design A, the same shortcut on every type of content, is learned in minutes; Design B, different for every type, takes a month; Design C, the same for everything except video, is the one on which users will take the longest to be error-free, trying the common keys on video for at least several months, perhaps forever. In a highly consistent system the operation of a function is predictable from its type, and a system in which every object has different actions will be hard to learn and remember no matter what user interface is plastered on it. A touch and hold opens a menu on every item of one kind or on none, since where it works in some places and not others "people won't know where they can use the feature and may think there's a problem." Check: for each action on this screen, does it work the same on every thing it is offered on, and if one thing is the exception, does the exception look like one? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 7, Our Attention is Limited; Our Memory is Imperfect (Implications of Long-Term Memory Characteristics for User Interface Design), `reference/books/johnson-2010/09-chapter-7-our-attention-is-limited-our-memory-is-imperfect.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 11, Many Factors Affect Learning (We Learn Faster When Operation Is Task-Focused, Simple, and Consistent, Consistency; The objects/actions matrix), `reference/books/johnson-2010/13-chapter-11-many-factors-affect-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Context menus (Best practices: Support context menus consistently throughout your app), `reference/hig/context-menus.md`. The appendix lists it as Norman (1983a), Lack of consistency leads to errors, and Shneiderman, Strive for consistency.

Example, not part of the claim: A messaging app's long-press. Pass: long-press opens Reply, Copy and Delete on texts, photos and voice notes alike. Fail: the same long-press opens the menu on texts and photos but starts recording on voice notes.

### One gesture per kind of act

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**One kind of act, editing text, choosing from a list, going back, is done by one gesture everywhere in the app, whatever the screen, so the hand learns it once.** Keystroke-level consistency is at least as important as conceptual consistency in determining how quickly operation becomes automatic; the goal is muscle memory, motor habits; it requires standardising the physical actions for all activities of the same type, text editing the same whether in documents, form fields or filenames; a system inconsistent at this level keeps people guessing what to use in each context, even when contexts differ only slightly. The motion that answers a gesture is one family too: a page moves the same way whether the finger scrolled it or a tap on the status bar sent it to the top, since "once they learn one behavior of your app, they can pick up another behavior really easily, because we learn through repetition." What the app does on its own is shown in the controls a person would use to do it by hand, so its result is refined or undone with the same ones: "Photos highlights the controls it uses to auto-crop a photograph so that people can use the same controls to refine or undo the results." Check: for each gesture on this screen, is the same act done by the same gesture on every other screen of the app? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 11, Many Factors Affect Learning (We Learn Faster When Operation Is Task-Focused, Simple, and Consistent, Keystroke consistency), `reference/books/johnson-2010/13-chapter-11-many-factors-affect-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Gestures (Best practices: In general, respond to gestures in ways that are consistent with people's expectations), `reference/hig/gestures.md`; Apple, Designing Fluid Interfaces, WWDC18 session 803 (2018) (Character, 39:57), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2018-803.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Corrections: Give people familiar, easy ways to make corrections), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A music app's lists. Pass: a swipe left removes a song, in playlists and in the queue. Fail: the same removal is a swipe left in playlists but a long-press in the queue.

### What the last app taught holds here

Called for by: new. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**Where the people the app is for learned a way of doing a thing in the app they came from, this app does it that way, and departs only where the departure is the point.** Make the system familiar, requiring users to think about it less: one way is to follow industry conventions, a second is to make new software applications work like older ones that users have used before, a third is a metaphor; finally, study users to learn what is and is not familiar to them. Less of the task is then new, so less of it is controlled, slow and prone to error, as checking email on an unfamiliar program in an internet cafe is. A practice essay names the cost of a departure a "novelty tax", quoting The Browser Company on its Arc browser, "People just couldn't handle how much there was to learn that was new", and proposes, as taste that cites no study, to "make 90% of the experience familiar, and 10% novel". A browser maker's account, practice that cites no study, puts every common feature "in a place where anyone who has used a browser before would immediately understand", so a person can switch "at 10am on a Tuesday morning", and adds to a familiar feature "only when it provides value above and beyond what a user is expecting — without diminishing the recognizable patterns"; Microsoft's principles ask the same, to "build off the familiar—designing for what you already understand". The same account names the limit: "Every product has a novelty budget. This is how much effort people will put into learning something new in order to get value out of it." It is spent only where "there are less established patterns", and even there a known pattern is borrowed, as a mention typed with @ is "the same experience as tagging an entity on social media". Keyboard shortcuts are part of what was learned: a changelog records shortcuts for headings and body text standardized "to match common editor conventions". Check: how do the people this screen is for do this in the app they use for it now, and where this screen differs, is the difference the one the screen is built to make? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 10, Learning from Experience and Performing Learned Actions are Easy; Problem Solving and Calculation are Hard (Performing Learned Actions Is Easy; Implications for User Interface Design, Make the system familiar), `reference/books/johnson-2010/12-chapter-10-learning-from-experience-and-performing-learned-a.md`; Freiberg, Novelty (2026) (novelty tax passage), `reference/essays/freiberg-novelty.md`; Deets, The strategy behind Dia's design (2025) (10am on a Tuesday Morning; Familiar, but elevated), `reference/essays/browser-company-dia.md`; Microsoft, Fluent 2 Design System (undated) (Design principles: Natural on every platform), `reference/essays/fluent-design-principles.md`; Deets, The strategy behind Dia's design (2025) (The novelty budget), `reference/essays/browser-company-dia.md`; Linear, UI refresh – Changelog (2026) (list of keyboard changes), `reference/essays/linear-ui-refresh.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A new camera app for people used to the phone's own camera. Pass: a volume button takes the photo, as it did there. Fail: the same volume button zooms instead.

### A picture alone only where it is known

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**An icon stands alone only where its picture already means that act to the person, from the world or from the platform; elsewhere a word sits with it, and one picture means one act everywhere in the app.** Pictures that people recognise from the physical world are useful because they can be recognised without needing to be taught, as long as the familiar meaning matches the intended meaning; users can learn to associate new icons with their meaning if they are well designed, and memorable icons and symbols hint at their meaning, are distinguishable from others, and consistently mean the same thing, even across applications; Wordpress labels its pages with symbols plus text. One act keeps one picture on every screen, so save is never a disk on one screen and a tick on another. The icons nearly everyone knows are few, home, print and search among them, so any other icon carries words and is tried on the people the app is for, and an icon that stands for an act is never used as a picture of a thing: "Only a few icons are consistently understood across the digital-using public of the world". A familiar picture keeps its familiar act: a trash can used "to mean something other than delete" goes against what people learned in other software, and a delete icon redrawn with "creative liberty" loses "that immediate recognition". Some acts have no clear picture, since "A pencil might suggest annotate, and a checkmark can look like confirm— making actions like Select or Edit easy to misread", and "When there's no clear shorthand, a text label is always the better choice." Check: for each icon on this screen without a word, what in the person's life or on their phone already gives it this meaning, and does this picture mean anything else elsewhere in the app? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 9, Recognition is Easy; Recall is Hard (Recognition versus Recall: Implications for UI Design, Use pictures where possible to convey function), `reference/books/johnson-2010/11-chapter-9-recognition-is-easy-recall-is-hard.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Buttons (Content: Try to associate familiar actions with familiar icons; Consider using text when a short label communicates more clearly than an icon), `reference/hig/buttons.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Tab bars (Best practices: Include tab labels to help with navigation; visionOS: Supply a symbol and a text label for each tab), `reference/hig/tab-bars.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 3.2.4 Consistent Identification (Examples of Consistent Identification), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/consistent-identification.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Icon (When to consider something else; Usability), `reference/uswds/components/icon.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Context menus (Content: Represent menu item actions with familiar icons), `reference/hig/context-menus.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Home Screen quick actions (Best practices: Provide a familiar interface icon for each quick action), `reference/hig/home-screen-quick-actions.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Notification actions: Provide a simple, recognizable interface icon for each notification action), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Apple, Principles of great design, WWDC26 session 250 (2026) (Familiarity, 7:23), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2026-250.md`; Apple, Get to know the new design system, WWDC25 session 356 (2025) (Continuity, 15:19), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-356.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A smart lights app's bottom bar. Pass: a bulb icon stands for Lights, and the sparkle icon carries the word Scenes. Fail: the same bar shows the sparkle icon with no word.

### Verbs act, nouns choose

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**The words on a control say what kind it is: a verb on a control that does something, a noun on one that is chosen or gone to.** Apple's buttons start their label "with a verb to help convey the button's action", as in "Add to Cart". Alert buttons: "Prefer verbs and verb phrases that relate directly to the alert text." Segment labels are "nouns or noun phrases". A tab label describes "the type of content or functionality the tab contains". Column headings are "nouns or short noun phrases". The pages give the rule but not the reason. The reason is an inference: the grammar can be read before the tap, so a person knows whether a tap will change something or only show something. The held "Said outright, not deduced" puts on a control what it will do; this claim makes the kind of control readable from the form of its words. A status is an adjective, since "Using a verb might make a user think that clicking on them will do something", and a task the person will do starts with a verb, "'check', 'declare', 'report'". Check: for each labelled control on this screen, is its label a verb if it acts and a noun if it chooses or goes somewhere, and which control breaks the pattern? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Buttons (Content: Consider using text when a short label communicates more clearly than an icon), `reference/hig/buttons.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Alerts (Buttons: Create succinct, logical button titles), `reference/hig/alerts.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Segmented controls (Content: Use nouns or noun phrases for segment labels), `reference/hig/segmented-controls.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Tab bars (Best practices: Include tab labels to help with navigation), `reference/hig/tab-bars.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Lists and tables (Content: Use descriptive column headings in a multicolumn table), `reference/hig/lists-and-tables.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Tag (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/tag.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Complete multiple tasks (Show related actions as a task; Status text), `reference/govuk/patterns/complete-multiple-tasks.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Button (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/button.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Home Screen quick actions (Best practices: For each quick action, provide a succinct title that instantly communicates the results of the action), `reference/hig/home-screen-quick-actions.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Notification actions: Provide beneficial actions that make sense in the context of your notification), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Context menus (Content), `reference/hig/context-menus.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A parcel tracking app's delivery page. Pass: the button reads Reschedule delivery and the tabs read Active and Delivered. Fail: the same button reads Delivery, which could be a tab or an action.

### A field's size says how much goes in

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**An entry field is drawn about the size of what it takes, so its shape tells the person how much to give before they type.** "To the extent possible, match the size of a text field to the quantity of anticipated text. The size of a text field helps people visually gauge the amount of information to provide." A one-line field is for "a small amount of information, such as a name or an email address", and a larger amount goes in a text view. A field for an email address shows at least 30 characters, so most people see their whole address once it is typed. Check: for each field on this screen, does its size match what a person will put in it, and does any short field invite a paragraph or any long one a word? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Text fields (Best practices: Use a text field to request a small amount of information, such as a name or an email address; To the extent possible, match the size of a text field to the quantity of anticipated text), `reference/hig/text-fields.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Text input (Use appropriately-sized text inputs), `reference/govuk/components/text-input.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Textarea (Use appropriately-sized textareas), `reference/govuk/components/textarea.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Email addresses (Help users to enter a valid email address), `reference/govuk/patterns/email-addresses.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Text input (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/text-input.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A dentist booking app's patient form. Pass: Reason for visit is a large multi-line box and Postcode a short field. Fail: the same form gives Reason for visit a one-line field the width of Postcode.

### An unusual word or short form is explained where it stands

Called for by: text. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**A word used in an unusual or narrow sense, an idiom, a piece of jargon or an abbreviation has its meaning available where it stands, unless the abbreviation has become a word of the language.** "Certain disabilities make it difficult to understand non-literal word usage and specialized words or usage." Authors may judge unusual use against "the intended target audience". An abbreviation that has "become part of the language" is exempt, as "laser" is, but otherwise "the criterion does not exempt abbreviations even if they are common or familiar". The held "Words the reader already has" keeps strange words off the screen; this covers the word that must stay. People seldom ask what a word means: in a diary study of meetings that Nielsen reports, people met "123 cases of unfamiliar jargon, but they only interrupted the speaker to ask for an explanation in 5 cases (4%)", and he recommends "Inline, tappable highlights" over "walls of text"; the version of Song and colleagues' study (2025) on the shelf does not report that diary study, so its figures stand as Nielsen's. Explaining every such word to everyone costs attention: in Song's small study of seven research interns at one technology company, four of them with doctorates, each watching six colleagues' recorded ten-minute talks, a sidebar that defined every term a model found, about 23 a talk of which the listener judged 47% helpful, raised self-rated understanding over no help (3.79 against 3.07 of 5) but not engagement (3.64 against 3.93), and listeners called it "annoying" and "offensive (because) the system treats me like I know nothing". Cut by a one-sentence description of the listener's background to about ten terms, 77.5% of them helpful, it raised self-rated understanding to 4.29, and the presenters graded listeners' written takeaways and questions deeper than with no help (4.04 against 3.25), though the cut also dropped about one helpful term in four (7.6 left of 10.3), so a meaning put in view unasked is kept to the words this reader likely lacks, and every other stays where it stands, a tap away. Check: which word or abbreviation on this screen would a newcomer not know in this sense, and where can they find what it means without leaving? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 3.1.3 Unusual Words, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/unusual-words.md`; WCAG 2.2, 3.1.4 Abbreviations, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/abbreviations.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 3.1.3 Unusual Words (Intent of Unusual Words), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/unusual-words.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 3.1.4 Abbreviations (Intent of Abbreviations), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/abbreviations.md`; Nielsen, UX Roundup: No More UI | Easy Quant | Personalized AI | State of AI: Growth but Risk | USA Gov Usability (2025) (The Meeting Hack: Personalized Jargon Help), `reference/essays/nielsen-no-more-ui.md`; Song et al., Breaking the Curse of Knowledge: Designing Personalized Jargon Support for Real-Time Online Meetings, arXiv (2025) (3.1 Initial Prototype of ParseJargon; 3.2 Methods; 3.3 Findings; Appendix B, Tables 6 and 8), `reference/papers/song-2025-breaking-the-curse-of-knowledge.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A mortgage app's offer screen showing APR 5.9%. Pass: tapping APR opens a line reading Annual percentage rate: the yearly cost, fees included. Fail: the same screen shows APR 5.9% with nowhere to learn what APR means.

### In the order of its source

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**Where a person copies a value from something in front of them, such as a card or a document, the fields follow that thing's order and format, so the eye moves between the two in step.** "When asking for a date exactly as it's shown on a passport, credit card or other document, make the fields match the format of the original. This makes it easier for users to copy the date across accurately." "Present all fields on a single page, in the order in which they appear on a credit or debit card. This makes it easy for users to transcribe the information from their payment card." Where a value must match a document, the question names the document, as in "Please enter your name exactly as it appears on your passport". Check: for each value on this screen that a person copies from elsewhere, do the order and format of its fields match the thing it is copied from? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Dates (Asking for dates from documents and cards), `reference/govuk/patterns/dates.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Payment card details (Help users enter valid card information), `reference/govuk/patterns/payment-card-details.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Name (Considerations), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/name.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A shop's card form at checkout. Pass: card number, expiry as MM/YY, name on card, then security code, in the card's own order. Fail: the same form asks for the name first, then the expiry as day, month and four-digit year, then the number.

### Numbers and times in the person's own form

Called for by: numbers. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**A number, an amount, a date or a time on a screen is written the way the person's language and region write it: thousands grouped and decimals marked their way, dates in their order and their time zone. A count agrees with its noun, and no raw value from the machine reaches the screen.** Apple lists among what a layout must handle "Locale-based internationalization features like left-to-right/right-to-left layout direction, date/time/number formatting, font variation, and text length", and warns that formatting "can vary significantly based on people's locale". A practice guide asks the same in so many words: "Format dates, times, numbers, delimiters, & currencies for the user’s locale". It names the failures: "1.284 in de-DE vs 1,284 in en-US", where "hardcoded separators are wrong for half the world"; "1 members"; "0.30000000000000004 rendered raw"; null, undefined and NaN "Rendered literally"; a zero timestamp shown as 1970-01-01; and an evening time that "Shows as a different day in UTC and the user's timezone". A time just past reads just now, not "0 seconds ago", and a time ago turns into a date "after a week or so". A number and its unit stay on one line, since the guide asks to "keep units, shortcuts & names together". The held "Typed stays typed" covers how a number the person entered is shown back. This claim covers every number the app shows. The guides cite no study. A date written relative to now, such as Today or Yesterday, is rewritten when the day turns while it is on the screen, a fix a changelog records as relative date labels "now appropriately updated when the document is open". Check: with the phone set to German and to a time zone eight hours away, do this screen's numbers, dates and times follow, and does any count of one carry a plural noun? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Layout (Adaptability), `reference/hig/layout.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Text fields (Best practices: Use a number formatter to help with numeric data), `reference/hig/text-fields.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Content: Locale-aware formats; Non-breaking spaces for glued terms), `reference/vercel/README.md`; Kowalski, Breaking UI: Worst-Case Catalog, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Numbers and money; Time), `reference/kowalski/skills/break-ui/CATALOG.md`; Kowalski, Breaking UI, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Phase 4 — Break it: Failure signatures), `reference/kowalski/skills/break-ui/SKILL.source.md`; Linear, UI refresh – Changelog (2026) (list of fixes, Documents), `reference/essays/linear-ui-refresh.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A shop app's basket holding one item over a thousand pounds. Pass: it reads 1 item, £1,284.00. Fail: the same basket reads 1 items, £1284.0000001.

## Flexibility: Adapt to diverse contexts and needs.

### The practised hand is not slowed

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**A screen used daily lets the practised user act on it without attending, and guards their slips by showing the result and letting it be undone, never by demanding care.** Slips occur more often to skilled people than to novices, because skilled behaviour is subconscious, fast, effortless and usually accurate; it might seem that one way to minimize slips is to ensure that people always pay close, conscious attention, and that is a bad idea, since if we had to pay attention to every little thing we would accomplish far less. Put the knowledge in the world for the non-expert and for the expert on a rare operation, but allow for efficient operation when people have learned all the requirements. The platform's own app switcher on the Mac goes further, as a practice essay observes: "if the time delta between pressing Command and Tab is low enough, the previously active window receives focus immediately without showing the menu", so a chooser the practised hand passes through faster than it could be read is not drawn at all. Check: what on this screen makes a user who has done this a hundred times stop and read, and could the same slip instead be made visible and reversible? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (The Classification of Slips; Minimizing Slips; Design Principles for Dealing with Error), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 10, Learning from Experience and Performing Learned Actions Are Easy (Performing Learned Actions Is Easy), `reference/books/johnson-2010/12-chapter-10-learning-from-experience-and-performing-learned-a.md`; Freiberg, Invisible Details of Interaction Design (2023) (Frequency & Novelty), `reference/essays/freiberg-invisible-details.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A to-do app's task list. Pass: ticking a task strikes it through and shows Undo for a few seconds. Fail: the same tick asks Are you sure this task is complete? every single time.

### Type and contrast are the person's

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**Text on a screen is set in the platform's own sizes and grows with the person's setting, and it stands on its background at the platform's minimum contrast; large and dark is read by everyone, in any light.** There is no such thing as the average person; make the lettering larger, with high-contrast type, and everyone can read it better, and in dim light even the people with the world's best eyesight will benefit. The best solution to the problem of designing for everyone is flexibility: allow people to adjust font size and contrast; fixed solutions will invariably fail with some people. Text so small that its readers cannot read it, or read it only laboriously, might as well not be there, and the platform's floor is 11 pt with 17 pt its default; and running text never sits on a pattern or a picture, since a background need not be strong to disrupt reading; a control's symbol or short label over a photo or a video stands on the platform's material, or on a dark layer where the picture is bright, and still meets the contrast minimum. The default meets the minimum, and a higher-contrast scheme under the device's Increase Contrast setting comes on top of it, never in place of it. The 3:1 allowance for large or bold text is also WCAG's, and Dark Mode's flat 4.5:1 is the stricter reading, used where text sizes are not known. The 17 pt default and 11 pt floor are iPhone and iPad's; Apple Watch's are 16 and 12 pt, Vision Pro's 17 and 12, Mac's 13 and 10, and Apple TV's 29 and 23, and text enlarges by at least 200 percent, or 140 percent on Apple Watch. The minimum covers icons as well as text, at 3:1 for an icon, as WCAG asks of any picture needed to understand the screen, and for the app's own colours the aim is 7:1, especially in small text. Bold counts as large only from 14 pt, about 18.5 CSS px, as WCAG has it, though the platform's table lets bold of any size go to 3:1; the stricter reading is used. At larger sizes the layout stacks rather than truncates, the content the person cares about grows first, the main elements stay near the top, and meaningful icons grow with the text. The ratios rest on sight: 3:1 is the least that the ISO and ANSI standards recommend, 4.5:1 makes up for the contrast lost at 20/40 acuity, usual near age 80, and 7:1 for 20/80. Where the screen is a web page, running text is at least 16 CSS px, with smaller sizes kept for captions, footnotes and table data: "use at least an effective size of 16px". In four online focus groups of 18 adults aged 60 to 83, small text and visually dense layouts were among their bad experiences of apps and high-contrast colours among what they asked for, and the authors add that "although text can be adjusted within device settings, ensuring an appropriate default size may improve perceived ease of use"; this is what they said, not a measured cost. Where the screen is a web page, focusing a field leaves the page at its size, so a field's text is at least 16 CSS px, since on iPhone the browser "zooms the page when focus lands on an input whose font size is under 16px, and it does not zoom back out on blur", in practice guides that cite no study. Apple puts its own controls over "media backgrounds — such as photos and videos" on its clearest material, and "If the underlying content is bright, consider adding a dark dimming layer of 35% opacity", since "Without it, legibility gets noticeably worse"; the 35 per cent is the platform's own figure and cites no study. Nor is the person's own zoom switched off to keep the page from enlarging: "Never disable zoom", since switching it off is one of the "accessibility failures", in practice guides that cite no study. A face of the app's own grows with the person's setting as the platform's style for the same role does, a title as a title and body text as body text, since "the different text styles may have different scaling behaviors", and the space set round the text grows with it, "in proportion to the text size instead of a fixed size". Check: at the person's largest text setting does every word on this screen still show, and does each text meet 4.5:1 against its background at 17 pt and under, 3:1 above 17 pt or bold at 14 pt and over, and each meaningful icon 3:1? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 6, Design Thinking (Designing for Special People; The Stigma Problem), `reference/books/norman-2013/06-chapter-6-design-thinking.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Vision: Support larger text sizes; Use recommended defaults for custom type sizes; Strive to meet color contrast minimum standards), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Typography (Conveying hierarchy: Prioritize important content when responding to text-size changes; Supporting Dynamic Type: Keep text truncation to a minimum as font size increases; Consider adjusting your layout at large font sizes; Maintain a consistent information hierarchy regardless of the current font size; Increase the size of meaningful interface icons as font size increases), `reference/hig/typography.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 4, Reading Is Unnatural (Poor Information Design Can Disrupt Reading, Tiny fonts; Text on noisy background), `reference/books/johnson-2010/06-chapter-4-reading-is-unnatural.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Typography (Ensuring legibility: Use font sizes that most people can read easily), `reference/hig/typography.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.4.3 Contrast (Minimum), Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/contrast-minimum.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Dark Mode (Dark Mode colors: Aim for sufficient color contrast in all appearances), `reference/hig/dark-mode.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Layout (Adaptability: Be prepared for text-size changes), `reference/hig/layout.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.4.4 Resize Text, Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/resize-text.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.4.6 Contrast (Enhanced), Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/contrast-enhanced.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.4.3 Contrast (Minimum) (Rationale for the Ratios Chosen), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/contrast-minimum.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.4.3 Contrast (Minimum) (Intent of Contrast (Minimum)), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/contrast-minimum.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.4.11 Non-text Contrast, Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/21/non-text-contrast.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Typography (Font size), `reference/uswds/components/typography/typography.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Privacy (Location button: Consider customizing the location button to harmonize with your UI), `reference/hig/privacy.md`; Berry et al., Designing an mHealth App to Encourage Uptake of Muscle-Strengthening Exercise in Older Adults: Co-Design Focus Group Study, JMIR Aging (2026) (Results: Theme 4: Desired App Elements and Features; Discussion: Theme 4: Desired App Elements and Features), `reference/studies/PMC13022557.md`; Kowalski, Feeling Native On Mobile, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (4.
