Page zooms into the input), `reference/kowalski/skills/mobile-native/SKILL.source.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Interactions: Mobile input size), `reference/vercel/README.md`; Freiberg, Web Interface Guidelines (2023) (Touch), `reference/freiberg/README.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Materials (Liquid Glass: Only use clear Liquid Glass for components that appear over visually rich backgrounds), `reference/hig/materials.md`; Apple, Meet Liquid Glass, WWDC25 session 219 (2025) (Principles, 14:00), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-219.md`; Kowalski, Feeling Native On Mobile, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Hard Rules), `reference/kowalski/skills/mobile-native/SKILL.source.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Interactions: Respect zoom), `reference/vercel/README.md`; Apple, The details of UI typography, WWDC20 session 10175 (2020) (Dynamic Type, 24:11; 27:57), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2020-10175.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A weather app's forecast list. Pass: text uses the system sizes, grows with the Larger Text setting, and is dark grey on white at 7:1. Fail: the same list is fixed at 10 pt light grey on pale blue, unchanged by Larger Text.

### The aid is the plain way

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**A thing a screen does for the people who need it is given to everyone as the better way, never as a special mode a person must switch on or admit to needing.** Many devices designed to aid people with particular difficulties fail: they are rejected by their intended users, since most people do not wish to advertise their infirmities; the OXO peeler, designed for an arthritic wife, was advertised as a better peeler for everyone, and it was. Special features made for people with special needs often turn out to be useful for a wide variety of people. A founder of a notes app used in meetings retells the kitchen-utensil maker's story, unsure it was OXO, as a method: designing for "the most extreme kind of user", for his app someone "running from back-to-back meeting all day long, frazzled by the context switching", ends in "a very kind of friendly, easy-to-use product for the rest of us too". Check: does any help on this screen live behind a setting that names a need, and could it be the default for all? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 6, Design Thinking (The Stigma Problem), `reference/books/norman-2013/06-chapter-6-design-thinking.md`; Stephenson, interviewed by Labenz, Calm AI for Crazy Days, The Cognitive Revolution (2026), `reference/interviews/stephenson-granola.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A recipe app's cooking view. Pass: every recipe shows one step per screen in large type by default. Fail: the same large-step view sits behind a setting called Low Vision Mode.

### Said in text

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**What a user must take in is written on the screen, where it can be read and skimmed, and anything the app speaks is also written; speech serves eyes that are elsewhere and is never the only copy.** Text can be skimmed, jumping ahead and back; listening is serial and, even sped up, not easy to skim. A video that carries what a task needs gives the same as a transcript. Check: is anything on this screen told only by voice, and can the user read it afterwards? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 7, Design in the World of Business (New Technologies Force Change), `reference/books/norman-2013/07-chapter-7-design-in-the-world-of-business.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Hearing: Support text-based ways to enjoy audio and video), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`; Android Developers, Principles for improving app accessibility (Make media content more accessible), `reference/android/principles.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A bus app's arrival alert. Pass: the spoken Bus 42 in 3 minutes also stays written on screen. Fail: the same alert is only spoken, with nothing written to read afterwards.

### Told apart in grey

Called for by: colour. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**Two colours that mean different things differ in brightness or saturation and not in hue alone, so that in grey, on a dim display, at an angle or in strong light, they are still two.** Distinguish colors by saturation and brightness as well as hue; avoid subtle color differences; one way to test whether colors are different enough is to view them in grayscale, and if you can't distinguish the colors when they are rendered in grays, they aren't different enough. Strong light on a display washes out colors before it washes out light and dark areas, reducing color displays to grayscale ones, as anyone who has tried to use a bank ATM in direct sunlight knows; designers don't have full control of the color viewing experience of users. Two colours count as told apart by lightness where they stand at 3:1 or more against each other. Check: render this screen in grey; can each colour that carries a meaning still be told from the colour beside it? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 5, Our Color Vision Is Limited (Guidelines for Using Color, guideline 1; Vision Is Optimized for Edge Contrast, Not Brightness; External Factors That Influence the Ability to Distinguish Colors), `reference/books/johnson-2010/07-chapter-5-our-color-vision-is-limited.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Color (Best practices: Test your app's color scheme under a variety of lighting conditions), `reference/hig/color.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.4.1 Use of Color (Intent of Use of Color), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/use-of-color.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A parcel tracking app's status dots. Pass: Delivered is dark green and Pending is pale yellow, still two different greys in greyscale. Fail: the same dots are a red and a green of equal brightness, which turn the same grey in greyscale.

### No pair the colour-blind cannot split

Called for by: colour. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**Two colours that must be told apart are never dark red and black, dark red and dark green, blue and purple, or light green and white, the pairs that about one man in twelve cannot split.** Approximately 8% of men and slightly under 0.5% of women have a color perception deficit, difficulty discriminating certain pairs of colors; the most common type is red/green. Avoid color pairs that color-blind people cannot distinguish: dark red versus black, dark red versus dark green, blue versus purple, light green versus white; don't use dark reds, blues, or violets against any dark colors, and if you are not color-blind you can get an idea of which colors will be hard to distinguish by converting the image to grayscale. Check: name each pair of colours on this screen a person must tell apart; is any one of the four pairs, and what does it look like in grey? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 5, Our Color Vision Is Limited (Color-Blindness; Guidelines for Using Color, guideline 3), `reference/books/johnson-2010/07-chapter-5-our-color-vision-is-limited.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Vision: Convey information with more than color alone), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A share-trading app's price list. Pass: rising prices are blue and falling ones orange. Fail: the same list shows rising prices in dark green and falling ones in dark red.

### Colour is never the only mark

Called for by: colour. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**Whatever a colour says on a screen is also said another way, by a word, a shape or a mark, so the person who cannot see the colour, or the display that cannot show it, still gets it.** Use color redundantly with other cues; don't rely on color alone; if you use color to mark something, mark it another way as well, as iPhoto uses both color and a symbol to distinguish smart albums from regular ones, and as ITN.net came to mark the current step with color and shape. When size, brightness and hue varied together in lockstep, people identified 4.1 bits against about 2.7 for any one alone (Eriksen, reported by Miller), so a meaning said in colour and in a second feature is told more surely by everyone, not only by people who cannot see the colour. A message's kind is said in its first words as well as by its colour and symbol, by saying what happened, such as Payment not sent or Changes saved, rather than a bare Error or Success. The other way may also be a size, a pattern, or a sound or a buzz. Check: for each thing this screen says with a colour, what else on the screen says it? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 5, Our Color Vision Is Limited (Guidelines for Using Color, guideline 4), `reference/books/johnson-2010/07-chapter-5-our-color-vision-is-limited.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Color (Inclusive color: Avoid relying solely on color to differentiate between objects, indicate interactivity, or communicate essential information), `reference/hig/color.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Vision: Convey information with more than color alone), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`; Miller, The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two: Some Limits on Our Capacity for Processing Information, Psychological Review 63 (1956), p. 88 (Absolute Judgments of Multidimensional Stimuli), `reference/papers/miller-1956-the-magical-number-seven.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.4.1 Use of Color, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/use-of-color.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Link (Usability should not), `reference/uswds/components/link.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Accessibility (Limited-vision users), `reference/uswds/documentation/guidance/accessibility.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Alert (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/alert.md`; Android Developers, Principles for improving app accessibility (Use cues other than color), `reference/android/principles.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A calendar app's event list. Pass: a declined event is grey, struck through and labelled Declined. Fail: the same list shows a declined event only in a paler colour.

### The long way shows the short way

Called for by: new. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**The path a newcomer takes shows the faster path beside it, since a person who has learned one way does the task that way for ever and never looks for another.** Once we learn one way to perform a task we may continue to do it that way and never discover a more efficient way; even if told there is a better way we stick with the old because it is familiar and requires little thought, "I'm in a hurry, so I'll do it the long way"; the slower paths for newcomers should show users faster paths if there are any, on a phone the short ways are a touch and hold on an item, which opens a menu of the commands it most often needs, and the app's actions on the Home Screen, and a person on the long way learns of one by a tip at that moment; on iPad with a keyboard the shortcuts show beside the commands in the app's main menus, never in a touch-and-hold menu. Where a short way is told by a tip, the tip runs "one or two sentences", is for a feature of no more than three actions, since "If a feature requires more than three actions, it's probably too complicated for a tip", holds nothing "promotional or related to a different feature or user flow", and is shown only to someone who has not used the feature, since "people who've already used a feature won't appreciate viewing a tip that describes it"; tips come no oftener than about "once every 24 hours". The app's own keyboard shortcuts are kept for its most used commands, since "defining too many new shortcuts can make your app seem difficult to learn." The short ways from the Home Screen are for the app's most valued tasks, no more than four, since "People tend to expect every app to provide at least one useful quick action; you can provide a total of four." A button whose act a gesture also does can show the gesture in its motion, as a browser's close button slides the tab off to the left, the way a swipe would: "by keeping the discrete animation and the gesture aligned, we can use one to teach the other." Check: for each task on this screen that has a short way, where on the long way is the short way shown? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 8, Limits on Attention Shape Thought and Action (We Prefer Familiar Paths, Help experienced users speed up), `reference/books/johnson-2010/10-chapter-8-limits-on-attention-shape-thought-and-action.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Offering help (Creating tips: introduction; Use tips for simple features; Make tips short, actionable, and engaging; Define rules to help ensure your tips reach the intended audience), `reference/hig/offering-help.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Keyboards (Custom keyboard shortcuts: Define custom keyboard shortcuts for only the most frequently used app-specific commands), `reference/hig/keyboards.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Home Screen quick actions (Best practices: Create quick actions for compelling, high-value tasks), `reference/hig/home-screen-quick-actions.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Virtual keyboards (introduction), `reference/hig/virtual-keyboards.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Context menus (Best practices: Show keyboard shortcuts in your app's main menus, not in context menus), `reference/hig/context-menus.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Offering help (Creating tips), `reference/hig/offering-help.md`; Apple, Designing Fluid Interfaces, WWDC18 session 803 (2018) (Teaching, 59:14), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2018-803.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A notes app whose New Note is also on the Home Screen. Pass: after a person makes a note from the app's New Note button, a tip says once that touching and holding the app's icon offers New Note. Fail: the same app offers New Note on the Home Screen and never tells of it.

### Near and wide along the way

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**Where a person reaches for a target on the screen, the time it takes grows with the target's distance from where the finger last was and shrinks with its width along the line of travel, and within the range measured, an arm moving a stylus, only their ratio counted; for a finger the least size comes first, from the platform, and the ratio orders the targets above it, so targets used one after another sit near each other and a far target is made wider in proportion.** In Fitts's tapping, disc and pin tasks, "For each category of Ws, movement time increased progressively as movement amplitude (A) increased. Likewise for each amplitude, movement time increased progressively as tolerance was decreased" (p. 265). The difficulty of a movement is log2(2A/Ws) bits (p. 266), and "movements of differing amplitude but of equal difficulty in terms of information tend to be of approximately equal duration" (p. 268). At 4 bits, a plate a quarter inch wide two inches away and a plate two inches wide sixteen inches away both took 0.36 to 0.39 seconds. Each further doubling of distance, or halving of width, added roughly a tenth of a second (Table 1, p. 264). Width here is the target's extent along the movement: the plates were 6 inches long across it (p. 263). Practice moved the times only 3 to 5 per cent (p. 265). The subjects were college students moving the arm back and forth along one line at their fastest. How large a target must be at the least is not Fitts's number but the platform's: on iPhone and Apple Watch a control's hit region is at least 44×44 pt, the stricter of the platform's two pages, even where the control drawn is as small as 28×28 pt. A practice guide's 1 cm by 1 cm, about 60 pt on a current iPhone, taken from a study of one-handed thumb use, is not the least here; the platform's 44 pt is (Timmy, 2026-10-16). In visionOS the hit region is 60×60 pt, and on Apple Watch a primary action spans the screen's width, since full-width buttons are easier to tap. On the web WCAG asks 44 by 44 CSS px at its highest level and 24 by 24 at its floor, which only a crowded page falls back on. An option's words are part of its target, so a tap on the words picks it: "Users should be able to select either the text label or the checkbox". For a finger on a phone a target acts as if narrower than drawn, a loss that weighs most on the smallest: in the same 24 adults, tapping times fitted better once 65 to 74 pixels, about 4 mm on that screen, were taken off every width, with or without a tremor. The pad of a fingertip is 1.6 to 2 cm wide and a thumb's contact about 2.5 cm, figures from the MIT Touch Lab as a practice guide reports them, so a finger covers more than most targets it is aimed at. The target is the whole of what is drawn as the control, its words, its icon and its padding, a run of rows or buttons leaves no gap between them for a tap to fall into, and nothing laid over a control for show, a glow or a gradient, takes the tap meant for it: "If part of a control looks interactive, it should be interactive." Where the person uses a pointer, a target on the screen's own edge or corner is as deep as the pointer can travel, since "the pointer can't overshoot past the corner" and so "the precision required for this interaction is very low", in a practice essay's reading of Fitts; for a finger on a phone a corner is instead the far reach that The frequent within reach of the holding hand keeps for rare acts. Check: for each target on this screen, how far is it from where the finger is just before, how wide is it along that line, is the target used next the nearest one, and does a tap anywhere on what is drawn as the control, or between two neighbours, land on one of them? Sources: Fitts, The Information Capacity of the Human Motor System in Controlling the Amplitude of Movement, Journal of Experimental Psychology 47 (1954), reprinted in Journal of Experimental Psychology: General 121 (1992), p. 263 (Experiment I: Reciprocal Tapping, Method), p. 264 (Experiment I, Results; Table 1), p. 265 (Experiment I, Results), p. 266 (Information Analysis), p. 268 (Discussion), `reference/papers/fitts-1954-information-capacity-of-the-human-motor-system.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Buttons (Best practices: Make buttons easy for people to use; watchOS: Prefer buttons that span the width of the screen for primary actions in your app), `reference/hig/buttons.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Mobility: Offer sufficiently sized controls), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.5.5 Target Size (Enhanced), Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/21/target-size-enhanced.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.5.8 Target Size (Minimum), Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/22/target-size-minimum.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Checkbox (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/checkbox.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Radio buttons (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/radio-buttons.md`; Dunlop and MacKenzie, Modelling and Correcting the Effects of Tremor on Touchscreen Interaction, Sensors (2026) (4.4. Design; 5.4. Fitts' Law Modelling), `reference/studies/PMC13611336.md`; Harley, Touch Targets on Touchscreens, Nielsen Norman Group (2019, updated 2023) (Size Matters; References), `reference/nngroup/touch-target-size.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Interactions: Match visual & hit targets; No dead zones; Forms: No dead zones on controls), `reference/vercel/README.md`; Freiberg, Web Interface Guidelines (2023) (Interactivity), `reference/freiberg/README.md`; Freiberg, Invisible Details of Interaction Design (2023) (Fitts's Law), `reference/essays/freiberg-invisible-details.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A podcast app's player, where Play and Skip back are tapped in turn. Pass: Skip back sits beside Play, both 44 pt. Fail: the same Skip back is a 20 pt icon at the top of the screen, far from Play.

### Small and far is mis-tapped

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Where a person reaches for a target on the screen, a narrower or farther target is hit wrongly more often even when the person is asked for care, and the wrong landing is on what sits beside it along the line, so a target whose mis-tap costs something is not small and is spaced off its neighbours.** In Fitts's tapping, errors were "movements that were either too long or too short", landing on the error plates on either side of the target (p. 264). They were 0.00 per cent at the widest, nearest condition. The largest share, "3.6% with the lighter stylus and 4.1% with the heavier stylus, was made at the most difficult task condition" (a quarter inch at 16 inches). The subjects had been told to "Emphasize accuracy rather than speed", yet "Ss did not slow down their movements quite as much as necessary to effect the required accuracy at the more exacting conditions" (p. 264). The numbers are for practised arm movements with a stylus. The spacing that keeps a mis-tap off a neighbour is the platform's: about 12 pt of padding round a bezelled control and about 24 pt round one without. This claim sits beside "A slip costs nothing", which governs what a mis-tap may cost; this one says where mis-taps come from. The spacing differs by surface: on visionOS button centres sit at least 60 pt apart, and on Apple Watch a row holds no more than three buttons with symbols, or two with text. A target is made larger than the least where it is used often, where its act cannot easily be undone, near the edge of the screen, in a run of steps, where it is the screen's main action, or where the screen is used on the move or by hands less steady, such as a child's or an older person's, and a target smaller than the least has a 24 CSS px circle round its centre that touches no other target or its circle. A tremor in the hand holding the phone shows as mis-taps far more than as slowness: in 24 adults in a lab with a tremor induced in that hand, misses rose from 3.9 per cent with none to 19.2 per cent at the strongest, while the time per tap rose only 21.9 per cent. Spacing does not make up for a target below the least: a dismiss button 2 mm wide, about 2 mm from the button with the opposite act, is still hard to hit, so a target is first big enough and then spaced. Two short targets that must sit close are better side by side than stacked, since a row's width leaves more room for a slip than its height. A target is sized for the finger, not only for the eye: a mark large enough to see but too small to hit, such as a 1 mm colour swatch under a product photo, draws misses and taps on what is beside it. Check: which targets on this screen are smallest or farthest from the finger, what sits next to each along the line of approach, and what does a tap on that neighbour do? Sources: Fitts, The Information Capacity of the Human Motor System in Controlling the Amplitude of Movement, Journal of Experimental Psychology 47 (1954), reprinted in Journal of Experimental Psychology: General 121 (1992), p. 264 (Experiment I: Reciprocal Tapping, Method and Results), `reference/papers/fitts-1954-information-capacity-of-the-human-motor-system.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Mobility: Consider spacing between controls as important as size), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Buttons (visionOS: Provide enough space around a button to make it easy for people to look at it), `reference/hig/buttons.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Layout (visionOS: Include enough space around controls for them to be easy to interact with; watchOS: Avoid placing more than two or three controls side by side in your interface), `reference/hig/layout.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.5.5 Target Size (Enhanced) (Intent), `reference/wcag/understanding/21/target-size-enhanced.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.5.8 Target Size (Minimum) (Spacing), `reference/wcag/understanding/22/target-size-minimum.md`; Dunlop and MacKenzie, Modelling and Correcting the Effects of Tremor on Touchscreen Interaction, Sensors (2026) (4.1. Participants; 4.4. Design; 5.2. Movement Time and Error Rate), `reference/studies/PMC13611336.md`; Harley, Touch Targets on Touchscreens, Nielsen Norman Group (2019, updated 2023) (Mind the Gap — Crowding Causes Errors; View–Tap Asymmetry; When Bigger Is Better), `reference/nngroup/touch-target-size.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A cinema ticket app's basket, where a thumb reaches for Buy tickets. Pass: Remove all seats is a full-size button 24 pt from Buy tickets. Fail: the same Remove all seats is a small icon touching Buy tickets.

### The last choice is the quickest again

Called for by: list. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**The option a person just took is the quickest for them to take again, so it stays on offer and in its place, and a rule that leaves out the last choice to shorten the next list slows the next choice instead of speeding it.** Hyman noticed in preliminary runs that "whenever a stimulus was immediately followed by itself in the series, S seemed to respond unusually fast to it". This held even though the subject "reported that in such cases he was not expecting the stimulus to follow itself" (pp. 194–195). The effect was "quite marked for the situation with four or more alternatives" and faded to nothing at two. With eight options, an answer to the same light again came in about 0.54 s, against about 0.69 to 0.74 s once one or more others had come between (Fig. 2, p. 195, read from the figure). In the three conditions where no light ever followed itself, the rule should have lowered the information "since it effectively decreases the number of alternatives by one. Instead of lowering S's reaction time, however, this restriction caused his reaction time to be higher than if no such restriction had been imposed" (p. 193). The conditions are Hyman's: four men practised over three months, told the rule, answering aloud. Turning the no-repeat rule into a screen that removes the last-taken option is an inference from these two results, not a test Hyman ran. This claim does not undo "What the answers settle is not asked". That claim cuts an option the person's earlier answers make impossible; this one keeps an option a rule would remove only because it was just taken. Check: after the person takes an option here, is it offered next time in the same place, and does any rule remove it only because it was just taken? Source: Hyman, Stimulus Information as a Determinant of Reaction Time, Journal of Experimental Psychology 45 (1953), p. 193 (Results, Experiment III), pp. 194–195 (Discussion, Reaction time as a function of the number of other stimuli intervening between successive occurrences of a stimulus; Fig. 2), `reference/papers/hyman-1953-stimulus-information-as-a-determinant-of-reaction-time.pdf`, PDF pages 3 and 4.

Example, not part of the claim: A coffee shop app's quick-order list. Pass: yesterday's Oat latte stays in its place. Fail: the same list hides yesterday's choice to shorten it, on the guess that no one orders the same twice.

### A likely value is offered filled in

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**Where one answer is far likelier than the rest, the field opens holding it, so the person confirms instead of deciding.** Apple: "You can also prefill fields with reasonable default values, which can minimize decision making and speed data entry." How a prefilled value is marked as the screen's is governed by the held "What the app filled in looks like the app's". The held "A tap is cheaper than a thought" says why confirming beats deciding. The source is thin: one sentence, with no measure of how likely a default must be. It holds for a question about the person too, where the device or their own past answers make one answer far likelier, such as their country from the phone's region, since Apple asks apps to "Get information from the system whenever possible"; a reasonable default is one the device, the person's past answers or most people's choices make far likelier, never a guess, and where nothing does, nothing is chosen. Where the app offers several guesses, the likeliest comes first and may be chosen already, "so people can quickly achieve their goals without reading through every option", but only where the app knows how often its first guess is right: "When you know how your confidence values correlate with the quality of your results, you might use them to rank the options." The nearest measure is of a suggestion shown before the person chooses, and it shows what rides on how likely the value is rather than a bar: in Buçinca's online experiment with 199 crowd workers, a simulated AI's suggestion right three times in four about doubled the decisions fully right over no suggestion, 35% against 17%, but where it was wrong, people shown it picked the right ingredient to replace 8% of the time against 49% with none, so a value offered is taken when it is wrong, and filling it in pays only where it is seldom wrong or a wrong one costs little. Check: for each field on this screen, is one answer far likelier than the rest, and is it there when the field opens? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Entering data (Best practices: Be clear about the data you need; Get information from the system whenever possible), `reference/hig/entering-data.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Radio buttons (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/radio-buttons.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Select (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/select.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Multiple options: List the most likely option first), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Buçinca et al., To Trust or to Think: Cognitive Forcing Functions Can Reduce Overreliance on AI in AI-assisted Decision-making, CSCW (2021) (3.3 The Simulated AI; 4.1 Objective measures; 6 Discussion), `reference/papers/bucinca-2021-to-trust-or-to-think.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: An electric car charging app, where most charge to 80%. Pass: the Charge to field opens holding 80%. Fail: the same field opens empty.

### A small change by a step, a large one typed

Called for by: entry, numbers. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**Where a number is changed, a small change is made with a step control and a large or far-ranging one can be typed, the two side by side, so neither change is made the slow way.** "Steppers work well by themselves for making small changes that require a few taps or clicks. By contrast, people appreciate the option to use a field to enter specific values, especially when the values they use can vary widely." Apple's example is a printing screen that has both a stepper and a field for the number of copies. On a Mac, a modified click moves the value further, "by 10 times the default, for example". A step control shows no number of its own ("the stepper itself doesn't display a value"), so the number it changes sits beside it, as the held "A control sits by what it changes" already requires. Check: for each number on this screen, how far does a person usually move it, and can they both nudge it and type it? Source: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Steppers (introduction; Best practices: Consider pairing a stepper with a text field when large value changes are likely; macOS: For large value ranges, consider supporting Shift-click to change the value quickly), `reference/hig/steppers.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A printing app's Copies setting, often 1 and sometimes 50. Pass: a stepper sits beside a field showing 1, and 50 can be typed. Fail: the same Copies has only a stepper, so 50 copies take 49 taps.

### More than one way to do it

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**Every action and every entry can be done with more than one kind of input: a visible control as well as any gesture, and keyboard, voice and assistive switch where the device has them, so no one is shut out by one way of moving.** "Give people more than one way to interact with your app. People commonly prefer or need to use other inputs — such as their voice, keyboard, or Switch Control — to interact with their devices. Don't assume that people can use a specific gesture to perform a given task." A custom gesture must be "Not the only way to perform an important action". "if you use a swipe gesture to dismiss a view, also make a button available so people can tap or use an assistive device." Entry should support "all available input methods so people can choose the method that works for them", including pasting and dragging. The held "Signified, not labelled after" asks that a gesture be signified; this claim asks that a gesture never be the only way. Everything also works from a keyboard with no timing on single keys, a gesture of several fingers or along a path also works with one pointer and no path, a drag also works without dragging, and no input the platform offers is refused. An aid that finds or picks a value, a lookup or a calendar, always has a typed entry beside it for what it cannot find ("Never make a calendar control that depends on JavaScript as the only input option"; an address lookup offers "a manual option"), and a value set by dragging can also be set without dragging. A view's grabber can be used without seeing the screen: "a grabber also works with VoiceOver so people can resize the sheet without seeing the screen." A touch and hold is never the only way to a command: "Always make context menu items available in the main interface, too." Where the app is worked from a keyboard on iPhone or iPad, it is through the system's Full Keyboard Access, which reaches every control. The app's own keyboard moves are kept to text fields, text views and sidebars, since for "buttons, segmented controls, and switches", Apple says "let people use Full Keyboard Access to activate controls". A gesture's action is also offered by name among the actions a screen reader, voice control or switch can call, such as Remove article from list for a swipe that dismisses it, so people using them "can perform actions that might otherwise be available only through gestures". Apple's principles put it under flexibility: "Designing for as many inputs as possible — including voice, touch, keyboard, and more — means more people can use your product the way that works best for them." Speaking an entry keeps typing beside it, since a person cannot always speak where they are: "I also loved having the option to type for [sic] when I was near others", said one of 18 adults in a 28-day pilot. There, those who could speak or type logged 30 times on average against 18 for those who could only type, though the arms were small, partly self-chosen, and also differed in their reminders. Check: for each action on this screen done by a gesture, what visible control does the same, and can each entry be typed, pasted and dictated? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Gestures (Best practices: Give people more than one way to interact with your app; Custom gestures: Add custom gestures only when necessary), `reference/hig/gestures.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Email address (What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/email-address.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Sheets (iOS, iPadOS: Include a grabber in a resizable sheet), `reference/hig/sheets.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Context menus (Best practices: Always make context menu items available in the main interface, too), `reference/hig/context-menus.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Keyboards (Best practices: Support Full Keyboard Access when possible), `reference/hig/keyboards.md`; Android Developers, Principles for improving app accessibility (Add accessibility actions), `reference/android/principles.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Design principles (Flexibility: Consider a variety of input methods), `reference/hig/design-principles.md`; Chikwetu et al., Automated Diet Capture Using Voice Alerts and Speech Recognition on Smartphones: Pilot Usability and Acceptability Study, JMIR Formative Research (2023) (Results: User Engagement Metrics; Discussion: Principal Findings), `reference/studies/PMC10230351.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A mail app's message list. Pass: a message is deleted by swiping its row, or through Edit, selecting it and tapping Delete. Fail: the same list, where the swipe is the only way to delete.

### The simplest gesture for the frequent act

Called for by: controls, motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**What a person does often is done with the simplest gesture there is: one finger, one hand, either hand. A gesture that takes several fingers, both hands or one particular hand is kept for rare acts and is given a simpler twin.** "For interactions people do frequently in your app or game, use the simplest gesture possible — avoid custom multifinger and multihand gestures — so repetitive actions are both comfortable and easy to remember." "People may not always have both hands available." A gesture for one hand "can increase someone's cognitive load if they need to remember which hand to use". "repeating very similar movements many times in succession can stress people's muscles and joints." Check: for each gesture on this screen, how often is it made, how many fingers or hands does it take, and could one finger do it? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Mobility: Support simple gestures for common interactions), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Gestures (visionOS: Prioritize comfort; Carefully consider complex custom gestures that involve multiple fingers or both hands; Avoid custom gestures that require using a specific hand), `reference/hig/gestures.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A reading app's page turn. Pass: a one-finger tap near the edge turns the page. Fail: the same app, where the page turns only with a three-finger swipe.

### The frequent within reach of the holding hand

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**A control a person uses often sits where the hand holding or resting on the device reaches it without changing grip, and a tiring reach (a far corner, a raised arm) is kept for rare acts.** People hold an iPhone "in one or both hands", and "it tends to be easier and more comfortable for people to reach a control when it's located in the middle or bottom area of the display". On a headset, touching things directly means raising the arms: "Because people may find it tiring to keep their arms raised for extended periods, direct gestures are best for infrequent use." Where the easy reach lies is the device's to say: the middle and bottom on iPhone. The held "Near and wide along the way" prices the move from the last place the finger was; this claim says where the hand rests to begin with. Search, essential "When content isn't visible upfront", has a tab of its own at the bottom of the iPhone screen, "making it quicker to access and easier to reach." Check: for each control on this screen that is used often, can the thumb of the hand holding the device reach it, and what sits in the far corner? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Designing for iOS (Ergonomics; Best practices: Support interactions that accommodate the way people usually hold their device), `reference/hig/designing-for-ios.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Gestures (visionOS: direct gestures), `reference/hig/gestures.md`; Apple, Get to know the new design system, WWDC25 session 356 (2025) (Structure, 10:10), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-356.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A camera app on a tall phone. Pass: the shutter button sits at the bottom centre, under the thumb of the hand holding the phone. Fail: the same app, with the shutter in the top corner.

### The device's settings hold here

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**What a person has chosen for their whole device (light or dark, larger or bolder text, more contrast, less transparency, less motion, dimmed flashes) holds in the app without being asked again, and the screen still reads and works under each choice.** "Avoid offering an app-specific appearance setting. An app-specific appearance mode option creates more work for people because they have to adjust more than one setting to get the appearance they want. Worse, they may think your app is broken because it doesn't respond to their systemwide appearance choice." Apps should "Adapt seamlessly to appearance changes — like device orientation, Dark Mode, and Dynamic Type — letting people choose the configurations that work best for them". With Reduce Motion on, the app reduces "automatic and repetitive animations". A custom font responds "when people turn on accessibility features, such as Bold Text". Every colour must "work well in light, dark, and increased contrast contexts", and contrast is checked "in both light and dark appearances". The held "Type and contrast are the person's" says this for text size and contrast, and the held "Few settings, none interacting" is extended by it: an app setting that rivals the device's is one more setting to learn. The platform's floating controls follow three of these settings: with Reduce Transparency they turn frostier and show less of the content behind them, with Increase Contrast they become "predominantly black or white" with "a contrasting border", and with Reduce Motion the material "disables any elastic properties"; a see-through layer the app draws itself does the same. Under reduced motion a slide becomes a fade and a spring stops bouncing, as Apple lists "Tightening animation springs to reduce bounce effects" and "Replacing transitions in x-, y-, and z-axes with fades to avoid motion", and the change itself is still shown, since reduced motion "doesn't mean *no* feedback — it means a gentler, non-vestibular equivalent". The device's language is one of these choices, and the app's language follows it, never where the person happens to be: "Never rely on IP/GPS for language." Where the app runs as a web page, what the device draws with it, the status bar, the browser's bars and the scroll bars, follows the page into light or dark too, since with one colour for both "light mode gets a dark bar or dark mode gets a white one." Check: with dark appearance, the largest text, bold text, increased contrast, reduced transparency and reduced motion each turned on in the device's settings, does this screen follow, and does every word and mark still show? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Dark Mode (Best practices: Avoid offering an app-specific appearance setting; Ensure that your app looks good in both appearance modes), `reference/hig/dark-mode.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Designing for iOS (Best practices), `reference/hig/designing-for-ios.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (introduction, Adaptable; Vision: Strive to meet color contrast minimum standards; Cognitive: Be cautious with fast-moving and blinking animations; Allow people to opt out of flashing lights in video playback), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Typography (Using custom fonts: Implement accessibility features for custom fonts), `reference/hig/typography.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Color (Best practices: Make sure all your app's colors work well in light, dark, and increased contrast contexts), `reference/hig/color.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.3.3 Animation from Interactions, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/21/animation-from-interactions.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.3.3 Animation from Interactions (Intent), `reference/wcag/understanding/21/animation-from-interactions.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Accessibility (Limited-vision users), `reference/uswds/documentation/guidance/accessibility.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Managing accounts (Best practices: In general, avoid offering an app-specific setting for opting in to biometric authentication), `reference/hig/managing-accounts.md`; Apple, Meet Liquid Glass, WWDC25 session 219 (2025) (Principles, 18:22), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-219.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Materials (Liquid Glass: Only use clear Liquid Glass for components that appear over visually rich backgrounds), `reference/hig/materials.md`; Kowalski, Apple Design, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (14. Reduced motion & accessibility), `reference/kowalski/skills/apple-design/SKILL.source.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Content: Prefer language settings over location), `reference/vercel/README.md`; Kowalski, Feeling Native On Mobile, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (10. Status bar color doesn't match), `reference/kowalski/skills/mobile-native/SKILL.source.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Design: Browser UI matches your background; Set the appropriate color-scheme), `reference/vercel/README.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A banking app at night, with the phone set to dark. Pass: the app turns dark with the phone and has no theme setting of its own. Fail: the same app stays white and offers its own Dark theme switch three menus deep.

### The same app at every size

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**What the app does stays the same when its screen or window changes size or turns; only how much shows at once changes, and on another device it is still recognisably the same app.** "Keep functionality the same as size classes change, and keep layout changes recognizable and familiar to the platform. Don't change your app's functionality based on the space it occupies. However, you can change the amount of functionality that's visible onscreen as the amount of space changes." "People expect your experience to remain familiar when they rotate their device, resize a window, add another display, or switch to a different device." The held "Same job, same place on every screen" holds a control's place across screens; this claim holds what the app does across sizes. It works held either way up unless one way is essential, since some people's devices are fixed to a mount, as on a wheelchair. What belongs together stays together as the layout adapts, "When content is intentionally grouped, it should stay together, even as the layout adapts", and content and controls keep "consistent, predictable positions", so the person's context is kept as the device turns or the window changes size. Turning the phone does not change the size of the text by itself, as a phone's browser may otherwise enlarge it in landscape: "Prevent text resizing unexpectedly in landscape mode on iOS". Check: at this screen's smallest and largest sizes, can the person do the same things, and is anything possible at only one of them? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Layout (Adaptability: Design a layout that adapts gracefully and consistently; Size classes: Keep functionality the same as size classes change, and keep layout changes recognizable and familiar to the platform), `reference/hig/layout.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.3.4 Orientation, Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/21/orientation.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.3.4 Orientation (Intent of this Success Criterion), `reference/wcag/understanding/21/orientation.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Design principles (Promote continuity), `reference/uswds/design-principles/overview.md`; Apple, Get to know the new design system, WWDC25 session 356 (2025) (Continuity, 15:01), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-356.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Design principles (Flexibility: Preserve a person's context), `reference/hig/design-principles.md`; Freiberg, Web Interface Guidelines (2023) (Typography), `reference/freiberg/README.md`; Kowalski, Feeling Native On Mobile, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Baseline), `reference/kowalski/skills/mobile-native/SKILL.source.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A mail app on a phone. Pass: turned sideways, it shows the list beside the open message and keeps every action. Fail: the same app, where Archive appears only in the sideways layout, so held upright a message cannot be archived.

### Named for those who do not see it

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**Every control, and every picture that means something, has a name in words that a screen reader can speak and a voice command can call, so a person who cannot see the screen can still use it.** "Describe your app's interface and content for VoiceOver. VoiceOver is a screen reader that lets people experience your app's interface without needing to see the screen." With Voice Control, "people can interact with their devices entirely by speaking commands... To ensure a smooth experience, label interface elements appropriately." For every assistive technology, Apple asks that "your interface elements are appropriately labeled". An accessible interface "doesn't rely on any single method to convey information". The VoiceOver page itself is not on the shelf, so how a name is written there is unverified. A control's spoken name holds the words written on it, best at the start, so a person who says those words to work it by voice reaches it. An action repeated on every line is named with the thing it acts on, "'Change name' or 'Change date of birth'", since a screen reader user "might hear a row action link out of context and might not know what it will do". A picture that only decorates, or repeats the words beside it, is silent to the screen reader, and an icon with its words is read once as one control, since "Announcing a decorative icon is redundant and can be annoying." Every item in a list has a name no other item shares, so a screen reader "can refer to exactly one on-screen element". Things that form a natural group, such as the "details of a song or attributes of a message", are read as one, only the group takes the tap, and each part is worded "as short as possible while still conveying the element's meaning", since all of it is said in one breath. An action reached by touch and hold is named for what it does, such as Open context menu, since without a name a screen reader says only "Double tap and hold to long press", which tells nothing of what the hold does. Besides its name, each control tells the screen reader what kind it is, such as a switch or a button, and its state, such as On or Checked; the platform's own controls do this already, and a control the app draws itself is given the same. Check: with the screen reader on, what does it say for each control and picture on this screen, and could a person say each name aloud to tap it? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (introduction, Perceivable; Vision: Describe your app's interface and content for VoiceOver; Mobility: Let people use Voice Control to give guidance and enter information verbally; Support mobility-related assistive technologies), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.1.1 Non-text Content, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/non-text-content.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.5.3 Label in Name, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/21/label-in-name.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.5.3 Label in Name (Intent), `reference/wcag/understanding/21/label-in-name.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Summary list (Adding actions to each row), `reference/govuk/components/summary-list.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Icon (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/icon.md`; Android Developers, Principles for improving app accessibility (Label elements: Elements in a collection), `reference/android/principles.md`; Android Developers, Principles for improving app accessibility (Label elements: Groups of related content), `reference/android/principles.md`; Android Developers, Principles for improving app accessibility (Label elements: Decorative elements), `reference/android/principles.md`; Android Developers, Principles for improving app accessibility (Add accessibility actions: Make available actions understandable), `reference/android/principles.md`; Android Developers, Principles for improving app accessibility (Use built-in accessibility features: Apply semantics to custom components), `reference/android/principles.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A music app's toolbar with a screen reader on. Pass: each button is read aloud by name, Play, Next, Shuffle, and the spoken Tap Play presses it. Fail: the same toolbar is read aloud as button, button, button.

### Motion is never the only sign

Called for by: motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Whatever a screen says by moving, it also says while standing still, in words, a shape or a mark, so a person who has turned motion off, or was looking away, loses nothing.** "Make motion optional. Not everyone can or wants to experience the motion in your app or game, so it's essential to avoid using it as the only way to communicate important information." An accessible interface "doesn't rely on any single method to convey information". The held claims "Colour is never the only mark" and "A sound says its source" say the same of colour and sound; this claim covers motion, which none of the held claims does. Check: with motion turned off, does anything this screen said by moving go unsaid? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Motion (Best practices: Make motion optional), `reference/hig/motion.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (introduction, Perceivable), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A sign-in box after a wrong password. Pass: the box shakes and also shows a warning symbol and the line That password does not match beside it. Fail: the same box only shakes.

### Focus is seen, and seen whole

Called for by: keyboard. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where a person moves through a screen one item at a time, the item that has focus is plainly marked at every moment and is never hidden behind anything the app draws.** For a person moving this way, "knowing the current point of focus is critical", and where they cannot see it "they may not know how to proceed, or may even think the system has become unresponsive"; the mark "must not be time limited". The AA floor lets a sticky header, footer or banner cover part of the focused item, the AAA level lets it cover none, and AAA sets the mark's strength: at least the area of "a 2 CSS pixel thick perimeter" of the item, changing by at least 3:1 between focused and unfocused. A banner is not pinned over the content, "to make sure it does not cover or obscure any content which has a focus applied". Check: moving through this screen item by item, is each item marked as it is reached, at least as strongly as a 2 CSS px outline changing by 3:1, and does any bar, banner or panel cover it? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 2.4.7 Focus Visible, Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/focus-visible.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.4.11 Focus Not Obscured (Minimum), Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/22/focus-not-obscured-minimum.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.4.12 Focus Not Obscured (Enhanced), Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/22/focus-not-obscured-enhanced.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.4.13 Focus Appearance, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/22/focus-appearance.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.4.7 Focus Visible (Intent of Focus Visible), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/focus-visible.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.4.11 Focus Not Obscured (Minimum) (Intent of Focus Not Obscured (Minimum)), `reference/wcag/understanding/22/focus-not-obscured-minimum.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.4.13 Focus Appearance (Minimum area; Change of contrast), `reference/wcag/understanding/22/focus-appearance.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Cookie banner (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/cookie-banner.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Button (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/button.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Link (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/link.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A banking website's payment form, used with a keyboard. Pass: each field and the Pay button get a 2 px dark ring as Tab reaches them, and the page scrolls each one clear of the cookie banner at the foot. Fail: the same ring, but when Tab reaches the Pay button it sits wholly under the cookie banner.

### Focus can always leave

Called for by: keyboard. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where a person moves through a screen one item at a time, they can move off every item and out of every part by the same means they moved in, and where leaving takes anything else, the screen says how.** Focus "can be moved away from that component using only a keyboard interface", and if that takes more than the usual keys "the user is advised of the method for moving focus away". Holding focus inside a part is allowed only for a reason the person can see, as "when the user is inside a modal dialog or popover", and that dialog has its own way out. This is the focus-by-focus twin of the held "A way out in sight". Check: moving item by item, does focus enter any part of this screen, such as an embedded map, a player or a dialog, that the same keys cannot leave, and if leaving needs another key, is that key written there? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 2.1.2 No Keyboard Trap, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/no-keyboard-trap.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.1.2 No Keyboard Trap (Intent of No Keyboard Trap), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/no-keyboard-trap.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Modal (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/modal.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A property listings website with a map, reached by Tab. Pass: Tab moves into the map's zoom buttons and on out to the first listing below. Fail: the same map keeps Tab circling its zoom buttons, and only the mouse gets out.

### Arriving is not acting

Called for by: keyboard. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Where a person moves through a screen one item at a time, reaching an item never acts: nothing is sent, opened or moved until the person acts on it.** "When any user interface component receives focus, it does not initiate a change of context". The failures it names are "forms submitted automatically when a component receives focus" and "new windows launched when a component receives focus", so that "functionality is predictable as visitors navigate their way through a document". It sits beside the held "Going is not doing", for the moment before any control is pressed. Check: stepping onto each item on this screen without pressing anything, does any item submit, open, move the view or send focus somewhere else? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 3.2.1 On Focus, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/on-focus.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 3.2.1 On Focus (Intent of On Focus), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/on-focus.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A flight booking website's search form, used with a keyboard. Pass: Tab rings the Search flights button and Enter starts the search. Fail: the same form starts the search the moment Tab lands on Search flights.

### Repeated blocks are passed in one step

Called for by: keyboard. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**Where a person moves through a screen one item at a time, a block that every screen repeats, such as a menu or a header, can be passed in one step to reach what is new on this screen.** A sighted person ignores repeated material by looking past it, but "Users who navigate sequentially through content will generally have to navigate through repeated content on each page" unless the screen offers "more direct access to the primary content". "Small repeated sections such as individual words, phrases or single links are not considered blocks". Check: moving item by item from the top of this screen, how many steps does it take to reach its own content, and is there one step that skips what every screen repeats? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 2.4.1 Bypass Blocks, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/bypass-blocks.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.4.1 Bypass Blocks (Intent of Bypass Blocks), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/bypass-blocks.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Skip link (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/skip-link.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A news website's article page, read with a keyboard. Pass: the first Tab shows Skip to article, which jumps past the 30-link menu. Fail: the same page makes the reader tab through all 30 menu links on every article.

### A one-key shortcut can be turned off

Called for by: keyboard. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Where a single letter, number or symbol key does something by itself, the person can turn that shortcut off or change it to need a modifier key, or else it works only while its own control has focus.** People "may trigger them accidentally". Speech-input users are hit too, since their software "converts any speech into simulated key presses, which can lead to unintended activations". The remedies the criterion allows are "Turn off", "Remap", or "Active only on focus". The app's own shortcuts start from Command, as the platform asks ("Prefer the Command key as the main modifier key in a custom keyboard shortcut"), so no single key acts alone. Check: does any single character key act on this screen, and where is the setting that turns it off or makes it need a modifier? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 2.1.4 Character Key Shortcuts, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/21/character-key-shortcuts.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.1.4 Character Key Shortcuts (Intent), `reference/wcag/understanding/21/character-key-shortcuts.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Keyboards (introduction; Custom keyboard shortcuts: Use modifier keys in ways that people expect), `reference/hig/keyboards.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: An email website where the D key deletes the open message. Pass: Settings has Keyboard shortcuts: Off, and with it off D does nothing outside a text field. Fail: the same D deletes, with no way to turn it off, so a stray keypress while reading loses the message.

### What appears on hover stays until sent away

Called for by: keyboard. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where something appears because a pointer rests on a control or focus reaches it, the person can send it away without moving the pointer or focus, can move the pointer onto it without it vanishing, and it stays until they leave, send it away or it stops being true.** Content that appears this way causes trouble because "the user may not have intended to trigger the interaction" and "the new content may interfere with a user's ability to do a task". So it must be "dismissible", "hoverable" and "persistent". It covers "custom tooltips, sub-menus and other non-modal popups". On a touch-only surface the case does not arise. Nothing needed is told only on hover, and what appears is placed so it covers nothing the person is reading: "Don't rely on hover to communicate critical information". On touch, a tap leaves no hover look behind, so a hover effect is kept to a pointer that can rest, since "touch fires false hovers on tap", and whatever a pointer is shown on hover has, on a touch screen, "to live in press, position, or nothing". A tip waits a moment before it first appears, "to prevent accidental activation", and once one tip is open, "hovering over other tooltips should open them with no delay and no animation", which "feels faster without defeating the purpose of the initial delay", in a practice account that cites no study and gives no length for the wait. Check: for each tip, card or menu on this screen that appears on hover or focus, does Escape close it where it is, can the pointer move onto it, does it stay until then, and once one tip is open, does its neighbour open at once and without motion? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 1.4.13 Content on Hover or Focus, Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/21/content-on-hover-or-focus.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.4.13 Content on Hover or Focus (Intent), `reference/wcag/understanding/21/content-on-hover-or-focus.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Accessibility (Limited-vision users; Limited-mobility users), `reference/uswds/documentation/guidance/accessibility.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Tooltip (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/tooltip.md`; Kowalski, Animation Standards Reference, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Accessibility), `reference/kowalski/skills/review-animations/STANDARDS.md`; Kowalski, Building Animations in Expo, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (introduction), `reference/kowalski/skills/animate-expo/SKILL.source.md`; Kowalski, You Don't Need Animations (undated) (Perception of speed), `reference/essays/kowalski-you-dont-need-animations.md`; Kowalski, Design Engineering, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Tooltips: skip delay on subsequent hovers), `reference/kowalski/skills/emil-design-eng/SKILL.source.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A spreadsheet website's cell comment, shown when the pointer rests on a cell. Pass: the comment stays while the pointer moves onto it to copy its text, and Escape closes it. Fail: the same comment vanishes as the pointer leaves the cell on its way to it.

### Shaking is never the only way

Called for by: motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**Where moving the device, or a gesture made at its camera, does something, a control on the screen does the same, and the person can turn the motion off, unless the motion is itself the activity.** Some people cannot move the device "because the device is on a fixed mount (perhaps a wheelchair) or due to motor impairments". Others "may accidentally activate sensors due to tremors", and honouring the system setting that turns motion detection off can meet the second half. The held "More than one way to do it" asks for a control beside a gesture on the screen; this adds motion of the device and the switch that turns it off. Check: does tilting, shaking or waving do anything on this screen, what control does the same, and where is the motion turned off? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 2.5.4 Motion Actuation, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/21/motion-actuation.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.5.4 Motion Actuation (Intent of this Success Criterion), `reference/wcag/understanding/21/motion-actuation.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A notes app where shaking the phone undoes typing. Pass: an Undo button sits in the toolbar, and Settings has Shake to undo: Off. Fail: the same shake undoes, with no Undo button and no way to turn shaking off.

### A control and its state stand out at 3:1

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**What a person must see to find a control and tell its state stands at a contrast of at least 3:1 against what touches it. That includes its edge, the tick in a box, the ring of focus, and any part of a picture needed to understand the content.** Controls and meaningful graphics must be "distinguishable by people with moderately low vision". "Low contrast controls are more difficult to perceive, and may be completely missed". The threshold is not rounded: "2.999:1 would not meet the 3:1 threshold". An inactive control is exempt, and two states need not contrast with each other when they are not side by side. But marks like "the check in a checkbox" must contrast with what surrounds them. Where content scrolls beneath a control that floats over it, the control stands on a material and never directly on the content, since "Without that separation, contrast can suffer", and at rest, as when the app first opens, the content is placed clear of the floating controls: "avoid intersections between content and Liquid Glass. Instead, reposition or scale the content to maintain separation." Check: for each control on this screen, which line or fill says it is there and which mark says its state, and does each reach 3:1 against what it touches? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 1.4.11 Non-text Contrast, Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/21/non-text-contrast.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.4.11 Non-text Contrast (Intent; User Interface Components), `reference/wcag/understanding/21/non-text-contrast.md`; Apple, Get to know the new design system, WWDC25 session 356 (2025) (Structure, 11:02), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-356.md`; Apple, Meet Liquid Glass, WWDC25 session 219 (2025) (Principles, 17:21), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-219.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A to-do app's list with a checkbox on each row. Pass: an empty box has a mid-grey outline at 3.5:1 on white, and a done box is filled dark blue with a white tick. Fail: the same boxes have a pale grey outline at 1.5:1, barely seen on white.

### One direction of scroll at any zoom

Called for by: text. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**Content can be read and used at a width of 320 CSS pixels while scrolling only up and down. That width is a 1280-pixel window at 400 percent zoom. A part that needs two dimensions, such as a map, a table or a video, is the only exception.** Lines that run past the edge force people "to scroll back-and-forth to read line by line", which "can cause them to lose their place". The 320 CSS px width matched the "reported viewport width of small displays of common mobile devices". It holds on every display because the CSS pixel "is an angular measurement (1.278 arc minutes), not a length measurement". This sits beside the held "The same app at every size", which keeps what the app does; this one keeps the reading in one direction. Where the screen is narrow, a list of like records stacks each record with its own labels, and only a grid of numbers compared across columns scrolls sideways: "Directory lists are more readable if the rows display in a stacked layout." A layout runs in one column wherever it can, as USWDS asks for people with limited vision and limited movement ("Design with linear layouts"; "Prefer linear layouts"). Check: at 320 CSS px wide, or at the largest text, does any line on this screen run past the edge, and must the person scroll sideways for anything but a map, table or video? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 1.4.10 Reflow, Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/21/reflow.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.4.10 Reflow (Intent of this Success Criterion; Why specifically 320px and 256px?), `reference/wcag/understanding/21/reflow.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Table (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/table.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Accessibility (Limited-vision users; Limited-mobility users), `reference/uswds/documentation/guidance/accessibility.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A recipe website's method, zoomed to 400 percent. Pass: the method rewraps to the narrow width and is read from top to bottom. Fail: the same method keeps its desktop line length, so each line has to be scrolled sideways to its end.

### Wider spacing loses nothing

Called for by: text. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**Where a person widens the spacing of text, no word is cut off, overlapped or lost, and every control still works. The widths are lines to 1.5 times the font size, space after paragraphs to 2 times, letters to 0.12 times and words to 0.16 times.** When people "override author-specified text spacing to improve their reading experience, content is still readable and operable". The screen need not use these values itself: "Content is not required to use these text spacing values", only survive them. The criterion is worded for markup, and the person's need it serves holds on every surface. Check: with those four spacings applied, does any label on this screen clip, overlap another or spill out of its box? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 1.4.12 Text Spacing, Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/21/text-spacing.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.4.12 Text Spacing (Intent), `reference/wcag/understanding/21/text-spacing.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A train ticket app's fare button, Off-peak return £24.10. Pass: with wider spacing the label wraps to two lines and the button grows to hold it. Fail: the same label sits in a fixed-height button, and the price is cut off.

### Words as text, not pictures of words

Called for by: text. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**Words on a screen are set as text, which the person can enlarge, recolour, respace and have read aloud, never as a picture of text, except in a logo or where the look of the words is itself the information.** Text lets people "who require the text in a particular font size, foreground and background color, font family, line spacing or alignment" adjust it. "Logotypes (text that is part of a logo or brand name) are considered essential." The AA level allows a picture of text that the person can customise, and the AAA level allows one only for decoration or where the look is essential. Check: is any word on this screen part of a picture, and does it grow with the person's text size? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 1.4.5 Images of Text, Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/images-of-text.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.4.9 Images of Text (No Exception), Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/images-of-text-no-exception.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.4.5 Images of Text (Intent of Images of Text), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/images-of-text.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A supermarket app's offer, 3 for 2 on pasta. Pass: the words are text beside a photo of pasta, and they grow with Larger Text. Fail: the same words are baked into the photo and stay the same size.

### An instruction never rests on look or place alone

Called for by: text. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**An instruction never points to a thing only by its shape, colour, size, place on the screen, direction or sound; it names the thing as well.** Some instructions rely on "the shape or position of objects", such as "round button" or "button to the right", and some people "are not able to perceive shape or position due to the nature of the assistive technologies they use". The Understanding gives a failing example: "Events marked with a blue diamond are played on field A". The held "Colour is never the only mark" covers what a colour means; this claim covers how an instruction points. Check: does any instruction on this screen say round, red, on the right, below or at the beep, and does it also name the thing? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 1.3.3 Sensory Characteristics, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/sensory-characteristics.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.3.3 Sensory Characteristics (Intent of Sensory Characteristics; Examples of Sensory Characteristics), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/sensory-characteristics.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A photo printing app's order screen. Pass: the hint reads Tap Add to basket to continue. Fail: the same hint reads Tap the green button on the right.

### A status is heard without moving the person

Called for by: wait, errors. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**A message that says how an action went, that something is under way, or that something is wrong, shown without moving the person, also reaches a person using a screen reader, spoken without taking them from their place.** The aim is "to make users aware of important changes in content that are not given focus, and to do so in a way that doesn't unnecessarily interrupt their work". Its examples are "Searching...", "18 results returned" or "No results returned". The criterion is worded for markup; the person-level need it serves is kept here. It is the screen-reader side of the held "Every action is answered at once". Only a failure or an emergency breaks in on what the screen reader is saying, a success waits until it finishes, and a standing notice is left where it can be found, so the person is interrupted only for "Important messages that demand the user's immediate attention". Check: with the screen reader on, when this screen shows a result, a wait or a fault without moving focus, is it spoken, and does the person stay where they were? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 4.1.3 Status Messages, Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/21/status-messages.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 4.1.3 Status Messages (Intent of this Success Criterion), `reference/wcag/understanding/21/status-messages.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Alert (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/alert.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Tag (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/tag.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Table (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/table.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A shop app's Add to basket button. Pass: after the tap the screen reader says Added, 3 items in basket, and stays on the button. Fail: the same tap changes the basket badge to 3 in silence, and the screen reader says nothing.

### More than one route to a place

Called for by: navigation. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**A place in the app can be reached by more than one route, such as a menu and a search, or a list and a link. A step in a process is the exception, reached from the step before it.** "More than one way is available to locate a web page within a set of web pages except where the web page is the result of, or a step in, a process", since "Users may find one technique easier or more comprehensible to use than another". In a small app, links to and from the home screen "may be sufficient". Check: for each place in the app that is not a step in a process, how many routes reach it, and which place has only one? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 2.4.5 Multiple Ways, Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/multiple-ways.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.4.5 Multiple Ways (Intent of Multiple Ways), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/multiple-ways.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A library app's page for one book. Pass: the book is reached by Search and by browsing Genres. Fail: the same book page is reached only by drilling through Genres, then Subgenre, then Author.

### Speech and meaningful sound are captioned

Called for by: sound. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where a screen plays sound or video with speech, captions show what is said, who says it and the sounds that carry meaning, kept in time with the picture, live as well as recorded.** Captions "not only include dialogue, but identify who is speaking and include non-speech information conveyed through sound, including meaningful sound effects". They are not needed where the media is "an alternate presentation of information that is also presented via text" on the screen and labelled as such. The held "Said in text" asks that speech also be written; this adds the speaker, the sounds, the timing and the live case. Check: with the sound off, does each video on this screen show who speaks, what they say and each sound that matters, in time? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 1.2.2 Captions (Prerecorded), Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/captions-prerecorded.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.2.4 Captions (Live), Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/captions-live.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.2.2 Captions (Prerecorded) (Intent of Captions (Prerecorded)), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/captions-prerecorded.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A cooking app's how-to video. Pass: the captions read [Chef] Now fold in the flour, then [oven timer beeps]. Fail: the same captions give the words only, so the beep that says the oven is ready goes unseen.

### What is only seen is also said

Called for by: sound. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.
