**Where a screen plays video, anything that matters and is only seen, such as an action, a change of scene or writing on screen, is also said. That is done by a spoken description in the pauses, or by a full text version of the whole; where the pauses are too short, the video waits for the description.** The description "provides information about actions, characters, scene changes, and on-screen text that are important and are not described or spoken in the main sound track". Where "all of the important information in the video track is already conveyed in the audio track, no additional audio description is necessary". Level A takes either a description or a text version, AA a description, and AAA a full text version and a video that pauses for description. A screen reader that names every control does not by itself say what a video shows: a review of the four apps found for blind and deaf users warns that "accessible interface features do not automatically translate into accessible ... instruction, content delivery, or navigation design", and three of the four gave guidance that needs no sight; the apps were inspected by researchers, with no blind or deaf testers. Check: with eyes shut, what is lost from each video on this screen, and where is it said? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 1.2.3 Audio Description or Media Alternative (Prerecorded), Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/audio-description-or-media-alternative-prerecorded.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.2.5 Audio Description (Prerecorded), Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/audio-description-prerecorded.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.2.7 Extended Audio Description (Prerecorded), Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/extended-audio-description-prerecorded.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.2.8 Media Alternative (Prerecorded), Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/media-alternative-prerecorded.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.2.5 Audio Description (Prerecorded) (Intent of Audio Description (Prerecorded)), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/audio-description-prerecorded.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Accessibility (Visually impaired and blind users), `reference/uswds/documentation/guidance/accessibility.md`; Hall et al., Discoverability and characteristics of health and fitness apps for deaf/hard-of-hearing and blind/low-vision users, Frontiers in Digital Health (2026) (2.4 Accessibility assessment of fitness-focused apps; 3.4.5 Comparison of fitness apps; 4.3 Accessibility features of targeted fitness apps), `reference/studies/PMC13526966.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A furniture shop's assembly video. Pass: a voice says Slide the two short rails into the top panel as the hands do it. Fail: the same video shows the hands at work over music, with nothing said.

### Sound alone or picture alone has its text

Called for by: sound. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where a screen plays sound with no picture, or picture with no sound, a text version gives the same information: a transcript for the sound, live sound included, and a description for the silent picture.** Text works because it "can be rendered through any sensory modality (for example, visual, auditory or tactile)". For a silent video an audio track that says the same is also allowed. Check: for each recording on this screen without a picture, or without sound, where is the text that says what it holds? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 1.2.1 Audio-only and Video-only (Prerecorded), Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/audio-only-and-video-only-prerecorded.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.2.9 Audio-only (Live), Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/audio-only-live.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.2.1 Audio-only and Video-only (Prerecorded) (Intent of Audio-only and Video-only (Prerecorded)), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/audio-only-and-video-only-prerecorded.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Accessibility (Deaf users), `reference/uswds/documentation/guidance/accessibility.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A podcast app's episode page. Pass: a Transcript tab holds every word of the episode. Fail: the same page has only the audio and a two-line summary.

### Speech stands 20 dB above the background

Called for by: sound. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**Where a screen plays recorded speech over other sound, the background is at least 20 dB quieter than the speech, or there is none, or the person can turn it off.** The aim is that non-speech sounds are "low enough that a user who is hard of hearing can separate the speech from background sounds". Occasional sounds "that last for only one or two seconds" are exempt. "The value of 20 dB was chosen based on" studies of assistive listening systems and hearing aids that are not on the shelf. The criterion is written for recorded sound without a picture; this claim extends it to any recorded speech, beyond its wording. Check: in each recording on this screen where someone speaks, how loud is the music or noise beneath, and can it be turned off? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 1.4.7 Low or No Background Audio, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/low-or-no-background-audio.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.4.7 Low or No Background Audio (Intent of Low or No Background Audio), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/low-or-no-background-audio.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A language learning app's listening lesson. Pass: the speaker's voice sits well above a soft café murmur, and a Background sound switch turns the murmur off. Fail: the same lesson's café noise is nearly as loud as the voice and cannot be turned off.

### Every true answer has a place

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**A list of answers holds one for every person who meets it: none, not sure and their own words are among the options wherever they can be true.** On every question page, "allow users to answer 'I do not know' or 'I'm not sure' if they are valid responses". Once a radio is picked, the person cannot clear it, "Therefore, you should include 'None of the above' or 'I do not know' if they are valid options." A 'none' checkbox "makes sure they do not skip the question by accident". It comes last, after the word or, and its label repeats the question: "No, I will not be travelling to any of these countries." Where a list cannot hold everyone, "Always give the user the option to enter their own description". A question many people cannot answer at all is not asked: "Do not ask users if they have a bank account or building society account as not all users know this." The held "Each fork offers every goal" covers the goals a person brings to a choice; this claim covers the answers they can truthfully give to a question. Where an address is asked, a person with no fixed address, or who gets post only at a box, has a way to answer, since "Some people do not have fixed addresses." A list with no place for what the person did leaves their record untrue: in posts on X about popular diet and fitness apps, a mother asked "how to add breastfeeding" and found the app "didn't subtract my calories", and the authors trace part of the apps' inaccuracy to "limited options available for personalization and tailoring". Check: for each question on this screen, who could not truthfully pick any option, and what do they tap? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Question pages (When to use this pattern), `reference/govuk/patterns/question-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Radios (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/radios.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Checkboxes (Add an option for 'none'), `reference/govuk/components/checkboxes.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Equality information (Asking about ethnic group), `reference/govuk/patterns/equality-information.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Bank details (Do not ask users for their account type), `reference/govuk/patterns/bank-details.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Address (Physical address, What not to do; Considerations), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/address.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Radio buttons (When to consider something else), `reference/uswds/components/radio-buttons.md`; Sheen et al., Living well? The unintended consequences of highly popular commercial fitness apps through social listening using Machine-Assisted Topic Analysis: Evidence from X, British Journal of Health Psychology (2025) (Theme 2: The complexity of tracking calories through oversimplified algorithms for diet and physical activity; Discussion: Quantification of real-world activities and implications for accuracy), `reference/studies/PMC12541294.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A travel insurance app asking Do you have any of these medical conditions?. Pass: the last option, after or, reads No, I do not have any of these conditions. Fail: the same list has no such option, so a healthy traveller leaves it blank and is stopped.

### As exact as the person knows

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**Where a person may not know or remember an answer exactly, the screen takes it as exactly as they know it, a month and a year, or a year alone, and accepts an answer that is valid but incomplete.** "Allow users to enter an approximate date when you are asking them for information they may not know or may struggle to remember. For example, allow users to enter 'December 2017'". "If a user enters information that's valid but incomplete, accept it. For example, they might just enter their year of birth without the day or month." An exact-date field is the wrong control here: "Do not use the date input component if users are unlikely to know the exact date of the event you're asking about." Check: for each question on this screen, what would a person who half-remembers the answer enter, and is that accepted? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Dates (Asking for approximate dates), `reference/govuk/patterns/dates.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Equality information (Validation and error messages), `reference/govuk/patterns/equality-information.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Date input (When not to use this component), `reference/govuk/components/date-input.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A home insurance app asking when the boiler was fitted. Pass: it accepts 2019 on its own. Fail: the same field refuses anything without a day and a month.

### Every real value fits

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**A field takes every value real people have: it is long enough for the longest, it accepts every character in people's own names and addresses, and where the parts of a value vary from person to person, it is one field.** "Fields must be long enough to accommodate the names of your users." "Support all the characters users may need to enter, including numbers and symbols." "Not everyone's name fits the first-name, last-name format. Using multiple name fields mean there's more risk that a person's name will not fit the format you've chosen and that it is entered incorrectly." An email field must "accommodate up to 254 characters", the longest an email address can be. For addresses, separate fields mean "it's hard to find a single format that works for all addresses", and a single box lets people "enter an address in any format". The held "Any way a person would write it" is about the form of a value; this claim is about its length, its characters and how it is split into parts. A field for one part of a name takes up to 128 characters and as few as one, and none is required where a person may have only one name: "Do support names as short as a single character." Where a name is split, its fields are named given name and family name, not first and last, since "In Chinese, Korean, Hungarian, as well as other languages, the first name is the family name." An email address is checked only for characters before and after an @, and no hyphen, underscore, plus sign or full stop is refused: "Do not invalidate characters frequently seen in email addresses". An address the system made to stand in for the person's own, as Hide My Email does, is taken as their address and never refused or queried: "Always respect people's choices to use system features like Hide My Email". Check: what is the longest and most unusual value people will bring to each field here, and does it fit unchanged? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Names (Make sure the fields work for most of your users; Single or multiple name fields), `reference/govuk/patterns/names.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Email addresses (Make sure the field works for all of your users), `reference/govuk/patterns/email-addresses.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Addresses (When to use multiple text inputs; How a textarea works), `reference/govuk/patterns/addresses.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Name (What to do; What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/name.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Name (Considerations), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/name.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Email address (What to do; What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/email-address.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Privacy (Best practices: Be transparent about how your app collects and uses people's data), `reference/hig/privacy.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A food delivery app's account name. Pass: one Full name field takes María-José Ó Briain García. Fail: the same app has First name and Last name fields that refuse the hyphen and the accents.

### What the device holds fills in

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**Where the device already holds a value that a field asks for, such as a name, an address, an email, a phone number, a password or a code, the field says what it is for, so the device can offer to fill it in.** Marking a field's purpose "lets browsers autofill the information on a user's behalf if they've entered it previously", and for a password it "indicates to browsers and password managers what kind of password is needed so it can be entered for the user". The pages tie this to WCAG 2.2 1.3.5 Identify input purpose for names, addresses, emails, phone numbers and dates of birth. Pasting is already held, under "More than one way to do it"; this claim is about the values the device keeps for the person. Each field says what kind of thing it takes, so that the device brings the matching keyboard and its suggestions as well as any stored value: "When you specify a semantic meaning for a text input area, the system can automatically provide a keyboard that matches the type of input you expect, potentially using this information to refine the keyboard corrections it offers." The page names neither a one-time code nor a passkey; how the device fills those is unverified here (see Set aside). The device's filling of a password is always let through, with pasting the fallback where filling is not offered: "Verifiers SHALL allow the use of password managers and autofill functionality. Verifiers SHOULD permit claimants to use the 'paste' function when entering a password to facilitate password manager use when password autofill APIs are unavailable." The platform names its own Password AutoFill over any scheme of the app's own. People new to an app say the same of what it could record for them: in think-aloud sessions with 20 adults, they were "particularly annoyed when they were asked to manually enter information about themselves", a stated preference, not a measured cost. A field that holds none of these, such as a search box, is marked so too, so the device does not offer a password or a stored code there: for "inputs like “Search” avoid reserved names (e.g., password)". Check: for each field here that asks for something the device stores, does the device offer to fill it in? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Text input (Use the autocomplete attribute), `reference/govuk/components/text-input.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Password input (Use the autocomplete attribute), `reference/govuk/components/password-input.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Names (Use the autocomplete attribute on name fields), `reference/govuk/patterns/names.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Email addresses (Help users to enter a valid email address), `reference/govuk/patterns/email-addresses.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Name (What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/name.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Email address (What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/email-address.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Phone number (What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/phone-number.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Address (Physical address, What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/address.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Virtual keyboards (Best practices: Choose a keyboard that matches the type of content people are editing), `reference/hig/virtual-keyboards.md`; NIST SP 800-63B-4 (2025), Authenticator and Verifier Requirements (Password Verifiers); Customer Experience Considerations (Usability Considerations by Authenticator Type, Passwords), `reference/nist/sp800-63b.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Privacy (Protecting data: Avoid inventing custom authentication schemes), `reference/hig/privacy.md`; Baretta et al., Exploring Users' Experiences of the Uptake and Adoption of Physical Activity Apps: Longitudinal Qualitative Study, JMIR mHealth and uHealth (2019) (Methods: Participants; Results: A Fair and Simple User Experience, Baseline), `reference/studies/PMC6384536.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Forms: Don’t trigger password managers for non-auth fields), `reference/vercel/README.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A taxi app's sign-up. Pass: tapping Email offers the address the phone has stored, above the keyboard. Fail: the same field offers nothing, so the address is typed out in full.

### What may be needed elsewhere can be copied

Called for by: numbers. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**A value the person may need to use somewhere else, such as an account number, a reference, an email address or a phone number, is written out in full as text they can copy, and an email address or a phone number can also be tapped to write or call.** USWDS: "write out email addresses and phone numbers so users can read or copy this information without selecting the link", with the country code included "to support international users". A value the person cannot change is still not locked away from them: a disabled field "cannot be edited or reached with the Tab key", so "Use read-only behavior when users need to review or copy a value." The pages rest on guidance. The held "Grouped in the person's own units" helps a person copy a code by eye; this claim lets the device copy it. A control's own words are not selected by a long press, which on a phone highlights them and raises the copy menu over the control, while the content around it stays selectable, since "Users copy addresses, error messages, and order numbers; that is content." Check: which values on this screen would a person need to paste or dial elsewhere, and can each be copied, or tapped to use, without retyping, and does a long press on a button select its words? Sources: U.S. Web Design System, Link (Usability should: Write out email and phone links; Encode email and phone links), `reference/uswds/components/link.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Text input (Accessibility: Choose disabled or read-only behavior deliberately), `reference/uswds/components/text-input.md`; Kowalski, Feeling Native On Mobile, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (8. Long-press selects button text), `reference/kowalski/skills/mobile-native/SKILL.source.md`; Freiberg, Web Interface Guidelines (2023) (Interactivity), `reference/freiberg/README.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A bank app's account details. Pass: the account number shows in full with Copy beside it. Fail: the same number sits in a greyed, disabled field that cannot be selected.

### The plain way stays when the help is gone

Called for by: generated, wait. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**Where a feature rests on something that may be missing, a model, a network, a capable device or the person's choice to use it, the task it helps with can still be done on the screen without it; the helped way sits beside the plain way, never in its place.** Apple: "Ensure a great experience even when generative features aren't available or people opt not to use them", and "When possible, consider offering a non-AI fallback." Its examples are of help added to a way that stands without it: "Genmoji offers a fun way to create new, original emoji, but people can still use regular emoji too", and the keyboard's suggestions are complementary because "the keyboard still works without these suggestions". The help may be missing for reasons outside the person's hands: "device compatibility, network access, and battery level." The held "More than one way to do it" keeps several kinds of input open; this claim keeps the task open when an assisting feature is not there. The pages are platform guidance and cite no study. The person's choice not to use the help is made on the screen at any moment: Nielsen asks for "easy invocation and dismissal" of an assistant, "a prominent 'cancel' or 'turn off' button", and where the help cannot do what was asked, "don't leave the user stranded — always suggest a next step"; this is practice and cites no study. A founder of a design tool keeps direct work beside the prompt for a reason of his own, that "you can't filter all of creation through the lens of AI" and that "it's very hard to prompt and say, 'I want the curve of the animation to be exactly like this'", so the helped way is also not the only precise way. Check: take away each helped way on this screen in turn, the suggestions, the network, the model, the person's yes to it; can the person still reach their goal here, and by what? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Best practices: Ensure a great experience even when generative features aren't available or people opt not to use them), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Models and datasets: Thoughtfully evaluate model capabilities), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (The role of machine learning in your app: Critical or complementary), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Nielsen, Hello AI Agents: Goodbye UI Design, RIP Accessibility (2025) (Agent Usability and Design Guidelines), `reference/essays/nielsen-ai-agents.md`; Field, interviewed by Thompson, An Interview with Figma CEO Dylan Field About Design and AI, Stratechery (2026) (Work in Figma; Code on the Canvas), `reference/interviews/field-figma.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A photo app's search by what is in a picture. Pass: on a phone that cannot run the feature, photos are still found by date, place and album. Fail: the same app's Search tab shows only Not supported on this device.

## Simplicity: Be clear and direct.

### A control sits by what it changes

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**A control sits next to the thing it changes, controls of one thing sit together, and its direction matches its effect, up for more.** Natural mapping, in Norman's word, is spatial correspondence between a control and its result; grouping and proximity say which controls belong together; a layout that matches no layout of what it controls is learned by rote and forgotten. Items are grouped by spacing them closer to each other than to anything else, without boxes or borders, and controls that belong together but sit too far apart are read as two sets. A control that shows no value of its own, as a stepper shows none, sits beside the value it changes, so the person can see which value they are changing. Controls in a bar are grouped "by function and frequency", related actions together, and a word and a picture are not put on one shared background, as Select beside the share icon, "since it could be perceived as a single button"; a control labelled in words sits on its own. A bar that stays in view across the app holds only what serves the whole app, such as the controls of what is playing, and an action of one screen sits with that screen's content: "a checkout button, for example, belongs with the content it supports", since mixing the two makes it "harder to distinguish what's persistent from what's contextual." The same holds for dividing lines: a design team's account, practice that cites no study, found that borders and separators "had quietly proliferated across the platform, sometimes appearing without clear reason", and cut and softened them, since "Structure should be felt not seen". Check: for each control on this screen, is it next to the thing it changes, grouped with its kin, and does its direction match its effect? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 1, The Psychopathology of Everyday Things (Mapping), `reference/books/norman-2013/01-chapter-1-the-psychopathology-of-everyday-things.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 2, Our Vision Is Optimized to See Structure (Gestalt Principle: Proximity), `reference/books/johnson-2010/04-chapter-2-our-vision-is-optimized-to-see-structure.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Layout (Visual hierarchy: Group related items to clearly express related information or functions), `reference/hig/layout.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Steppers (introduction; Best practices: Make the value that a stepper affects obvious), `reference/hig/steppers.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Typography (Whitespace), `reference/uswds/components/typography/typography.md`; Apple, Get to know the new design system, WWDC25 session 356 (2025) (Structure, 9:22), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-356.md`; Apple, Get to know the new design system, WWDC25 session 356 (2025) (Structure, 10:32), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-356.md`; Aufmann and Heckel, A calmer interface for a product in motion (2026) (Structure should be felt not seen), `reference/essays/linear-calmer-interface.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A thermostat app's room screen. Pass: the up and down arrows sit right beside the 21° target, up raising it. Fail: the same arrows sit at the foot of the screen under the humidity reading, far from the target they change.

### Controls are read as the model

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**People read a screen's layout as the shape of the thing beneath, two separate controls as two independent things, so a screen never puts two controls over one coupled thing or one control over two.** The user's conceptual model is built from the system image alone, the screen, its words and whatever help there is, since the designer is not in the room; a layout that implies independence where the things beneath are coupled, two dials over one thermostat, cannot be set even by a user who knows the truth. Check: what structure do this screen's controls imply, and is it the structure the thing beneath has? Source: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 1, The Psychopathology of Everyday Things (Conceptual Models; The System Image), `reference/books/norman-2013/01-chapter-1-the-psychopathology-of-everyday-things.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A smart fridge app's temperature screen. Pass: a Fridge dial and a Freezer dial each cool their own compartment. Fail: the same two dials both drive one shared cooler, so turning Freezer colder also chills the fridge.

### Nothing to remember

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**A screen puts on itself what a user must know to act, a rule, a code, an order of steps, so nothing is learned by rote; the most effective help to memory is to make it unnecessary.** Knowledge in the world, in Norman's word, is read at first encounter and needs no learning; knowledge in the head must be learned and is gone when attention goes. A sequence learned by rote gives no hint when it goes wrong, so where an order of steps exists its reason is visible, as a structure the user can read. Knowledge in the world also helps the expert on a rare operation, or one returning after a long absence. Check: what must a user already know to use this screen, and which of it could the screen show? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 3, Knowledge in the Head and in the World (Knowledge Is in the World; Memory for Arbitrary and Meaningful Things; The Tradeoff Between Knowledge in the World and in the Head), `reference/books/norman-2013/03-chapter-3-knowledge-in-the-head-and-in-the-world.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Design Principles for Dealing with Error), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 7, Our Attention Is Limited; Our Memory Is Imperfect (Implications of Short-Term Memory Characteristics for User Interface Design), `reference/books/johnson-2010/09-chapter-7-our-attention-is-limited-our-memory-is-imperfect.md`; chapter 9, Recognition Is Easy; Recall Is Hard (Recall Is Hard), `reference/books/johnson-2010/11-chapter-9-recognition-is-easy-recall-is-hard.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A street-parking app's zone screen. Pass: the zones near the car are drawn on a map with their codes, to be tapped. Fail: the same screen asks for a four-digit zone code the driver had to read off a street sign and keep in mind.

### Three to five in mind, nothing carried

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**A screen asks a user to hold at most three to five things in mind, and anything they must carry, from one screen to the next or from a message to the act, is shown where it is used.** Working memory holds three to five items in practice and fades during any distraction, the more the longer it lasts; a number or a word told on one screen and wanted on the next is lost on the way, and a message that vanishes before the user acts on it was never delivered. The same holds the other way: a fact that arrives mid-task is entered where and when it arrives, the entry control at hand, since the less a thing is held the less it is misremembered. One way to combat a lapse is to minimize the number of steps, since many steps between the start and finish of an operation overload working memory. Miller's seven was too high: with unintended chunking disallowed the capacity is four give or take one, and the unit is the feature, so where the things held resemble each other fewer are held; instructions for a multistep operation stay on the screen while the steps are done, never closed to begin. Miller's own seven was the span for repeating back a list just presented, about seven decimal digits, nine binary digits or five one-syllable words (Hayes), counted in chunks, units the person has already learned, not in bits or in things on the screen, and Miller himself suspected that the sevens of judgement, attention and memory were 'a pernicious, Pythagorean coincidence'. Information a person must compare is not split across tabs, since "having to memorise the information and switch backwards and forwards can be frustrating and difficult". Something of the task survives a short distraction, but not enough to work from: in three online experiments of 48 young adults each, the kind of step done just before a single interrupting judgement was taken up again faster after it, and in 110 adults in a lab, accuracy after 30 seconds away stayed only a little above chance. In Song's study of seven listeners to recorded talks, a definition held on screen for at least seven seconds, a time set from average reading speed, then gave way to the next, and four of the seven wanted to go back to one: "There are too many definitions... I haven't finished reading and it disappeared... I cannot go back and check (the definition)." Check: what must the user carry in their head between two points of this change, and does any message here disappear on its own? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 3, Knowledge in the Head and in the World (Short-Term or Working Memory; Knowledge in the Head, How Pilots Remember What Air-Traffic Control Tells Them), `reference/books/norman-2013/03-chapter-3-knowledge-in-the-head-and-in-the-world.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Memory-Lapse Slips), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 7, Our Attention Is Limited; Our Memory Is Imperfect (Characteristics of Short-Term Memory; Implications of Short-Term Memory Characteristics for User Interface Design, Instructions), `reference/books/johnson-2010/09-chapter-7-our-attention-is-limited-our-memory-is-imperfect.md`; Miller, The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two: Some Limits on Our Capacity for Processing Information, Psychological Review 63 (1956), pp. 91 to 93 (The Span of Immediate Memory), p. 96 (Summary), `reference/papers/miller-1956-the-magical-number-seven.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Cognitive: Minimize use of time-boxed interface elements), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Tabs (When not to use this component), `reference/govuk/components/tabs.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Best practices: Avoid sending a notification that tells people to perform specific tasks within your app), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Hirsch et al., Examining the cognitive processes underlying resumption costs in task-interruption contexts: Decay or inhibition of suspended task goals?, Memory & Cognition (2024) (Experiment 1; Experiment 2; Experiment 3; General discussion), `reference/studies/PMC10896823.md`; Labonté and Vachon, Resuming a Dynamic Task Following Increasingly Long Interruptions: The Role of Working Memory and Reconstruction, Frontiers in Psychology (2021) (Results; Discussion), `reference/studies/PMC8247645.md`; Song et al., Breaking the Curse of Knowledge: Designing Personalized Jargon Support for Real-Time Online Meetings, arXiv (2025) (3.1 Initial Prototype of ParseJargon; 3.4 Design Implications), `reference/papers/song-2025-breaking-the-curse-of-knowledge.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A parcel locker app's pickup screen. Pass: the six-digit locker code shows on the same screen as the Open Locker keypad. Fail: the same app shows the code on the screen before, which closes as the keypad opens.

### The app does the arithmetic

Called for by: numbers. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**A user is never asked to compute what the screen can, and the screen shows the number a person acts on while the precise one is kept beneath.** People estimate and get on; machines do precision; a screen that leaves the user to add, convert or total puts the machine's job in the head. A date relative to today or to another date is offered as the person thinks of it, "'tomorrow' or '1 day before'", with the day of the week shown where it matters. Values shown side by side for comparing share one unit and one form, the app converting them, so no row counts in weeks where the rest count in days: "Take care not to vary units or formatting within the same column." Check: is there arithmetic on this screen a user does by hand, and where a rounded number is shown, is the exact one kept? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 3, Knowledge in the Head and in the World (Approximate Models: Memory in the Real World), `reference/books/norman-2013/03-chapter-3-knowledge-in-the-head-and-in-the-world.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 10, Learning from Experience and Performing Learned Actions Are Easy (Implications for User Interface Design, Let the computer do the math), `reference/books/johnson-2010/12-chapter-10-learning-from-experience-and-performing-learned-a.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Entering data (Best practices: Get information from the system whenever possible), `reference/hig/entering-data.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Designing for iOS (Best practices: With people's permission, integrate information available through platform capabilities), `reference/hig/designing-for-ios.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Dates (Asking for relative dates), `reference/govuk/patterns/dates.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Table (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/table.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A payments app's split-the-bill screen. Pass: it shows each of four people owes £23.50, the exact pence kept beneath. Fail: the same screen shows only the £94 total and asks each person to type their own share.

### What the answers settle is not asked

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**An option that earlier answers rule out is not offered, and a question they settle is filled in and shown, not asked; the four kinds of constraint, physical, cultural, semantic and logical, each cut the set.** Norman's logical constraint is the one piece left with one place to go; his semantic constraint is the option that makes no sense in the situation. A screen that still offers what the user has already excluded puts the exclusion in the head. What the person already entered in the same process is filled in or offered to pick, never asked for again. A file already given in the task is offered to pick again rather than asked for a second time. What a signed-in person gave on an earlier visit is offered again rather than asked afresh: "Assure that repeat website visitors who have logged in can auto-populate forms with saved information." A list cut by an earlier answer comes after that answer, with the answer in view, and two lists shown side by side never change each other's options, since people "often don't understand how choosing an item in one impacts another". What the person gave once to set a feature up is not asked for again, "Avoid asking people to participate in calibration more than once", what follows being learned from their use and their corrections. Check: for each question or list in this change, what do earlier answers rule out or settle, and is that cut or filled on the screen? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 4, Knowing What to Do: Constraints, Discoverability, and Feedback (Four Kinds of Constraints: Semantic Constraints; Logical Constraints), `reference/books/norman-2013/04-chapter-4-knowing-what-to-do-constraints-discoverability-and.md`; WCAG 2.2, 3.3.7 Redundant Entry, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/22/redundant-entry.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 3.3.7 Redundant Entry (Intent of Redundant Entry), `reference/wcag/understanding/22/redundant-entry.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Question pages (How it works), `reference/govuk/patterns/question-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, File upload (Let users reuse uploaded files), `reference/govuk/components/file-upload.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Create accounts (Make the sign-up process clear), `reference/govuk/patterns/create-accounts.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Design principles (Promote continuity, Practical actions), `reference/uswds/design-principles/overview.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Address (Considerations), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/address.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Select (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/select.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Combo box (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/combo-box.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Calibration: Avoid asking people to participate in calibration more than once), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A food delivery app's address form. Pass: once the postcode is entered, the town fills in and shows. Fail: the same form still asks for the town in a blank field after the postcode has settled it.

### Grouped by activity, and an exception keeps the activity

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**A screen's controls are grouped by the activity a user is doing, not by the kind of thing beneath, and a manual step taken as an exception mid-activity does not cancel the activity.** Device-centred controls send the user to one screen for lights and another for sound; activity-centred controls put what the activity needs in one place, and their known failure is the exception not foreseen, so raising the lights ends the talk. Well-designed devices package together the tasks an activity requires, making sure the work done for one does not interfere with the requirements of another. Check: are this screen's controls those of one activity, and if the user steps out for an exception, is the activity still there when they return? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 4, Knowing What to Do: Constraints, Discoverability, and Feedback (Activity-Centered Controls), `reference/books/norman-2013/04-chapter-4-knowing-what-to-do-constraints-discoverability-and.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 6, Design Thinking (On the Differences between Tasks and Activities), `reference/books/norman-2013/06-chapter-6-design-thinking.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A home cinema remote app's Watch Film screen. Pass: volume, pause and lights sit together, and raising the lights for a snack leaves the film playing. Fail: the same screen, but tapping Lights Up ends Watch Film and stops the film.

### Choices differ where people already look

Called for by: list. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**Each option on a screen is told from every other by a feature people already use to tell such things apart, and a new option that matches an old one on that feature is confused with it.** People keep only the description that distinguishes the choices in front of them, so two coins of one size are confused while two bills of one size are not; the feature is the one the user discriminates on, not the one the designer sees. Two options whose words both fit the same thing are one option, or are recut until the user's words separate them. Two concepts the people the app is for cannot tell apart are one concept and are collapsed, as a site's membership, subscription, access and entitlements were four to its developers and one to its users. Where a name must be shortened, the cut keeps the part that tells it from its neighbours, as an ellipsis in the middle keeps both the beginning and the end. Where the app offers several results of its own, each differs from the rest in a way the person cares about, as Maps offers "a route without tolls, a scenic route, or a route that uses highways", and where they look alike each carries "a brief description" with "particular care to highlight the differences". Where two things in one list may share a name, as two people both called Jo, each row also shows a second feature that tells them apart, such as an email address, since lists break "where the name is the only distinguishing field". Check: for each list on this screen, by what feature does a user tell each option from each other option, and does any new option collide with an old one on it? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 3, Knowledge in the Head and in the World (When Precision Is Unexpectedly Required), `reference/books/norman-2013/03-chapter-3-knowledge-in-the-head-and-in-the-world.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 4, Reading Is Unnatural (Poor Information Design Can Disrupt Reading, Information buried in repetition), `reference/books/johnson-2010/06-chapter-4-reading-is-unnatural.md`; chapter 11, Many Factors Affect Learning (We Learn Faster When Operation Is Task-Focused, Simple, and Consistent, Excess Complexity Due to Separate Concepts Being Too Similar), `reference/books/johnson-2010/13-chapter-11-many-factors-affect-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Lists and tables (Content: Consider ways to preserve readability of text that might otherwise get clipped or truncated), `reference/hig/lists-and-tables.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Multiple options: Prefer diverse options; Make options easy to distinguish and choose), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Kowalski, Breaking UI: Worst-Case Catalog, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Collections), `reference/kowalski/skills/break-ui/CATALOG.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A cloud drive's file list. Pass: two files show as Tax 2024.pdf and Tax 2025.pdf, the years kept. Fail: the same list truncates both to Tax 20…, cutting off the year that tells them apart.

### What the user came for comes last

Called for by: steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 5, Error prevention.

**Whatever a screen needs from the user is asked before the thing they came for is given, since people seldom forget the goal of an activity and often forget what comes after it.** Automated teller machines require removal of the bank card before delivering the requested money, capitalizing on the fact that people seldom forget the goal of the activity, in this case the money; walking off without the card had been such a frequent error. Of course, it is then possible to walk off without the money, but this is less likely. Check: what does the user come to this screen for, what does the screen want from them, and which comes last? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 5, Human Error? No, Bad Design (Memory-Lapse Slips), `reference/books/norman-2013/05-chapter-5-human-error-no-bad-design.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 8, Limits on Attention Shape Thought and Action (After We Achieve a Task's Primary Goal, We Often Forget Cleanup Steps), `reference/books/johnson-2010/10-chapter-8-limits-on-attention-shape-thought-and-action.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A cash machine's withdrawal. Pass: the card comes out first, then the cash. Fail: the same machine gives the cash first, then the card.

### Learned only what the activity needs

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**A screen asks the user to learn only what belongs to the activity as a person doing it already understands it; a demand that is the machine's, which the activity would not make, is resented and not learned.** Although people are unwilling to learn systems that appear to have arbitrary, incomprehensible requirements, they are quite willing to learn things that appear to be essential to the activity; let the conceptual model of the product be built around the conceptual model of the activity. People will tolerate complexity and the requirement to learn something new as long as the new things feel appropriate to the task. Check: for each thing this screen requires the user to know or do, would a person doing this activity without the app recognise it as part of the work? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 6, Design Thinking (Activity-Centered versus Human-Centered Design), `reference/books/norman-2013/06-chapter-6-design-thinking.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 11, Many Factors Affect Learning (We Learn Faster When Operation Is Task-Focused, Simple, and Consistent), `reference/books/johnson-2010/13-chapter-11-many-factors-affect-learning.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A coffee shop app's order screen. Pass: it asks for drink, size and milk. Fail: the same screen first asks for the barista station ID and an order queue type.

### Complex, not confusing

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**A screen may hold as many parts as its activity has, and it is wrong only where a part has no reason the user can see; the cure for confusion is a visible structure, not fewer parts.** Complexity is essential, it is confusion that is undesirable; someone else's kitchen looks complicated and your own does not, so the confusion is not in the kitchen but in the mind, and it is one of knowledge. The most important principle for taming complexity is to provide a good conceptual model: complex things are no longer complicated once they are understood. After two weeks of use, 17 adults still called ease of use a precondition, yet what they asked for was an app "organised in an easier way", as one put it, and features it lacked, not fewer parts; this is what they said, not a measured cost. Check: for each part of this screen a reviewer would call too much, can the user see why it is there and where it sits, and if not, is the fix a reason shown or a part removed? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 6, Design Thinking (Complexity Is Good; It Is Confusion That Is Bad), `reference/books/norman-2013/06-chapter-6-design-thinking.md`; Baretta et al., Exploring Users' Experiences of the Uptake and Adoption of Physical Activity Apps: Longitudinal Qualitative Study, JMIR mHealth and uHealth (2019) (Results: A Fair and Simple User Experience, Follow-Up; Discussion: Summary of Main Findings), `reference/studies/PMC6384536.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A car's climate screen with fourteen controls. Pass: the controls sit in labelled groups, Driver, Passenger and Fan. Fail: the same fourteen controls are spread over one unlabelled grid.

### A word is read in its list

Called for by: list. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**An option's words are judged in the company of the other options on the screen, never alone, since the same phrase is read differently depending on the list it appears in.** The word in which a character appears may affect how we identify the character, and our overall comprehension of a sentence or paragraph can influence what words we see in it; the same letter sequence can be read as different words depending on the meaning of the surrounding paragraph (Polish silverware among chores, Polish silverware among nationalities). The context includes other nearby perceived objects and even reactivated memories of previously perceived ones. Check: read each option on this screen beside the others and under its question; does any take a meaning there it would not have alone? Source: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 1, We Perceive What We Expect (Perception Biased by Current Context), `reference/books/johnson-2010/03-chapter-1-we-perceive-what-we-expect.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A train ticket app's ticket menu. Pass: Return sits beside Single and Open return, where it reads as a round trip. Fail: the same Return sits beside Refund and Exchange, where it reads as giving the ticket back.

### A label is nearest its value

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**A label sits nearer to its own value than to any other, and before it in reading order, so the pairing is seen without thought.** A mortgage calculator put the labels for calculated amounts below their values, and just as close to the value below as to their own, so proximity could not be used to perceive that labels were grouped with their values; to understand the table, users had to scrutinize it carefully and slowly figure out which labels went with which numbers. In a form at Elsevier.com the labels are too far from their fields to seem connected to them. A hint written inside an empty field is not its label, since it disappears once typing starts, so a separate label stays, and fields are spaced so each one's label is plainly its own. It governs a label paired with a value; a caption under an icon, a tab or a segment follows the platform's convention, as The platform's way for a control requires. A hint or an example of what to enter may sit inside the empty field, in the platform's own placeholder colour, as Apple shows a hint there and Android "an example of valid input", so long as the label stays above it; a hint too long for the field sits under the label as one short sentence, with no link in it. The options of a group run one under another, never along a row, since "A horizontal layout can make it difficult to tell which label belongs to which radio button." A list's first option, such as Select a state, is not its label either, and the label stays above the list. Check: for each label on this screen, is the nearest thing to it its own value, and does it come first? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 3, We Seek and Use Visual Structure (opening section, the mortgage summary, Figure 3.5), `reference/books/johnson-2010/05-chapter-3-we-seek-and-use-visual-structure.md`; chapter 2, Our Vision Is Optimized to See Structure (Gestalt Principle: Similarity, the Elsevier form), `reference/books/johnson-2010/04-chapter-2-our-vision-is-optimized-to-see-structure.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Text fields (Best practices: Show a hint in a text field to help communicate its purpose; Evenly space multiple text fields), `reference/hig/text-fields.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Segmented controls (Content), `reference/hig/segmented-controls.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Tab bars (Best practices: Include tab labels to help with navigation), `reference/hig/tab-bars.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Text input (Hint text), `reference/govuk/components/text-input.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Textarea (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/textarea.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Checkbox (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/checkbox.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Radio buttons (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/radio-buttons.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Select (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/select.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Combo box (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/combo-box.md`; Android Developers, Principles for improving app accessibility (Label elements: Editable elements), `reference/android/principles.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Color (iOS, iPadOS: Placeholder text), `reference/hig/color.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A loan calculator app's results. Pass: each label, such as Monthly payment, sits just left of its own amount, £820. Fail: the same labels sit under their amounts, as close to the next amount as to their own.

### Facts in labelled lines, not prose

Called for by: text. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**A set of facts is shown as labelled lines, one fact per line, never as a sentence that holds them, since structured text is scanned and prose must be read.** The structured presentation of a reservation (Flight, Depart, Arrive, each on its line) can be scanned and understood much more quickly than the prose presentation; the more structured and terse the presentation, the more quickly and easily people can scan and comprehend it. Prose text format makes people read everything. Each line holds a key and its value, and the rule between lines ties a line's action to its fact, which helps "users who zoom in on pages or use assistive technologies to magnify their screen". Facts in a table run long rather than wide, fewer columns and more rows, since "It's easier for users to read down a long list of rows than it is to read across a long list of columns." Check: which facts on this screen are inside a sentence, and could each stand as a label and a value on its own line? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 3, We Seek and Use Visual Structure (opening section, Figure 3.1; Visual Hierarchy Lets People Focus on the Relevant Information), `reference/books/johnson-2010/05-chapter-3-we-seek-and-use-visual-structure.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Lists and tables (Best practices: Prefer displaying text in a list or table), `reference/hig/lists-and-tables.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Summary list (How it works; Adding actions to each row; Removing the borders), `reference/govuk/components/summary-list.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Table (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/table.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Design principles (Earn trust, Practical actions), `reference/uswds/design-principles/overview.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: An airline app's booking confirmation. Pass: Flight BA117, Departs 09:40 and Gate 22 each sit on their own labelled line. Fail: the same facts sit in one sentence: your flight BA117 departs at 09:40 from gate 22.

### The shared words are factored out

Called for by: list. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**Where several lines or options share the same words, the shared words are said once and only what differs is listed, since repetition buries the word that matters and hides which line the eye is on.** The DMV's wordy, repetitive links slow users down and bury the important words users need to see; a revision that factors out needless repetition and marks only the words that represent options consumes less screen space and is easier to scan. If successive lines of text contain a lot of repetition, readers receive poor feedback about what line they are focused on, plus it is hard to pick out the important information, as Apple's keyboard options buried the one difference, the language, in repetition. The app's name is not written in its notifications, their buttons or its Home Screen actions, since the system already shows the app's icon with each: "Don't include your app name or any extraneous information in the title or subtitle". The same holds for pictures: where actions are closely related, "such as multiple ways to copy, don't repeat or tweak icons. Use the symbol once to introduce the group, and let text do the rest." Check: for each list on this screen, which words do all its lines share, and are they said once above the list instead? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 3, We Seek and Use Visual Structure (opening section, Figures 3.2 to 3.4), `reference/books/johnson-2010/05-chapter-3-we-seek-and-use-visual-structure.md`; chapter 4, Reading Is Unnatural (Poor Information Design Can Disrupt Reading, Information buried in repetition), `reference/books/johnson-2010/06-chapter-4-reading-is-unnatural.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Home Screen quick actions (Best practices: For each quick action, provide a succinct title that instantly communicates the results of the action), `reference/hig/home-screen-quick-actions.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Content: Avoid including your app name or icon; Notification actions: Provide beneficial actions that make sense in the context of your notification), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Apple, Get to know the new design system, WWDC25 session 356 (2025) (Continuity, 15:38), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-356.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A phone Settings list of keyboards. Pass: under the heading Keyboard layouts, rows read English, French and German. Fail: the same rows read Keyboard layout for English, Keyboard layout for French and Keyboard layout for German.

### Sections labelled, in rank

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**A screen is broken into sections, each labelled prominently with what it holds, and a higher section is shown more strongly than a lower one, so a person skips what is not theirs.** A visual hierarchy breaks the information into distinct sections, labels each section prominently and in such a way as to clearly identify its content, and presents the sections as a hierarchy, with higher level sections presented more strongly than lower level ones; it allows people, when scanning, to separate what is relevant to their goals from what is irrelevant instantly. Visual hierarchy is equally important in interactive control panels and forms, perhaps even more so. Few typefaces are used, since many obscure the hierarchy and hinder reading. The structure the type shows is also there in text for a person who does not see the type. A heading sits nearer the text it heads than the text before it, with at least one and a half times as much space above it as below: "Use at least 1.5 times the amount of whitespace above the heading as below it." A heading is marked as a heading for the screen reader, so a person can "navigate between headings instead of between paragraphs or between words". A sheet or panel that opens over a screen has a title of its own that a screen reader can say as it appears, never a generic one such as List or Details that another screen also uses. The ranks are the platform's own text styles, each a size, a weight and a line spacing made to go together, and where a difference of rank is too small for a step in size, the same style is set heavier, as Calendar sets its year and month, since "sometimes the sizes alone are not enough to highlight more subtle differences". Check: name this screen's sections; does each have a label a stranger could read, and is the strongest type on the highest one? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 3, We Seek and Use Visual Structure (Visual Hierarchy Lets People Focus on the Relevant Information), `reference/books/johnson-2010/05-chapter-3-we-seek-and-use-visual-structure.md`; chapter 4, Reading Is Unnatural (Design implications: don't disrupt reading; support it!), `reference/books/johnson-2010/06-chapter-4-reading-is-unnatural.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Layout (Visual hierarchy: Group related items to clearly express related information or functions), `reference/hig/layout.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Typography (Conveying hierarchy), `reference/hig/typography.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Typography (Conveying hierarchy: Minimize the number of typefaces you use, even in a highly customized interface), `reference/hig/typography.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.4.6 Headings and Labels, Level AA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/headings-and-labels.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.4.10 Section Headings, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/section-headings.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.3.1 Info and Relationships, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/info-and-relationships.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Typography (Whitespace), `reference/uswds/components/typography/typography.md`; Android Developers, Principles for improving app accessibility (Label elements: Headings within text), `reference/android/principles.md`; Android Developers, Principles for improving app accessibility (Label elements: Accessibility pane titles), `reference/android/principles.md`; Apple, The details of UI typography, WWDC20 session 10175 (2020) (Text styles, 16:45), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2020-10175.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Typography (Conveying hierarchy: Adjust font weight, size, and color as needed to emphasize important information and help people visualize hierarchy), `reference/hig/typography.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A hotel app's room page. Pass: bold headings Room, Price and Cancellation, with a smaller Breakfast heading under Price. Fail: the same page sets every section in one size, unlabelled, divided only by lines.

### The first thing sits first in reading order

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**What a person reads first sits first in reading order, from the top and the leading edge, since people often start there and look where they will act next; where the first thing is a control used often, it sits where the holding thumb reaches, the middle or bottom of a phone, not at the top by habit.** In Western societies, people tend to traverse forms and control panels from upper left to lower right; the current platform says no more than that "People often start by viewing content in reading order", from the top and the leading side, the side that a right-to-left language reverses; people usually read, on- or offline, from top to bottom, which is why labels below their values could not be read as theirs. Designers can use this predictability to position what matters near where they expect users to be looking. That order was measured on forms and control panels read on a desk; a phone is held, and "it tends to be easier and more comfortable for people to reach a control when it's located in the middle or bottom area of the display", so the order of reading and the place of reaching are two questions, the held "The frequent within reach of the holding hand" answering the second. Moving from field to field, by keyboard or assistive device, follows the same order. The order a screen reader or a keyboard follows is the order of meaning. The first tab holds the section most people need. Where a menu opens from a finger, the commands used most sit nearest the finger, in reverse order when the menu opens above rather than below, since "people often read it starting from the part that's closest to where their finger or pointer revealed it." Check: what does the person read first on this screen, and is it first in reading order; and if the first act is a control used often, can the thumb reach it? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 6, Our Peripheral Vision Is Poor (Common Methods of Making Messages Visible, Put it where users are looking), `reference/books/johnson-2010/08-chapter-6-our-peripheral-vision-is-poor.md`; chapter 3, We Seek and Use Visual Structure (opening section, the mortgage summary), `reference/books/johnson-2010/05-chapter-3-we-seek-and-use-visual-structure.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Layout (Visual hierarchy: Order content by relative importance), `reference/hig/layout.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Designing for iOS (Ergonomics; Best practices: Support interactions that accommodate the way people usually hold their device), `reference/hig/designing-for-ios.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Text fields (Best practices: Ensure that tabbing between multiple fields flows as people expect), `reference/hig/text-fields.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.4.3 Focus Order, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/focus-order.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.3.2 Meaningful Sequence, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/meaningful-sequence.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Tabs (Order the tabs according to user needs), `reference/govuk/components/tabs.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Accessibility (Visually impaired and blind users), `reference/uswds/documentation/guidance/accessibility.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Context menus (Best practices: Aim to place the most frequently used menu items where people are likely to encounter them first), `reference/hig/context-menus.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A taxi app's home screen. Pass: the map fills the top, and the Where to? field sits in a card at the bottom, under the thumb. Fail: the same Where to? field sits in the top left corner, out of the thumb's reach.

### The least text that gets most people to the goal

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**A screen carries the least text that gets most people to what they came for; instructions are cut until only what moves people remains, and more is given on request.** Instructions can often be shortened with no loss of clarity: Jeep's Find a Dealer went from a paragraph with numbered instructions buried in it (2002) to three bullet points (2003) to one field and a Go button (2007). Too much text loses poor readers, who are a significant percentage of the population, and alienates good ones, turning the system into an intimidating amount of work; in instructions, use the least amount of text that gets most users to their intended goals, and let users request more detail if they want it. A hint under a question is for help "relevant to the majority of users" and is kept to "a single short sentence"; a question that needs a long explanation gets a heading that is a statement, the explanation, then the question itself as the label. Links inside running text are few and sit at the start or the end of a sentence, since "Links can be a distraction when users read blocks of text." Help is tied to "the precise action or task people are doing right now". A person who does not need it can "dismiss or avoid the help", and it says what a standard control does here, never how a standard control works: "Avoid bloating your help content by explaining how standard components or patterns work." Apple asks for "exactly the words you need to convey a concept or label a control", words that "avoid redundancy and get straight to the point." Check: for each sentence of instruction on this screen, which person would fail without it, and could the control's own look say it instead? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 4, Reading Is Unnatural (Much of the Reading Required by Software Is Unnecessary; Design implications: minimize the need for reading), `reference/books/johnson-2010/06-chapter-4-reading-is-unnatural.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Alerts (Content: Include informative text only if it adds value), `reference/hig/alerts.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Layout (Visual hierarchy: Use progressive disclosure to make layouts cleaner and easier to interact with), `reference/hig/layout.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Action sheets (Best practices: Provide a message only if necessary), `reference/hig/action-sheets.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Alerts (Content: Avoid explaining alert buttons), `reference/hig/alerts.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Segmented controls (Content: Use nouns or noun phrases for segment labels), `reference/hig/segmented-controls.md`; WCAG 2.2, 3.3.5 Help, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/help.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 3.3.5 Help (Intent of Help), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/help.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Question pages (Hint text; Asking complex questions without using hint text), `reference/govuk/patterns/question-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Text input (Hint text), `reference/govuk/components/text-input.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Link (Usability should; Research findings), `reference/uswds/components/link.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Offering help (Best practices: Let your app's tasks inform the types of help people might need; Avoid bloating your help content by explaining how standard components or patterns work), `reference/hig/offering-help.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Design principles (Simplicity: Be concise), `reference/hig/design-principles.md`; Apple, Principles of great design, WWDC26 session 250 (2026) (Simplicity, 11:51), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2026-250.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A library app's loans page. Pass: one button, Renew all, sits beside the due dates. Fail: the same page opens with a paragraph of numbered instructions on how renewing works before the button.

### Four at a glance

Called for by: numbers, list. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**A count a person takes in at a glance is four or fewer; more than four are grouped in fours or the number is written beside them, since beyond four a person counts, at about 300 milliseconds each.** Subitizing, determining the number of up to four or five items in the visual field, takes about 50 milliseconds per item and feels instantaneous; beyond four or five you are starting to have to count, at about 300 milliseconds per item, or, if the items fall into separate groups, you subitize each group and add; this is why tick-marks are written in groups of four with the fifth across (Card et al., 1983). Dots flashed for a fifth of a second were named without error up to five or six (Kaufman, Lord, Reese and Volkmann, reported by Miller), a limit of accuracy beside this claim's limit of speed, so four stays the line for a count taken without counting. Check: where this screen shows a count as things, dots, rows or ticks, are more than four ungrouped, and is the number written beside them? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 12, We Have Time Requirements (The Many Time Constants of the Human Brain, Time to subitize; Table 12.1), `reference/books/johnson-2010/14-chapter-12-we-have-time-requirements.md`; Miller, The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two: Some Limits on Our Capacity for Processing Information, Psychological Review 63 (1956), p. 90 (Subitizing), `reference/papers/miller-1956-the-magical-number-seven.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A pharmacy app's tablet tracker. Pass: seven doses left show as dots in a group of four and a group of three, with 7 written beside. Fail: the same tracker shows seven dots in one ungrouped row with no number.

### A tap is cheaper than a thought

Called for by: glance. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**On a screen used in short spells between other things, a path with one more tap and nothing to decide beats a shorter path with a choice on it, since a person will type more to think less.** Avoiding thought when using computers is important; people are willing to type more in order to think less; sometimes mindlessness trumps keystrokes: with software for casual or infrequent use, allowing users to become productive quickly and reducing their need to problem-solve while working is more important than saving keystrokes, whereas in software used all day by highly trained users every extra keystroke adds high cumulative costs. Even after some 15,000 practised trials, each doubling of known, equally likely options added 0.13 to 0.22 seconds to a choice, on top of 0.16 to 0.21 seconds to answer at all (Hyman, four subjects), while a practised reach cost about a tenth of a second per doubling of distance (Fitts), so even a choice among answers known by heart costs about what a tap does. The same founder names the opposite assumption as the builder's trap, that the person comes "in this calm state of mind where they're going to give it their full attention" and can string together "quite complicated sequences of maneuvers", when work for most people is "way more reactive and way more like chaotic". Check: where this screen saves a tap, does it do so by asking the person to decide something, and which kind of use is this screen's? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 8, Limits on Attention Shape Thought and Action (We Prefer Familiar Paths, Sometimes mindlessness trumps keystrokes), `reference/books/johnson-2010/10-chapter-8-limits-on-attention-shape-thought-and-action.md`; Hyman, Stimulus Information as a Determinant of Reaction Time, Journal of Experimental Psychology 45 (1953), pp. 189 to 190 (Procedure, Subjects), p. 192 (Fig. 1), `reference/papers/hyman-1953-stimulus-information-as-a-determinant-of-reaction-time.pdf`, PDF pages 1 to 3; Fitts, The Information Capacity of the Human Motor System in Controlling the Amplitude of Movement, Journal of Experimental Psychology 47 (1954), reprinted in Journal of Experimental Psychology: General 121 (1992), p. 264 (Experiment I, Results; Table 1), `reference/papers/fitts-1954-information-capacity-of-the-human-motor-system.md`; Stephenson, interviewed by Labenz, Calm AI for Crazy Days, The Cognitive Revolution (2026), `reference/interviews/stephenson-granola.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A parking app's start screen, used in a hurry. Pass: two taps, Start, then Confirm 2 hours. Fail: the same start saves a tap with one screen asking whether to pay by Quick, Saved or Custom tariff.

### See and choose before recall and type

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**Where the person's options can be shown, they are shown and the person picks; a field to type into is kept for what a person recalls without effort and a list cannot hold.** See and choose is easier than recall and type; show users their options and let them choose among them, rather than force users to recall their options and tell the computer what they want, the rule behind the GUI's replacement of the command line and Nielsen and Molich's "recognition rather than recall"; recall and type remains useful where users can easily recall what to type, as keywords into a search box. On a phone typing costs more than at a desk, so the balance tips further toward options to pick, since text inputs "are more difficult for mobile users". Where the person already has the number before them, typing it beats picking it: in the same 146 hospital staff, the keypad took 2.81 to 4.34 seconds against 5.13 to 8.35 to pick the same number on a wheel, at much the same accuracy. A correction of what the app guessed offers the likely alternatives to pick, as dictation offers "a list of alternative text completions from which to choose", with free editing beside them, since guided corrections "require less user effort". A field open to anything, such as a search or a request in the person's own words, has examples beside it to tap, "curated suggestions that make it easy to get started" and "diverse, predefined example inputs that hint at what's possible for a feature". A typed shorthand, such as an @ to name a thing, has a visible control beside the field that offers the same things to pick: a browser lets a person "use the @ symbol in the input to reference tabs, history, bookmarks and more" and also "manually attach any of these references from the menu UI at the bottom left of the composer"; practice that cites no study. A request written out in the person's own words is recall and type at its hardest: from literacy research Nielsen judges it likely that "half the population in rich countries is not articulate enough" to get good results from a chat box that is the only way in, an estimate and not a measure. A request people find themselves typing again is kept as a choice: a founder turned what practised users had learned to ask of their notes into "a prompt essentially that you can run at the click of a button", to make it "repeatable and very easy to trigger". Check: for each entry on this screen, could the options be shown and tapped, and where the person must type, is it something they recall without effort?
