Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 9, Recognition is Easy; Recall is Hard (Recognition versus Recall: Implications for UI Design), `reference/books/johnson-2010/11-chapter-9-recognition-is-easy-recall-is-hard.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Entering data (Best practices: When possible, offer choices instead of requiring text entry), `reference/hig/entering-data.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Text input (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/text-input.md`; Ehrler et al., Assessing the Usability of Six Data Entry Mobile Interfaces for Caregivers: A Randomized Trial, JMIR Human Factors (2015) (Results; Table 3; Table 4), `reference/studies/PMC4802909.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Corrections: When possible, use guided corrections instead of freeform corrections), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Transparency: Set clear expectations about what your AI-powered feature can and can't do; Inputs: Guide people on how to use your generative feature), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`; Deets, The strategy behind Dia's design (2025) (The novelty budget), `reference/essays/browser-company-dia.md`; Nielsen, AI Is First New UI Paradigm in 60 Years (2023) (Paradigm 3: Intent-Based Outcome Specification), `reference/essays/nielsen-new-ui-paradigm.md`; Stephenson, interviewed by Labenz, Calm AI for Crazy Days, The Cognitive Revolution (2026), `reference/interviews/stephenson-granola.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A translation app's language picker, where the languages can be listed. Pass: languages show in a list to tap. Fail: the same picker asks for a language code to be typed, such as fr or de.

### Visible in proportion to who needs it

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**An action most of the people the app is for will take is in view on the screen; an action few will take, and those practised, may sit behind a tap.** If software hides its functionality and requires its users to recall what to do, some percentage of users will fail, and with many users even a small percentage adds up to a significant number; make functions that many people need highly visible, so users see and recognise their options rather than having to recall them; functionality that few people will use, especially when those few are highly trained, can be hidden behind details panels, right-click menus or special key combinations. Content most people need is never folded behind a reveal, since "Accordions hide content from the user. Not all users will notice them or understand how they work", and there is evidence that some people avoid the link that shows more details, "as they think it will take them away from the page". What a person needs to finish a task is on its screen or over it, never behind a link that leaves, since in USWDS's test people with a goal "won't leave that page and follow a link elsewhere until they complete their task on the current page", and a tip shown on hover holds nothing needed to finish. On a phone the touch-and-hold menu is not where the rare is kept: it holds the commands an item most often needs, each also found in the main interface, and a rare action sits behind a tap in the main interface instead, such as a More button. A screen made bare by hiding its actions is not simple: "If you bury all your functionality inside a single place, that might make your interface look more minimal, but it doesn't make it simple", and "Simplicity isn't minimalism." Where a bar is too crowded, what is not needed is removed and the secondary actions move into a More menu, "to keep things clean and accessible". A control for a feature the person has not taken up may stay out of view until they take it up: a browser's profile selectors are "only shown if you are actively using a profile", since "Most people don’t use profiles, but people who do desire them to be very prominent"; practice that cites no study. A founder who tucks a feature for the few who "want to go deep and really optimize their workflow" behind the calm main view names the price of hiding it: "A lot of users haven't even discovered that, it's way too hidden"; what is put behind a tap is meant for those few. Check: for each action on this screen, how many of the people the app is for take it, and is the one most take the one most seen? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 9, Recognition is Easy; Recall is Hard (Recognition versus Recall: Implications for UI Design, The larger the number of people who will use a function, the more visible the function should be), `reference/books/johnson-2010/11-chapter-9-recognition-is-easy-recall-is-hard.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Designing for iOS (Best practices: Help people concentrate on primary tasks and content), `reference/hig/designing-for-ios.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Details (When not to use this component; Research on this component), `reference/govuk/components/details.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Accordion (When not to use this component), `reference/govuk/components/accordion.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Tabs (When not to use this component), `reference/govuk/components/tabs.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Progress easily (What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/progress-easily.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Link (Research findings), `reference/uswds/components/link.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Tooltip (When to consider something else), `reference/uswds/components/tooltip.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Button (When to consider something else), `reference/uswds/components/button.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Context menus (Best practices: Prioritize relevancy when choosing items to include in a context menu; Always make context menu items available in the main interface, too), `reference/hig/context-menus.md`; Apple, Principles of great design, WWDC26 session 250 (2026) (Simplicity, 11:20), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2026-250.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Design principles (Simplicity: Include just what's necessary), `reference/hig/design-principles.md`; Apple, Get to know the new design system, WWDC25 session 356 (2025) (Structure, 9:22), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-356.md`; Deets, The strategy behind Dia's design (2025) (Simplicity.), `reference/essays/browser-company-dia.md`; Stephenson, interviewed by Labenz, Calm AI for Crazy Days, The Cognitive Revolution (2026), `reference/interviews/stephenson-granola.md`. The appendix lists two kinds of common, how many users and how often, as Johnson (2007), Principle 4, Design for the common case.

Example, not part of the claim: A calculator app's keypad. Pass: digits and plus, minus, times and divide are in view, with sinh and cosh behind a swipe. Fail: the same keypad shows sinh and cosh and hides divide behind a More button.

### Already seen is recognised small

Called for by: list. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**A thing the person has seen before may be shown small, as a card or a thumbnail, and more of them at once, since recognition does not depend on size; a thing not yet seen is shown at full size.** Recognition is fairly insensitive to the size at which objects and events are displayed, since we must recognise things independently of their distance from us; as long as most of the same features are present, the new perception stimulates the same neural pattern; a great way to display pictures people have already seen is as small thumbnails, and the more familiar a picture, the smaller the thumbnail can be and still be recognisable; this lets people see more of their options, their data, their history at once. Check: which things on this screen has the person seen before, and are they shown small enough that the ones they might want are all in view? Source: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 9, Recognition is Easy; Recall is Hard (Recognition versus Recall: Implications for UI Design, Use thumbnail images to depict full-sized images compactly), `reference/books/johnson-2010/11-chapter-9-recognition-is-easy-recall-is-hard.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A music app's Recently Played row. Pass: album covers show as small thumbnails, a dozen in view. Fail: the same row shows each cover full width, one per screen.

### Said outright, not deduced

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**A screen says the fact itself, exactly, and never a clue from which the person must deduce it nor a set of options from which to work by elimination; what a control will do if changed is written on it.** Tell users explicitly and exactly what they need to know; don't expect them to deduce information; don't require them to figure things out by a process of elimination. "The software may be incompatible with a plug-in already on my computer": may be? is it or isn't it, and which plug-in? "Align icons horizontally": if I uncheck it, will they be aligned vertically, or not aligned? "Member ID": is that the same as my username? A button says whether it keeps what was entered ("'Continue' when the service does not save a user's information", "'Save and continue' when the service does save") and names what it sends ("'Change your tax details' or 'Send your claim form'"), and a list says in words whether one or several may be picked, since people should not be assumed to "know how many options they can select based on the visual difference between radios and checkboxes alone". A box or a switch is worded in the positive, "I want to receive a promotional email" rather than "I don't want to receive a promotional email", since "Negative language in labels can be counterintuitive." A field that needs a particular kind of value says so in its label before the entry, such as a mobile number that can take texts: "Do clearly state if you need an SMS-capable mobile phone number". Where a menu acts on several things at once, its title says how many, "reminding people that the command they choose affects all the messages they selected." The keyboard's Return key is named for what it does in this field, Search where it searches, "so the experience is consistent with other places people initiate search." An option that tells the app what the person wants of it says what will follow, as "Suggest less pop music" or "Mute politics for a week", so that it is clear "what happens when they choose the option". A control that opens a further view to ask for more before it acts ends its title in an ellipsis, as Apple's push buttons do on the Mac, since "an ellipsis in a control title signals that people can provide additional input", and a practice guide gives the menu item Rename… as its case; a control that acts at once has none. A bare pair of thumbs under a result, which Apple's page on generated content offers as "simple thumbs-up and thumbs-down buttons", stands only as a rating that changes nothing the person is shown; where the answer changes what comes next, the options say so in words, since "Avoid using an icon by itself, because it might not be clear enough to communicate granularity or consequences." Check: for each message and each control on this screen, is the fact stated, or must the person infer it from a hint, a hedge or what is left over? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 10, Learning from Experience and Performing Learned Actions are Easy; Problem Solving and Calculation are Hard (Implications for User Interface Design, Tell users explicitly and exactly what they need to know), `reference/books/johnson-2010/12-chapter-10-learning-from-experience-and-performing-learned-a.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Button (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/button.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Check answers (Make the page easy to understand), `reference/govuk/patterns/check-answers.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Radios (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/radios.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Checkboxes (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/checkboxes.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Checkbox (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/checkbox.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Phone number (What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/phone-number.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Context menus (Content: Include a title in a context menu only if doing so clarifies the menu's effect), `reference/hig/context-menus.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Virtual keyboards (Best practices: Consider customizing the Return key type if it helps clarify the text-entry experience), `reference/hig/virtual-keyboards.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Explicit feedback: Use simple, direct language to describe each explicit feedback option and its consequences), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Buttons (macOS, Push buttons: Append a trailing ellipsis to the title when a push button opens another window, view, or app), `reference/hig/buttons.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Interactions: Ellipsis for further input & loading states), `reference/vercel/README.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Generative AI (Continuous improvement: Let people share feedback on outputs), `reference/hig/generative-ai.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Explicit feedback: Add icons to an option description if it helps people understand it), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`. The appendix lists it as Johnson (2007), Principle 5, Don't make users reason by elimination.

Example, not part of the claim: A browser's page-failure message. Pass: it reads Ad Blocker 2 is stopping this page loading, with Turn Off. Fail: the same message reads An extension may be incompatible.

### Few settings, none interacting

Called for by: settings. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**A screen offers few settings, each with a meaning of its own, and never a set whose effects depend on one another, since people cannot find the best combination of interacting settings.** Minimize the number and complexity of settings; don't expect people to optimize combinations of many interacting settings or parameters, people are really bad at that; and a setting is a concept, each of which interacts with the others and multiplies what there is to learn. Check: which settings does this screen offer, what does each change on its own, and does changing one change what another does? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 10, Learning from Experience and Performing Learned Actions are Easy; Problem Solving and Calculation are Hard (Implications for User Interface Design, Minimize the number and complexity of settings), `reference/books/johnson-2010/12-chapter-10-learning-from-experience-and-performing-learned-a.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 11, Many Factors Affect Learning (We Learn Faster When Operation Is Task-Focused, Simple, and Consistent, As simple as possible), `reference/books/johnson-2010/13-chapter-11-many-factors-affect-learning.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A camera app's settings. Pass: Grid and Flash, each on or off and each acting alone. Fail: the same settings add ISO, shutter speed and exposure, where changing one changes what the others do.

### A judgement by eye, not a number to compute

Called for by: numbers. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**Where the person must judge how far, how much or where, the screen draws it as a length, a position or a shape read at a glance, rather than numbers to compare or compute.** Let people use perception rather than calculation; some problems that might seem to require calculation can be represented graphically, allowing people to achieve their goals with quick perceptual estimates instead of calculation; to reach the middle of a document, the editors of the 1970s made you look up the length, halve it and issue a command, while today you drag the scrollbar to the middle of the bar; snap-to grids and alignment guides eliminate the need to compute coordinates. Of the single features measured, position between two scale markers was judged finest, 10 to 15 positions (Hake and Garner; Coonan and Klemmer), against about five for the size of squares and about three for curvature, so what is drawn for judging is drawn as a position or a length on a common scale, not as an area or a curve. A slider sets a value only where its place in the range matters more than its exact number, as with a volume, its two ends are labelled with what they mean, and an exact number is typed instead: "Use a regular text input if a user needs to enter a precise number." A share seen at a glance is judged more closely from a plain ring, pie or bar than from a picture that fills as it grows: in 202 adults online, each shown a watch face for 5 seconds, plain charts were off by 8.98 percentage points against 13.50 for cartoon figures and flowers. Apple's principles ask the same of every screen: "Is there complex data that might be better understood as a graphic?" Check: where on this screen does the person judge one quantity against another, and is it drawn so the judgement is made by eye? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 10, Learning from Experience and Performing Learned Actions are Easy; Problem Solving and Calculation are Hard (Implications for User Interface Design, Let people use perception rather than calculation), `reference/books/johnson-2010/12-chapter-10-learning-from-experience-and-performing-learned-a.md`; Miller, The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two: Some Limits on Our Capacity for Processing Information, Psychological Review 63 (1956), pp. 85 to 86 (Absolute Judgments of Unidimensional Stimuli), `reference/papers/miller-1956-the-magical-number-seven.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Range slider (When to use; When to consider something else; Usability), `reference/uswds/components/range-slider.md`; Li et al., Exploring the Design Space of Glanceable Smartwatch Feedback Displays: Experimental Study, JMIR mHealth and uHealth (2026) (Methods: Experimental Task; Results: Stylization Negatively Affects the Accuracy of Progress Assessment), `reference/studies/PMC13448195.md`; Apple, Principles of great design, WWDC26 session 250 (2026) (Simplicity, 12:47), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2026-250.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A phone's storage screen, where the person judges what fills it. Pass: one bar is split into lengths for Photos, Apps and Messages. Fail: the same screen lists 41.2 GB, 18.7 GB and 9.3 GB of 128 GB as numbers.

### A choice costs its uncertainty, not its count

Called for by: list. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**The time a person takes to choose among options they know grows with how unsure they are which one is theirs, not with how many are shown: each doubling of equally likely options adds about the same time, so dropping one option from a long list saves almost nothing, and a list with one usual answer is quicker than its length suggests.** Hick fitted his own reaction times over 2 to 10 lamps as 0.518 log(n + 1) (p. 14), and Merkel's nine subjects as 0.626 log(n + 1) (p. 13). Information was gained at "of the order of five 'bits' per second" (p. 11), roughly a fifth of a second per doubling. Hyman's four men took 127 to 215 ms more per bit (Fig. 1, p. 192). The line was the same whether the bits came from the number of options, their unequal frequency or a sequential rule (pp. 190–193). Within a condition the usual option was quick and the rare one slow. For G. C., a light shown 13 times in 16 drew answers in 306 ms and each light shown once in 16 drew 585 ms (p. 194). So going from eight equally likely options to four saves what going from four to two saves, about a sixth of a second for these men, and dropping one option of ten saves about a seventh of that. The conditions are the papers': options known and practised for thousands of trials, a learned mapping, errors held near zero, at most ten options (Hick) or three bits (Hyman), and frequencies the person had been told (Hyman, p. 189). Hick warned that real options are seldom equally likely (p. 25). He also reasoned that a search through options one by one would grow in step with their number, not by the logarithm (p. 21). So this claim prices a choice among options a person knows, never a list they must read through for the first time. Check: for each choice on this screen, how many options are live for the person and how often do they take each, and where options were removed to save time, what fraction of the list went? Sources: Hick, On the Rate of Gain of Information, Quarterly Journal of Experimental Psychology 4 (1952), p. 11 (abstract), p. 13 (II, Theoretical Background), p. 14 (IV, Experiment I), p. 21 (VIII, Conceptual Models), p. 25 (IX, Discussion), `reference/papers/hick-1952-on-the-rate-of-gain-of-information.pdf`; Hyman, Stimulus Information as a Determinant of Reaction Time, Journal of Experimental Psychology 45 (1953), p. 189 (Statement of the problem; Procedure), p. 190 (Experimental conditions), p. 192 (Fig. 1), p. 193 (Comparison of the three experiments; The joint regression line), p. 194 (Discussion, Nonadditive combination of components within conditions), `reference/papers/hyman-1953-stimulus-information-as-a-determinant-of-reaction-time.pdf`, PDF pages 1 to 4.

Example, not part of the claim: A coffee shop app's drinks list, known to its regulars. Pass: all ten drinks stay listed. Fail: the same list is cut to nine to make choosing quicker, saving a few hundredths of a second and losing a drink people order.

### Steps to tell apart, not to save time

Called for by: steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**A choice is split into steps where one look cannot tell its options apart, never to make it quicker, since what the person must decide is the same however it is split and each step adds a fixed time of its own.** In Hyman's fitted lines every answer costs 160 to 212 ms before any bit is decided (Fig. 1, p. 192). Hick's log(n + 1) charges even a single option one unit, since the person must still tell that something has come (p. 13). One choice among eight equally likely options is three bits. Three choices between two are the same three bits plus two more fixed costs, and plus whatever each step takes to appear and to reach. That is arithmetic on the papers' lines. Neither paper tested a chain of choices, so the time half of this claim is inference, labelled as such. The reason to split comes from Miller. Where one judgment cannot tell the options apart, one way round the limit is "to arrange the task in such a way that we make a sequence of several absolute judgments in a row" (p. 90), as speech does with sequences of phonemes (p. 89). That is his argument, not an experiment he reports. A sequence brings in "memory as the handmaiden of discrimination" (p. 91), so earlier steps stay in view, as "The state and the place are on the screen" already requires. Check: where this screen splits a choice into steps, what does each step let the person tell apart that one look could not, and could the options it divides be shown at once? Sources: Hyman, Stimulus Information as a Determinant of Reaction Time, Journal of Experimental Psychology 45 (1953), p. 192 (Fig. 1), `reference/papers/hyman-1953-stimulus-information-as-a-determinant-of-reaction-time.pdf`, PDF page 3; Hick, On the Rate of Gain of Information, Quarterly Journal of Experimental Psychology 4 (1952), p. 13 (II, Theoretical Background), `reference/papers/hick-1952-on-the-rate-of-gain-of-information.pdf`; Miller, The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two: Some Limits on Our Capacity for Processing Information, Psychological Review 63 (1956), p. 89 (Absolute Judgments of Multidimensional Stimuli), pp. 90–91 (The Span of Immediate Memory), `reference/papers/miller-1956-the-magical-number-seven.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A clothes shop's size picker. Pass: all eight sizes, XS to 3XL, sit in one row. Fail: the same sizes are split into Small, Medium or Large, then a second screen for the exact size, to seem quicker.

### One feature carries about six levels

Called for by: colour, sound. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**A meaning carried on one feature alone, a shade, a size, a length, a loudness, a pitch or a buzz's strength, has no more than about five to seven levels a person names without confusion, however long they look; where more are needed, the person compares against levels shown beside it rather than naming one alone.** Pollack's listeners named tones. "When only two or three tones were used the listeners never confused them", but "with five or more tones confusions were frequent" (p. 83). The best to expect was "about six different classes without error" (p. 84). Loudness gave about five, saltiness about four (p. 85), sizes of squares about five, and hue 3.1 bits. A vibrator on the chest gave about four intensities, five durations and seven locations (p. 86). Across all of them the mean was 2.6 bits, about 6.5 categories, with a range of 3 to 15 (p. 86). Spacing did not help: five high tones and five low ones, each told apart alone, could not be combined into ten, "it does not work" (p. 84). Time did not help either: the observer had "as much time as he wants", and was told the right answer after each response (p. 83). The finest single feature was position between two scale markers, 10 to 15 positions (pp. 85–86). The first of Miller's three ways round the limit is "to make relative rather than absolute judgments" (p. 90), which he names without reviewing. The subjects were adults judging laboratory stimuli that varied on one dimension, mostly in studies Miller reports second-hand. Where the feature is a buzz or a sound that people pick up in use rather than by drill, about three levels is what to count on: Hoggan reports that for touch Geldard found "three levels is the most that can be expected without extended training", that "similar results apply to the audio modality", and that although Geldard's subjects told fifteen strengths of vibration apart in a lab, "realistically, Geldard recommends that three levels should be used"; her own alerts, played on add-on actuators rather than a phone's own, kept every feature to two or three levels for that reason. Check: for each meaning this screen carries on one feature alone, how many levels does it use, and where there are more than five, what is shown beside them to compare against? Sources: Miller, The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two: Some Limits on Our Capacity for Processing Information, Psychological Review 63 (1956), pp. 83–86 (Absolute Judgments of Unidimensional Stimuli), p. 90 (The Span of Immediate Memory), `reference/papers/miller-1956-the-magical-number-seven.md`; Hoggan, Crossmodal Audio and Tactile Interaction with Mobile Touchscreens, PhD thesis, University of Glasgow (2010) (chapter 2, 2.2.2.1 Tactons: Intensity; chapter 4, introduction; chapter 5, 5.1 Designing Multi-Dimensional Crossmodal Icons), `reference/papers/hoggan-2010-crossmodal-audio-and-tactile-interaction.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A home energy app's room usage map. Pass: usage shows in five shades with a legend beside. Fail: the same map uses twelve shades of orange with no legend to compare against.

### A rating scale of about seven points

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**Where a person rates an amount on a scale, the scale offers about five to seven points, and a finer scale is not presented as finer knowledge, since more points do not raise what a person can tell.** Miller: "psychologists have been using seven-point rating scales for a long time, on the intuitive basis that trying to rate into finer categories does not really add much to the usefulness of the ratings. Pollack's results indicate that, at least for pitches, this intuition is fairly sound" (p. 84). In Hake and Garner's study, observers either used any number from 0 to 100 or only the positions actually shown, and "the number of responses available to the observer had nothing to do with the channel capacity of 3.25 bits" (p. 85). This is Miller's remark, resting on pitch, together with one study of judging a pointer's position. No study of rating scales themselves is on the shelf. A slider for a rough amount moves in steps of a tenth to a fifth of its range, five to ten steps in all, to "prevent unnecessary precision and cognitive strain". Check: for each scale on this screen a person rates on, how many points does it offer, and does anything on the screen present a one-point change as if the person meant it? Sources: Miller, The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two: Some Limits on Our Capacity for Processing Information, Psychological Review 63 (1956), pp. 84–85 (Absolute Judgments of Unidimensional Stimuli), `reference/papers/miller-1956-the-magical-number-seven.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Range slider (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/range-slider.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A restaurant review app, where a meal is rated. Pass: the rating is 1 to 5 stars. Fail: the same rating is a 0 to 100 slider, and the restaurant's page treats 73 and 74 as different.

### Many kinds, several coarse distinctions

Called for by: colour, sound. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**Where a screen must tell more kinds apart than one feature can carry, each kind differs from the others on several features at once, each a coarse distinction, rather than on one feature finely graded.** A dot placed in a square, two dimensions to judge, was identified to 4.6 bits, about 24 positions, against 3.25 bits for one dimension, and pitch with loudness gave 3.1 bits against 2.5 for pitch alone (p. 87). Pollack and Ficks varied six features of a tone, five levels each, and listeners transmitted 7.2 bits, "about 150 different categories" (p. 88); eight features of two levels each gave 6.9 bits, about 120 (p. 89). Miller concludes that "by adding more dimensions and requiring crude, binary, yes-no judgments" on each, the span extends "from seven to at least 150" (pp. 90–91). The cost is that "as we add more variables to the display, we increase the total capacity, but we decrease the accuracy for any particular variable" (p. 88), so each feature carries few levels. Miller suspected a limit somewhere near ten features but had "no objective evidence" for it (p. 91). This claim sits beside "Choices differ where people already look": that one says which feature to use, this one says how many. Two events whose sounds differ only slightly can feel the same, so their haptics are made to differ coarsely on their own: Apple's left and right navigation cues on the watch sounded slightly different but felt "the identical pattern" until one was doubled, which gave "true contrast between left and right", in Apple's own practice, which cites no study. Haptics told apart by their count of taps differ by at least two taps, and a pattern that must be noticed opens with a light first tap: Apple's designers report, citing no study, that a quick run loses its first tap, so that "the sequence of four taps is actually reported as a triple tap only", and they use that first tap "to wake up the skin"; Hoggan's guideline of "up to 4 different rhythms each with a different number of notes or pulses", drawn from studies with add-on actuators rather than a phone's own, is kept within that. The three rhythms her alert studies used had two, four and six beats, each two more than the last, because "Using a different number of beats in each rhythm helps to make the rhythms distinguishable", and the tacton studies she took them from report them identified at 96.7 per cent. Check: for each family of icons, marks or sounds on this screen, how many kinds does it hold, and on how many features does each pair of kinds differ? Sources: Miller, The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two: Some Limits on Our Capacity for Processing Information, Psychological Review 63 (1956), pp. 87–89 (Absolute Judgments of Multidimensional Stimuli), pp. 90–91 (The Span of Immediate Memory), `reference/papers/miller-1956-the-magical-number-seven.md`; Apple, Designing Audio-Haptic Experiences, WWDC19 session 810 (2019) (Techniques, 22:12), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2019-810.md`; Apple, Designing Audio-Haptic Experiences, WWDC19 session 810 (2019) (21:03; 21:34), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2019-810.md`; Hoggan, Crossmodal Audio and Tactile Interaction with Mobile Touchscreens, PhD thesis, University of Glasgow (2010) (8.6 Guidelines), `reference/papers/hoggan-2010-crossmodal-audio-and-tactile-interaction.md`; Hoggan, Crossmodal Audio and Tactile Interaction with Mobile Touchscreens, PhD thesis, University of Glasgow (2010) (chapter 5, 5.1.1 Type of Message; 5.5 Discussion), `reference/papers/hoggan-2010-crossmodal-audio-and-tactile-interaction.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A metro app's map of twelve lines, more than colour alone can tell apart. Pass: lines differ by colour, a letter and solid or dashed. Fail: the same twelve lines differ only by twelve shades of blue.

### Grouped in the person's own units

Called for by: numbers. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**Where a screen breaks something long into groups for a person to hold or copy, each group is a unit the person already knows, a word, a year, a name or a familiar code, since a grouping they must translate as they go loses the next group while they translate the last.** "Immediate memory is limited by the number of items": about nine binary digits, about seven decimal digits and about five monosyllabic words (Hayes, p. 92), and it is the number of chunks, not of bits, that stays constant (pp. 92–93). Recoding, grouping items under a new name, raises the bits each chunk carries (p. 93), but only once it is fluent: Smith's twenty subjects studied their recodings "for about 5 or 10 minutes" and gained less than expected, because "the translation from one code to the other must be almost automatic or the subject will lose part of the next group while he is trying to remember the translation of the last group" (p. 94); Smith, drilled at length on himself, reached about 40 binary digits (p. 94). A chunk is the person's learned unit, not a group the screen draws. A code the person has not learned by heart, a reference or a security code, shows each character apart so it can be checked against its source; one they know, such as their own postcode, needs no such help. A reference the person must read or copy is shown in a typeface where look-alike characters such as 0 and O differ, "to ensure numbers and letters can be differentiated easily". Check: for each long thing on this screen a person must hold or copy, what groups does it show, and is each one a unit the person already knows? Sources: Miller, The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two: Some Limits on Our Capacity for Processing Information, Psychological Review 63 (1956), pp. 91–93 (The Span of Immediate Memory), pp. 93–95 (Recoding), `reference/papers/miller-1956-the-magical-number-seven.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Text input (Codes and sequences), `reference/govuk/components/text-input.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Keep a record (Optimize for print), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/keep-a-record.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Typography (Included typefaces), `reference/uswds/components/typography/typography.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A home router app's Wi-Fi key, which a guest must copy. Pass: the key shows as three words, maple tiger rocket. Fail: the same key shows as 4F9A 2C7E B013 in hex groups.

### A task over the screen is short and runs one way

Called for by: covers. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where a task is done in a view over the screen, it is short and runs one way, with no tree of further views inside it and no button that could be taken for the way out.** "If a modal task is too complicated, people can lose track of the task they suspended when they entered the modal view, especially if the modal view obscures their previous context." And: "presenting a hierarchy of views within a modal task can make people forget how to retrace their steps. If a modal task must contain subviews, provide a single path through the hierarchy and avoid including buttons that people might mistake for the button that dismisses the modal view." Apple names the failure "a modal experience that feels like an app within your app". The held "Over the screen, not in its place" says that the new view lies over the old one; this claim says how much may happen inside it. Check: how many steps does the task over this screen hold, can it branch into further views, and which of its buttons could be read as the one that closes it? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Modality (Best practices: Aim to keep modal tasks simple, short, and streamlined; Take care to avoid creating a modal experience that feels like an app within your app), `reference/hig/modality.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Modal (When to consider something else), `reference/uswds/components/modal.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Sheets (introduction; Best practices: For complex or prolonged user flows, consider alternatives to sheets), `reference/hig/sheets.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A contacts app's New Contact sheet, over the list. Pass: one form with Cancel and Done. Fail: the same sheet opens into Labels, then Ringtones, then Groups, each with its own Back and Close.

### One view over the screen at a time

Called for by: covers. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where a view lies over the screen, the person closes it before another opens on top, and two alerts are never shown at once, since each covered view is a context the person must hold in mind.** "People need to remember the context they were in before a modal view appears, so presenting multiple views adds to people's cognitive load, especially when a modal view hides another one by appearing on top of it." And: "Although an alert can appear on top of all other content — including other modal views — you never want to display more than one alert at the same time." Where something done in a view over the screen calls up another, the first closes before the second opens and may come back once the second is closed: "If necessary, you can display the first sheet again after people dismiss the second one." Check: can anything on this screen open a second view over one already open, and what can the person then see of the first? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Modality (Best practices: Let people dismiss a modal view before presenting another one), `reference/hig/modality.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Sheets (Best practices: Display only one sheet at a time from the main interface), `reference/hig/sheets.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A video call app with views that lie over the call. Pass: the Invite sheet closes before the Share Screen sheet opens. Fail: the same Share Screen sheet opens on top of the open Invite sheet.

### Going is not doing

Called for by: controls. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**A control that takes the person somewhere changes nothing, a control that changes something does not also move them, and the two kinds never share one group, so a person can look around without risk.** Apple says: "Use a tab bar to support navigation, not to provide actions... If you need to provide controls that act on elements in the current view, use a toolbar instead." Within a segmented control: "Don't assign actions to segments in a control that otherwise represents selection state, and don't show a selection state for segments in a control that otherwise performs actions." In a list row, an info button "doesn't support navigation", while a disclosure indicator "reveals the next level in a hierarchy; it doesn't show details about the item". The held "Alike in look, alike in kind" keeps kinds apart by their look; this claim names the kinds and keeps their effects apart. Changing a setting never takes the person elsewhere unless they were told beforehand that it would. Filling one part of an entry never moves the person on to the next by itself: "Never automatically tab users between the fields of the date input because this can be confusing and may clash with normal keyboard controls." A control that shows more in place does not look like one that goes elsewhere, since some people avoid the link that shows more details, "as they think it will take them away from the page". Picking an option never sends the entry or moves on by itself, since "Users often change their choices multiple times." A button that moves on to the next step and a button that acts on the screen in view differ in look as well as in effect. Check: does each control on this screen move the person, change something, or pick an option; does any do two of these; and does any group mix them? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Tab bars (Best practices: Use a tab bar to support navigation, not to provide actions), `reference/hig/tab-bars.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Segmented controls (Best practices: Keep control types consistent within a single segmented control), `reference/hig/segmented-controls.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Lists and tables (iOS, iPadOS, visionOS: Use an info button only to reveal more information about a row's content), `reference/hig/lists-and-tables.md`; WCAG 2.2, 3.2.2 On Input, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/on-input.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Date input (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/date-input.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Details (Research on this component), `reference/govuk/components/details.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Select (Usability; Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/select.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Combo box (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/combo-box.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Time picker (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/time-picker.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Memorable date (Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/memorable-date.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Date of birth (What not to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/date-of-birth.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Button (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/button.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A library app's bottom bar. Pass: Loans, Search and Account each only switch the view. Fail: the same bar's Loans tab also renews every loan when tapped.

### One or two things stand out

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**A screen gives its strongest mark (a filled button, a colour, a badge, a warning symbol) to one or two things, since each further one weakens them all and leaves the person weighing more before they choose.** "Keep the number of prominent buttons to one or two per view. Presenting too many prominent buttons increases cognitive load, requiring people to spend more time considering options before making a choice." Colour on controls is kept "for elements that truly benefit from emphasis"; "Refrain from adding color to the background of multiple controls." Badges are kept "for critical information so you don't dilute their impact and meaning". A caution symbol used "too frequently in your alerts diminishes its significance". The count of one or two is Apple's, for a view on its platforms. The rule holds across the app as well as on one screen: a strong mark used often stops working, since a second main button "reduces their impact, and makes it harder for users to know what to do next", interruption pages "get less effective the more often users see them", and inset text: "it's less effective if it's overused". Apple's principles put it as hierarchy, "When your hierarchy is strong, the most important item on the screen is always the most obvious one", and colour on the floating controls is kept for the primary action, since "When every element is tinted, nothing stands out, and it can be confusing." A view's primary action, such as Done, "stays separate and appears tinted". The rank runs across the whole screen and not only among its buttons: "not every element of the interface should carry equal visual weight", the parts central to the task "should stay in focus" and those that "support orientation and navigation should recede", so a sidebar that stayed bright "even after a user had reached their destination" was dimmed; a design team's account, practice that cites no study. Icons are marks too: where "their presence had grown excessive", the same team cut their number and size and removed "unnecessary visual treatments like colored team icon backgrounds". Check: what on this screen carries the strongest mark, how many things carry it, and is each one the thing a person most needs? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Buttons (Style: In general, use a button that has a prominent visual style for the most likely action in a view), `reference/hig/buttons.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Color (Liquid Glass color: Apply color sparingly to the Liquid Glass material, and to symbols or text on the material), `reference/hig/color.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Tab bars (Best practices: Use a badge to indicate that critical information is available), `reference/hig/tab-bars.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Alerts (macOS: Use a caution symbol sparingly), `reference/hig/alerts.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Button (Default buttons; Warning buttons), `reference/govuk/components/button.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Interruption pages (How it works), `reference/govuk/patterns/interruption-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Inset text (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/inset-text.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Button (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/button.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Tag (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/tag.md`; Apple, Principles of great design, WWDC26 session 250 (2026) (Simplicity, 12:12), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2026-250.md`; Apple, Meet Liquid Glass, WWDC25 session 219 (2025) (Principles, 17:21), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-219.md`; Apple, Get to know the new design system, WWDC25 session 356 (2025) (Structure, 9:56), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-356.md`; Aufmann and Heckel, A calmer interface for a product in motion (2026) (Don’t compete for attention you haven’t earned), `reference/essays/linear-calmer-interface.md`; Linear, UI refresh – Changelog (2026) (UI refresh), `reference/essays/linear-ui-refresh.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A car insurance app's home screen. Pass: one filled button, Start a claim, stands out. Fail: the same screen has five filled coloured buttons, each with a badge.

### Choices for one decision are seen whole

Called for by: list. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**Where the choices for one decision are offered together, in a row or over the screen, they all fit in view without scrolling or an overflow, so each is compared with the rest at a glance; where they cannot fit, they become a list.** "Avoid letting an action sheet scroll. The more buttons an action sheet has, the more time and effort it takes for people to make a choice." "When possible, avoid displaying an alert that scrolls." "The More tab makes it harder for people to reach and notice content on tabs that are hidden." "Too many segments can be hard to parse and time-consuming to navigate." The counts are the platform's. Segments: "no more than about five to seven segments in a wide interface and no more than about five segments on iPhone". On Apple Watch, an action sheet has no more than four buttons including Cancel, since "When there are fewer buttons onscreen, it's easier for people to view all their options at once". For a very large number of items, Apple points to "a list or table". The held "A choice costs its uncertainty, not its count" prices the time a choice takes; this claim is about seeing every option at once. Fewer than about seven options are shown at once as a list to tap, not folded into a menu, a menu is kept for about seven to fifteen, a longer list can be filtered by typing, and where several may be picked the options are always a shown list: "Use the select component only when a user needs to choose from about seven to 15 possible options". The platform's counts are firm: a touch-and-hold menu holds few commands, in no more than "about three groups"; the Home Screen offers no more than four of the app's own actions; and a notification offers no more than "four buttons". Where the app offers results of its own to choose among, it offers few, since "having more options increases cognitive load", and "When possible, list options on one screen so people don't have to scroll to find the right one." Check: for each group of choices on this screen, does every one show without scrolling at the largest text size, and how many are there against the platform's count? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Action sheets (iOS, iPadOS: Avoid letting an action sheet scroll; watchOS: Avoid displaying more than four buttons in an action sheet, including the Cancel button), `reference/hig/action-sheets.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Alerts (iOS, iPadOS: When possible, avoid displaying an alert that scrolls), `reference/hig/alerts.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Tab bars (Best practices: Avoid overflow tabs), `reference/hig/tab-bars.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Segmented controls (Best practices: Limit the number of segments in a control), `reference/hig/segmented-controls.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Pickers (Best practices: Consider using a picker to offer medium-to-long lists of items), `reference/hig/pickers.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Select (When to use; When to consider something else), `reference/uswds/components/select.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Combo box (When to use), `reference/uswds/components/combo-box.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Modal (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/modal.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Context menus (Best practices: Aim for a small number of menu items; Follow best practices for using separators), `reference/hig/context-menus.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Home Screen quick actions (Best practices: Create quick actions for compelling, high-value tasks), `reference/hig/home-screen-quick-actions.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Notification actions), `reference/hig/notifications.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Machine learning (Multiple options: In general, avoid providing too many options), `reference/hig/machine-learning.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: An e-reader app's sheet of five font choices. Pass: all five fonts show at once in the sheet. Fail: the same sheet shows three fonts and hides the other two behind a More button.

### A long list in an order people can predict

Called for by: list. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**Where most of a list is out of view, its items run in an order the person can predict, so they know which way and how far to go for one they cannot see.** "Before people interact with a picker, many of its values can be hidden. It's best when people can predict what the hidden values are, such as with an alphabetized list of countries, so they can move through the items quickly." For a very large list, a table "can include an index, which makes it much faster to target a section of the list". This rule is about locating a thing not yet seen. The held "Stays where it was learned", in which an alphabetic keyboard was no faster than a random one, is about a layout learned by heart, so the two do not meet. Options run alphabetically by default; an order from the most to the least common is used "with extreme caution as it can reinforce bias in your service. If in doubt, order alphabetically." Each option of a long list holds the words people look for it by, short form and full name together, as in "MD - Maryland", and the list can be narrowed by typing those words instead of scrolling. A long table whose columns hold names or numbers can be sorted by any of them: "Add row sorting to individual columns of long tables where the data can be logically ordered either alphabetically or numerically." Where every option is in view at once and none describes the person, the most used may come first; a list about the person, or one longer than the view, runs alphabetically. Check: for each list on this screen that is longer than the view, what order does it follow, and could a person say where an unseen item lies? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Pickers (Best practices: Use predictable and logically ordered values; Consider using a picker to offer medium-to-long lists of items), `reference/hig/pickers.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Radios (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/radios.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Checkboxes (How it works), `reference/govuk/components/checkboxes.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Combo box (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/combo-box.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Address (Physical address, What to do; Usability guidance), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/address.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Table (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/table.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Checkbox (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/checkbox.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Radio buttons (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/radio-buttons.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A car hire app's country picker, longer than the screen. Pass: countries run alphabetically. Fail: the same list runs in order of bookings, so Norway could be anywhere.

### The optional is marked, not the required

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**A question a person may leave unanswered says so in words in its label, and a required one carries no mark.** For optional information, "in most contexts, add '(optional)' to the labels of optional fields", and for a group of radios or checkboxes "add '(optional)' to the legend". "Never mark mandatory fields with asterisks." The exception is a field that people it does not fit will skip anyway: middle names are optional, but their label carries no mark, since "Users will enter their middle names if they have them and skip the field if they do not." Check: which fields on this screen may be left empty, does each say optional in words, and does any mark rest on an asterisk? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Question pages (When to use this pattern), `reference/govuk/patterns/question-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Names (Middle names), `reference/govuk/patterns/names.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A hotel booking app's guest form. Pass: Special requests (optional) is the only field with a mark. Fail: the same form puts a red asterisk beside every field except Special requests.

### One question to a step, to start

Called for by: steps, entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**Where a person answers questions in steps, each step starts by asking one question, and questions share a step only where use by people shows the grouping helps them.** "Asking just one question per question page helps users understand what you're asking them to do, and focus on the specific question and its answer." Grouping is allowed on evidence: "User research will tell you when you can group pages together", for example for people "who need to repeat and switch between tasks quickly". The page gives no measure of its own for the one-question rule; it points to the Service Manual. It governs a short run of questions: the parts of a long task may still be done in any order, as the held "No order the task does not have" requires. An answer in parts, such as an address in its several fields, is one question and one step, however long it takes to give. Apple asks the same of the simpler mode it offers people with cognitive disabilities, to break up multistep workflows so people can focus on a single interaction per screen, and The aid is the plain way gives that to everyone. Check: how many questions does each step of this screen ask, and where it asks more than one, what use by people showed that the grouping helps? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Question pages (Start by asking one question per page; Asking multiple questions on a page), `reference/govuk/patterns/question-pages.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Progress easily (What to do), `reference/uswds/patterns/complex-form/progress-easily.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Addresses (Multiple text inputs), `reference/govuk/patterns/addresses.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Cognitive: Optimize your app's UI for Assistive Access), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A car insurance quote app. Pass: one step asks only When did you pass your driving test?. Fail: the same step also asks for mileage, where the car is parked overnight and the driver's job.

### Each step is headed by its own question

Called for by: steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Each step of a sequence has a heading of its own that names what this step asks, never the section it belongs to and never a heading another step also uses.** "Do not use the same page heading across multiple pages." "The page heading should relate specifically to the information being asked for on the current page, not any higher-level section the page is part of", and the section, where it must show, sits above it as a caption. On a review page, "Use the page title to tell the user what they need to do – otherwise, they might miss the 'submit' button at the end of the page." The held "The state and the place are on the screen" gives each screen a title; this claim says what that title names. Check: read the headings of every step in this sequence in a row; are any two the same, and does each name the question on its own step? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Question pages (Start by asking one question per page), `reference/govuk/patterns/question-pages.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Check answers (Make the page easy to understand), `reference/govuk/patterns/check-answers.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Step indicator (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/step-indicator.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A mortgage app's About you section. Pass: its three steps are headed What is your name?, What is your date of birth? and Where do you live?. Fail: the same three steps are each headed About you.

### A question opened by an answer is one simple question

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**Where an answer opens a further question in place beneath it, that question is one simple question, and anything longer or in parts is asked on the next step instead.** "Keep it simple. If the related question is complicated or has more than one part, show it on the next page in the process instead." Only questions are opened this way: "Conditionally reveal questions only - do not show or hide anything that is not a question." The page names research: "we found that screen reader users did not have difficulty answering a conditionally revealed question - as long it's kept simple. Users we tested with did get confused when complicated questions were conditionally revealed to them, particularly questions with more than one part." Check: for each answer on this screen that opens something beneath it, is what opens one question, and is it a question at all? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Radios (Conditionally revealing a related question; Known issues), `reference/govuk/components/radios.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Checkboxes (Conditionally revealing a related question; Known issues), `reference/govuk/components/checkboxes.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A parcel app's delivery choices, where Leave with a neighbour opens a field. Pass: it opens one field, Neighbour's house number. Fail: the same choice opens fields for the neighbour's name, house number and phone, and a consent box.

### In the question's words

Called for by: errors. Cross-check: Nielsen 9, Help users recognize, diagnose, and recover from errors.

**A message about a refused entry uses the words of the question it belongs to, so the person matches the message to its place without searching.** "Error messages should directly include language from the question or fieldset label. This helps match up the error message with the relevant form field." The label "How many hours do you work a week?" gets the message "Enter how many hours you work a week". Check: for each message this screen gives about an entry, do its words repeat the words of that entry's label? Source: GOV.UK Design System, Error message (Match up error messages to labels), `reference/govuk/components/error-message.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A bank app's transfer form, with a Reference field. Pass: left empty, it reads Enter a reference for the payment. Fail: the same field reads This field is required.

### Each way of being wrong has its own message

Called for by: errors. Cross-check: Nielsen 9, Help users recognize, diagnose, and recover from errors.

**Each way an entry can be wrong has its own message saying so: empty, too long, too short, a character not allowed, the wrong form or an impossible value. Where an entry is wrong in more than one way at once, the most basic is told first.** "General errors are not helpful to everyone. They do not make sense out of context", so messages like "'An error occurred'" or "'This field is required'" are avoided, and "Different errors need different messages." Where a date has several problems, the page says "show the highest priority error message": first missing or incomplete information, then information that cannot be correct, then anything else. The page names research: messages written this way "have been tested with all types of users in live services", and research showed that users "understood what went wrong", "knew how to fix the problem" and "were able to recover from the error". Precise means in the person's terms, not the code's: a message exact to how the app was built can still be one the person cannot follow, since their picture of the app differs from its code. Check: list every way each entry on this screen can be refused; does each have its own message, and which one shows when two apply? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Error message (Be specific; Research on this component), `reference/govuk/components/error-message.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Date input (Error messages), `reference/govuk/components/date-input.md`; Neusesser and Sunwall, Error-Message Guidelines, Nielsen Norman Group (2023, updated 2024) (Communication Guidelines: Concisely and precisely describe the issue), `reference/nngroup/error-message-guidelines.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A flight booking app's travel date. Pass: 31 February gets Travel date must be a real date, and an empty field gets Enter a travel date. Fail: the same two entries both get Invalid date.

### The refusals are listed where the screen begins

Called for by: errors. Cross-check: Nielsen 9, Help users recognize, diagnose, and recover from errors.

**Where a screen asks for several entries and refuses some, a list at its top names every refusal in the same words as beside the entry, each item leads to its entry, and the person is taken to the list.** "Summarise all errors at the top of the page the user is on". The summary must "link to each of the answers that have validation errors", and keyboard focus moves to it. Its messages are "worded the same as those which appear next to the inputs with errors", so that they "look, sound and mean the same", "make sense out of context" and "reduce the cognitive effort needed to understand what has happened". GOV.UK shows the summary "even if there's only one" error; on a screen of one short field, the message beside the field already does the job. The list is never the only mark: each entry carries its own message too, since a list alone sends the person looking for the field, and by the time they reach it the list may have scrolled away. Check: send this screen with two entries wrong, one of them out of view; what does the person see first, and does each item take them to its entry? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Error summary (When to use this component; How it works; Linking from the error summary to each answer), `reference/govuk/components/error-summary.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Error message (Be consistent), `reference/govuk/components/error-message.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Recover from validation errors (How to tell the user about validation errors), `reference/govuk/patterns/validation.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Alert (When to consider something else), `reference/uswds/components/alert.md`; Krause, 10 Design Guidelines for Reporting Errors in Forms, Nielsen Norman Group (2019, updated 2024) (8. Don't Use Validation Summaries as the Only Indication of an Error), `reference/nngroup/errors-forms-design-guidelines.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A flat rental app's application form, longer than the screen. Pass: Submit with two gaps scrolls to a box at the top, There is a problem, listing Enter your employer and Enter your monthly income, each a link to its field. Fail: the same form outlines the two fields in red, one below the fold, and shows nothing at the top.

### Leaving the app to confirm is the last resort

Called for by: account. Cross-check: Nielsen 7, Flexibility and efficiency of use.

**A step that sends the person out of the app and back again, to an email or a text message, is used only where the task cannot be safe without it.** "Email confirmation loops can be disruptive because they force users to switch from your service to their email account and back again"; they "should be avoided as far as possible". They are used only where something critical is "only available via email" or where "accidentally using the wrong email address would give someone else access to sensitive information about the user". A loop that blocks everything else has its own risk: "if a user cannot complete the loop then they're unable to use the service at all". Check: which steps of this task send the person out of the app, what would go wrong without each, and what can the person do while it waits? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Confirm an email address (When to use this pattern; How it works; Blocking or non-blocking loops), `reference/govuk/patterns/confirm-an-email-address.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Email addresses (Check the user has access to their email account), `reference/govuk/patterns/email-addresses.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Email address (Usability guidance), `reference/uswds/patterns/create-a-profile/email-address.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A note-taking app's sign-up. Pass: notes can be written at once, and the email is confirmed only when a note is first shared. Fail: the same app shows Check your inbox and nothing else until the link is clicked.

### Done recedes, what is left stands out

Called for by: list, steps. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**In a list of things to do, a part that is done is marked plainly and quietly, and the strongest mark goes to what still needs the person, so the parts left are found at a glance as the list fills up.** "Once the user has completed the task, the status should show as 'Completed' and be black text with no background colour. This will draw more attention to tasks that require action." The page names research: "User research has also shown that once a few tasks have been completed, it is harder for users to scan the page and spot incomplete tasks." In steps that run one way, the step the person is on carries the strongest mark, the steps still to come stay readable and never look disabled, and each step's state is also said in words for a person who cannot see the marks: "The current segment should be the most visually prominent segment in the step indicator." Check: with most of this list done, what draws the eye first, the done or the not done? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Complete multiple tasks (Show the status of the tasks), `reference/govuk/patterns/complete-multiple-tasks.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Task list (Formatting of statuses), `reference/govuk/components/task-list.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Step indicator (Usability; Accessibility), `reference/uswds/components/step-indicator.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A tax filing app's checklist, with seven of nine sections done. Pass: the seven read Completed in plain text and the two read In progress on a coloured patch. Fail: the same seven carry bright green filled badges that outshine the two.

### Nothing folded inside the folded

Called for by: list. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**Content a person must open to see is never put inside other content they must open.** "Do not put accordions within accordions, as it will make content difficult to find." Of the three ways to fold content away, accordions, tabs and details: "Avoid using any of these components within one another." Questions are not folded away at all: "Do not use accordions to split up a series of questions. Use separate pages instead." A menu of commands may hold one level of submenu, titled so its contents can be predicted unopened, and never a second; content to read is never folded inside the folded. Check: what on this screen must be opened to be seen, and does any of it hold something else that must be opened? Sources: GOV.UK Design System, Accordion (When not to use this component; Decide between using accordions, tabs and details), `reference/govuk/components/accordion.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Details (Decide between using details, accordions and tabs), `reference/govuk/components/details.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Context menus (Best practices: If you need to use submenus to manage a menu's complexity, keep them to one level), `reference/hig/context-menus.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A mobile network's help screen. Pass: a list of topics, each opening straight to its answer. Fail: the same Billing topic opens to more folded topics, one of which opens to the answer.

### The glance screen carries least

Called for by: glance. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**Where a screen is used while the person's attention is mostly on something else, it holds only what that moment's task needs, and a thing is added to it only where its use outweighs what it takes from the glance; what serves work done with full attention goes on a screen meant for that.** A founder of a notes app used during calls puts the app's share of attention in a meeting at "2% of your attention and the other 98% is on the person you're talking to", against "more like 80% of your attention and therefore we can afford to throw more stuff at you" when the person sits down to ask questions of past notes; the percentages are his estimate, not a measure. On the in-meeting view he is "almost paranoid of adding stuff to that view": an addition has "got to pass a really high bar" to be worth "making the notepad feel less of a calm place". Such a view is judged in use, not from a drawing of it, since what matters is "how does it feel ergonomically to have it open on a meeting and be glancing at it out of the side of your eye while you're also trying to talk to somebody". The held "Complex, not confusing" lets a screen hold as many parts as its activity has, and this claim says the activity of a glance has few; the held "A tap is cheaper than a thought" governs the decisions on a path, and this claim governs what a view holds. The account is one guest's practice and cites no study. Check: while this screen is in use, where are the person's eyes and mind most of the time, and which thing on it does that moment not need? Source: Stephenson, interviewed by Labenz, Calm AI for Crazy Days, The Cognitive Revolution (2026), `reference/interviews/stephenson-granola.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A maps app's driving view. Pass: it shows the next turn, its distance and the arrival time, with reviews and photos of places kept on the search screen. Fail: the same driving view also runs a strip of nearby restaurants with their star ratings along its foot.

## Craft: Care about every detail.

### A sound says its source

Called for by: sound. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where the app plays a sound or a haptic, each one is tied to one kind of event and played for nothing else, the platform's own patterns used only for their documented meanings, and nothing it carries is lost when it is off; a sound or a haptic that marks no particular event is annoyance.** Sound and touch interfere little with the visual task and reach a user whose eyes are elsewhere; a sound heard often must not annoy, and silence where sound was expected reads as nothing happened. Sound is muted or turned down on some devices and masked in noisy places, so a sound never carries a message that is not also on the screen; and hearing detects a gap of a millisecond, so a sound tolerates no drop-out or lag longer than that. A sound is matched by a haptic for a person who cannot hear it or has turned sound off, and where the app plays haptics of its own beyond the platform's controls, the person can turn them off. A haptic goes with something the screen also shows, matched to it, as "you generally want to match the intensity and sharpness of a haptic with the intensity and sharpness of the animation it accompanies." A haptic is short and marks a single event, since long-running ones "dilute the meaning of the feedback and distract people from their task". None plays while the camera or microphone is in use, since haptics must not "disrupt experiences involving device features like the camera, gyroscope, or microphone." On iPhone the system's patterns come in three kinds: "Notification haptics provide feedback about the outcome of a task or action", impact haptics "provide a physical metaphor", as "a tap when a view snaps into place", and "Selection haptics provide feedback while the values of a UI element are changing"; the platform's own switches, sliders and pickers already "play Apple-designed system haptics by default". A sound people know as a phone's still costs a little: in 69 students judging letters on a monitor, a phone's notification vibration played before each letter slowed answers by about 3 ms against an unfamiliar tone, and left errors unchanged. A haptic lands in the same frame as what it marks, at the moment it happens, such as a value catching on its step, and not when the motion that shows it ends, since "A haptic that lags its animation reads as a glitch, not as feedback"; and it marks only what the person did, "never on an entrance animation the user didn't cause". Each one is the sound or the touch the event would make if the thing acted on were a physical object, so that it is plain what caused it, since "for feedback to be useful; it must be obvious what caused it"; Apple chose its payment sound as "not too serious, and it's clearly a confirmation", in its designers' own practice, which cites no study. Where one event has a sound, a haptic and a picture, the three start together and keep one beat: the haptic need not copy the sound, but the two "do have to play in the same tempo", and with "latency between the visual and the rest of the feedback" the effect is "clearly broken". Sound and haptics are kept for the few events where they "communicate something important" and left off a screen's minor interactions, since more "will overwhelm people, and it will diminish the value of what's really important". In a study of 12 adults aged 20 to 25 typing on a touchscreen on an underground train, four with each kind of feedback, sound stopped helping from about 94 dB of noise around them, between heavy traffic and a jackhammer in the thesis's table, and touch stopped helping where the hand was shaken hardest; where both were high, as they often are together, typing fell to what the screen alone gave. With sound through the device's own speaker, the gain seen in a quiet lab was gone on the train, so the screen carries the whole of every answer. Over eight days of use, 9 adults aged 23 to 32 came to want less of it, "At the beginning participants appreciated all of the crossmodal feedback but by the end, they said 'less is more'", and they "often moved on to the next interaction before the previous feedback had completed", so a sound or a haptic on an act done often is shorter than the gap before the next. A pattern that is not the event's own sound has to be taught, since its "mappings are arbitrary and require users to be trained": in Hoggan's lab studies with add-on actuators, 16 staff and students learning 18 such alerts, each a rhythm, a texture and a place, needed two or three runs of 36, told right or wrong after each answer, about "20 to 30 minutes", to name them at 90 per cent, a drill no app's user sits through. Check: for each sound or haptic in this change, which one event does it mark, what does the screen show of the same event, and could it be heard or felt a hundred times? Sources: Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 3, Knowledge in the Head and in the World (Short-Term or Working Memory, modalities), `reference/books/norman-2013/03-chapter-3-knowledge-in-the-head-and-in-the-world.md`; Norman, The Design of Everyday Things (2013), chapter 4, Knowing What to Do: Constraints, Discoverability, and Feedback (Using Sound as Signifiers; When Silence Kills), `reference/books/norman-2013/04-chapter-4-knowing-what-to-do-constraints-discoverability-and.md`; Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 6, Our Peripheral Vision Is Poor (Heavy Artillery for Making Users Notice Messages: Use Sparingly, Use sound), `reference/books/johnson-2010/08-chapter-6-our-peripheral-vision-is-poor.md`; chapter 12, We Have Time Requirements (Designing to Meet Real-Time Human Interaction Deadlines, 0.001 second), `reference/books/johnson-2010/14-chapter-12-we-have-time-requirements.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Feedback (Best practices: Make sure all feedback is accessible), `reference/hig/feedback.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Hearing: Use haptics in addition to audio cues; Augment audio cues with visual cues), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Playing haptics (Best practices: Prefer using haptics to complement other feedback in your app or game), `reference/hig/playing-haptics.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Playing haptics (Best practices: In most apps, prefer playing short haptics that complement discrete events; Be aware that playing haptics might impact other user experiences), `reference/hig/playing-haptics.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Playing haptics (introduction; iOS: Notification; Impact; Selection), `reference/hig/playing-haptics.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Notifications (Content: Consider providing a sound to supplement your notifications), `reference/hig/notifications.md`;
