Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Playing haptics (Best practices: Avoid overusing haptics), `reference/hig/playing-haptics.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Playing haptics (Best practices: Use system-provided haptic patterns according to their documented meanings; Use haptics consistently throughout your app or game; Avoid overusing haptics; Make haptics optional), `reference/hig/playing-haptics.md`; Upshaw et al., The hidden cost of a smartphone: The effects of smartphone notifications on cognitive control from a behavioral and electrophysiological perspective, PLOS ONE (2022) (Methods; Results), `reference/studies/PMC9671478.md`; Kowalski, Building Animations in Expo, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (8. Haptics), `reference/kowalski/skills/animate-expo/SKILL.source.md`; Apple, Designing Audio-Haptic Experiences, WWDC19 session 810 (2019) (Causality, 5:58), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2019-810.md`; Apple, Designing Audio-Haptic Experiences, WWDC19 session 810 (2019) (Causality, 8:33; Harmony, 11:19), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2019-810.md`; Apple, Designing Audio-Haptic Experiences, WWDC19 session 810 (2019) (Utility, 15:40), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2019-810.md`; Hoggan, Crossmodal Audio and Tactile Interaction with Mobile Touchscreens, PhD thesis, University of Glasgow (2010) (chapter 6, 6.5.5 Discussion; 6.6.4 Methodology; 6.6.5 Results; 6.6.6 Discussion), `reference/papers/hoggan-2010-crossmodal-audio-and-tactile-interaction.md`; Hoggan et al., Audio or tactile feedback: which modality when?, CHI (2009) (abstract), `reference/papers/hoggan-2009-audio-or-tactile-feedback.md`; Hoggan, Crossmodal Audio and Tactile Interaction with Mobile Touchscreens, PhD thesis, University of Glasgow (2010) (chapter 7, 7.5.3 Discussion), `reference/papers/hoggan-2010-crossmodal-audio-and-tactile-interaction.md`; Hoggan, Crossmodal Audio and Tactile Interaction with Mobile Touchscreens, PhD thesis, University of Glasgow (2010) (chapter 3, 3.5.1 Audio and Tactile Crossmodal Icons; chapter 5, 5.2.4 Training; 5.2.5 Training Results; 5.6 Conclusions), `reference/papers/hoggan-2010-crossmodal-audio-and-tactile-interaction.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A map app's driving directions, which use sound and haptics. Pass: a turn and a speed camera each have their own chime and their own tap, both also shown on screen. Fail: the same directions play one beep and one tap for a turn, a camera and a lost signal.

### Letters keep the shapes reading was learned on

Called for by: text. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**Text on a screen is set in mixed case in a plain face of regular weight or heavier; capitals, outline and thin faces hide the features by which words are recognised.** Bottom-up, context-free, automatic reading is based on recognition of letters and words from their visual features, so a typeface with difficult-to-recognize features and shapes will be hard to read; text in all caps is generally hard to read because letters look more similar to each other, and outline typefaces complicate feature recognition. Long runs of italic or bold are avoided as well as long runs of capitals, since "Both italic and bold text can degrade readability" and "Uppercase text has a serious negative effect on readability". The space between letters follows their size, wider when small, as the platform's own face sets it at every size, since "When type becomes smaller, letters tend to need more space between them"; spacing the app sets itself is set for each size, since "a tracking you provided for small sizes may not look good on large sizes". Check: is any text on this screen in capitals, in an outline, or in a light weight, and what does it look like in mixed case and regular? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 4, Reading Is Unnatural (Poor Information Design Can Disrupt Reading, Difficult scripts and typefaces), `reference/books/johnson-2010/06-chapter-4-reading-is-unnatural.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Typography (Ensuring legibility: In general, avoid light font weights), `reference/hig/typography.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Task list (Formatting of statuses), `reference/govuk/components/task-list.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Tag (Text in tags), `reference/govuk/components/tag.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Typography (Font style), `reference/uswds/components/typography/typography.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Button (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/button.md`; Apple, The details of UI typography, WWDC20 session 10175 (2020) (Optical sizes, 4:21; Recap, 30:01), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2020-10175.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A bus app's departure board. Pass: destinations read Central Station in mixed case and regular weight. Fail: the same board reads CENTRAL STATION in thin capitals.

### Text starts at the same edge

Called for by: text. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**Text of more than one line, and every list, is aligned to the leading edge and never centred, since the eye returns to the same horizontal position for each new line.** In automatic reading our eyes are trained to go back to the same horizontal position and down one line; if text is centered or right-aligned, each line starts in a different horizontal position, so automatic eye movements take our eyes back to the wrong place and we must consciously adjust, which drops us out of automatic mode and slows us greatly. With poetry and wedding invitations that is probably OK, but with any other type of text it is a disadvantage, and centred numbered lists really mess up readers' eye movement. Text is never justified either, since stretched word spacing opens rivers of white that hide where words end. Numbers compared down a column are the exception: they line up on their units, so digits of equal place sit under one another. Only numbers that are amounts line up on their units, in figures of equal width, while phone numbers, codes and dates stay at the leading edge, as numbers "that can't be totaled". The platform moved its own text this way: system type is "now bolder and left-aligned to improve readability in key moments like alerts and onboarding." Check: is any text or list on this screen centred, and does it run to more than one line at the person's largest text size? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 4, Reading Is Unnatural (Poor Information Design Can Disrupt Reading, Centered text), `reference/books/johnson-2010/06-chapter-4-reading-is-unnatural.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Layout (Visual hierarchy: Align elements to make them easier to scan, and use indentation to convey hierarchy), `reference/hig/layout.md`; WCAG 2.2, 1.4.8 Visual Presentation, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/visual-presentation.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.4.8 Visual Presentation (Intent of Visual Presentation), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/visual-presentation.md`; GOV.UK Design System, Table (Numbers in a table), `reference/govuk/components/table.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Table (Usability), `reference/uswds/components/table.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Typography (Text alignment), `reference/uswds/components/typography/typography.md`; Apple, Get to know the new design system, WWDC25 session 356 (2025) (Design Language, 2:24), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2025-356.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A recipe app's ingredients list. Pass: every line starts at the left edge. Fail: the same list is centred, each line starting at a different place.

### A meaningful colour is strong, large and beside its contrast

Called for by: colour. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**A colour that carries a meaning is saturated, laid on a patch big enough to see, and placed next to the colour it is told from, since pale, thin and far-apart colours cannot be distinguished.** The paler two colors are, the harder it is to tell them apart; the smaller or thinner objects are, the harder it is to distinguish their colors, and text is often thin; and the more separated color patches are, the more difficult it is to distinguish their colors, especially if the separation is great enough to require eye motion between patches. ITN.net used white and pale yellow to show which step of the reservation the user was on, and some visitors couldn't see which step they were on; color patches in chart legends should be large. Check: for each colour that carries a meaning here, how saturated is it, how big is its patch, and how far is it from the colour it is told from? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 5, Our Color Vision Is Limited (Ability to Discriminate Colors Depends on How Colors Are Presented; Guidelines for Using Color, the ITN.net example), `reference/books/johnson-2010/07-chapter-5-our-color-vision-is-limited.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Color (visionOS: Prefer using color in bold text and large areas), `reference/hig/color.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A cinema ticket app's progress bar. Pass: the current step is a large, saturated blue dot beside grey ones. Fail: the same bar marks the current step with a thin pale-yellow underline on white.

### Opponent colours kept apart

Called for by: colour. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**Red is not laid on or directly beside green, nor blue on yellow, since opponent colours next to each other shimmer.** Placing opponent colors right next to or on top of each other causes a disturbing shimmering sensation, and so should be avoided; a site that places blue against red causes an annoying shimmering. Check: does any red touch green, or blue touch yellow, on this screen? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 5, Our Color Vision Is Limited (Guidelines for Using Color, guideline 5), `reference/books/johnson-2010/07-chapter-5-our-color-vision-is-limited.md`; chapter 4, Reading Is Unnatural (Poor Information Design Can Disrupt Reading, Combining flaws that disrupt reading), `reference/books/johnson-2010/06-chapter-4-reading-is-unnatural.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A bike hire app's map. Pass: available bikes are white numbers on green pins. Fail: the same pins show their numbers in red on the green.

### Motion to catch the eye lasts a quarter to half a second

Called for by: motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where a message must draw the eye from where it is, it moves once, for a quarter to half a second, and then stands still; motion that lasts longer or recurs is ignored or resented.** Our peripheral vision is good at detecting motion, and motion in the periphery causes reflexive eye movements that bring it into the fovea; it doesn't take much, just a tiny bit of motion is enough to make a viewer's eyes zip over in that direction. If motion or blinking is used, it should be brief, about a quarter to a half second, no longer, or it quickly goes from an unconscious attention-grabber to a conscious annoyance; most experienced computer users consider wiggling, blinking objects on screen to be annoying, and a few users have attentional impairments that make it difficult to ignore them. WCAG's five seconds is the outer limit past which a way to stop the motion is owed, not a length to aim for. The motion is on a symbol beside the message, never on its words, which are hard to read while they move, and not on several at once, which overwhelms. Check: does anything on this screen move to be noticed, for how long, and how often in a session? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 6, Our Peripheral Vision Is Poor (Heavy Artillery for Making Users Notice Messages: Use Sparingly, Flash or wiggle briefly), `reference/books/johnson-2010/08-chapter-6-our-peripheral-vision-is-poor.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Motion (Providing feedback: Aim for brevity and precision in feedback animations; In apps, generally avoid adding motion to UI interactions that occur frequently), `reference/hig/motion.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Cognitive: Be cautious with fast-moving and blinking animations), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`; WCAG 2.2, 2.2.2 Pause, Stop, Hide, Level A, `reference/wcag/sc/20/pause-stop-hide.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 2.2.2 Pause, Stop, Hide (Intent of Pause, Stop, Hide), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/pause-stop-hide.md`; Krause, 10 Design Guidelines for Reporting Errors in Forms, Nielsen Norman Group (2019, updated 2024) (5. Add Iconography or Subtle Animation for Easy Scanning), `reference/nngroup/errors-forms-design-guidelines.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A messaging app's new-message banner. Pass: the banner slides in once, in 0.3 seconds, and stays still. Fail: the same banner wiggles every two seconds until it is tapped.

### A second alone

Called for by: motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**A thing that appears on a screen on its own is left alone for about a second before anything else appears or moves, since a person needs that long to react to what has arrived.** Visual-motor reaction time, the intentional response to an unexpected event, is 700 milliseconds, rounded to the one-second deadline; when information suddenly appears on the screen, designers can assume that users will take at least a second to react to it; the table of deadlines lists, at one second, the required wait time after presenting important information before presenting more. Check: after anything appears on this screen on its own, does anything else appear, move or replace it within a second? Source: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 12, We Have Time Requirements (The Many Time Constants of the Human Brain, Visual-motor reaction time; Designing to Meet Real-Time Human Interaction Deadlines, 1.0 second; Table 12.1), `reference/books/johnson-2010/14-chapter-12-we-have-time-requirements.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A parcel tracking app's delivery notice. Pass: Driver arriving appears and stays still for several seconds. Fail: the same notice is replaced by a Rate your driver card half a second after it appears.

### Frames before detail

Called for by: motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**A thing that moves on the screen moves at a steady sixty frames a second, and where the device cannot keep that, the thing is drawn with less detail rather than fewer frames.** The older figure, from a 2010 book, is 10 frames a second "as an approximate minimum frame rate for perception of smooth animation, even though smooth animation really requires a rate more like 20"; the one rate the current platform states, given for games, is "a consistent frame rate of 30 to 60 fps", and its top, sixty, is the rate held here, so steadiness counts as well as speed; jerky, hard-to-follow animations are poor responsiveness; a graphics engine that cannot keep the frame rate simplifies its rendering, sacrificing labels, shading and colour, since it is better to reduce an image temporarily to a line drawing than to let the frame rate drop. Steadiness is not the whole of it: a thing that moves too far between one frame and the next strobes even at a steady rate, so a fast move is kept within what the rate can carry, and sixty frames a second carries a faster one than thirty, since "it's not just about framerate. It's what's in the frames." Sixty is the floor and not the ceiling: on a display that draws 120 frames a second, a practice glossary that cites no study gives that rate as smooth there, "60fps is the baseline for smooth motion; 120fps on newer displays". Check: does anything move on this screen, and on the oldest device the app's users hold, does it move steadily at sixty frames a second, and does any fast move jump so far between frames that it strobes? Sources: Johnson, Designing with the Mind in Mind (2010), chapter 12, We Have Time Requirements (Designing to Meet Real-Time Human Interaction Deadlines, 0.1 second; Additional Guidelines for Achieving Responsive Interactive Systems, Monitor time compliance; decrease the quality of work to keep up), `reference/books/johnson-2010/14-chapter-12-we-have-time-requirements.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Motion (Leveraging platform capabilities: a consistent frame rate of 30 to 60 fps, stated for games), `reference/hig/motion.md`; Apple, Designing Fluid Interfaces, WWDC18 session 803 (2018) (Smooth frames of motion, 18:14), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2018-803.md`; Kowalski, Animation Vocabulary, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Performance: Frame rate (FPS)), `reference/kowalski/skills/animation-vocabulary/SKILL.source.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A weather app's rain radar loop. Pass: on an old phone the loop drops street names to keep a steady 60 frames a second. Fail: the same loop keeps every label and falls to 30 frames a second.

### Told apart by rhythm, not strength

Called for by: sound. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where the app's sounds or haptics must be told apart from one another, they differ in rhythm, the number and spacing of their beats; a difference only in strength, loudness or pitch never tells two events apart, and a difference only in feel is the weakest of the rest.** Rhythm is the feature hearing and touch share best: "Blattner et al. describe rhythm as the most prominent characteristic of a motif", and in the tacton work Hoggan builds on, three rhythms "could be identified with 96% accuracy". In a study she reports, "users obtained the most information from the rhythmic pattern compared to the frequency/amplitude modulation". In her own lab studies, 16 staff and students at a time heard alerts that each carried three things, by ear through headphones or by touch from add-on actuators on a belt. Rhythm and place told the alerts apart well. Texture "produced significantly poorer identification rates" than both, in each sense, so that it "is not only a poor parameter for use in crossmodal interaction but is also ineffective in unimodal interaction, most prominently in the tactile modality", and "audio and tactile roughness recognition rates are significantly lower when mobile", walking on a treadmill. A better-made texture raised its rate "from 52% to 72%". Pitch and frequency are no better: "it is much harder to absolutely identify the pitch of a sound without having a reference note for comparison", and on the skin "frequency can be difficult to distinguish so cannot be used effectively as a multidimensional parameter". When nine staff and students named textures made by frequency alone, they got 81 per cent right, against 94.2 for waveform, and said "the cues were not long enough". Strength is for the person to set, not a code: "observing the absolute intensity of a vibration signal is difficult; however users are able to observe changes in intensity", people "report annoyance when the volume level is out of their control", and "it is best to leave amplitude under the control of the user instead of using it to encode information". This agrees with Apple's practice held in "Many kinds, several coarse distinctions": two watch cues whose sounds differed slightly were made to differ in their count of taps. A haptic's intensity and sharpness still give it its character and match its animation, as "A sound says its source" holds, but they do not say which event it is. The percentages come from add-on actuators driven from a PC and from headphones, and a phone's own engine offers intensity and sharpness rather than her waveforms, so the numbers do not carry over to it; what carries is the order, rhythm first. Check: for each pair of sounds or haptics this screen plays for different events, do they differ in the number or spacing of their beats, or only in strength, loudness, pitch or feel? Sources: Hoggan, Crossmodal Audio and Tactile Interaction with Mobile Touchscreens, PhD thesis, University of Glasgow (2010) (chapter 2, 2.1.1.2 Pitch; 2.2.1.3 Rhythm; 2.2.2.1 Tactons: Intensity; chapter 4, introduction; 4.1 Rhythm; 4.3.4 Results), `reference/papers/hoggan-2010-crossmodal-audio-and-tactile-interaction.md`; Hoggan, Crossmodal Audio and Tactile Interaction with Mobile Touchscreens, PhD thesis, University of Glasgow (2010) (chapter 5, 5.3.2 Individual Parameter Results and Discussion; 5.5 Discussion; 5.6 Conclusions), `reference/papers/hoggan-2010-crossmodal-audio-and-tactile-interaction.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A cooking timer app's two alerts, felt with the phone in a pocket. Pass: the oven timer ends with two taps and the hob timer with four. Fail: the same timers both end with one tap, the hob's a little stronger.

### Motion follows the hand and shows where things went

Called for by: motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where something on the screen moves, it moves as the person's gesture moved it and goes back the way it came, so the motion says where the thing went; motion that says nothing is left out.** "Strive for realistic feedback motion that follows people's gestures and expectations... feedback motion that doesn't make sense can make them feel disoriented. For example, if someone reveals a view by sliding it down from the top, they don't expect to dismiss the view by sliding it to the side." "Don't add motion for the sake of adding motion. Gratuitous or excessive animation can distract people and may make them feel disconnected or physically uncomfortable." Under reduced motion, Apple's advice includes "Tracking animations directly with people's gestures". A gesture's feedback "helps them predict its results". The held "A control sits by what it changes" makes a control's direction match its effect; this claim carries that mapping into motion. Delight is not added on top: "The way to make a design delightful isn't by adding confetti or tacking on extra flourishes", and "Don't mistake delight for decoration", since "people are trying to accomplish a task". A dragged thing stays under the finger at the point where it was taken hold of, so the screen should "never use the center of the image as the dragging point", and it starts to move only once the finger has gone the distance by which the platform tells a drag from a tap, "usually 10 points in iOS", so a tap that wobbles moves nothing. Apple's talk gives the reason: what leaves and returns on "symmetric paths" matches how things persist in the world, so a view that slid in from the right on a tap goes back to the right on Back, while one sent down instead "feels like I'm sending it somewhere"; the shared path also tells the hand where to drag it, "because I know that's where it lives". On its way from one state to the next, the thing grows from where it was toward where it will be, so the end is seen before it arrives, as a control panel's tile swells "up and out towards your finger" before it opens: "It makes the gestures feel expected, and predictable." A thing comes to rest without overshooting, and overshoots a little only where the gesture that sent it had momentum: Apple starts from "100% damping, or no overshoot" so the motion "doesn't distract from the task at hand", and gives a music player swiped closed "a little bit of bounce" while the same player opened by a tap gets none. A practice standard that cites no study names the purposes a motion may serve, "Feedback", "Spatial consistency", "State indication", "Preventing a jarring change" and "Explanation", and a motion that can name none is cut: "Can't name it? Don't build it". What the person is reading or acting on never moves for style, since "data the user is reading or acting on should not move for style". A view that opens from a control grows out of that control and goes back into it, never out of the middle of the screen or out of nothing, since "Nothing in the real world appears from nothing": it starts a little smaller than full size and transparent, at nine-tenths of its size or more in a practice standard that cites no study, and a view over the whole screen that belongs to no one control opens from the centre, as "Modals are exempt". A sideways slide says forward or back, so it is kept for going deeper and returning, and a switch between peers, such as tabs, changes the view in place: "Tabs are peers, not a hierarchy — sliding implies depth that isn't there, and the user pays for it dozens of times a session". A second finger that lands while a thing is being dragged does not take it over, since "switching fingers mid-drag causes the element to jump to the new position", and nothing else on the screen is selected or lit while the drag runs, "so selection/hover don't occur simultaneously". A pinch is held the same way: the zoom grows from an anchor point between the fingers, so what was pinched stays under them, and a practice essay notes that a precise zoom starts with the fingers together, since "it's a lot easier and more precise to pick the anchor point with the fingers pinched together", "kind of like grabbing an object". Check: for each thing that moves on this screen, does it move with the finger, does it leave the way it came, does a view opened from a control grow out of that control, does it settle without a bounce unless a flick sent it, and what would be lost if it did not move at all? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Motion (Best practices: Add motion purposefully, supporting the experience without overshadowing it; Providing feedback: Strive for realistic feedback motion that follows people's gestures and expectations), `reference/hig/motion.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Accessibility (Cognitive: Be cautious with fast-moving and blinking animations), `reference/hig/accessibility.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Gestures (Best practices: Handle gestures as responsively as possible), `reference/hig/gestures.md`; Apple, Principles of great design, WWDC26 session 250 (2026) (Delight, 16:05), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2026-250.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Design principles (Delight: Don't mistake delight for decoration), `reference/hig/design-principles.md`; Apple, Designing Fluid Interfaces, WWDC18 session 803 (2018) (Swipe, 51:12), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2018-803.md`; Apple, Designing Fluid Interfaces, WWDC18 session 803 (2018) (Spatial consistency, 11:47), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2018-803.md`; Apple, Designing Fluid Interfaces, WWDC18 session 803 (2018) (Hint in the direction of the gesture, 13:28), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2018-803.md`; Apple, Designing Fluid Interfaces, WWDC18 session 803 (2018) (Character, 35:47), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2018-803.md`; Kowalski, Building Animations, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (2. What is the purpose?), `reference/kowalski/skills/animate/SKILL.source.md`; Kowalski, Animation Standards Reference, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Physicality), `reference/kowalski/skills/review-animations/STANDARDS.md`; Kowalski, Building Animations in Expo, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (1. Should this animate at all?), `reference/kowalski/skills/animate-expo/SKILL.source.md`; Kowalski, Animation Vocabulary, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Transitions Between States: Direction-aware transition), `reference/kowalski/skills/animation-vocabulary/SKILL.source.md`; Kowalski, Design Engineering, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Gesture and Drag Interactions: Multi-touch protection), `reference/kowalski/skills/emil-design-eng/SKILL.source.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Interactions: Clean drag interactions), `reference/vercel/README.md`; Freiberg, Invisible Details of Interaction Design (2023) (Metaphors), `reference/essays/freiberg-invisible-details.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A notification panel pulled down from the top of a phone. Pass: pushed back up, it slides up and away the way it came. Fail: the same panel, on closing, shrinks into the centre of the screen and fades.

### Lines of no more than 80 characters

Called for by: text. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**A block of running text can be read in lines of no more than 80 characters, or 40 in Chinese, Japanese or Korean, so the eye finds the start of the next line.** For some readers "long lines of text can become a significant barrier. They have trouble keeping their place and following the flow of text." "Lines should not exceed 80 characters or glyphs (40 if CJK)". The same AAA criterion also asks for line spacing of "space-and-a-half" and lets the person choose text and background colours. Paragraphs of running text are parted by about one blank line, not by an indent, since less than half the text size "doesn't provide enough separation" and more than one and a half times "disturbs the flow of the text". Long reading aims at about 66 characters a line, and the ceiling stays 80. Running text of several lines is never set with less space between its lines than the platform's own, since "If you need to display three or more lines of text, avoid tight leading even in areas where height is limited", and more space "can make it easier for people to keep their place while moving from one line to the next"; Apple's talk tries the tighter spacing on a paragraph and finds it "too tight for such a long paragraph". The screen itself sets the platform's own spacing, not the 1.5: Apple's body text style, 17-point text, has "22-point line height", about 1.3 times, and for a long paragraph its loose leading gives "24 points of line height", which "does look much better", in a talk that cites no study; the 1.5 is what a person can widen the spacing to without loss. Check: in this screen's widest window, how many characters does the longest line of running text hold? Sources: WCAG 2.2, 1.4.8 Visual Presentation, Level AAA, `reference/wcag/sc/20/visual-presentation.md`; WCAG 2.2, Understanding 1.4.8 Visual Presentation (Intent of Visual Presentation), `reference/wcag/understanding/20/visual-presentation.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Typography (Whitespace), `reference/uswds/components/typography/typography.md`; U.S. Web Design System, Typography (Measure (line length)), `reference/uswds/components/typography/typography.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Typography (Using system fonts: Modify the built-in text styles if necessary), `reference/hig/typography.md`; Apple, The details of UI typography, WWDC20 session 10175 (2020) (Text styles, 19:17), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2020-10175.md`; Apple, The details of UI typography, WWDC20 session 10175 (2020) (19:17), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2020-10175.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A news app's article on a tablet held sideways. Pass: the text sits in a column of about 70 characters, with margins either side. Fail: the same article runs edge to edge at 140 characters a line.

### Motion never makes a person wait

Called for by: motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 3, User control and freedom.

**Where something on the screen is moving, the person can act on it at once: a touch on the moving thing takes hold of it where it is now and can send it back, and a tap elsewhere is taken then, never held until the motion ends.** People change their minds partway through an act, as someone walking across a stage who has forgotten something "could just turn back immediately". A screen that cannot be redirected makes them "think before what you did", deciding first and acting after, while on one that can, "the thought and gesture happen in parallel", which "is way faster than thinking before doing". Apple's own examples are an app sent back home while it is still opening, and an app used "as it's launching". "As much as possible, don't make people wait for an animation to complete before they can do anything, especially if they have to experience the animation more than once." A thing caught partway moves on from where the eye sees it and does not jump, and a closing view that is grabbed again "should follow the finger — not finish closing first, then reopen", in a practice account that cites no study. A change of mind inside a gesture is answered as it happens: the platform reads a finger's pause from its sudden slowing "instead of waiting for some timer". The held "A wait can be abandoned" cuts short the app's work; this claim cuts short its motion. A motion a person can set off "twice in a second" picks up "from the current value" and never restarts "from zero", and items that come in one after another are "decorative — never block interaction while it plays", in a practice standard that cites no study. Check: for each thing that moves on this screen, can a finger catch it partway and send it back without a jump, and is a tap elsewhere taken at once while it moves? Sources: Apple, Designing Fluid Interfaces, WWDC18 session 803 (2018) (Redirection and interruption, 6:51), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2018-803.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Motion (Best practices: Let people cancel motion), `reference/hig/motion.md`; Kowalski, Apple Design, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (3. Interruptibility — the single most important principle), `reference/kowalski/skills/apple-design/SKILL.source.md`; Kowalski, Building Animations, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (6. Interruption and exit), `reference/kowalski/skills/animate/SKILL.source.md`; Kowalski, Animation Standards Reference, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Interruptibility; Stagger), `reference/kowalski/skills/review-animations/STANDARDS.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A maps app's place card, which rises from the bottom when a pin is tapped. Pass: a finger catches the card halfway up and pushes it back down, and it follows from where it was. Fail: the same card ignores touches until it has finished rising, and only then can it be dragged down.

### A released thing keeps the finger's speed

Called for by: motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**Where a person lets go of a thing they dragged or flicked, it carries on at the speed the finger had and comes to rest where that speed carries it, slowing as a scrolled list does, so a short, light flick sends it as far as a long drag would.** The platform's scrolling keeps "all your energy and momentum", so the finger is "only onscreen for a brief amount of time"; without that, people "would have to do these long, laborious swipes". Where a flicked thing must settle in one of several places, Apple picks the place by where its speed would carry it, "a projection", not by the place nearest to where the finger let go. When the place was picked by position alone, a picture-in-picture video reached the far corner only when dragged "past halfway over the screen", and a flick "kind of goes back to the nearest corner, which isn't necessarily what I expected". The slowing is borrowed from the platform's scrolling, since "we have this natural intuition for how far content goes when I scroll", and its friction "is consistent, which makes it predictable, and very easy to master". The speed is read from the last stretch of the drag, "the history of the touch", not from its last point alone, so the thing leaves the finger without a jolt. A flick back the way it came sends it back, even from past halfway, since a practice account that cites no study decides between going back and going on by "velocity sign, not position". Check: for each thing on this screen that can be dragged and let go, does a short, quick flick take it to the place it was flicked toward, and does it leave the finger at the finger's speed? Sources: Apple, Designing Fluid Interfaces, WWDC18 session 803 (2018) (Lightweight interactions and amplification, 14:39; Aligning motion with intent, 41:06; Swipe, 52:34), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2018-803.md`; Kowalski, Apple Design, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (5. Velocity handoff; 6. Momentum projection; Quick Reference), `reference/kowalski/skills/apple-design/SKILL.source.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A maps app's sheet of search results, which rests at three heights. Pass: a short, quick flick up from the middle height takes it to full height. Fail: the same flick lets it fall back to the middle, and it leaves the middle only when dragged past halfway to the top.

### An end gives before it stops

Called for by: motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where a person drags or scrolls a thing past its end, it still follows the finger, less far the further it goes, and springs back when let go, so the end is told apart from a screen that has stopped answering.** Apple calls this "softly indicating boundaries": the thing "is tracking you throughout. It's always letting you know that it's understanding you." A thing that stops dead "would feel broken", and "you actually wouldn't know the difference between a frozen phone, and phone that's just at the top of the edge of the screen". People notice at once when a thing stops moving one-to-one with the finger, and "in the case of scrolling, it shows us that we've reached the end of the content". On release, the thing is pulled back inside its end, "ready for the next time you want to scroll". The same holds where one moving thing hands over to another under the finger: the two "smoothly hand off in smooth curves", rather than one stopping as the other starts. A practice account that cites no study puts the same thing in its own words: "A hard stop reads as 'frozen'." The platform's own lists already do this, so the claim matters most for what the app draws itself, such as a sheet at its tallest, a slider at its end or the last card in a row. Check: for each thing on this screen that can be dragged or scrolled, what does a finger pulling it past its end see: does it give a little and spring back, or does it stop dead? Sources: Apple, Designing Fluid Interfaces, WWDC18 session 803 (2018) (Rubberbanding, 17:01; Seamless motion, 30:59; Swipe, 53:02), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2018-803.md`; Kowalski, Apple Design, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (9. Rubber-banding — soft boundaries), `reference/kowalski/skills/apple-design/SKILL.source.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A photo app's album, swiped sideways from one photo to the next. Pass: on the last photo, a further swipe drags it a little way, harder the further it goes, and it springs back. Fail: the same last photo does not move at all under the swipe.

### The more often it is seen, the less it moves

Called for by: motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 8, Aesthetic and minimalist design.

**A change a person causes many times in a session, or from the keyboard, happens at once and does not move; one caused a few times in a session moves slightly and quickly, if at all; full motion is kept for what is met now and then, such as a sheet or a message.** Apple: "In apps, generally avoid adding motion to UI interactions that occur frequently. The system already provides subtle animations for interactions with standard interface elements. For a custom element, you generally want to avoid making people spend extra time paying attention to unnecessary motion every time they interact with it." A practice standard that cites no study grades this by count. At "100+ times/day" there is "No animation. Ever."; at "Tens of times/day", "Near-imperceptible only — fast and subtle, or nothing"; "Occasional (modals, drawers, toasts)" take "Standard animation". An act from the keyboard is "a disqualifier, not a judgment call", since actions "repeated hundreds of times a day, animation makes them feel slow, delayed, and disconnected". Its own example is a launcher with no open or close animation, "correct for something opened hundreds of times a day". What goes is the motion, not the answer: under the held "Every action is answered at once", each change still shows within a tenth of a second. The held "Motion to catch the eye lasts a quarter to half a second" already finds that motion seen often is ignored or resented. A practice essay gives a reason, as its writer's belief and not a finding: a flourish met again and again goes the way of a word in "semantic satiation", where "repetition of a word or phrase causes it to temporarily lose meaning"; the writer's own animated list "began to feel sluggish" after a couple of days, and with motion removed from its core interactions he "suddenly felt like I was moving much faster". Where motion marks a moment met once, such as the first arrival after signing in, it plays on that arrival only, and a return or a reload shows the screen at once without it, since a person need not be greeted "every single time I return" and doing so "diminishes the novelty of the occasion", in a practice essay that cites no study. Check: for each thing that moves on this screen, how many times in a session does a person cause it, does a change caused often happen at once, without moving, and does a motion that marks a first arrival play on that arrival only? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Motion (Providing feedback: In apps, generally avoid adding motion to UI interactions that occur frequently), `reference/hig/motion.md`; Kowalski, Animation Standards Reference, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Should it animate? (frequency table)), `reference/kowalski/skills/review-animations/STANDARDS.md`; Kowalski, Building Animations, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (1. Should this animate at all?), `reference/kowalski/skills/animate/SKILL.source.md`; Kowalski, Finding Animation Opportunities, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (The Gate: 1. Frequency), `reference/kowalski/skills/find-animation-opportunities/SKILL.source.md`; Freiberg, Novelty (2026) (introduction), `reference/essays/freiberg-novelty.md`; Freiberg, Invisible Details of Interaction Design (2023) (Frequency & Novelty), `reference/essays/freiberg-invisible-details.md`; Freiberg, Novelty (2026) (onboarding passage), `reference/essays/freiberg-novelty.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A calculator app's keypad. Pass: each key darkens the instant it is touched and the digit appears in the display at once. Fail: the same keypad flies each digit up from its key into the display over 0.4 seconds.

### Quick, and fastest at the start

Called for by: motion. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**A motion that answers an action, or brings a small thing in or out, ends within about three tenths of a second and starts at its fastest, slowing as it settles, so the person never waits to see the result; a view that fills much of the screen may take up to half a second, and a move between screens keeps the platform's own time.** Apple: "When animated feedback is brief and precise, it tends to feel lightweight and unobtrusive, and it can often convey information more effectively than prominent animation." The numbers come from a practice standard that cites no study: "UI animations stay under 300ms", with "Button press feedback" at "100–160ms", "Tooltips, small popovers" at "125–200ms", "Dropdowns, selects" at "150–250ms" and "Modals, drawers" at "200–500ms"; "A 180ms dropdown feels more responsive than a 400ms one." The start is fast because the start is when the eye is on it: a motion that starts slow ends up "delaying the exact moment the user is watching", so one that starts fast feels quicker at the same length. For a move between screens the same standard yields to the platform: "match the platform for navigation, beat it everywhere else". The held "Motion to catch the eye lasts a quarter to half a second" is for a message drawing the eye from elsewhere, and it stands; this claim is for the answer where the eye already is. Check: for each motion on this screen, how long does it take from start to rest, is it fastest at its start, and does a move between screens keep the platform's own? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Motion (Providing feedback: Aim for brevity and precision in feedback animations), `reference/hig/motion.md`; Kowalski, Animation Standards Reference, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Easing; Duration), `reference/kowalski/skills/review-animations/STANDARDS.md`; Kowalski, Building Animations in Expo, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (5. Timing or spring), `reference/kowalski/skills/animate-expo/SKILL.source.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A shopping app's size menu, which drops from the Size button. Pass: the menu grows out of the button in 0.2 seconds, fast at first, and the sizes can be read almost at once. Fail: the same menu creeps out of the button and takes 0.6 seconds to settle.

### The worst real value still shows

Called for by: list, numbers. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**A screen that shows values it does not choose, such as names, addresses, titles, counts and lists, works at the realistic worst of each: the longest and the shortest real one, one that is missing, none, exactly one, thousands, other scripts and emoji. At the worst, no value pushes a control off the screen or out of reach, covers another value, or shows as a raw stand-in such as undefined.** Screens are usually built against kind data: "Demo data is chosen, usually without anyone noticing, to make the design look good", and the guide's example is a screen made for "Jane Doe, jane@acme.com, 12 members" that then meets "Aleksandra Wiśniewska-Kowalczyk" and a workspace of 1,284. The worst case is real, never contrived: "Every worst-case value must be something a real user could plausibly produce, or the longest value the backend actually accepts." Long text is not the only break. The ones that ship are "the short name that leaves an orphaned dash, the missing avatar, the count of exactly 1 ("1 members"), the empty list, the badge whose text got translated". A picture that fails to load falls back to something drawn, such as initials, never the broken-image mark. A break is worst where it leaves "content unreadable, action unreachable, wrong data shown". The worst case is tried at the narrowest phone width, at the largest text, in dark appearance and right to left. Every state is drawn, "Empty, sparse, dense, & error states", and "Layouts handle short, average, & very long content." The held "Every real value fits" makes a field take every real value. This claim makes the screen show it. The held "An empty place says why" covers the case of none. Both sources are practice guides that cite no study. Text in a script with tall parts above and below the line, such as Arabic, keeps the taller lines the platform gives it, since such parts "can encroach on the visual space of surrounding lines", so a line height fixed for Latin text does not clip or overlap it. Check: fill this screen with the longest real name, a one-letter name, a missing picture, counts of 0, 1 and 1,284, and a name in a right-to-left script; does every control stay in reach, and does every value read right? Sources: Kowalski, Breaking UI, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Operating Posture; Hard Rules; Phase 4 — Break it: Failure signatures; Required Output Format), `reference/kowalski/skills/break-ui/SKILL.source.md`; Kowalski, Breaking UI: Worst-Case Catalog, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (People and names; Collections; Images and media; Environment), `reference/kowalski/skills/break-ui/CATALOG.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Content: All states designed; Resilient to user-generated content), `reference/vercel/README.md`; Apple, The details of UI typography, WWDC20 session 10175 (2020) (Leading, 14:47), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2020-10175.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A team chat app's member list. Pass: Aleksandra Wiśniewska-Kowalczyk wraps onto a second line, and the ••• button stays at the end of her row. Fail: the same name runs on in one line and pushes the ••• button off the edge of the screen.

### Nothing is cut that cannot be read whole

Called for by: text, list. Cross-check: Nielsen 6, Recognition rather than recall.

**Where a value is too long for its place, it is cut only where the person can reach the whole of it with a tap, and the part kept still says which thing it is. A number, an amount, a date, or anything else the person compares, is never cut.** Apple: "Avoid truncating text in scrollable regions unless people can open a separate view to read the rest of the content." A practice guide that cites no study chooses field by field. "Wrap text the user needs in full to identify something", where "Two lines is usually fine; four is a sign the column is too narrow". The end is cut only where the start carries the meaning, and then: "Always pair with a way to see the full value." The middle is cut where like values differ at the end, which the held "Choices differ where people already look" already requires from Apple. And: "Never truncate numbers, amounts, dates, or anything the user compares. Give them the room." The held "Type and contrast are the person's" makes a layout stack rather than truncate at large text sizes. This claim covers a long value at any text size. A label a little too long may be drawn slightly tighter to fit, as the platform does "within a range that's reasonable for legibility", but is never squeezed past it, "because there is such a thing as too much tracking", and past that the rule for cutting holds. A long machine string may stand on the screen as the thing's own name, a page's title for its web address, where the part that says whose it is stays in view and the whole is shown and editable on request: a browser shows the title, "The domain remains visible for recognition and security", and "the URL is shown and you can edit it inline", with a setting "to see the full URL at all times"; practice that cites no study. Check: for each value this screen may cut, is it a number, an amount or a date, which part is kept, and where does the person read the whole? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Typography (Supporting Dynamic Type: Keep text truncation to a minimum as font size increases), `reference/hig/typography.md`; Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Lists and tables (Content: Keep item text succinct so row content is comfortable to read; Consider ways to preserve readability of text that might otherwise get clipped or truncated), `reference/hig/lists-and-tables.md`; Kowalski, Breaking UI, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (Phase 4 — Break it: Truncate, wrap, or clamp; Failure signatures), `reference/kowalski/skills/break-ui/SKILL.source.md`; Apple, The details of UI typography, WWDC20 session 10175 (2020) (Tracking, 12:46), `reference/wwdc/wwdc2020-10175.md`; Deets, The strategy behind Dia's design (2025) (Simplicity.), `reference/essays/browser-company-dia.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A banking app's list of payments. Pass: the payee Northwind Industries Holdings Ltd wraps onto two lines, and the amount £12,345,678.90 shows whole. Fail: the same row shows the amount as £12,345,6….

### Clear of the device's own parts

Called for by: every screen. Cross-check: Nielsen 4, Consistency and standards.

**Nothing a person must read or tap sits under what covers the edges of the screen: the camera housing, the rounded corners, the home indicator, the status bar and, on a web page, the browser's own bars. The background runs out to the edges.** Apple: "Respecting the safe area is essential to make sure system UI and hardware features like the Dynamic Island don’t obstruct content and controls", and "For full-screen background content, be sure to extend it underneath sidebars, toolbars, and tab bars to fit the entire screen or window." A practice guide that cites no study says the same for a web app: "A native app paints edge to edge" and keeps its content clear of "the notch, Dynamic Island, and home-indicator zones". It names the web page's own trap as well: a screen sized to the tallest view of the page "overflows by the height of that bar", so "a bottom-pinned button sits under it" while the browser's bar is showing. A control along the bottom edge would also sit where the system's swipe home begins, which the held "One place, one gesture" forbids. The held "The keyboard covers nothing being worked on" covers the keyboard, and this claim covers the device's other parts. Check: on a phone with a camera cut-out and a home indicator, and in a phone's browser with its bars showing, is any text or control on this screen under either? Sources: Apple Human Interface Guidelines, Layout (Guides and safe areas; Visual hierarchy: Differentiate controls from content; Adaptability: Design a layout that adapts gracefully and consistently), `reference/hig/layout.md`; Kowalski, Feeling Native On Mobile, emilkowalski/skills (2026) (3. Layout has the wrong height; 7. Content stops at the notch), `reference/kowalski/skills/mobile-native/SKILL.source.md`; Vercel, Web Interface Guidelines (2025) (Layout: Respect safe areas), `reference/vercel/README.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A music app's mini player along the foot of the screen. Pass: Play sits above the home indicator, and the player's colour runs to the bottom edge. Fail: the same Play sits half under the home indicator, where a thumb meant for it swipes the app away.

### Felt before heard

Called for by: sound. Cross-check: Nielsen 1, Visibility of system status.

**Where the app answers an action by touch or sound as well as on the screen, the answer is felt by default: a haptic of its own is on from the start, and a sound of its own plays only once the person has turned it on.** A sound reaches everyone nearby and is lost in noise, while a haptic reaches only the hand that acted. In an eight-day study, 9 adults aged 23 to 32 used a touchscreen game on a handheld tablet wherever they liked. Each spent two days on the screen alone, two on sound and two on touch, and then chose for themselves: touch for 82 per cent of their games, sound for 18 and the screen alone never. Seven of the nine said they chose touch because it disturbs others less, though they were free to wear headphones, and the thesis concludes that "tactile feedback should be the default setting, as most people preferred it". Touch was also the sturdier of the two in noise. Typing on an underground train, people stopped gaining from sound at about 94 dB, but kept their gain from touch until about 100 dB. A haptic is lost where the device is away from the body, "If the device is not in contact with the user's skin, tactile feedback may not be detected", so the screen still carries the whole of every answer, as the held "A sound says its source" requires. That claim also keeps the rest: one event per sound or haptic, and haptics the person can turn off. The evidence is weak. It comes from a few young university staff and students on 2008 hardware, and it measures what they chose, not how well they did with it. The platform's own controls play their sounds as the platform sets them. Check: for each answer this screen gives beyond the screen, is it a haptic, and does any sound of the app's own play before the person has turned sounds on? Sources: Hoggan, Crossmodal Audio and Tactile Interaction with Mobile Touchscreens, PhD thesis, University of Glasgow (2010) (chapter 7, 7.5 Experiment 6: The Long-Term Use of CrossTrainer; 7.5.2.5 Modality Preference and Location of Interaction; 7.5.2.7 Participant Preference; 7.5.3 Discussion), `reference/papers/hoggan-2010-crossmodal-audio-and-tactile-interaction.md`; Hoggan, Crossmodal Audio and Tactile Interaction with Mobile Touchscreens, PhD thesis, University of Glasgow (2010) (chapter 6, 6.6.6 Discussion; 6.7 Conclusions), `reference/papers/hoggan-2010-crossmodal-audio-and-tactile-interaction.md`; Hoggan, Crossmodal Audio and Tactile Interaction with Mobile Touchscreens, PhD thesis, University of Glasgow (2010) (chapter 8, 8.6 Guidelines: Modality Preference; Social Situation; 8.6.1 Guideline Limitations/Caveats: Device Location versus User Location), `reference/papers/hoggan-2010-crossmodal-audio-and-tactile-interaction.md`; Hoggan et al., Audio or tactile feedback: which modality when?, CHI (2009) (abstract), `reference/papers/hoggan-2009-audio-or-tactile-feedback.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A banking app's Send button. Pass: on Send the phone gives one short tap and the screen reads Sent £20 to Sam, with no sound unless Sounds is turned on in the app. Fail: the same button plays a loud cash-register chime by default, heard by everyone on the bus.

## Delight: Make it human.

### One miss does not zero what was done

Called for by: numbers. Cross-check: Nielsen 9, Help users recognize, diagnose, and recover from errors.

**Where a screen counts a run of days or of times in a row, what the person has done stays shown beside the run, so one miss is not read as the loss of everything before it.** In posts on X about five top-grossing health and fitness apps, the authors note "A few posts" on losing a run, such as "I missed one day and it's back at zero", and observe that "rather than focusing on the progress that had been made until that point, the user was preoccupied by what had not been achieved". In four online focus groups of 18 adults aged 60 to 83, the same device split people: one said "I got fanatical about continuing my streak", while those new to such features found them "childish and condescending". The evidence is a few posts and what a focus group said, not a measured effect; the claim governs how a run is shown, not whether the app keeps one. Check: where this screen counts a run, what does it show the day after one miss, and is what was done before still in view? Sources: Sheen et al., Living well? The unintended consequences of highly popular commercial fitness apps through social listening using Machine-Assisted Topic Analysis: Evidence from X, British Journal of Health Psychology (2025) (Theme 4: Emotional and psychological impacts of logging daily activity; Discussion: Implications of quantification-of-the-self on human experience and behaviour), `reference/studies/PMC12541294.md`; Berry et al., Designing an mHealth App to Encourage Uptake of Muscle-Strengthening Exercise in Older Adults: Co-Design Focus Group Study, JMIR Aging (2026) (Results: Theme 2: Motivation and Engagement Through App Features), `reference/studies/PMC13022557.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A language-learning app's home screen the day after a missed lesson. Pass: it reads 99 of the last 100 days practised, with Current run: 0 days beneath. Fail: the same screen shows only a grey flame and 0 days.

### An entry is taken without a verdict

Called for by: entry. Cross-check: Nielsen 2, Match between the system and the real world.

**What a person records of what they did is taken as they give it, and the screen's answer says what was recorded and what it now holds, never whether it was good; a verdict on a true entry teaches the person to leave the next one out.** In posts on X about five top-grossing health and fitness apps, read qualitatively from 13,799 filtered for negative sentiment, the authors found people expressing "a desire to avoid logging their food for the day or to input data that was not accurate", shame ("I'm ashamed I just ate dominos"), and "frustration that the app had sent a notification reminding them to lower their sugar intake after they logged eating a banana". Many of the posts are jokes, and the authors read them as reports of feeling, not as a measure of what was left out. A query of a likely slip, under the held "An entry far from the usual is queried", asks whether the entry is what was meant; a verdict judges what was done, and the held "Guidance in place, not an error message" already keeps blame off a wrong entry, where this claim keeps it off a true one. Check: after each entry this screen takes, what does its answer say beyond what was recorded and what that leaves? Source: Sheen et al., Living well? The unintended consequences of highly popular commercial fitness apps through social listening using Machine-Assisted Topic Analysis: Evidence from X, British Journal of Health Psychology (2025) (Theme 4: Emotional and psychological impacts of logging daily activity; Discussion: Implications of quantification-of-the-self on human experience and behaviour), `reference/studies/PMC12541294.md`.

Example, not part of the claim: A budgeting app, after a £40 takeaway is entered. Pass: it reads Takeaway £40 added to Eating out, £60 left this month. Fail: the same entry is answered with Another takeaway? Try cooking at home.
